Verified by `Spark.Docs.Verify`, whose citation check now scans `.cs`, `.csproj`, `.props`, `.targets`, `.yml` and `.gitattributes` as well as Markdown — which is why it can see build-file citations at all. **Note what no test can catch:** a citation naming a record that exists but is about something else. Both original citations were exactly that, and an existence check passed them. Semantic correctness of a citation is a review matter |
| E1-T30 | Write the three remaining agent definitions: `ui-shell`, `viewport`, `reviewer` | Done | **Written.** `ui-shell`, `viewport` and `reviewer` exist under `.claude/agents/`, so file ownership is disjoint across all eight. **Needed by M2**, which is the first milestone that touches `Spark.UI`, `Spark.Desktop` and `Spark.Viewport` — the three areas nobody currently owns. `viewport`'s definition carries a rule the others do not: the project is Avalonia-free ([NOTES.md N6](NOTES.md)), and nothing fails loudly when that is broken. Ownership stays disjoint per the table in [AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md) |
| E1-T31 | `global.json` pinning the SDK and selecting Microsoft.Testing.Platform | Done | `10.0.100` with `rollForward: latestFeature`, plus `"test": { "runner": "Microsoft.Testing.Platform" }`. **Not a preference.** The .NET 10 SDK has removed the VSTest bridge, so a VSTest-shaped test project fails at build rather than degrading quietly. `actions/setup-dotnet` reads this file in CI, so the SDK is pinned in one place for everyone. See [NOTES.md N11](NOTES.md) |
| E1-T32 | `.editorconfig` naming rule: private `const` fields are PascalCase | Done | Added 2026-08-27 after the geometry value layer found the gap — `dotnet format` was failing IDE1006 on `Tolerance`'s private constants. **CA1802 actively pushes code towards `const`**, so requiring an underscore on private consts would have had two analyzers arguing with each other over the same field. It must precede the underscore rule in the file: naming rules are evaluated in file order and the first match wins. Sits beside the existing private-`static readonly` PascalCase rule, and the two together make the underscore itself informative — it marks something that can change ([NOTES.md N16](NOTES.md)) |
| E1-T33 | Suppress RS0026 in `.editorconfig`, with the reasoning recorded | Done | Settled 2026-08-27. RS0026 forbids two overloads of one name both carrying optional parameters, to protect against a future **source-breaking** change in a published library. Spark publishes nothing (ADR-0019), the overloads it flagged differ in a **required** parameter type so every call site resolves unambiguously, and genuine ambiguity is caught by the compiler as CS0121 regardless. Mangling a good API — `Contains(point)` and `Contains(box)`, each with an optional tolerance — to satisfy a rule aimed at a constraint Spark does not have would be the tail wagging the dog. **RS0016 stays at its default error severity**; that is the rule that matters ([NOTES.md N17](NOTES.md)) |

## E2 — Geometry kernel

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E2-T1 | Value structs: `Point3d`, `Vector3d`, `Point2d`, `Vector2d`, `UV`, `Quaternion` | Done | **`Quaternion` landed 2026-08-29 and completes the row.** It carries `FromAxisAngle`, `FromRotationBetween`, composition, `OfVector`/`OfPoint` named to match `Transform`, `Slerp`, `ToAxisAngle`, `ToTransform`, `TryGetInverse` and `IsSameRotation` — the last because **`q` and `-q` are the same rotation and component equality says otherwise**, which is a trap worth putting in the API rather than in a comment. `default(Quaternion)` is not a rotation and every geometric member throws on it, on the `default(Plane)` precedent. **Deliberately excluded and said so on the type:** matrix-to-quaternion extraction, which needs a policy for a matrix that is nearly a rotation, and Euler angles, which have twelve conventions and no defensible default. Verified against `Transform.Rotation` by example and by property across nine decades of scale, and the Slerp short-path branch was mutation-tested. The five others landed on 2026-08-27: `Point3d`, `Vector3d`, `Point2d`, `Vector2d` and `UV`, all `readonly struct`, all `IEquatable<T>`, all passed by `in`, with `Equals`/`GetHashCode`/`ToString` overridden and an exact `operator ==`. `Quaternion` **does not exist**, which is why this row is not `Done`. **`Rgba` has been removed from this task**: it is a display concern, and the kernel carries no styling or screen awareness at all, so it belongs beside `Appearance` in `Spark.Api` (**E5**). Listing it here was an inconsistency in the original plan, which ruled out styling in the kernel on one page and then listed a colour type on another; it was caught in review rather than by either rule. Point-versus-vector separation is enforced by explicit conversion in both directions, and there is deliberately no `point + point`. Documented in [concepts.geometry-basics](help/concepts/geometry-basics.md) |
| E2-T2 | `Transform`: 4×4, compose, inverse, scale, translate, rotate, mirror | Done | Landed 2026-08-27. A 4×4 `readonly struct` with `M00`…`M33`, a `[row, column]` indexer, `Determinant`, `TryGetInverse`, `Multiply`/`operator *`, and factories for `Identity`, `Translation`, `Rotation` (about an axis, and about an axis through a center), `Scale` (uniform, per-axis, and about a center), `Mirror`, `PlaneToPlane` and `ChangeBasis`. `OfPoint`, `OfVector` and `OfBoundingBox` apply it, with the vector overload applying only the linear part — the whole reason points and vectors are separate types. `IsIdentity`, `IsAffine` and `IsRigid` are tolerance-taking predicates. `VTransform` was discarded outright as planned and this was written from nothing |
| E2-T3 | `Plane` and `CoordinateSystem` | Done | Landed 2026-08-27. `Plane` carries an origin plus a right-handed orthonormal frame with `XAxis × YAxis == Normal`, factories `FromOriginNormal`, `FromOriginXAxisYAxis` and `FromThreePoints`, the three world planes, and `DistanceTo`/`ClosestPoint`/`Project`/`Flipped`/`To2d`/`To3d`/`Contains`/`IsCoplanar`. `CoordinateSystem` adds `FromOrigin`, `FromOriginZAxis`, `FromOriginXAxisYAxis`, `FromPlane`, `Identity`, and `ToWorld`/`ToLocal` for both points and vectors plus `ToPlane`/`ToTransform`. **`default(Plane)` is not a plane and says so:** every geometric member throws `InvalidOperationException`, while `IsValid`, equality, hashing and formatting answer on any value. That behaviour is the direct product of the review — the first version returned `true` from `default(Plane).Contains(anyPoint)` |
| E2-T4 | `Tolerance { Linear, Angular, RelativeEpsilon }` with `ForScale` | Done | Landed 2026-08-27, and reshaped as planned rather than extracted. Explicit, passed, never ambient (ADR-0010). `in Tolerance tol = default` with a zero backing `Linear` as the "use the default" sentinel is on **every** tolerance-taking predicate in the assembly. `ForScale` and `Scaled` are floored at 1e-15 so a derived tolerance can never collapse back into the sentinel. `AreEqual`, `IsLessThan` and `IsGreaterThan` are a genuine three-way partition **because all three compare the same single subtraction against the same single threshold** — the earlier version used two roundings that disagreed by an ulp on the boundary, and `2.0` against `2.000001` fell into none of the three buckets while the docs invited callers to depend on the partition. `IsNegligible(separation, scale)` is the one hybrid absolute/relative rule every geometric `EqualsWithin` in the kernel routes through. The default epsilons are **private** consts, not public ones: a public `const` bakes into every consuming assembly at compile time, which is an ADR-0019 hazard hiding inside a constant |
| E2-T5 | `Angle` readonly struct | Done | Landed 2026-08-27 (ADR-0011). Radians stored, `FromDegrees`/`FromRadians` construction, **no implicit conversion from `double`**, full operator set, `IComparable<Angle>`, `Normalised` into `[0, 2π)` and `NormalisedSigned` into `(−π, π]`. It appears in **every** public angular signature in the assembly — `Vector3d.Rotate`, `AngleTo`, `SignedAngleTo`, `Vector2d.Rotate`, `Transform.Rotation` and `Tolerance.Angular` — which was the acceptance criterion, checked member by member against `PublicAPI.Unshipped.txt`. Non-finite input is accepted and propagates rather than throwing, so an arithmetic accident surfaces where it happened rather than one call later |
| E2-T6 | `Interval` and `BoundingBox` | Done | Landed 2026-08-27. `Interval` has `Min`/`Max`/`Mid`/`Length`, `Includes`, `Clamp`, `Normalise`/`Denormalise`, `Expanded`, `Union`, a nullable `Intersect`, `Reversed` and `MakeIncreasing`. **`IsValid` means "both bounds finite" and nothing else** — an earlier version also required `Min <= Max`, which would have made the guard everybody writes without thinking, `if (!domain.IsValid) throw`, reject every reversed curve domain the moment M3 arrived. Direction is asked about with `IsDecreasing`. `BoundingBox` has `Min`/`Max`/`Center`/`Diagonal`/`Volume`/`Area`, `Corners`, `Contains` for points and boxes, `Intersects`, `Union`, `Inflated`, `ClosestPoint`, `Empty` and `FromPoints` over both a span and an `IEnumerable`. `Includes` and `Intersects` reject `NaN`, which they did not before the review |
| E2-T7 | The curve contract | Done | `Domain`, `PointAt`, `TangentAt`, `NormalAt`, `PlaneAt`, `CoordinateSystemAt`, `Length`, `LengthAt`, `ParameterAtLength`, `PointAtLength`, `DivideEqually`, `DivideByLength`, `Tessellate`, `Reversed`, `Trimmed`, `TransformedBy`, `BoundingBox`, `IsClosed`, `StartPoint`, `EndPoint`. **Arc-length reparameterisation is in the contract rather than after it**, per [E2-T41](#e2--geometry-kernel) and [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.2](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#32-curves--11-types-187-members-138-reachable): analytic on the five constant-speed types, Gauss–Legendre plus Newton on the ellipse. **Named exclusions:** no `ClosestPoint`, no `Split`, no `CurvatureAt`, no `IsPlanar`, no `ToNurbsCurve`, and no value equality on curves — each is M3 and each is stated on the type rather than left to be discovered. C2VGeometry's `ICurve` is discarded as an interface but kept as a node checklist |
| E2-T8 | `Line`, `Arc`, `Circle`, `EllipseCurve` | Done | Four sealed immutable types over one `Curve` base whose constructor is `private protected`, so the set of curve types is closed to the assembly. `Arc` normalises a negative sweep by flipping its plane, which keeps every domain increasing; `Arc.FromThreePoints` takes its direction from the circumcircle's orientation rather than from a branch ([NOTES N20](NOTES.md)). `EllipseCurve` collapses Dynamo's `Ellipse` and `EllipseArc`, and is the only curve here whose speed varies — which is what makes the arc-length tests able to fail. **`VArc`'s eight `From*` algorithms were not harvested**: three constructions are built and the other five are M3, listed rather than assumed |
| E2-T9 | `PolyLine`, `PolyCurve` and `PolyCurveBuilder` | Done | `PolyLine` is Dynamo's `Polygon` when closed and its `Rectangle` through a factory, per [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.2](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#32-curves--11-types-187-members-138-reachable). `PolyCurve.FromJoinedCurves` **takes its join tolerance as a parameter** (ADR-0010) and refuses a chain that does not meet within it, naming the index and the gap; nested polycurves are flattened so the parameterisation does not depend on assembly order. **No `PolyCurveBuilder`**: nothing yet accumulates a chain incrementally, and a mutable builder that no caller needs is a mutable type in the kernel for nothing. It returns when curve intersection needs it at M3 |
| E2-T10 | `NurbsCurve`: the type, evaluation and derivatives | Done | M3. `VSpline` is **discarded** — Catmull-Rom is the wrong basis for a kernel. Landed 2026-08-30. Control points, weights and a `KnotVector`, stored and evaluated in **homogeneous coordinates** with one projection at the end: evaluating the rational form directly is slower, less accurate, and makes the derivative formulae unreadable. Weights must be **positive**, refused at construction, because a zero or negative weight puts a pole inside the domain where the curve runs to infinity at a parameter that looks like any other. `ComputeLength` and `TessellationSeedSpans` work **one knot span at a time**, because a NURBS curve's speed is generally discontinuous at an interior knot and Gauss–Legendre across a corner is quietly wrong. Checked against `Line` and `Arc` rather than against itself — a degree-1 curve *is* a polyline and a rational quadratic with weights `1, cos(θ/2), 1` *is* a circular arc, exactly |
| E2-T50 | `KnotVector`: invariants, span search, basis functions | Done | Landed 2026-08-30 **before any curve**, because `Curve` is abstract over six members and a `NurbsCurve` cannot exist half-built — so the first slice is the piece underneath it, and it is also where NURBS goes wrong. Owns the invariants (non-decreasing, finite, `knots = controlPoints + degree + 1`, interior multiplicity at most `degree`, end multiplicity at most `degree + 1`, non-empty domain), `Domain` — the **interior** end knots, not the first and last — `IsClamped`, `FindSpan` (A2.1) and `BasisFunctions` (A2.2). Equality is **exact**, because a knot vector is data and a tolerant `Equals` breaks transitivity; the tolerant question belongs to `Multiplicity`, which takes a `Tolerance` because a clamped vector produced by arithmetic rarely has bitwise-equal end knots. The reflection round-trip test ([E2-T31](#e2--geometry-kernel)) caught it the moment it existed |
| E2-T51 | Knot insertion, and exact `Trimmed` | Done | Landed 2026-08-30. `WithKnotInserted` is Boehm's algorithm on the **homogeneous** control points — blending the projected ones is right for a non-rational curve, wrong for a rational one, and produces a curve visibly close to the original and not equal to it, which is the hardest kind of wrong to notice. `Trimmed` raises both ends to full multiplicity and keeps the control-point window between them, so it is **exact** rather than approximate, and it keeps the requested parameter range as its domain so a caller's existing parameters still mean something. The test that proves it is the one that says **nothing changed** |
| E2-T52 | `Curve.ClosestPoint` on every curve type | Done | Landed 2026-08-30, and it landed on **`Curve`** rather than on `NurbsCurve` — there was no closest-point anywhere in the assembly, so every curve type gained one at once. A sweep **per span** (`SpanBoundaries`, overridden by `PolyLine`, `PolyCurve` and `NurbsCurve`) to bracket the right basin, golden section to narrow it without derivatives, then Newton on `(C(t) − P) · C′(t) = 0`, every iterate clamped to the bracket. `Line` overrides with the closed-form projection, which is the cross-check the general search is measured against. **The `E2-T33` property is the whole test** and it holds over eight curve types. A first version swept uniformly in *parameter* and answered a polyline's long segment from a different segment entirely; its own property test missed it because every probe was far from the curve |
| E2-T53 | Degree elevation | Done | Landed 2026-08-30 by **Bézier decomposition**: raise every interior knot to full multiplicity, elevate each segment with the one-line blend, reassemble. The direct algorithm is faster and considerably longer, and all its extra complexity is in *avoiding* the decomposition — a trade worth making with a benchmark, later. **Exact but not minimal**: nothing lowers the multiplicities again, so a curve smooth across a knot comes back with more control points than it needs. That is a representation cost, not a geometric one, and removing it is [E2-T55](#e2--geometry-kernel) |
| E2-T54 | Interpolation and approximation through points | Done | Landed 2026-08-30–31. **Interpolation** uses chord-length parameters, de Boor's averaged knots and a Gaussian solve with partial pivoting: chord length rather than uniform because uniform produces visible overshoot on unevenly spaced points, and the averaged knots are what guarantee the Schoenberg–Whitney condition rather than merely a square system. **Approximation** is least squares with the two end points pinned and taken out of the system, solved through the normal equations, reusing the same parameters and solver. **The caller states a control-point count, not a tolerance** — that overload is [E2-T56](#e2--geometry-kernel) and is a step rather than a parameter. Everything here is measured **geometrically**; comparing two curves at the same parameter cannot converge even for a perfect fit, and doing it that way first produced an error that plateaued and looked exactly like a broken solver |
| E2-T55 | Knot removal | Done | Landed 2026-08-31. The inverse of insertion, and **the only operation in this family allowed to change the curve** — which is precisely why it needs a `Tolerance` and a stated rule for what *unchanged enough to drop a knot* means. Until it exists, [E2-T53](#e2--geometry-kernel)'s output is exact but not minimal, and a curve that has been elevated or refined carries control points it does not need. **The deviation is measured rather than bounded**: the textbook check uses Wolters' algebraic bound mid-recurrence and reports success without ever measuring, which makes the tolerance the caller passed not quite the tolerance they got. Here the candidate is built, the two curves are sampled against each other, and the removal is kept only if the measured deviation is inside. `Reduced` measures every candidate against the **original**, so a hundred removals each just inside tolerance cannot accumulate past it. Removal is attempted and **refused**, never performed badly, and the count returned is how a caller learns which happened |
| E2-T56 | Fit to a stated tolerance | Done | Landed 2026-08-31. The friendly signature — *fit these points within 0.1 mm* — built on [E2-T54](#e2--geometry-kernel). **The loop is the whole difficulty, not the algebra**: raising the control-point count until the worst deviation fits terminates on noisy data only at one control point per sample, which silently returns an interpolation dressed as a fit. The search caps at `points.Count - 1` and **reports**: `Fits` comes back false with the deviation actually achieved, and the caller still gets the best available curve. It **keeps the best measured result rather than the last**, because more control points do not always fit better — as the count nears the point count the system is nearly square and the normal equations ill-conditioned, and on a fifty-point wave the deviation falls to 0.0037 at forty and rises to 0.33 at forty-nine |
| E2-T57 | `Split` as its own operation | Done | Landed 2026-08-31. One curve into two at a parameter. [E2-T51](#e2--geometry-kernel) already does the hard half — splitting is knot insertion to full multiplicity followed by taking each side — so this is mostly the API and the property that the two halves rejoined equal the original, which `E2-T33` names and which is now asserted directly: same total length, meeting exactly at the cut, and every sampled point of the original on whichever half it falls in |
| E2-T58 | `By` becomes `From` on every factory, in both layers | Done | **Asked for by the client**, who chose *nodes and geometry both* when shown why one layer alone would not do: a code block calls the **geometry** type - `Circle.FromCenterRadius` in a block is `Spark.Geometry.Circle`, and the node of the same name is a thin facade over it - so renaming only the library would have the canvas and the code block disagree about the same call, and every node's *In a code block* help line contradict its own node name. **32 distinct factory names, 715 replacements across 115 files**, 60 of them in `DemoGraphs.cs`, which is production code rather than tests. **Four infix `By` methods are untouched** - `Curve.DivideByLength`, `Curve.TrimByParameter`, `Number.RangeByCount`, `Number.RangeByCountAndStep` - because the client said *constructor method name* and there the word is doing its ordinary English work; `Spark.Api.NumberRange.ByCount` and its two siblings stay for the same reason, being the plumbing behind those nodes. **Node kind and constructor dedup could not move**: `NodeImporter.InferKind` already read `By`, `From` and `Create` as equivalent, which is why 57 methods changed name without one node appearing or disappearing - the library still reports 138. **Each renamed node carries a `[SparkNodeAlias]`** ([E3-T23](#e3--the-file-format)); the geometry methods need none, because nothing serialises a geometry method name. **The three example graphs were run *unedited* first** and evaluated with zero diagnostics, which is the only real proof the alias works, and were then healed to the new keys. **The sweep had to be held back from three places**: `JOURNAL.md` and `NOTES.md` are historical records and rewriting them would falsify what was decided when, and the alias attribute's own example and tests must keep spelling the *old* name or they assert nothing. Two latent test defects surfaced: the camel-hump expectations were data (`CBCR` became `CFCR`), and `AGeometryTypeFromAPackageIsTheHostsType` took *the first `Point.*` node* and had been relying on `ByCoordinates` sorting ahead of `Point.X` - it names the node it means now. ADR-0004 is **amended rather than contradicted** |
| E2-T59 | A constructor beside every kernel factory | Done | **Asked for by the client**, who typed `new Circle(center, 10)` into a code block and was told there was no such constructor, then asked for the general case: *for all Geometry elements, whatever constructors are available in Library, all those should be available in codeblock (C#) too*. A code block **is** C#, and in C# one says `new`; the kernel reached its geometry through named factories, which is what a node needs and not what a person writing C# reaches for first. **Surveyed by reflection before a line was written** rather than guessed at - 28 factories had no constructor - and the survey is now the test rather than a throwaway: `ConstructorParityTests` diffs the assembly against itself in **both** directions, so a factory added next year with no constructor is a red build naming itself, and an exemption that has quietly been fixed is also a red build. That two-way shape is `NodeLibraryCoverageTests`' lesson applied a second time. **26 constructors, and every one forwards to its factory** - a struct assigns `this = Factory(...)`; a class cannot, so each gained one private copy constructor and reads `: this(Factory(...))`. The arithmetic stays in the factory, which remains its only copy, so the two cannot drift. **Two are deliberately absent and say why in the test**: `Angle.FromDegrees` and `FromRadians` are both `(double)`, and `Plane.FromOriginXAxisYAxis` and `FromOriginNormalXAxis` are both `(Point3d, Vector3d, Vector3d)` while disagreeing about what the second vector is - a call whose meaning depends on which of two things the author had in mind is worse than no call, and both would fail by tilting rather than by throwing. **`NurbsCurve`'s copy constructor takes the fields across rather than going back through a public one**, because handing the weights back in turns a non-rational curve into a rational one carrying weights that all happen to be 1. **The node count did not move** - still 138 - which is the check that matters: the importer sweeps `Spark.Nodes.Core` and not the kernel, so a count that had moved would have meant ADR-0004's dedup rule missed one and the library gained duplicates |
| E2-T60 | `Centre` becomes `Center`, everywhere | Done | **Asked for by the client**, who was shown that Spark spelled it both ways and answered *keep it 'Center' only*. **776 replacements across 110 files**, plus the two `PublicAPI.Shipped.txt` files, done as one case-sensitive word swap so `centres`, `centred` and `centreline` moved with the rest. **Four node keys changed** - `Circle.FromCentreRadius`, `Circle.FromCentreNormalRadius`, `Arc.FromCentreStartPointSweepAngle` and `BoundingBox.Centre` - and each carries a new `[SparkNodeAlias]`, so a circle node now answers to three names across two renames and every file that ever named it still opens. **Only keys need aliases**: a wire is stored as `"port": 1`, an index, so renaming the `centre` parameter to `center` moved every port label without touching a saved wire. **Held back from four places** - `JOURNAL.md` and `NOTES.md` are historical records, `SparkNodeAliasAttribute`'s own example quotes a real alias that keeps the old spelling, and `NodeAliasTests`' fixtures must go on spelling the old name or they assert nothing. **The three example graphs were opened *unedited* first** and evaluated with zero diagnostics, which is the only real proof an alias works, and were regenerated afterwards - `DemoGraphs` derives node ids from the seed name, so renaming `sphereCentre` moved ids in `surfaces.spark` too, which is the mechanism working rather than failing. **That proof used to be disposable**, so it is now `ShippedAliasTests`: every alias in the real library resolves to a live node, none shadows one, and the seven renamed keys are named individually. It goes red when an alias is deleted, which was checked by deleting one. **Two documentation defects surfaced that predate this row**: `lacing.md` and `design-language.md` still taught `Circle.ByCenterRadius`, a node that has not existed since [E2-T58](#e2--geometry-kernel), and `PRD` FR-27's camel-hump example was arithmetically impossible - `cfcr` is C-F-C-R and cannot match `ByCenterRadius`. The library still reports **138** nodes |
| E2-T61 | `Brep.Join` puts several models into one, as separate shells | Done | **Written for `E9-T15`**: a viewport showing three solids has to become one STEP file, and the interchange writer takes one model. **A join is not a union, and the difference is the whole reason it exists.** A boolean union asks the kernel what the *combined solid* is - it intersects the parts, removes what is inside, and can refuse on geometry that is merely near-tangent. A join asserts nothing about how the parts relate: it puts three solids in one model the way three solids sit in one STEP file, and therefore cannot fail. **A model has always been able to hold several shells** - reading a STEP file with three solids in it produces exactly this - so nothing here is a new shape, only a way to build one from parts. **Nothing is welded**: two parts sharing a face keep two copies of it. Merging is `Sew`'s job and needs a tolerance; this is index arithmetic and needs none. **One part is returned as it came rather than copied**, which is what keeps a single kernel-held solid *resident* and therefore what keeps an export of one solid a solid. **Eight offsets, and getting one wrong produces a model with the right number of everything that walks to the wrong place** - so the tests assert the geometry as well as the counts: the second box's corners are still a hundred units away, its faces still name surfaces the first box did not have, and every face-loop-trim-edge-vertex chain resolves. Nine tests, plus a STEP round trip through the real kernel |
| E2-T11 | Curve/curve intersection | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - curve/curve intersection, in the kernel and as two nodes.** Step C: `Curve.IntersectWith(curve, other)` returns the points and `Curve.OverlapWith(curve, other)` each shared stretch as a piece of the first curve, trimmed to its overlap. Help topics and code examples came from the live library by rule, so the coverage tests pass by construction; the node library's `PublicAPI.Unshipped.txt` took the RS0016 entries. 3 tests in `CurveIntersectionNodeTests`, through the engine: a line through a circle gives two points, two lines on one line give their shared stretch, curves apart give an empty list. Returning no points fails the first; cutting the overlap at the other curve's parameters fails the second. **The whole row**: closed forms for lines, circles and arcs in 3D (A), the sampled-and-refined general case checked against them (B1), one public entry point (B2), and the nodes (C). **Step B2 done 2026-09-11 - one public entry point.** `Curve.IntersectWith(Curve other, in Tolerance tolerance = default)` answers in closed form when `AnalyticCurveIntersection` has one and by the sampled path otherwise. `CurveIntersectionPoint` and `CurveOverlap` became public record structs, and `CurveIntersections` a plain sealed class - so it publishes its two lists and `IsEmpty`, and not a record's copy constructor and equality contract. The `PublicAPI.Unshipped.txt` entries were taken from the RS0016 messages themselves rather than written by hand. 4 tests in `CurveIntersectWithTests` on the routing: a closed-form pair gets the closed form's answer to the last bit, an ellipse gets the sampled path's, a null curve is refused, and curves apart are empty. Always taking the sampled path fails the first. **The first gate run failed, and was right to**: `GeometryJsonTests.EveryPublicGeometryTypeHasASample` holds every public type in `Spark.Geometry` to a JSON sample or an argued exclusion, and the three result types had become public with neither. They are excluded, each with its reason - an answer to a question about two curves, recomputed from the curves, which are what is saved. **Step C**: the nodes. **Step B1 done 2026-09-11 - the general intersector, for any two curves.** `GeneralCurveIntersection.Intersect` samples each curve into 128 chords over its own domain, puts the second curve's chord boxes - each grown by a tenth of its length and the tolerance - into the `BoundingVolumeHierarchy` from `E2-T15`, and asks it which chords are near each chord of the first; every near pair starts a Gauss-Newton refinement of `a(s) - b(t)`, kept only if it lands inside both domains within the linear tolerance. Where the tangents are parallel the step is taken along the first curve and the second follows by closest point, which carries a tangency or an overlap to a point on both. Candidates within the tolerance are one point; a run of candidates joined by curve that stays on the other is a touch if it spans under one sample spacing - what refinement near a tangency produces - and an overlap otherwise, its ends found by bisection. **An overlap shorter than one sample spacing is reported as a point**: the price of sampling, stated. **A touch on a closed curve's seam** is found from both ends of its domain - circles touching at angle zero came back as two points on the first run - so the last run and the first are joined across the seam when they are short and the curve between them lies on the other. Still internal; step B2 makes it public. Tests in `GeneralCurveIntersectionTests`: on all 16 closed-form pairs the general path agrees with step A (to the tolerance, or its square root where the curves only touch), plus a line through an ellipse, two NURBS curves crossing where they share a point, and a straight NURBS curve overlapping a line. No refinement fails 3, runs never joined fails 7 - every tangency and every overlap - and every run reported as a point fails 4. Keeping duplicates survived, and rightly: the run grouping merges them anyway, so the explicit merge is an economy - one distance instead of three closest-point queries - and is commented as one. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - curve/curve intersection in closed form, for lines, circles and arcs.** The seed the row names is in neither this tree nor its docs (`CurveGeometry` appears only as the topology member `Edge.CurveGeometry`), so it is built from the curve API. `AnalyticCurveIntersection.TryIntersect` answers with `CurveIntersections` - points carrying a parameter on each curve, and overlaps as a pair of intervals, the second decreasing when the curves run opposite ways - and returns null for a pair with no closed form, which is the general case's cue. **In three dimensions**: skew lines, parallel and colinear lines (an overlap, or one point when they only meet end to end), a line in a circle's plane or piercing it, circles in one plane (crossing, touching outside and inside, concentric) and in crossing planes (where the planes' shared line meets both), and arcs, which answer only where they reach and overlap by the stretch two arcs of one circle share. Tangency is decided by comparing distances to the tolerance before any square root, so a grazing line is one point, not two a rounding error apart. **Internal for now**: the public API does not move until step B puts the general case behind one entry point. 19 tests in `AnalyticCurveIntersectionTests`, every answer checked with `Curve.PointAt` on both curves - which the intersector does not use to find them. Skew lines taken as meeting, an arc taken as its whole circle, and inside tangency put on the wrong side each fail their case - the last only after the test was made to ask both ways round: with the larger circle first, the two branches agree, so the mutation survived the first run. **Step B**: the general intersector, with these as its oracle; **step C**: the node. Earlier note follows. Extract `CurveIntersection` and `CurveGeometry` as the **2D seed**: the analytic solutions are correct in-plane. The 3D cases are new work |
| E2-T12 | Curve offset and fillet | Done | Landed 2026-08-31 as `CurveOffset`, separate from `Curve` because their honest signatures do not fit on it — an offset needs a plane to happen in, and a fillet is a relationship between two curves rather than a property of either. **The offset of a NURBS curve is not a NURBS curve**, so `Offset` takes a `Tolerance` and returns an approximation built on [E2-T56](#e2--geometry-kernel), and reports whether the answer was exact. Lines, circles and arcs *are* answered exactly — a fitted circle is a circle only to within a tolerance, and everything downstream asking *is this an arc?* would start saying no. **Self-intersection is not repaired**: trimming offset loops needs curve-curve intersection, which is not built, so the result is the true offset locus, loops included, and the remarks say so. **Fillet is two lines only**, and deliberately: a general curve-curve fillet is a tangency problem needing intersection to find the corner at all, and approximating it now would produce something that looks like the feature and is not. The lines come back trimmed, because a fillet leaving the original corner in place is not what anybody asked for |
| E2-T13 | `Spark.Geometry.Planar`: `Point2d`, `Curve2d`, `Region` | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 on the planar pipeline it delivered; `Curve2d` moved to `E2-T64`.** Steps A and B built `Spark.Geometry.Planar` - `Region`, its four booleans, offset, simplify and centroid, through Clipper2 from one internal file - and closed `E2-T14` with it. **Step C was a decision, not code**: the row says `Curve2d` is *required for BRep trim curves in UV space*, and nothing in the managed kernel would read a trim curve today - trims are topology only, and trimmed faces are tessellated behind the OCCT seam. So `Curve2d` waits for its first consumer, in a row of its own. **Step B done 2026-09-11 - offset and simplify.** `Region.Offset(distance, join, miterLimit)` grows a region, or shrinks it when the distance is negative - far enough and it is empty - with corners joined round, mitred or square (`RegionJoin`); `Region.Simplify(tolerance)` drops the vertices that barely bend an edge. `Region.Centroid()` balances the area, holes included - `E2-T14`, closed with this step. Both through `ClipperBridge`, still the only file that touches Clipper2 (`InflatePaths`, `SimplifyPaths`). **Round corners are held to the region's tolerance**: Clipper's default arc tolerance left a 2 x 2 square grown by one 0.008 short of its quarter circles, so the bridge passes the larger of the region's linear tolerance and a hundred-thousandth of the distance - faithful arcs, and no explosion of vertices on a large offset. Tests in `RegionOffsetTests`, areas as the oracle: a 2 x 2 square grown by one is 4 + 8 + π round and 16 mitred, and shrunk by one is empty; the simplify case uses a vertex a hair off an edge, because making a region already removes exactly collinear points and a test of simplify has to give it something to do. A miter given as a round join, a negative offset that grows, Clipper's default arc tolerance, a simplify that changes nothing, and a hole weighed as solid in the centroid each fail. **FR-60's pipeline is complete**; what remains of the row is **step C, `Curve2d`**, which BRep trims need for their pcurves. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - `Region` and the planar booleans, through Clipper2.** Nothing planar existed beyond the 2D values and `Plane.To2d`/`To3d`. `Spark.Geometry.Planar.Region` is an area in a plane - outer boundaries with holes - made from closed polylines lying in the plane and handed back as closed polylines, so a graph never meets a 2D coordinate. **A hole is decided by nesting**: the caller's loops are normalised with even-odd fill, so a loop inside another is a hole whichever way round it was drawn. Union, intersection, difference and symmetric difference; area; containment. **Coplanar is enough**: a region in the same plane with a turned frame is re-measured into the first's frame, and regions in different planes are refused, since their boolean is not a region. Clipper2 (pinned at 2.0.0, the kernel's one permitted third-party dependency) is referenced for the first time and used from **one internal file**, `Planar/ClipperBridge.cs`, as the package manifest requires; its precision in decimal places comes from the tolerance. **Not yet saved to a file**: a region has no `GeometryJson` form - it is made from polylines, which are. Tests in `RegionTests` with areas as the oracle (two overlapping 2 x 2 squares: 7, 1, 3, 6). Intersection answered as union, a hole counted as inside, different planes combined, and a coplanar region taken in the wrong frame each fail. **Step B**: offset and simplify (FR-60's other two). **Step C**: `Curve2d`, which BRep trims need for their pcurves. Earlier note follows. A supporting layer, **not a peer 2D API**. `Curve2d` is required for BRep trim curves in UV space. Bridged by `Plane.To2d`/`To3d`. Shipping an XY-only `VCircle` beside a 3D `Circle` would double the library and split the type system |
| E2-T14 | Extract the Clipper2-backed boolean, offset and simplify pipeline | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 with `E2-T13` step B.** Everything the row lists is in `Spark.Geometry.Planar`: the boolean, offset and simplify pipeline, and a region's area, containment, offset and **centroid** - `Region.Centroid()`, the signed-area centroid over every loop, so a hole pulls the centre away from itself with no case of its own, and an empty region refuses rather than inventing a centre. Clipper2 is used from one internal file, as the row asks. Tested on an L-shape balancing at (10/6, 5/6) and a square with an off-centre hole balancing at (31/15, 31/15); weighing a hole as solid fails the second. **Taken up 2026-09-11 with `E2-T13` step A, because it is the same work.** The seed it names - C2VGeometry's `VPolygon`, `VPolyline` and `Region` - is not in this tree, so the pipeline was built rather than harvested: `Spark.Geometry.Planar.Region` and its booleans, through Clipper2 from one internal file, and **this is what re-added the Clipper2 `PackageReference`** the row names. Area and containment landed with step A; offset and the centroid come with step B. Earlier note follows. Working and tested already, and Clipper2 is already isolated in one file in C2VGeometry. Keep it that way — [E1-T20](#e1--foundations-build-and-ci) depends on it. Harvest `VPolygon`, `VPolyline` and `Region`'s area, centroid, containment and offset into `Planar`. **This task is what re-adds the Clipper2 `PackageReference`** that [E2-T39](#e2--geometry-kernel) removed; the version stays pinned in `Directory.Packages.props` meanwhile |
| E2-T15 | Extract and promote `RayCaster.cs` and its BVH | Done | **Landed 2026-08-29 as `Ray` and `BoundingVolumeHierarchy`, and the rename is the finding.** C2VGeometry's `RayCaster.cs` bundled three things: a ray, an acceleration structure, and ray-triangle maths. **There are no triangles yet**, so what could be extracted honestly is the first two — and separating them is better anyway, because the hierarchy indexes *boxes* and therefore serves mesh booleans, viewport picking, intersection seeding and `Curve.ClosestPoint` from one implementation rather than four. `Ray` normalises its direction so a parameter is a **distance**, and excludes what is behind the origin, which is the difference that matters for picking. The hierarchy is **immutable and therefore safe to query from many threads**, which `ParallelEvaluationScheduler` requires of anything the evaluator can reach; `FirstHit` prunes on the caller's reported distance rather than on the box, so a large box entered early cannot mask a nearer hit. **The split is a median on the index, not on the coordinate** — a thousand coincident boxes is the input that turns the usual choice into a linked list, and there is a test for exactly that. A surface-area heuristic is the obvious later change and is the one that gives up the depth guarantee. `Curve.ClosestPoint` is now unblocked |
| E2-T16 | Extract and generalise `KDTree` | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - the k-d tree, and `Point.PruneDuplicates` on it.** Step B: `Point3d.PruneDuplicates(points, tolerance = 0)` is the kernel's public door onto the internal tree - the node library must not see the kernel's internals, since the importer treats it as it would a third-party package - and `Point.PruneDuplicates(points, tolerance = 0.001)` is the node, with a typed list port so it sees the whole list. **A point is removed only when one already kept is within the tolerance**, so a chain of points each closer than the tolerance to the next keeps every other one, and the answer depends on nothing but the input's order. 5 tests in `PointPruneDuplicatesTests` - the tree against the O(n^2) loop it replaces on a dense random cloud, the first of each cluster kept in order, the chain, zero removing exact copies only, nonsense refused - and 1 in `PruneDuplicatesNodeTests` through the engine. Letting removed points remove their neighbours fails the chain test; ignoring the tolerance fails three - the cluster, the chain and the scan. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - a k-d tree over points, checked against brute force.** Nothing named `KDTree` existed in `src`, and the seed the row says to extract is not in the docs, so it is built here. **Welding was not a caller**: `Mesh.Welded` already merges through a 27-neighbour spatial hash. The caller the Dynamo inventory names is `Point.PruneDuplicates`. `PointKdTree` (internal, `Spark.Geometry`) is implicit - one index array arranged so the median of every range on its depth's axis sits at the range's middle, placed by median-of-three quickselect - and answers three questions: the nearest point (lower index on a tie), the k nearest (by distance, then index), and everything within a radius (inclusive, ascending indices), so the same cloud and question give the same answer everywhere. Non-finite points and probes are refused. 13 tests in `PointKdTreeTests`, each query against a linear scan over random clouds of a thousand points, plus duplicates, a row and a pile of coincident points, and an empty tree. Pruning the far side too eagerly, building on one axis while searching on three, and leaving the radius boundary out each fail. **Step B**: `Point.PruneDuplicates`. Earlier note follows. Needed for welding, dedup and point queries |
| E2-T17 | The surface contract | Done | `DomainU/V`, `PointAt(u,v)`, `NormalAt`, `DerivativeAt`, `PrincipalCurvatures`, `ClosestPoint`, `IsoCurve`, `Trim`, `Area`, `ToNurbsSurface`. **Landed 2026-08-31**, mirroring `Curve`: sealed types over a `private protected` base, two independent domains neither of which is assumed to be [0, 1], wrapping in a closed direction, and **numeric derivatives with analytic overrides** so a new surface type is correct before it is fast. `IsoCurveU`/`IsoCurveV` return **real `Curve` objects**, so arc length, division and tessellation all work on them without a second implementation. **Two members of the row are deliberately not built and say so on the type**: `Trim` needs the planar layer (`E2-T13`) and `ToNurbsSurface` needs `NurbsSurface` (`E2-T19`) — each is a row, not an omission. **The degeneracy test is the subtle part**: at a sphere's pole `cos v` is 6.1e-17 rather than 0, so a check against zero reports a normal where there is none, and the two derivatives are still perpendicular there, so a cross-product ratio passes it too — the test that works compares one derivative's length against the other's |
| E2-T18 | Analytic surfaces: `PlaneSurface`, `SphericalSurface`, `CylindricalSurface`, `ConicalSurface`, `ToroidalSurface`, `ExtrusionSurface`, `RevolutionSurface`, `RuledSurface` | Done | **All eight landed 2026-08-31**, each with analytic first and second derivatives and a closed-form area where one exists. **Every one is cross-checked three ways**: its area against a formula it does not use, its curvature against the textbook value, and its hand-differentiated derivatives against a central difference — the last being the test that catches a sign error in a cosine, which produces a surface that evaluates perfectly and whose normal points the wrong way. **Two closed forms were deliberately not written.** A cone's area is measured along the slant, not the axis, and the obvious wrong version is out by exactly `sec α`; an extrusion has *no* closed form worth having, because "length × height" is wrong the moment the sweep is not perpendicular to the profile — extrude a line along itself and the honest answer is zero. A sphere, cylinder, cone and torus all **refuse a non-uniform scale** rather than returning the wrong shape, because the kernel has no ellipsoid. **First-class, not NURBS in disguise**, which the row always asked for: required for exact analytic-analytic booleans, and for memory |
| E2-T19 | `NurbsSurface` | Done | M5. **Landed 2026-08-31, and most of it was already written.** A NURBS surface is a tensor product, so its basis functions are the curve's evaluated twice — `BasisDerivatives` moved from `NurbsCurve` onto `KnotVector`, where it belonged, rather than being copied. Weights are held homogeneously once at construction; the bounding box is the control net's, exactly, by the convex-hull property; and every affine transform is exact, which is why the analytic types convert *to* this one. **`ToNurbsSurface` came with it** for plane, cylinder, cone, sphere and torus, all *exact* as rational quadratics — the weight is `cos(θ/2)` and the corner control point is the tangent intersection at `r / cos(θ/2)`, not the arc's midpoint. **The finding is what exact does and does not mean**: the sheet is exact and the parameterisation is not preserved, because a rational quadratic's parameter is a projective function of the angle. The first six conversion tests asserted point-for-point agreement, failed, and were wrong to — they now assert each surface's *implicit equation* over an odd grid, at 1e-9, and one test pins the parameterisation difference so nobody assumes it away ([N48](NOTES.md)) |
| E2-T20 | `Mesh` with indexed vertices, tri and quad faces, optional normals/UVs/colours, lazily built halfedge adjacency | Done | M5. **Landed 2026-08-31.** `MeshFace` is one struct for both, with `D = -1` on a triangle rather than a repeated `C` - the repeat convention makes a degenerate quad and a triangle indistinguishable and gives four edges where there are three. Faces index-check at construction, because the alternative is an index-out-of-range from inside a renderer that names nothing a user could act on. **A quad's normal is Newell's, not the first triangle's**, so a warped quad's normal does not flip when the winding starts from a different corner; vertex normals are area-weighted, which costs nothing because an unnormalised Newell normal already has twice the area as its length. **`Volume` is signed on purpose**: a negative volume is the cheapest reliable way to notice a mesh that will shade inside-out. **Colours are packed `uint` rather than `Rgba`** - `Rgba` is in `Spark.Api`, which references the kernel, so the kernel cannot reference it back; a scanned vertex colour is data rather than styling, and reading a PLY lossily would be worse than the shift. **`MeshTopology` describes a malformed mesh rather than refusing it**: three faces on an edge, a face wound backwards and a hole are all things a boolean or a scan produces, and each has a question that answers it |
| E2-T21 | `PointCloud` and `GeometryGroup` | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - `PointCloud` built; `GeometryGroup` declined, with the reason.** The row's note was empty, PRD FR-51 names both types beside `Mesh`, and the Dynamo inventory has neither, so each had to earn its place against a plain list. **`PointCloud` does**: it keeps its bounding box instead of walking every point for it, and answers nearest and within-a-radius through the `E2-T16` k-d tree, built once on the first question instead of a scan per question - plus `PruneDuplicates` and `TransformedBy`, immutable in and out. It round-trips through `GeometryJson` (points only; the tree and box are derived), its sample holds a point twice so a round trip that deduplicated would fail, and the viewport draws it as its points. **`GeometryGroup` does not**: a list already holds mixed geometry, and a transform wired to a list already reaches every member through replication, so a group type would be a second way to say what a list says, with nothing a list cannot do. Tests in `PointCloudTests` and `PointCloudSceneTests`, and the JSON sample. The box taken from the first point, a transform that moves nothing, and a JSON writer that drops duplicate points each fail. **Nodes**: none yet - the Dynamo inventory asks for none, and a code block reaches it by `new PointCloud(points)`. |
| E2-T22 | BRep topology: `BrepVertex`, `BrepEdge`, `BrepTrim`, `BrepLoop`, `BrepFace`, `BrepShell`, `Brep` | Done | **Index-based** — arrays and int indices, no object references. It serialises trivially, has no cycles to break for immutability, is cache-friendly, and makes an OCCT adapter mechanical if [R1](PRD.md#12-risks) ever forces one. **It did, and the decision paid off exactly as designed:** a flat array-of-indices model is what marshals well across a C ABI, which is the shape [ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md) chose. One thing changes — under [ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md) a `Brep` may be **resident in the provider**, so it stops being a pure value and carries a finalizable native resource, and **equality and hashing must be defined on the materialised model, never on the handle** **Landed 2026-08-31.** Everything contiguous - trims within a loop, loops within a face, faces within a shell, each an offset and a count - which is what makes a whole BRep nine arrays and no indirection, and is the shape that marshals in one copy. **Validation is a list, not a throw**, and the constructor checks nothing on purpose: reading a malformed BRep in order to find out what is wrong with it is what a repair tool does, so the constructor cannot be the gate. `IsSolid` asks the topological form of the question `MeshTopology.IsClosed` asks - every edge used exactly twice, once each way - which catches both a hole and a face wound backwards. **`BrepPrimitives` builds a box and a cylinder**, so the seam has something to be tested against before a provider exists; the cylinder is the interesting one, because its seam edge is used twice by *one* face, once each way, which is the case a naive builder gets wrong and which makes the trim's direction flag earn its keep. **The winding was wrong on first writing and the tests caught it**: a box's bottom loop runs the reverse of the obvious circuit, because anticlockwise seen from outside is what makes every shared edge oppose |
| E2-T23 | `BrepBuilder` and `readonly ref struct` navigator views | Done | Ergonomics recovered without giving up the index-based model. **Landed 2026-08-31 with `E2-T22`.** The builder owns the ordering the layout depends on - loops are added by naming edges, faces by naming loops, shells implicitly over the faces since the last one - and **checks as it goes where the constructor does not**, because a builder is used by code that is *making* a model. An open loop is refused with the position and the two vertices that do not meet, which is the commonest mistake in a hand-built BRep and is otherwise invisible: every index is in range and the face simply describes a different shape. **A refused loop leaves no trims behind**, so the builder can be used again, which is what a caller catching the exception is usually about to do. The navigators are `readonly ref struct`s: a pair of registers, no allocation, and the compiler will not let one escape to the heap and outlive the model it points into |
| E2-T24 | Validate, heal and sew | **Withdrawn** | **Not descoped — discarded.** OCCT's `ShapeFix` and `BRepBuilderAPI_Sewing` do this, and the work moves behind the seam as [E13-T10](#e13--occt-provider) (**ADR-0020**). Withdrawn rather than deleted so that nobody re-adds a managed sewer because its absence looks like an omission |
| E2-T25 | Extrude, revolve, loft, sweep | **Withdrawn** | **Discarded, and replaced by [E13-T9](#e13--occt-provider)** (**ADR-0020**). These were always going to be built on top of exact surface work we are no longer doing |
| E2-T26 | Tessellation and `ITessellationSink` | Done | **Restated by ADR-0021, and the split matters.** **Mesh** tessellation stays ours and this row keeps it, along with `ITessellationSink` and the `RenderPackage` path. **`Brep` tessellation moves behind the seam** — tessellating a trimmed BRep face is genuinely hard and OCCT solves it — as [E13-T11](#e13--occt-provider). The consequence must not be swallowed: **NFR-8's watertightness property then tests a third party's mesher**, and OCCT's is not guaranteed watertight at default deflection. Extract only the *pattern* from `Rendering/*`: `IPrimitiveSink` takes a `Shape` and cannot be used as-is. `PenSpec` moves to `Spark.Api` **Landed 2026-08-31.** Adaptive in each direction on a tensor grid, refined until the chord sag is inside tolerance, and **the sag is probed at several parameters in the other direction** - measuring a cone along one v samples either the narrow end or the wide one and under-refines the other. **Seams and poles are welded, and that is the decision that makes the output useful**: a sphere tessellated without welding looks perfect, has naked edges everywhere it should not, reports a nonsense volume and cannot be booleaned - a closed direction reuses the first column's indices and a collapsed row becomes one vertex with a fan around it, at the cost of a texture seam. A cell touching a pole is emitted as a triangle rather than a quad naming one vertex twice. **The sink is what the viewport wanted**: `SceneBuilder` streams triangles with the surface's own normals into the buffers it already owns, so a sphere shades smoothly rather than facetted, and no facet edges are drawn over the shading. Nine surface nodes and a `--graph surfaces` demo make it visible. |
| E2-T27 | Robust mesh boolean | Open | **Moves from M6 to 1.x** (**ADR-0020**), with `Capabilities` greying the operation out meanwhile. Ported BVH plus adaptive-precision exact predicates, pure managed. **Reduced, not eliminated, and do not let this be read as eliminated: OCCT is poor at mesh booleans and Dynamo has them.** What it loses is its urgency — it was the thing that made booleans ship long before the exact ones existed, and the exact ones now arrive first |
| E2-T28 | `IBrepKernel` with `Capabilities` and `Result<T>` | Done | Operations cross the seam; the data model never does. In front (always ours): every value type, analytic and NURBS evaluation, the `Brep`/`Mesh` model and validation, **mesh** tessellation, bounding boxes, transforms, all serialization, all of `Planar`, ray/BVH. Behind: intersection, extrude/revolve/loft/sweep/offset/thicken/shell, fillet/chamfer/split/trim, boolean, sew/heal/validate, **and now `Brep` tessellation**. **[ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md) replaces the handle-cache clause**: residency is **canonical, not cached** — after an operation the provider's representation is authoritative, ours is materialised lazily on structural demand, and a chain of ten operations performs zero imports and one materialisation. Exactly two crossings, `Import` and `Materialise`. **Round-trip is not identity and no test may assert that it is**; assert tolerance-bounded equivalence — volume, area, bounding box, topology counts, watertightness. Two seam moves the original framing did not predict: **curve and surface extraction** (`Face.SurfaceGeometry`, `Edge.CurveGeometry`) partially crosses, and `Brep` tessellation crosses entirely. For 1.0 there is exactly **one** provider and a second is not planned — **do not build one to justify the abstraction** **Landed 2026-08-31.** The seam is `IBrepKernel` in `Spark.Api`, `KernelResult<T>` for a refusal that is a value rather than an exception, and `BrepCapabilities` as flags so the library can grey an operation out rather than letting a user find out by pressing it. **`UnavailableBrepKernel` is a null object, not a null reference** - a build with no provider reports `Capabilities.None` and refuses by name, which is also what keeps `Spark.Geometry` provably useful with no native component present. **Residency is implemented**: `BrepResidency` is the opaque hold, `Brep(BrepResidency)` builds a shape that has not been read out, and every structural member goes through the `Raw*` accessors that materialise once - asserted by a counting fake, not by comment. `Brep.NativeBytes` exists for the cache consequence ADR-0021 records. **The kernel is ambient** (`BrepKernel.Current`), because a node is a static method discovered by reflection and a kernel parameter would appear as a port on every solid node. **One thing the no-provider kernel does do**: tessellate an *untrimmed* shape, because a face bounded by its surface's own edges is a surface and surfaces are in front of the seam - and it flips **per face**, since a box has one reversed face and flipping the finished mesh turned the other five over and gave a negative volume |
| E2-T29 | Native serialization: source-generated `System.Text.Json`, polymorphic discriminators, **per-type `schemaVersion`** | Done | **`GeometryJson`, landed 2026-08-29.** Every value carries its own `type` and `version`, nested values included, which is what makes *a `NurbsCurve` at v2 and a `Mesh` at v1 coexist* true rather than aspirational — a document-wide version cannot express it and could not be added later without breaking every file already written. **An unknown version is refused by name, not read approximately.** **One deliberate deviation from this row as worded: the serializer is hand-written rather than source-generated.** With an explicit converter per type the generator's work is already done by hand, and what remains is trimming and AOT support, which [ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md) rules out for the shipping application; if trimming returns, so does the decision. **Non-finite numbers are written as strings** because `BoundingBox.Empty` is built from infinities and is a legal value — a serializer that cannot write it cannot save what a caller can hold. JSON-to-JSON migrations have nowhere to run yet: there is one version. `.sparkgeo` remains [E2-T30](#e2--geometry-kernel) |
| E2-T30 | Compact binary `.sparkgeo` for bulk data | Open | JSON for a 500k-triangle mesh is roughly 30× the size and 50× the parse time |
| E2-T31 | Reflection-driven serialization round-trip test over every concrete geometry type | Done | **Landed with `E2-T29` and it earned itself on the first run.** It walks every exported type in `Spark.Geometry` and fails on any that has neither a sample nor an entry in an `Excluded` table **with a stated reason** — the same two-way diff the node importer applies, in both directions, so a stale sample for a deleted type fails too. **It found a real defect immediately:** `BoundingBox.Empty` did not survive a round trip, because the public two-corner constructor sorts its corners and therefore turns the inverted infinite box into the infinite box — its exact opposite ([N32](NOTES.md)). Proven to fire: removing the `Ray` sample fails the run and names `Ray`. Pairs with [E11-T9](#e11--quality-and-verification) |
| E2-T32 | Harvest the DoodleSharp geometry tests | Open | **Timeboxed to one week, hard stop** ([R10](PRD.md#12-risks)). 897 Fact / 58 Theory exist; roughly 400 pure-maths-on-values tests are retargetable and give an instant regression net. Anything needing a `Shape` is discarded without argument |
| E2-T33 | Property-based kernel tests with CsCheck | Done | **43 properties pass — the value layer and the curve layer are both covered** (ADR-0018), up from 28 at the last count. Generators span **1e-9 to 1e9, log-uniform so every decade is equally likely**, and generate a whole scene at one shared scale — comparing a plane at 1e9 against a coordinate at 1e-9 tests nothing but the loss of the smaller one. **What is left is named and it is one thing: there is no `SurfaceProperties` or `SolidProperties` file.** `tests/Spark.Geometry.Properties` holds `ValueLayerProperties.cs` and `CurveProperties.cs` and nothing else, so the criteria naming **union volume**, **watertight tessellation** and **`ClosestPoint` on a surface** are untested by any property — the types exist now (`Brep`, `NurbsSurface`, the OCCT provider), which is what changed since this row last said they did not. **A property that cannot fail is worse than no property:** the first version drew two independent uniforms and gave zero violations in five million simulated draws against CsCheck's hundred per run, where a generator straddling the threshold finds 908 in 12,006 pairs  **Closed 2026-09-10 by `SurfaceProperties.cs`, and it paid for itself four times over on its first run.** Eight properties over all eight surface types at every scale: `TransformedBy` commutes with `PointAt`, a point on a surface is its own closest point, the answer is never worse than the seed grid, normals are unit and perpendicular, bounding boxes contain their surface, declared closedness matches the seam, tessellations land on the surface, and a torus tessellates watertight. **Four defects in `Surface.ClosestPoint`, one symptom** ([N139](NOTES.md)): numeric derivatives were **half** their true value at every open boundary and second derivatives about a **million times** too large, because `Sample` clamps and the denominator did not; a seed landing *on* a pole left Newton with a singular Jacobian and no way to move, so a point beside a sphere's pole came back as the pole, out by 1.6e-2; the iteration returned wherever it stopped rather than the best point it had seen; and a budget of eight iterations, sized for full Newton steps, was too small once a backtracking line search could halve them. **The traps the scouting found are in the file's own remarks**: `NormalAt` *throws* at a degeneracy, and `ToMesh` at the default tolerance collapses a 1e-6 surface to no faces at all — which then reports `IsClosed`, because an empty mesh is vacuously watertight. **And the generator lesson arrived on schedule**: written first with evenly-spaced interior fractions, the closest-point property passed against the pole defect it was meant to catch, because that defect lives inside half a seed cell of the boundary. The fractions now crowd towards both ends |
| E2-T62 | `Surface.ClosestPoint` stops 8.5e-5 short on a revolution surface | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - and neither hypothesis this row named was the cause.** The failing surface is a line perpendicular to the axis revolved into a flat annulus, whose parameterisation **folds** along `v = 0`: the profile runs tangent to its circle there, so `S_u` and `S_v` are parallel. A trace of the real iteration showed Newton's first step overshooting below `v = 0`, being clamped back onto the fold, and **converging there in three steps** - both orthogonality conditions hold on the fold, because the radius changes only at second order in `v`, so it was a stationary point of the distance, but a **saddle**, 8.5e-5 of the reach away ([N152](NOTES.md)). `Surface.EscapeSaddle` now checks a converged point's Hessian - the matrix Newton solves with - and when it has a clearly negative eigenvalue, moves along that eigenvector with halvings and lets Newton resume; the Newton loop became `Surface.Refine` so it can run again. **Two changes were tried first and rejected by measurement**, and neither shipped: comparing the line search with the current point rather than the best, and a Gauss-Newton fallback for an indefinite Hessian; the trace showed neither branch was ever reached. The query now answers to **1.7e-17 of the reach**. `SurfaceClosestPointNearDegeneraciesTests.APointBesideAFoldIsItsOwnClosestPoint` holds it to 1e-12 and fails with the escape removed (2.5e-4 and 2.5e-6); `SurfaceProperties` tightens from 1e-3 to 1e-4, which is all it can assert until **`E2-T63`**. Original note follows. **Found by `SurfaceProperties` on 2026-09-10 and deliberately left open rather than hidden in a tolerance.** Asked for the closest point to a point *on itself*, at the seam in `u` and one hundredth of the way along `v`, `RevolutionSurface` answers **8.5e-5 of its own reach** away. Seven of the eight surface types answer to 1e-12 of theirs. **It is systematic, not noise**: the same relative figure at every scale from 1e-9 to 1e9, run after run. **It is not the iteration budget** — raising it from 24 to 64 changes the answer in the eleventh digit and nothing else. At the point it settles on, Newton's own residuals are already down at 1e-7 and the backtracking line search has run out of halvings, so the next thing to look at is why a step of about 1.75e-6 fails to improve the distance when the true answer is 1.5e-5 away. `SurfaceProperties` asserts 1e-3 and says so in a comment naming this row — still three orders tighter than the pole defect it was written to catch |
| E2-T63 | `Surface.ClosestPoint` creeps on a point exactly on a fold | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11, the day it was found.** A trace of the `(0.999, 0)` rim query showed the reseed stepping *off* the rim - `ReseedOffDegeneracy` treats the smaller derivative as the collapsed one, and on a fold neither is - and Newton's first step from there, enormously better than the reseed but a hair worse than the seed, refused by a line search that compared with the seed; `Contract` then crept to 1.8e-5. **`Descend` now compares with where the iteration is, and `Refine` keeps the best point apart** - the change measured and rejected for `E2-T62`, where nothing reached it, and proved here ([N152](NOTES.md)). That point now answers to 2.4e-13 of the reach. **A second change was tried and taken out**: stretching a step by 1/(1 - r) once Newton went linear finished `(0.99, 0)` off exactly, but made `(0.01, 0.001)` worse by eight orders of magnitude - found only because the property was measured one order tighter than it asserts. So a rim point stops near 1e-10 of the reach, and `SurfaceProperties`' closest-point bound goes from 1e-4 to **1e-9**, measured (worst 1.3e-10); six orders tighter than the 1e-3 it read two rows ago. `APointOnAFoldIsItsOwnClosestPoint` holds four rim points to 1e-10 and fails against the committed `E2-T62` code. Original note follows. **Found 2026-09-11 by `E2-T62`, and there before it**: with `E2-T62`'s own defect fixed, tightening `SurfaceProperties` further reached the next sample. A point exactly on the annulus's inner rim - the fold, `v = 0` - at 99.9% round is answered **1.8e-5 of the reach** away, identically before and after `E2-T62` (u = 6.254039672, v = 4.578e-3 both times). There the answer is a genuine minimum with a **singular Hessian**: the radial error is v²/0.4, so the distance is quartic in `v` and Newton converges linearly at best; at 99% round it gets to v = 3.8e-6 and 1.3e-11 of the reach, at 99.9% it stops at v = 4.6e-3. **Find first**, with a trace as `E2-T62` did, why that query stops - line search out of halvings into `Contract`, or the step test calling it converged - and then either accelerate convergence at a degenerate minimum or recognise the fold and solve along it. When it closes, `SurfaceProperties`' closest-point bound tightens from 1e-4 towards the 1e-12 the other seven types meet. |
| E2-T64 | `Curve2d`: lines and NURBS in a face's parameter space, for trim pcurves | Open | **Filed 2026-09-11, out of `E2-T13`, and waiting for its consumer.** `BrepTrim`'s remarks say a trim carries no pcurve - the edge's path through the face's own parameter space - because `Curve2d` does not exist. **Nothing in the managed kernel would read one yet**: trims are used only as topology (the loop views, `Brep` validation, `Brep.Join`'s index offsets), and a genuinely trimmed face is tessellated behind the OCCT seam (ADR-0021), where the provider holds its own pcurves. A 2D curve type built for no caller is the mistake `PreferenceFile`'s remarks warn against. **Build it with the first managed consumer** - a managed trimmed-face tessellator, or a `BrepBuilder` that accepts a trimmed face - as lines and NURBS in UV, which is what OCCT hands back. |
| E2-T65 | Brep topology navigation in the direction the model does not store | Done | **Done 2026-09-12, the same day it was filed.** `BrepAdjacency`, built from a `Brep` in one pass and **kept by the caller**: CSR arrays (offsets plus values) for vertex→edges and edge→trims, flat arrays for trim→loop and loop→face. `EdgesAt`, `TrimsOf` return spans into the index and cost nothing; `FacesAt`, `EdgesOf`, `VerticesOf` are derived and return arrays; `LoopOf`, `FaceOf` and `PartnerOf` are single reads. **`PartnerOf` answers -1 rather than throwing** when an edge is not used by exactly two trims, because a naked edge is a legitimate model and a caller walking every trim of an open sheet should not have to guard each call. **Four of the twelve needed no index**: `CoEdge.Next` / `Previous` / `StartVertex` / `EndVertex` are `BrepLoopView.NextPosition` / `PreviousPosition` / `StartVertex` / `EndVertex`, arithmetic over a loop's contiguous trims. **What was deliberately NOT done**: no back-pointers on `BrepEdge` and friends, and `BrepEdgeView.AdjacentFaces` still scans rather than building an index for one call - the two are held to the same answer on every edge of three fixtures by `BrepAdjacencyTests.TheIndexAndTheScanAgreeOnEveryEdge`, because two ways to ask a question means two answers unless something says otherwise. **The fixture is a plate with a square hole**, because a face with an inner loop is where a lookup that stops at a face's first loop gives a wrong answer a box never shows, and because every one of its edges is naked. **Watched red three ways**: the self-loop guard removed, a face claiming only its first loop (four tests), and `StartVertex` reading its edge's direction instead of its trim's (the cylinder seam only). 25 new tests. §3.5 goes to **31 of 33**; the two left are `E2-T64`'s pcurves. **Filed 2026-09-12 out of `E2-T44`'s assessment of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.5](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#35-topology--6-types-33-members-31-reachable), and every gap it found is one gap.** Spark's topology is index-based by [`E2-T22`](#e2--geometry-kernel) and stores each relationship exactly once, in one direction: a face names its loops, a loop names its trims, a trim names its edge, an edge names its vertices. **Ten §3.5 members ask for the other direction** and have no member to answer them - `Vertex.AdjacentEdges`, `Vertex.AdjacentFaces`, `Edge.CoEdges`, `Loop.Face`, `CoEdge.Loop`, `CoEdge.Partner`, and `Face.Edges` / `Face.Vertices`, plus `CoEdge.Next` / `Previous` / `StartVertex` / `EndVertex`, which are index arithmetic rather than a lookup and are cheap. **The private code already exists twice**, which is the argument for the row: `BrepEdgeView.AdjacentFaces()` scans the trims for an edge and `BrepEdgeView.FaceOf` scans the faces for a loop, both private, both O(trims) per call. **The design question is where the reverse index lives**, and it is not obvious: `BrepEdgeView.AdjacentFaces`'s own remarks argue that a back-pointer array would be a second description of the same fact that has to be kept in step, and that a caller wanting it repeatedly should build the index once and keep it. **That argument survives this row** - the answer is probably a public `BrepAdjacency` built on demand from a `Brep` and held by the caller, not fields on the records - and the row must not quietly turn the kernel into an object graph, which `E2-T22` decided against. `CoEdge.ParameterCurve` and `Face.SurfaceGeometry()` are **not** here: they need pcurves and are [`E2-T64`](#e2--geometry-kernel). |
| E2-T66 | §3.3: the eighteen members `E2-T42` found missing | Open | **Filed 2026-09-12 out of `E2-T42` step A**, which assessed all 46 of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.3](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#33-surfaces--5-types-106-members-48-reachable)'s `Surface` rows. These are the ones that are neither behind the kernel seam nor waiting on pcurves - they are simply not written, and none is large. **Step B added seven on 2026-09-12**: `NurbsSurface`'s `IsPeriodicInU` / `IsPeriodicInV` (which are **not** `IsClosedU` / `IsClosedV` - closed is geometric, periodic is a property of the knot vector), its three interpolating constructors `ByPoints`, `ByPointsTangents` and `ByPointsTangentsKnotsDerivatives`, and `PolySurface`'s `LocateSurfacesByLine` / `LocateSurfacesByPoint`, whose pieces exist - `Ray`, and `BoundingVolumeHierarchy` over the faces, which `Spark.Viewport` already casts against for picking - with no member in `Spark.Geometry` to ask. `ByJoinedSurfaces` is the same gap as `Surface.Join`. **`PrincipalDirectionsAtParameter` is the one to do first and is nearly free**: `Surface.PrincipalCurvatures` already forms the shape operator and takes its two eigenvalues, then throws away the eigenvectors that go with them; returning them is a different return type, not a different calculation. **`ToNurbsSurface` on the base**: `SurfaceConversion` converts five of the eight analytic types exactly - plane, cylinder, cone, sphere, torus - and extrusion, revolution and ruled have none, so a `Surface`-typed value cannot be asked at all. **`Surface.Offset`**, **`ApproximateWithTolerance`** (the inexact counterpart of `SurfaceConversion`, which is exact and so takes no tolerance), **`ProjectInputOnto`** (projection along a direction, which is not `ClosestPoint`), **`Join`** × 2 (`Brep.Join` joins Breps; a surface must become a face with a loop first), **`ByRuledLoft` over a sequence** (`RuledSurface` is the two-curve case, and N lines give N-1 surfaces in Spark against one in Dynamo), and **`ToString` on the base** - only `NurbsSurface` overrides it, so a general surface prints its type name. **Take them in that order**: the curvature directions close a family that is otherwise complete, and the rest are independent. **`ByPointsTangents`'s algorithm exists on the curve side as of 2026-09-13** (`NurbsCurve.InterpolatePointsWithTangents`, `E2-T72`): the surface form is the same end-derivative rows applied along one parametric direction and then the other, so that row is bookkeeping over a written solver rather than a new one, and the magnitude rule — unit direction times total chord length — is decided and tested there ([N166](NOTES.md)). |
| E2-T67 | Mass properties on a `Brep`: volume, area and centroid, from the kernel | Open | **Filed 2026-09-12 out of `E2-T43`'s assessment of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.4](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#34-solids--5-types-55-members-24-reachable).** `Solid.Volume` and `Solid.Area` are `Done` and both are measured on the **tessellation**: `Spark.Nodes.Core.Solid.Volume` meshes the shape and sums, `Mesh.Area` does the same. That is honest and it is **a different number** from the exact one — it converges with the meshing tolerance and never equals the figure OCCT would give. `Centroid()` has no member at all. **The three are one member**, because a kernel computes them in one pass over the same integral and asking three times costs three passes. **Where it goes**: `IBrepKernel`, returning a `KernelResult<T>` like everything else there, so a failure is diagnosable rather than a `NaN` (`E2-T28`). **Keep the tessellated ones**: a mesh volume is the right answer for a mesh, and a graph that has already tessellated should not pay for an exact integral it did not ask for — the two must simply be distinguishable by name. **Blocked in practice by `E13-T21`**, the missing OpenCascade install, since the shim cannot be rebuilt. |
| E2-T68 | Mesh repair and remeshing: the seven members `E2-T45` found missing | Open | **Filed 2026-09-13 out of `E2-T45`'s assessment of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.6](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#36-mesh--2-types-65-members-41-reachable).** `Repair`, `MakeWatertight`, `Remesh`, `Reduce`, `Smooth`, `Explode` — and not `CloseCracks`, which is `Mesh.Welded(tolerance)` and was the row's one wrong name. **This is not one task and should not be one commit**: `Explode` is a connected-component walk over `MeshTopology.AdjacentFaces` and is an afternoon; `Reduce` is quadric error metrics and is its own literature; `MakeWatertight` is a voxel or SDF remesh and is the largest thing in E2 that nobody has costed. **Take `Explode` first**, because it is the one the topology already answers. `IBrepKernel.Heal` repairs a **`Brep`** and cannot be pointed at a mesh — the seam is the wrong shape for this, and that is the point: FR-58 keeps the mesh layer ours because it must work in a build with no native component. | E2-T69 | Mesh primitives and mesh queries: the eight members `E2-T45` found missing | Open | **Filed 2026-09-13 out of `E2-T45`.** Two small families that have nothing in common but their size. **The primitives (4)** — `Cuboid`, `Sphere`, `Cone`, `Plane` — are *not* a Brep primitive tessellated, and the difference is the whole point: a mesh primitive's argument is its **subdivision counts**, which give a grid to deform, where a tessellation's density comes from a tolerance. | E2-T70 | A solid that cannot be moved, and queries that take only a point | Done | **Closed 2026-09-13, both steps in one day.** **Step A done 2026-09-13, the same day it was filed: a solid can be moved.** `Brep.TransformedBy(in Transform)` moves points, edge curves and face surfaces and leaves every index alone, because an affine map changes no connectivity; `Solid.Translate`, `Solid.Rotate` and `Solid.Mirror` are the nodes. **It needs no provider** — it is a construction rather than an operation — and it **materialises a resident shape**, which `BrepResidency.Materialise`'s own remarks already named as a structural demand; asking the provider to transform in place would keep residency and there is no such operation on `IBrepKernel` to ask, and `E13-T21` blocks adding one. **A transform that reverses handedness flips every `BrepFace.IsReversed`**, or a mirrored solid is inside out: every index still checks out, `Validate` is still empty, `IsSolid` is still true, and only the normals are wrong. **The same bug was live in `Mesh.TransformedBy`** — it moved vertices, inverse-transposed normals and never reversed winding — and is fixed with `MeshFace.Reversed()`.
