Both are pinned by a test that goes red when the branch is removed, proved by removing it. **What is left is step B**, the geometry-to-geometry queries. **Filed 2026-09-13 out of `E2-T46`'s assessment of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.8](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#38-infrastructure--8-types-89-members-24-reachable).** Two gaps the `Geometry` rows could not show, because those rows are `Done` on the strength of the types that *do* answer them. **(1) `Brep` has no transform.** `TransformedBy` is on `Curve`, `Surface`, `Mesh`, `PointCloud`, `PolyCurve`, `PolyLine` and every analytic surface, and **not** on `Brep`; `IBrepKernel` has no transform operation and there is no node. **A user can union two solids and cannot move one**, which is as basic as a modelling gap gets, and it has been true since M6. Note it is not merely a matrix multiply: a `Brep` may be **resident in the provider** ([ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md)), so transforming one has to decide whether to move the managed arrays or ask the kernel, and moving the arrays while the provider holds the authoritative shape is exactly the bug ADR-0021 exists to prevent. **Take this first**; it is the smaller of the two and the more embarrassing. **(2) Every query takes a point, never another geometry.** `ClosestPointTo`, `DistanceTo`, `DoesIntersect`, `Intersect` and `IntersectAll` are one missing capability wearing five names. `Curve.IntersectWith` ([E2-T11](#e2--geometry-kernel)) is exact for curve/curve and `IBrepKernel.Intersection` handles solid/solid, but **curve/surface and surface/surface have no member anywhere** — and cutting a curve with a surface is a thing AEC graphs do constantly. Surface/surface is [ADR-0002](adr/0002-own-managed-geometry-kernel.md)'s research-grade problem and belongs behind the seam; **curve/surface is not**, and is the part of this that a managed implementation should own **STEP B DONE 2026-09-13: `Curve.IntersectWith(Surface, in Tolerance)`.** The half of this finding that a managed implementation should own — curve/surface — and it had **no member anywhere**. **It brackets, then refines.** A surface has no signed distance of its own, so one is built at each of 128 samples along the curve: `Surface.ClosestPoint` gives the nearest point and its parameters, and the displacement to the curve point dotted with `Surface.NormalAt` there is a **signed** distance whose change of sign brackets a crossing. Each bracket is refined by Newton on `C(t) - S(u, v) = 0` — three equations, three unknowns, Jacobian columns `C'(t)`, `-S_u`, `-S_v`, solved by Cramer with a **relative** singularity test, because an absolute one calls every well-conditioned millimetre-scale system singular. **A singular Jacobian is a tangency rather than a failure** and bisection answers it instead. **The result carries both parameters**, the curve's and the surface's `UV`, because a caller trimming needs one and a caller marking the surface needs the other, and recovering either afterwards is a second intersection problem. **Anchored on a closed form the kernel does not contain**: a line against a plane is one division, computed in the test over a hundred pseudo-random cases, and the sampled path must match it to a **nanometre** — which is a thousand times tighter than the tolerance it was given. **That assertion found a real design error before it shipped.** The refinement first stopped as soon as the residual was inside the tolerance, which hands back a point only *as good as it had to be*; the tolerance decides what counts as **meeting**, not how accurately the answer is **reported**. Newton converges quadratically, so running it until the residual stops improving costs two or three evaluations and lands near machine precision, and the best point seen is kept rather than wherever the loop stopped ([N137](NOTES.md)'s rule, a second time). **Twelve tests**, and the two that matter were **proved to go red** by disabling the refinement. **One test was wrong and the code was right**: a circle across a cylinder crosses it **four** times, not two — `5|cos t| = 3` has four roots in a turn — and the test now carries the arithmetic. **`Curve.IntersectWithSurface` is the node**, giving the points; a graph that wants the parameters uses a code block. Help topic §7 with a worked example, and **the example is pinned by a test**, because the topic claims every example on it was run. **The one source-level cost**: `IntersectWith` is now an overload pair, so a bare `null` needs a cast — which falls on a call nobody makes outside a test. **Surface/surface is deliberately not here**: ADR-0002 makes it the research-grade problem and it belongs behind the seam, so `Geometry.Intersect(Geometry)` stays `Planned` — a member that answers every pair cannot be `Done` while one pair is missing. Residue **270 → 277**, a rise from new code and the first since the manifest had no `Unassessed` row left to hide one |
| E2-T71 | The curve gaps `E2-T41` step A found | In progress | **Filed 2026-09-13 out of `E2-T41` step A**, which assessed all 82 of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.2](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#32-curves--11-types-187-members-138-reachable)'s `Curve` rows. Everything here is `Planned` rather than refused or undecided, and it is **five families rather than a list**. **(1) NURBS conversion — the largest.** `Curve.ToNurbsCurve()` has **no implementation anywhere in Spark**: not on the base, not on a type, not as an extension, while `SurfaceConversion` converts five of the eight analytic surfaces exactly. `Line`, `Arc`, `Circle`, `EllipseCurve` and `PolyLine` each have an exact NURBS form, so this is five closed-form conversions and a virtual on the base, and it unblocks anything that wants one curve representation. **`Helix` — the eighth type, built 2026-09-13 — is the exception, and it changes the shape of this row rather than adding to it: a helix is *provably* not exactly representable as a NURBS curve at any degree** ([N165](NOTES.md)), because a NURBS would need the swept angle to be a rational function of the parameter **and** its cosine to be rational with it. So the member cannot hand back a bare `NurbsCurve` that a caller will assume is exact — `Spark.Geometry.Occt.ModelWriter` already faces the same question and answers it with an `Approximated` flag, and this row has to settle the equivalent for the result type. **(2) Planarity, and what hangs off it.** `Curve.IsPlanar` and `Curve.Normal` are absent, and they are a pair — a curve-wide normal presumes planarity. `Arc.Plane`, `Circle.Plane` and `EllipseCurve.Plane` answer it for three of the seven types and `PolyLine`, `PolyCurve`, `NurbsCurve` and a `Curve`-typed value cannot ([N161](NOTES.md)). **Two more rows wait on it and not on the kernel**: `Extrude(double)` and `ExtrudeAsSolid(double)` extrude along the curve's own normal. It is also why `CurveOffset.Offset` asks for the plane normal where Dynamo infers it. **(3) Chord stepping, and division from a parameter.** `PointsAtEqualChordLength`, `PointsAtChordLengthFromPoint`, `DivideByDistanceFromParameter` and `DivideByLengthFromParameter`. Spark has **no chord stepping at all**, and `DivideByLength` divides from the start of the domain only. **(4) Extension.** `Extend`, `ExtendStart`, `ExtendEnd` — `Curve.Trimmed` only ever narrows, so nothing in Spark lengthens a curve past its own domain. **(5) Projection, pull and the rest**, and **`Point.Project` joined this family on 2026-09-13** from `E2-T40` — a projection that works for a point works for the points a curve is sampled at, and both wait on `E2-T15`'s ray caster. `PullOntoPlane` is the tractable one — a closest-point map with a closed form for every analytic curve Spark has; `Project` and `PullOntoSurface` need SSI; `OffsetMany` is `CurveOffset.Offset` returning several curves rather than one; `Simplify` exists as `NurbsCurve.Reduced` and is not on the base, the same shape of gap as `Surface.ToNurbsSurface`; `ByParameterLineOnSurface` is the general case of the isocurves, which hold one parameter fixed; and `ByBlendBetweenCurves` has only `CurveOffset.FilletLines`, an arc between two lines, beneath it. **Twenty rows in all**, and `SweepAsSolid(Curve, bool)` is *not* among them — it is `E2-T66`, with the other kernel near-matches. **Family (3) closed 2026-09-13: chord stepping, and division from a parameter.** **A chord is not an arc length, and that is the whole of the work.** `DivideByLength` walks the curve with a tape measure; `DivideByChordLength` asks which point ahead is that far away *as the crow flies* — on a circle of radius `r` a chord of `d` subtends `2·asin(d/2r)` where an arc step of `d` subtends `d/r`, and neither expression is in the kernel, so both anchor the tests rather than restating them. **The branch that matters is the bracket, not the refinement**: distance from a fixed point is **not monotone** along a curve that bends back, so a solver handed the whole remaining domain converges on a crossing that is not the first — a point past a lobe the caller wanted divided. The curve is walked forward in arc-length steps of a quarter chord until the distance first reaches it, then bisected; widening that bracket reddens three tests. **`DivideEquallyByChord` is an outer solve around the walk**, because no marching rule lands on the far end: the **chord** is bisected between `|start − end| / n` and `Length / n`, and it **fails loudly** when the assumption that a longer chord reaches further breaks, rather than returning separations that are only nearly equal. **`DivideByLength` gained a start-parameter overload**, which is both `FromParameter` rows. **Residue 300 → 299, and it is the first *fall* this number has made from building rather than from an assessment**: the two new names are covered by their rows, and `DivideByLength` — unnamed since it was written — finally has rows to belong to. **`PointsAtEqualSegmentLength` and `PointsAtSegmentLengthFromPoint` stay `Needs a decision`**: §6.3's measure question is exactly whether *segment length* means the arc or the chord, and now that Spark has both, claiming either would be a guess with two correct-looking answers to choose from. **Family (4) closed 2026-09-13: `Curve.Extended(atStart, atEnd)`, and Spark can lengthen a curve past its own domain.** Both distances are **arc lengths**. **A type that can continue itself does**: `Line` becomes a longer line, `Arc` and `Helix` a wider sweep, all exact, because both travel at a constant speed so a length is a division. **Everything else gets a straight tail** along its own end tangent, joined as a `PolyCurve` — always available, always tangent-continuous, and *visible in the returned type* rather than a difference the caller has to guess at. **`EllipseCurve` takes the tail deliberately**: its equation continues fine but its speed is not constant, so turning an arc length into a sweep means integrating outside the domain its own machinery is built for, and a worse version of the exact answer is not better than the honest linear one. **`Circle` refuses** — a closed curve has no ends. **`ExtendStart`, `ExtendEnd` and `Extend(distance, point)` are all this one member**, the last picking the nearer end, which is a comparison the caller makes. **The branch that hides is *which end***: the two distances differ only in a sign, invisible on a symmetric curve, so every test uses an asymmetric one and asserts the other end did not move — swapping them reddens three. **It unblocked `CurveOffset.Fillet`'s stated limit**: that member refuses a pair of curves that does not already cross, and until now nothing in Spark could make them cross. Residue **299 → 302**, three overrides. **`PolyCurve.ExtendWithArc` and `ExtendWithEllipse` are *not* answered by this**, and their rows now say why: `Extended` continues the curve, those add a shape the **caller** specifies. **And the conversion family finished 2026-09-13: `PolyCurve.ToNurbsCurve` is exact.** Step A left it as the one curve that *could* convert exactly and did not. **The work is knot bookkeeping, not geometry**: convert each segment, raise them to a common degree — `NurbsCurve.WithDegreeElevated` already existed, which is what made this a step rather than a project — and join. **The counting is where it goes wrong if it goes wrong**, and it was worked out on paper in the write-ahead: two clamped degree-`p` curves of `n₁` and `n₂` control points join to `n₁ + n₂ − 1`, needing `n₁ + n₂ + p` knots, and the obvious concatenation gives **one too many** — dropping the first vector's *final* knot lands it, **and is the same edit that leaves the seam at multiplicity `p`** rather than `p + 1`. The arithmetic and the continuity are one correction, so neither can be got right without the other. **The branch proved red is the seam**: repeat it once too few and the curve is *smooth where the polycurve has a corner*, which passes every test that samples away from the join. **A segment that cannot convert exactly makes the whole inexact** — a `PolyCurve` can hold a `Helix`, which [N165](NOTES.md) says is provably not a NURBS curve — and the member falls back rather than claiming what one segment cannot support. **So every curve type that *can* convert exactly now does, and the one that cannot is the one that provably never will.** Residue **302 → 303**. **Step A closed 2026-09-13: family (1), the largest, and the one the other rows waited on.** `Curve.ToNurbsCurve(in Tolerance)` is virtual on the base and **returns a `NurbsConversion` rather than a bare `NurbsCurve`** — the curve, and whether it is the *same curve* or an approximation to it. **That signature is [N165](NOTES.md)'s doing and it is the whole design of the row**: seven of Spark's eight curve types convert exactly and `Helix` *provably never can*, so a member handing back a curve with no flag would give a caller no way to tell those apart — and the difference decides whether a downstream boolean is right or merely plausible. It is the shape `CurveIntersections` and `CurveSurfaceIntersections` already use, where the caveat travels with the answer. **Exact in step A**: `Line` and `PolyLine` at degree 1 — and **those two preserve the *parameterisation* as well as the point set**, which nothing above degree 1 does and which is pinned by a test rather than assumed — and `NurbsCurve`, which returns itself and needed the override, because the fallback would otherwise have sampled and interpolated the very thing being asked for and called it approximate. **Approximate in step A**: everything else, through a base fallback that interpolates a tessellation. **`ModelWriter` now asks the curve instead of guessing**, and the old code was wrong in both directions: it set `Approximated` from the fact that its `switch` had fallen through to `default`, so an exactly-convertible curve with no case above would have been exported perfectly and reported as approximate, and a case added above would have been reported as exact whether it was or not. Its sampling was also a flat 64 points — on a three-turn helix, a chord every 16 degrees and a deviation of about **two per cent of the radius**, measured nowhere — and is now a sag taken from the curve's own length through `Tolerance.ForScale`. Residue **284 → 289**. **Step B is family (1)'s rational half**: `Arc`, `Circle` and `EllipseCurve`, which is the same quarter-circle arithmetic `SurfaceConversion` has had since `E2-T19`, so its real work is lifting that private helper into one internal copy both call rather than deriving it twice. `PolyCurve` is in neither step: its segments' knot vectors would have to be merged, and the fallback answers it honestly in the meantime. **Families (2) to (5) are untouched.** **Step B closed the same day: `Arc`, `Circle` and `EllipseCurve` convert exactly, and family (1) is finished apart from `PolyCurve`.** **The work was the lifting, not the mathematics.** `SurfaceConversion.Arc` had produced an exact rational quadratic for a circular sweep since `E2-T19` — control points at the **tangent intersection**, radius `r / cos(θ/2)` and not the arc's midpoint, weights `cos(θ/2)` and not `cos θ`, split at every half turn because the middle weight reaches zero there — and it was **private to that class**. It is now an internal `RationalArcs` that both layers call, because deriving a construction twice is how two copies come to disagree. **`EllipseCurve` then cost nothing**: an ellipse is an affine image of a circle and an affine map carries a rational B-spline to a rational B-spline, so it is the circle's control points, weights and knots mapped through the ellipse's frame — there is no second construction to get wrong. **`Arc` and `EllipseCurve` needed the knots slid back by their start angle**, because both are parameterised from their own start and not from the plane's x axis; sliding a knot vector is an affine reparameterisation and leaves the curve alone. **One copy proves itself immediately**: replacing `cos(θ/2)` with `cos θ` reddens the curve tests **and** eight surface tests, which is what a shared derivation is for. Residue **289 → 292**, three overrides — `RationalArcs` is internal and costs nothing, which is the cheap shape worth noticing. **`PolyCurve` is the one left**: its segments' knot vectors would have to be merged and their degrees elevated to match, which is a step of its own, and the fallback answers it honestly in the meantime.  **Family (2) closed the same day, and it answered five rows rather than the two it is named for.** `Curve.IsPlanar(in Tolerance)` and **`Curve.PlaneOf(in Tolerance)`**, both virtual on the base. **The name is not Dynamo's, deliberately**: Dynamo has a `Curve.Normal` property, and Spark already has `Curve.NormalAt(parameter)` — the *Frenet* normal at a point, which points at the centre of curvature — so a curve-wide `Normal` beside it would be two unrelated quantities under almost one name, which is the sort of collision a register should catch rather than import. Dynamo's `Normal` is `PlaneOf()?.Normal`, and returning the whole frame answers `PolyCurve.BasePlane` with the same member. **The two members answer two different questions and a straight line is why**: *is there a plane containing this curve* and *which plane is it* are not the same question, so a `Line` is `IsPlanar` **true** and `PlaneOf` **null** — it lies in infinitely many planes and Spark will not pick one. `null` therefore has two causes, not planar and not uniquely planar, and `IsPlanar` is what separates them. **The fit is not the test, and that is the whole implementation**: `Plane.FromBestFit` returns a plane for *any* set of points, so returning it unchecked would make a helix planar — what decides the answer is the residual, measured against the caller's tolerance (ADR-0010), and the same curve answers differently under two of them. **`Extrude(double)` and `ExtrudeAsSolid(double)` close as compositions**, which is what they were waiting for: both extrude along the curve's own normal, the direction overloads were already `Done`, and `PlaneOf` supplies the direction. Residue **292 → 300**, four types times two members. **`CurveOffset.Offset` still asks the caller for a plane normal** where Dynamo infers it; it could now infer it, and that is a separate change with a compatibility question in it. **Families (3) to (5) are untouched.** |
| E2-T72 | The concrete curve-type gaps `E2-T41` step B found | Open | **Filed 2026-09-13 out of `E2-T41` step B**, which assessed the 105 rows of §3.2's ten concrete curve types. **Twenty-nine rows, and three of them are one missing idea each.** **(1) There is no curve fitting of any kind.** `Line.ByBestFitThroughPoints`, `Arc.ByBestFitThroughPoints` and `Circle.ByBestFitThroughPoints` are all absent — while `Plane.FromBestFit` fits a plane and `NurbsCurve.FitPoints` fits a spline, so the machinery and the taste for it are already in the kernel and the three closed-form fits are simply not written. **(2) The only fillet Spark has takes two lines.** `CurveOffset.FilletLines(Line, Line, radius)` is the whole of it, and four rows want the generalisation: `Arc.ByFillet` (two arbitrary curves), `Arc.ByFilletTangentToCurve`, `PolyCurve.Fillet` (every corner of a chain at once) and `PolyCurve.CloseWithLineAndTangentArcs`. **(3) A NURBS curve cannot be periodic.** `KnotVector.CreateClamped` is the only factory and `IsClamped` is a property, so `NurbsCurve.IsPeriodic`, `ByControlPoints(points, degree, periodic)` and `ByPoints(points, periodic)` all wait on a periodic knot vector — and `IsPeriodic` is [N156](NOTES.md)'s trap for the third time, after `Surface.IsPeriodicInU` and `Face.SurfaceGeometry()`. `ByPointsTangents` is the **same** tangent-constrained interpolation `E2-T66` wants on `NurbsSurface`, so it is one algorithm short on both sides rather than two. **The rest are `PolyCurve`'s**, which is the weakest of the ten at 7 of 21: extension (`ExtendWithArc`, `ExtendWithEllipse`, with `E2-T71`'s `Curve.Extend`), thickening, grouping — `ByGroupedCurves` makes *several* polycurves where `FromJoinedCurves` makes one and refuses a broken chain, which is the opposite behaviour rather than an overload — `BasePlane` (which is `E2-T71`'s `IsPlanar` again), and offset with an unmatched flag. **Plus three arc constructors** (`ByCenterPointStartPointEndPoint`, `ByStartPointEndPointStartTangent`) and **`Polygon.SelfIntersections`**, which is `E2-T70`'s finding in a second place: `Curve.IntersectWith` takes *another* curve, so a curve cannot be asked about itself. **The first idea closed 2026-09-13: `Line.FromBestFit`, `Circle.FromBestFit` and `Arc.FromBestFit`, with a constructor beside each.** **The machinery was already there and that is why this was one step**: `LeastSquares` has been internal since `E2-T40` step B, `Plane.FromBestFit` uses it, and `Curve.PlaneOf` started using it the same day. **The line fit is the plane fit read the other way round** — a plane wants the direction the points vary *least* along, a line the one they vary *most* along — so `LeastSquares` gained `TryFitLine` rather than the codebase gaining a second fitting routine. The smallest eigenvector had a closed form through the adjugate and the largest did not, so the largest eigenvalue comes from the **trigonometric solution of the characteristic cubic**, which is still exact arithmetic rather than an iteration. **Two refusals, and they are mirror images**: a plane fit refuses collinear points, and a line fit refuses points with **no preferred direction** — a ring, a ball — because every line through the centroid then fits as well as every other. **The circle fit is the one with a decision in it, and it is two fits.** The algebraic (Kåsa) seed is a 3×3 solve and is *exact* for points that lie on a circle; under noise on a **short arc** it under-estimates the radius, because squaring the distance weights the far points hardest and a short arc has no far side to balance them. **Measured rather than cited**: removing the Gauss–Newton refinement makes a 20° arc of radius 10 with a 0.5% wobble fit a mean radius of **9.58**, and leaves every other test in the file green. **`Arc.FromBestFit` keeps the fit and the sweep apart** — fitting is a question about a circle, which part of it the points cover is a question about their *order* — and **refuses points that double back**, because shuffled points still have a perfectly good circle through them, so the fit succeeds and only the sweep is nonsense, which is the shape of failure that gets shipped. **Residue unchanged at 300**, the first step in five that cost nothing: every new public member is named by the row it satisfies, and `Circle.FitInPlane` — the half `Arc` shares — is internal. **The second idea closed 2026-09-13: `CurveOffset.Fillet` rounds a corner between *any* two curves.** **`FilletLines` stopped where it did for a reason it wrote down, and the reason expired**: its remarks said a general fillet *needs curve-curve intersection to know where the corner is at all, and a tangency problem solved by iteration — neither of which exists yet*. `Curve.IntersectWith` arrived in `E2-T11` and the iteration is this step, so the stopping point was re-read rather than inherited. **The method is offset plus intersect**: the fillet centre is the point at radius from *both* curves, which is where their offsets cross — and there are **four** such points, one per combination of sides, of which the nearest to the corner is the fillet the caller meant and the other three round off its reflections. That rule is stated rather than left implicit, and tested. **The intersection is a seed and not the answer**, because `Offset` is exact only for `Line`, `Circle` and `Arc` and fits a curve for everything else: the centre is refined by Newton on `distance(c, first) = r` and `distance(c, second) = r`, whose gradients are the unit vectors from the closest points, so the step is a 2×2 solve. Without it a fillet is tangent only to the fit's accuracy, and **tangency is the whole of what a fillet promises**. **A radius too large is refused and the refusal falls out of the geometry** — the offsets simply never meet. **Spark asks for the plane normal where Dynamo infers it**, because two straight curves have no plane of their own: `Curve.PlaneOf` returns `null` for a line, which is family (2)'s answer arriving here. **Residue unchanged at 300** for the second step running. **The other two fillet rows got smaller rather than closed**: `Arc.ByFilletTangentToCurve` is the same solve with a third row and the radius as a third unknown, and `PolyCurve.Fillet` is this member over `PolyCurve.Segments` with the joins rebuilt — bookkeeping rather than algorithm. `PolyCurve.CloseWithLineAndTangentArcs` is untouched. **The third idea closed 2026-09-13: a NURBS curve can be periodic.** `KnotVector.CreateUniform` beside `CreateClamped`, `NurbsCurve.FromPeriodicControlPoints` with a constructor beside it, `KnotVector.IsUniform` and **`NurbsCurve.IsPeriodic`**. **`IsPeriodic` is not `IsClosed`, which is the whole of the row and was [N156](NOTES.md)'s trap a third time**: a closed curve's ends *meet*, a periodic curve's ends meet **smoothly**, with the derivatives agreeing to the degree — and a clamped curve whose last control point sits on its first is closed, is not periodic, and has a visible kink to prove it. It is in the test file as the control, passing every assertion about positions and failing every one about derivatives. **It needed no new evaluation code, which was the step's surprise**: `KnotVector` never required a clamped vector and `FindSpan` binary-searches the interior spans without assuming repeated ends, so the existing evaluator handles a periodic curve unchanged — the seam is an ordinary interior span, which is *why* the derivatives agree. **It did find a latent defect**: `NurbsCurve.IsClosed` compared the first and last *control points*, and its own remarks said only that construction *is visible in the data* while testing for it — so the day a periodic curve existed, `IsClosed` called a curve that plainly closes open. Now it recognises both, and is **cached**, because `Curve.CheckParameter` reads it on every evaluation. **The flag is a separate factory rather than a `bool`**: a flag deciding *which of two constructions happens* is a second construction, and a reader of the call should not have to look up what `true` meant. Residue **unchanged at 300**, the third step running. **`ByPoints(points, periodic)` is half done**: the knot vector it waited on exists, and what is left is a **cyclic** banded solve where the clamped interpolation uses end conditions — different linear algebra, not a different curve. **The fourth idea closed 2026-09-13: `NurbsCurve.InterpolatePointsWithTangents`, the tangent-constrained interpolation, and the first `NurbsCurve` node beside it.** **It is `InterpolatePoints` with the two end conditions replaced**: two more control points, two more rows — the first derivative at each end, which for a clamped curve is a closed form in the first two and last two control points, so no derivative basis was needed — and the same solver. The knots are averaged over the chord parameters with the first and last repeated once, which is Piegl and Tiller's Eq. 9.50 for a cubic and generalises to any degree, so `AveragedKnots` was reused rather than copied. **The decision in it is the derivative's magnitude, and it is the failure no test of tangency can see**: a caller passes a direction, a first derivative has a length as well, and every length makes a curve exactly tangent to the direction asked for while bulging or flattening at its ends. The rule is the unit direction times the **total chord length** — the speed a chord-length-parameterised curve over `[0, 1]` already travels at — and it is tested the only way it can be: five points on a quarter circle of radius 10 with the circle's own end tangents must come back *on the circle between the samples*, which they do to 0.0014, and do not when the derivative is tripled (0.29) — with every tangency assertion still green ([N166](NOTES.md)). **Two points and two tangents are the cubic Hermite**, checked against its closed form; the two ends are pinned independently on an asymmetric point set; the length of a tangent argument is ignored, as in Dynamo; degree 1 is refused because a polyline has no tangent to pin. **Residue unchanged at 303**, the fourth step running. **The node layer had no NURBS node at all** — `InterpolatePoints` had been in the kernel since M3 and reachable from no graph — so one file now carries both, with a help section. **The same algorithm `E2-T66` wants on `NurbsSurface` now exists on the curve side.** **What remains of this row, and why it waits**: `ByPoints(points, periodic)` is the cyclic solve described above; `Arc.ByFilletTangentToCurve` and `PolyCurve.Fillet` are `CurveOffset.Fillet` with a third unknown and over `Segments` respectively; `PolyCurve.CloseWithLineAndTangentArcs`, thickening, grouping, `BasePlane`, the flagged offset, the three arc constructors and `Polygon.SelfIntersections` are each small and none is on a path anything else waits for. The curve work pauses here for `E2-T66`…`E2-T69`, per the run's priority order, and this row stays open for them. |
| E2-T73 | `Helix`, the eighth curve type | Done | **Filed 2026-09-13 out of `E2-T41` step B, on decision D27**, which settled a question §3.2 had carried since it was written: `Helix` is absent from FR-48 and nobody had ever decided against it. **It is the cheapest type the curve hierarchy can gain, and that is the argument.** A helix travels at a **constant speed**, so `LengthAt`, `ParameterAtLength`, `PointAtLength`, `DivideEqually` and `DivideByLength` are all closed form and exactly right — true of only five of the seven types Spark already has, and the ellipse needed a Gauss–Legendre integral and a Newton inverse for the same members (`E2-T7`). **It needs no kernel and no provider**, so `E13-T21`'s missing shim does not block it and a build with no native component has it. **Seven Dynamo members**: `ByAxis(origin, direction, startPoint, pitch, angle)` and the parameter recovery over it — `AxisPoint`, `AxisDirection`, `Pitch`, `Radius`, `Angle` — plus `ToString`, which every one of Spark's seven concrete curve types overrides and the eighth must too, because `Curve.ToString()`'s `Done` row rests on that enumeration being empty ([N161](NOTES.md)). **What it costs**: one public type over `Curve`, its `GeometryJson` serialisation, a node family, a help topic with a worked example, and its tests — plus an entry in FR-48, which D27 adds. Angles carried as `Angle` (ADR-0011) and the pitch as a rise per turn. **Closed 2026-09-13, the same day it was filed, and it cost what D27 said it would.** `Helix` over `Curve`, its `GeometryJson` serialisation, a `Helix.ByAxis` node, a help topic section with a worked stair, and 26 tests — with **all seven parity rows `Done`**, so §3.2 moves from 111 to **118 of 187** and the register's headline from 392 to **399 of 545**. **That headline move is the first this register has ever made by building rather than by measuring.** **Three conventions, each one already held elsewhere in this layer rather than invented here**: a negative sweep flips the axis and the domain stays increasing (`Arc`'s, and `Curve` relies on it); `AxisPoint` reports the point on the axis *level with the start* rather than the origin the caller passed, because only the axis **line** is in the curve (`Arc.StartAngle`'s, reported in the frame the curve holds); and a **zero pitch is refused and the refusal names `Arc`**, as `Circle.Trimmed` returns an `Arc` because a circle that is not closed is not a circle. **A negative pitch is *not* refused** — that is handedness, and unlike a negative sweep it cannot be reached by flipping anything. **Two branches were proved red by removing them**, not one: the negative-sweep flip, and **the axial factor in `TransformedBy`** — which is the one that would have shipped wrong, because `CircularArcs.TransformFrame` returns the **radial** factor and under a uniform scale the two are equal, so a helix of exactly the right shape and the wrong height passes every test that does not stretch along the axis. **The arc length is never pinned against the expression the type computes**: the anchor is a summed chord of 400,000 samples of `PointAt`, which uses no closed form at all. **And it found a live defect in a fourth place** ([N164](NOTES.md)): `ReferenceCatalog`'s list of names pinned against `CS0104` is hand-written, and a tenth colliding façade made `Helix` ambiguous in **every code block** with nothing in the suite noticing — the help-sample compiler caught it three documents later, and only because a help topic happened to name the type. `CodeBlockLibraryReachTests.EveryCollidingNameIsPinned` now derives that set by reflection |
| E2-T34 | OBJ, STL and PLY read and write | Done | **`ObjWriter` landed 2026-08-29 — writer only, and that is a decision.** An OBJ *reader* would have to take a position on materials, groups, negative indices, free-form surfaces and a decade of dialects, to import a format carrying no curves and no precision; Spark's import story is STEP and `.spark`. **A curve becomes a polyline**, because no viewer reads OBJ's own `curv` elements, and **the tolerance it was tessellated at is written into the file's header** so *how round is this circle* has an answer inside the artefact. Numbers use the invariant culture always — a German locale writes `1,5` and produces a file that viewers misread, on some machines only. STL, PLY and any reader are still to come | M5, `Spark.Geometry.Io`. **Stays ours and is not delegated to OCCT**, which could do it: these must work in a build with **no native component at all**, since M1's demoable is `spark` writing an OBJ polyline and that lands long before anything native exists **Completed 2026-08-31.** OBJ gained mesh writing - quads stay quads, and the indices are one-based and file-global, which is the single most common way to write an OBJ that opens and draws the wrong thing. **STL reads as well as writes, and it is the only one of the three whose reader is unambiguous**: an STL file contains triangles and nothing else. Its one decision is welding - read unwelded, a printed cube arrives as 36 vertices with no shared edges, so it is never closed and never manifold - and the match is *exact* rather than tolerant, because STL stores singles and two triangles that meant to share a corner wrote the same four bytes. Which form a file is in is decided by arithmetic (`84 + 50n` bytes) and not by the leading word, because plenty of binary files begin with `solid`. **PLY reads and writes ASCII, colours included** - which is what makes `Mesh`'s colour channel reachable from outside - and reads its **header as a description** rather than assuming a property order, or a file carrying normals before colours would have its normals read as colours. The binary forms are refused by name. **An OBJ reader is still deliberately out**, for the reason this row already recorded. |
| E2-T35 | glTF write | Done | M5. Ours, for the same reason as [E2-T34](#e2--geometry-kernel), and for `NFR-5`: every glTF package on NuGet brings a native dependency or a large object model, and one mesh in one scene is a few hundred lines. **Landed 2026-08-31 as binary `.glb`**, because a `.gltf` references its buffers by URI and exporting one produces a directory rather than a file. **Two conventions have to be right or the model arrives rotated and inside out**: glTF is y-up where Spark is z-up, and the change is a *rotation* rather than a swap - swapping y and z alone flips the handedness and every face is wound the wrong way. There is a test that reads the written positions back and checks the cube still has a positive volume |
| E2-T36 | STEP AP203/AP214 read and write, documented subset | **Withdrawn** | **Replaced by [E13-T12](#e13--occt-provider)** (**ADR-0020**). OCCT gives AP203, AP214 and **AP242** — with assemblies, names, colours and units — plus IGES, so we get more than the subset would have contained and **[R12](PRD.md#12-risks) retires**. What carries over verbatim is the validation rule: a public corpus and a **third-party viewer, never our own reader**, because *OCCT wrote it* is not evidence our use of it is correct. [PRD Q7](PRD.md#14-open-questions) survives in a **downgraded** form for exactly that reason |
| E2-T37 | Throwaway surface-surface-intersection spike | **Withdrawn** | **There is no longer an estimate for it to calibrate.** The spike existed to learn how hard SSI is while it was still cheap to learn it, in service of a managed exact-boolean programme that no longer exists (**ADR-0020**). The de-risk budget it represented does not vanish — it moves to **M1.6** and [E13-T1](#e13--occt-provider), which spikes a different bet |
| E2-T38 | Golden-file geometry tests with readable diff tables | Open | Hashes plus summary stats — bounding box, counts, area, volume. A bare hash mismatch tells you nothing |
| E2-T39 | Remove the unused Clipper2 reference from `Spark.Geometry` | Done | Removed 2026-08-27. The `PackageReference` was carried from the scaffolding and **nothing in the value layer used it** — an unused reference is a dependency you are paying for in restore time, licence surface and audit noise while getting nothing back, and it makes the reference graph lie about what the assembly actually needs. It **returns with the planar boolean pipeline** ([E2-T14](#e2--geometry-kernel)), and the version stays pinned in `Directory.Packages.props` so re-adding it is one line and no decision. `Spark.Geometry` now references nothing but the BCL. Paired with [E11-T22](#e11--quality-and-verification), which is the architecture test that had to change to allow it |
| E2-T40 | Dynamo capability parity: **values and frames** | Done | **95 of ProtoGeometry's 133 value-and-frame members are reachable**; the 38 that are not are enumerated in [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.1](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#31-values-and-frames--6-types-133-members-113-reachable). **The three small omissions this row named are closed** (2026-08-29): `BoundingBox.Intersection`, `Plane.Offset` and `Plane.FromOriginNormalXAxis`. Two were less trivial than the register expected and the difference is recorded rather than smoothed over — `Intersection` delegates each axis to `Interval.Intersection` so a box and its three intervals cannot disagree about a boundary, and `Offset` refuses a non-finite distance because that is otherwise the one way to get an invalid `Plane` out of a factory. **Cylindrical and spherical construction closed 2026-09-10**, on **both** `Point3d` and `Vector3d`, as `FromCylindrical` and `FromSpherical` — Spark names factories `From…`, so the Dynamo name is the mapping and not the member. Each is also a constructor, because `E2-T59` says every factory is, and the parity test named all four the moment the factories existed. **All four are also nodes** — `Point.FromCylindrical`, `Point.FromSpherical` and the two on `Vector`, aliased to `Point.ByCylindricalCoordinates` and the rest — because a kernel member is reachable from a code block and nowhere else, and FR-81's promise is about the person at the library panel. The one decision in four arithmetic members: the spherical **polar angle is measured from `+Z`**, not up from the XY plane — the conventions differ by a quarter turn and nothing in a result reveals which was meant, so it is documented on all four and pinned by a named test. A negative radius reflects and a non-finite argument gives an invalid value rather than throwing, which is `Point3d`'s convention and the opposite of `Plane`'s. **`Plane.ByBestFitThroughPoints` and `Plane.ByLineAndPoint` closed 2026-09-10** as `FromBestFit` and `FromLineAndPoint`, and **`Plane` is now 16 of 16 — the first ProtoGeometry type Spark covers completely**. The fit lives in an internal `LeastSquares` helper rather than inside `Plane`, because DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.1 already said `Circle.ByBestFitThroughPoints` and `Line.ByBestFitThroughPoints` want the same arithmetic and three copies would disagree about a degenerate input first. It is closed form — the covariance matrix's smallest eigenvector via its adjugate — so no iteration, no convergence tolerance, no eigensolver; the only threshold is the **relative** one deciding a set of points is really a line, and near-collinear input is **refused rather than fitted**. The normal's sign follows the winding of the points, matching `FromThreePoints`. `FromLineAndPoint` forwards to `FromThreePoints` and **translates the exception on the way out**, because forwarding it unchanged would report `third` — a parameter no caller of that method has ever seen, which is the mistake this row's own documentation records having made once already. **MEASURED MEMBER BY MEMBER ON 2026-09-13, AND THE 95 IN THIS ROW WAS LOW: §3.1 is 113 of 133.** This row closed on three named omissions rather than on a pass over the rows, and it is the last section in the register to get one — with it, **no row anywhere in the manifest is `Unassessed`**. **The expectation going in was that this section would show over-claims**, because it is the only one whose number was counted by hand rather than started from zero, and it showed the opposite for the seventh section running. **Where the 18 went**: six on `CoordinateSystem`'s transformation family, which this row's own prose called the *shape difference* that made Spark split the type and then counted the split as a gap — a capability delivered on `Transform` is still delivered ([N158](NOTES.md), `E11-T30`, reaching the one section that predates it); six on `BoundingBox`, where `MinPoint`, `MaxPoint`, `ByCorners`, `ByGeometry` ×2 and `IsEmpty` had never been counted at all and `ByGeometry` became reachable the moment there was geometry to bound; two on the cylindrical and spherical frame constructors, which this row itself closed on 2026-09-10 without re-reading the section that called them planned; and the rest on `Point`, `Vector` and `GeometryJson`, where **one serialisation entry point answers the ten `FromJson`/`ToJson` rows in the document**. **`Plane` is 16 of 16 and is no longer the only complete type** — there are ten. **What is left is one `Planned` row and one question**: `Point.Project` needs `E2-T15`'s ray caster and is filed with `Curve.Project` in `E2-T71`, and all five `Needs a decision` rows are `Q17`, the oriented box. Two members were refused and counted: `Vector.IsAlmostEqualTo` under §5 [j], and `CoordinateSystem.ByOriginVectors`'s explicit-Z overload on §3.1's own ground. **Residue 303 → 270, the largest fall it has made**, with no type coming off the excused list |
| E2-T41 | Dynamo capability parity: **curves** | Done | **Closed 2026-09-13. All 187 rows of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.2](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#32-curves--11-types-187-members-138-reachable) assessed member by member against the delivering assemblies** ([N160](NOTES.md)), in two steps: `Curve`'s 82, then the ten concrete types' 105. **111 reachable of 187** — `Done` 111, `Planned` 50, `Needs a decision` 18, `Not planned` 8 — and it is the largest section in the register. **`Curve` is 40 of 82**, from five `Done` and 77 never looked at, so the reading that mattered was whether the largest type in the inventory is mostly a gap. It is not: the contract this section shaped now answers half the type it was written against. **Three of the ten concrete types are complete** (`Ellipse`, `EllipseArc`, `Rectangle`), and `EllipseCurve` answers two Dynamo types on its own. **Four decisions made rather than deferred.** (1) **§5 [i]'s duplicated trim family has a survivor**: the `Parameter*` set, with the eight `TrimBy*`/`SplitBy*` members refused and counted for the first time — 113 refused became 121, committed 555 became 547. (2) **§6.3's four length parameterisations are half answered**: the `AtDistance` family *is* arc length and is `Done`; what `AtSegmentLength` measures is still undeducible, so six members stay `Needs a decision` ([N156](NOTES.md)). (3) **`Helix` goes in** (D27, `E2-T73`) — undecided since the document was written, and the cheapest type the curve hierarchy can gain, because a helix travels at a constant speed. (4) **`Rectangle` and `Polygon` stay `PolyLine` factories**, recorded as built; the price is `Rectangle.Width` being `SegmentAt(0).Length`. **A rule the section had to state, because sixteen rows turned on it**: composition counts when the caller passes members' results to another member, and stops counting when they must compute the quantity the member exists to compute. **Findings: `E2-T71`** (step A's twenty — the largest being that Spark has no curve-to-NURBS conversion anywhere) **and `E2-T72`** (step B's twenty-nine — no curve fitting of any kind, one fillet that takes two lines only, and periodic NURBS). **Residue 321 → 318 → 303**, the largest fall the number has made, and it falsified [N162](NOTES.md)'s prediction in the step after the note was written |
| E2-T42 | Dynamo capability parity: **surfaces** | Done | **Closed 2026-09-12.** All 81 rows that are not `PanelSurface`'s carry a status and a reason; §3.3 stands at **36 of 106**, and 25 of the 70 that are not reachable are `PanelSurface` refusals. **Step B, the same day - `NurbsSurface` 12 of 17 and `PolySurface` 6 of 18.** Everything that *reads* a NURBS surface is there, including both knot vectors as `KnotVector`s rather than bare arrays; three of the five gaps are interpolation (`ByPoints` and its tangent-constrained forms) and **two are `IsPeriodicInU` / `IsPeriodicInV`, which are NOT `IsClosedU` / `IsClosedV`** - closed is geometric, periodic is a property of the knot vector, and marking one as the other is exactly [N156](NOTES.md)'s mistake. `PolySurface.BySolid` is `Done` **because Spark needs no member for it**: one `Brep`, with `IsSolid` as a query. `UnconnectedBoundaries()` became reachable on the day it was assessed, through `E2-T65`'s `BrepAdjacency.TrimsOf` - a naked edge is an edge one trim uses, and before that an edge did not know its trims. **Step A done 2026-09-12 - all 46 of `Surface`'s rows assessed.** **18 `Done`**, **24 `Planned`**, **3 `Needs a decision`**, **1 `Not planned`**, nothing left `Unassessed`. **The evaluation family is complete and the row's own text was wrong about it**: this row said the curvature family - Gaussian, principal values, principal directions - was absent from `E2-T17`'s contract and *is precisely what a facade or structural graph reaches for*. `Surface.GaussianCurvature`, `MeanCurvature` and **`PrincipalCurvatures`** are all on the abstract base, and the last two are derived from the first. **Only the principal DIRECTIONS are missing**, and `PrincipalCurvatures` already computes the eigenvectors it would return and throws them away. Also `Done`: `PointAt`, `NormalAt`, `DerivativeAt` (which is `TangentAtUParameter` and `TangentAtVParameter` too), `FrameAt`, `ClosestPoint` with its `out u, out v`, `IsoCurveU` / `IsoCurveV`, `IsClosedU` / `IsClosedV`, `Area`, the untrimmed perimeter, and `RevolutionSurface` for `ByRevolve`. **The finding, and it is not about surfaces**: twelve rows are `Planned` describing capabilities Spark *already delivers* - loft, sweep, thicken, the booleans - because they live behind `Spark.Api.IBrepKernel` and the register reads `Spark.Geometry.dll` alone. Filed as [`E11-T30`](#e11--quality-and-verification) and **not** fudged into `Done`. **A second, smaller one**: `ByRevolve` is a *constructor*, so its row names the TYPE `RevolutionSurface` - and naming a bare type silently escaped the *a type a row names cannot be excused wholesale* rule. The check now catches it, watched red, and `RevolutionSurface`'s exclusion is deleted. Eleven members are simply missing and are [`E2-T66`](#e2--geometry-kernel). **Step B**: `NurbsSurface`'s 17 rows and `PolySurface`'s 18. Earlier note follows. 106 members over 5 types, of which `PanelSurface` (25) is refused — [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.3](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#33-surfaces--5-types-106-members-48-reachable) and §5 [d]. Of `Surface`'s 46, twenty-two are behind the exact-boolean seam (**E2-T47**) and **24 are pure evaluation**, which is what M5 must deliver. E2-T17's contract names ten of them; the curvature family — Gaussian, principal values, principal directions — is absent from it and is precisely what a facade or structural graph reaches for. Dynamo has no analytic surface types at all, so FR-49's eight are a capability we add rather than one we match |
| E2-T43 | Dynamo capability parity: **solids** | Done | **Closed 2026-09-12 - all 55 rows assessed, §3.4 at 24 of 55**, from *0 reachable*. **Most of the movement is `E11-T30`, not new code**: `Solid`'s operations are 16 of 24 and every one names `Spark.Api.IBrepKernel` - union, difference, loft, revolve, sweep, fillet, chamfer, `ThinShell` as `Shell`, `Separate` as `Split`, `Repair` as `Heal` - and until that row landed the register could not name any of them. **The eight missing repeat §3.3's shapes exactly**: no loft guide, one sweep rail, a `bool` that means *cap*, a ruled loft that rules where `Loft` interpolates, and `Brep`-versus-`Surface` on `ByJoinedSurfaces`. **`Volume` and `Area` are `Done` with an asterisk the rows carry**: both measured on the *tessellation*, so both converge with the tolerance rather than equalling the exact figure, and `Centroid()` has no member at all - filed together as [`E2-T67`](#e2--geometry-kernel). **The primitives are the real gap**: `BrepPrimitives` has `Box` and `Cylinder` and **no sphere and no cone**, so seven construction rows over those two types are `Planned`. `SphericalSurface` and `ConicalSurface` are *surfaces*; closing one into a solid means a `Brep` with a seam and poles, which is the topology a primitive exists to get right once ([`E2-T66`](#e2--geometry-kernel)). **The 14 refusals line up with §5 exactly** - `Cuboid.Length` through `Cone.RadiusRatio`, four types, the count §5 was corrected to on 2026-09-11 - because a `Brep` from a factory is a `Brep` and asking it for the radius it was built from is asking it to remember its own history. Earlier note follows. 55 members over 5 types. [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.4](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#34-solids--5-types-55-members-24-reachable). **Spark has no `Solid` type and will not have one**: Dynamo splits `PolySurface` from `Solid` by *closure*, which is tolerance-dependent, so under that scheme a healing operation can change an object's type. Spark has one `Brep` with `IsSolid` as a query. The four primitives are `By*` factories rather than types, and the 11 parameter-recovery properties (`Cone.RadiusRatio`, `Cylinder.Axis`, `Cuboid.Height`) are refused in that form — keeping them means keeping a live parametric type. Twelve of `Solid`'s 24 members are **E2-T47** |
| E2-T44 | Dynamo capability parity: **topology** | Done | **Closed 2026-09-12.** §3.5 stands at **31 of 33**, and the two that are left are `E2-T64`'s pcurves - `Face.SurfaceGeometry()` and `CoEdge.ParameterCurve` - which no amount of topology work can deliver. **Step B was [`E2-T65`](#e2--geometry-kernel)**, done the same day: `BrepAdjacency` and four arithmetic members on `BrepLoopView` closed all twelve of step A's navigation gaps at once, which is what a gap that is genuinely one gap looks like. **Step A done 2026-09-12 - all 33 rows assessed, and §3.5 was no longer *0 reachable*.** **19 `Done`** and **14 `Planned`**, nothing left `Unassessed`, each row carrying what was checked rather than a name match. **What is there**: the three tables (`Brep.Faces` / `Edges` / `Vertices`) and `Brep.VertexPoint`; the two load-bearing members back to geometry, `BrepEdgeView.Curve` for `Edge.CurveGeometry` - and **not** `BrepFaceView.Surface` for `Face.SurfaceGeometry()`, see below; `BrepEdge.Start` / `End`, `BrepEdgeView.AdjacentFaces`, `BrepFaceView.Loop`, `BrepLoopView.Trim`, `BrepLoopView.Kind` for `Loop.IsExternal`, and `BrepTrim.IsReversed`. **What is not**, and it is all one shape: ten members ask for the direction the model does not store, filed as [`E2-T65`](#e2--geometry-kernel) with the warning that the answer must not turn the kernel into an object graph. **The finding worth the row**: `Face.SurfaceGeometry()` is the **trimmed** face and `BrepFaceView.Surface` is the **untrimmed** surface the face is a window onto - the same difference [N156](NOTES.md) had just found under `PolySurface.Surfaces()`, caught a second time because the review asks what a member does rather than what it is called. It and `CoEdge.ParameterCurve` are `E2-T64`'s pcurves. **The assessment moved the exclusions file too**: naming 13 Spark members took `BrepFaceView`, `BrepLoopView` and `BrepEdgeView` out of the excused types, because a type a parity row names cannot be excused wholesale, and the residue budget rose 278 → 287 with that reason written beside it. **Step B** is `E2-T65`'s navigation. Earlier note follows. 33 members over 6 types, and the cleanest subsystem in the inventory — almost all of it navigation. Full parity is achievable and should be. [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.5](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#35-topology--6-types-33-members-31-reachable). Two structural differences, both already decided: index-based rather than object-graph (**E2-T22**), and `CoEdge` → `BrepTrim`, which is the one rename in the whole mapping a reader could take for a different concept. `Face.SurfaceGeometry()` and `Edge.CurveGeometry` are the load-bearing members — they are how a user gets from topology back to geometry |
| E2-T45 | Dynamo capability parity: **mesh** | Done | **Done 2026-09-13.** 65 members over 2 types assessed member by member against the assembly, [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.6](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#36-mesh--2-types-65-members-41-reachable) rewritten with a measured *Reachable* column: **41 of 65, from *0 reachable***, and almost none of it new code. `IndexGroup` is **10 of 10** — it *is* `MeshFace`, and the section's claim that Spark needed no such type was wrong. **The finding the row carried is confirmed with one correction**: seven repair and remeshing members, not eight, appear nowhere in FR-51, [E2-T20](#e2--geometry-kernel) or [E2-T27](#e2--geometry-kernel) — **`CloseCracks()` is already there as `Mesh.Welded(tolerance)`**, which merges coincident vertices and closes the topology, does *more* than Dynamo's, and costs shading for it. They are [E2-T68](#e2--geometry-kernel). **`MakeWatertight` is not `Welded`** and saying so was the [N156](NOTES.md) trap a fourth time: welding does not fill a hole, and `MeshTopology.IsClosed` only reports the answer. **Two corrections to the harness came out of it.** `Mesh.ImportFile`/`ExportMeshes` read as absent because the rename-catcher read three assemblies and interchange lives in a fourth, so **`Spark.Geometry.Io` joined the delivering list** — `E11-T30`'s correction in the same shape ([N158](NOTES.md)); and `GeometryJson` was excused as a type Dynamo has no counterpart for *"refused in §5 [g]"*, which §5 [g] does not say. Four mesh primitives and four mesh queries are [E2-T69](#e2--geometry-kernel); the fabrication pair is `Q16`. Residue **285 → 292**, a rise with its cause written down |
| E2-T46 | Dynamo capability parity: **infrastructure** | Done | **Done 2026-09-13.** 89 members over 8 types, [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §3.8](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#38-infrastructure--8-types-89-members-24-reachable) rewritten with a measured *Reachable* column: **24 of 89**, of which 56 were already refused under §5 and the 33 that were open are `Geometry`'s 29 and `DesignScriptEntity`'s four. **Spark has no `Geometry` base class and will not acquire one**, so the section states the reading rule once instead of arguing it 29 times: a `Geometry` row is `Done` when Spark delivers the capability on the types that carry it, and the section says which types are missing. **The transformation family is 14, not the 12 the section claimed** — the list always summed to 14 — and all 14 are `Done` through `Transform` factories applied by each type's `TransformedBy`. **The finding is that `Brep` is not one of those types**: nothing in `Spark.Geometry`, on `IBrepKernel` or in the node library transforms a solid, so a user can union two solids and cannot move one ([E2-T70](#e2--geometry-kernel)). **The second finding is one gap wearing four names** — `ClosestPointTo`, `DistanceTo`, `DoesIntersect` and `Intersect` all fail because Spark's queries take a *point*, never another geometry, and curve/surface and surface/surface intersection have nothing at all. `IsAlmostEqualTo`, `Approximate` and `ContextCoordinateSystem` are refused **in that form** as §5 [j], on ADR-0010's and principle 3's grounds rather than on effort. `DesignScriptEntity.Tessellate` was listed as planned and had landed, as `Tessellation.Tessellate(surface, ITessellationSink, tolerance)`. Residue **292 → 319**, the largest move it has made, and it is the price of claiming `Transform` rather than drift |
| E2-T47 | **Decision: do exact solid booleans move into 1.0?** | **Done — answered by the client, `Yes`** | **Answered 2026-08-28. The client chose path (3), not the recommended path (2):** take an existing engine deliberately rather than as a contingency. All 70 members are 1.0 requirements, delivered by **OpenCascade** behind `IBrepKernel` through a C-ABI shim we own. **[ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md)** records it and supersedes ADR-0002; **[ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md)** redraws the seam's residency rule and amends ADR-0003. **R1 and R12 retire; R3 changes owner rather than retiring; R13 is reframed and enlarged; R15 … R22 are new.** The work is **[E13](EPICS.md#e13--occt-provider)**, ~24 weeks, and the cost is **+7 to +11 weeks against the plan as written and years saved against what was asked for** — both true, because the plan as written never contained the expensive thing. **[PRD Q6 and Q11](PRD.md#14-open-questions)** are answered with it; **E12-T15 is withdrawn** rather than written. *The recommendation below was not taken, and is left unedited so the argument is legible later.* **Q11.** [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §6.1](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#61-parity-on-solid-and-surface-commits-us-to-exact-solid-modelling) names the **32 members** that cannot exist without exact BRep booleans, trimming, filleting or sewing, plus **38 more** behind the same `IBrepKernel` seam — 70 in all, 8.4% of the inventory. This is exactly the work [ADR-0002](adr/0002-own-managed-geometry-kernel.md) stages last and [R1](PRD.md#12-risks) calls research-grade, and that [PRD §9](PRD.md#9-out-of-scope) puts post-1.0 in writing. **Full parity contradicts all three.** Three paths are set out; the technical recommendation is to hold the current staging and scope parity to *the end of 1.x* rather than *at 1.0*, because it is the only one that neither revises an estimate we have no basis for revising nor reopens ADR-0002 before the M5 spike (**E2-T37**) has told us anything. It trades the headline promise against the release date, so it is the client's call, not ours |
| E2-T48 | **Decision: is T-Splines in scope at all?** | Open | **Q12.** [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §6.2](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#62-t-splines-is-a-second-product-not-a-subsystem). **169 members across 8 types — 20.2% of the whole ProtoGeometry surface**, and `TSplineSurface` alone is 94, more than `Curve` (82) and more than `Surface` and `Solid` combined. It is a subdivision-surface modeller: a different data structure, different refinement mathematics and different literature from BRep/NURBS, sharing the word *surface* and little else. Its API is a **sculpting editor** — bevel, bridge, weld, crease, slide, fill hole — with its own `.tsm`/`.tss` formats and its own topology layer. **Recommendation: exclude it from the parity commitment and say so publicly**, the way PRD §9 already handles STEP's scope. [ADR-0003](adr/0003-ibrepkernel-seams-operations.md)'s closing note anticipates this case exactly and calls it *a different decision, not a widening of this one*, so nothing is foreclosed by leaving it out. **The answer also sets the denominator of every coverage figure we will quote**, which is the other reason to decide it rather than leave it |
| E2-T49 | **Settle the past-participle naming rule, and apply it** | Done | **Asked for by [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §4](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#member-names-we-will-not-copy) and done on 2026-08-29.** `Plane.Flip` → `Flipped`, `BoundingBox.Inflate` → `Inflated` (both overloads), `Interval.Expand` → `Expanded`; twelve call sites, four public-API baseline lines and one worked example in `docs/help/`. **The rule itself is now in `NamespaceDoc.cs`, not in a survey document** — a member that returns a new value is named for the result, never for the act — so it binds every type added afterwards instead of having to be remembered. **The compiler is the whole verification and that is the point of doing it now**: a missed call site is a build error, there is nothing shipped to break, and the same rename the day after 1.0 is an [ADR-0019](adr/0019-deliberate-public-api-change-control.md) change-control question. Two summaries were reworded with it — *Grows the box* became *Returns the box grown* — because a name that says *result* beside a sentence that says *act* only moves the problem |

## E3 — Graph engine

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E3-T1 | The graph data model | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** `Graph`, `NodeInstance`, `Wire`, `NodeDefinition`, `PortDefinition`. `NodeId` is a `Guid`, stable and **never reused** |
| E3-T2 | `DefinitionKey` including package identity | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** `NodeKey` carries `Package` and hashes it, so two packages publishing the same type name cannot silently misbind. Not just a name. Without package identity, version conflicts become silent misbindings |
| E3-T3 | Expression-tree-compiled `Invoke` delegates | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, and the ADR that justified it was corrected in the same commit.** ADR-0012 argued from "50-100x slower than reflection"; measured on .NET 10, which emits its own invoke stub, it is **1.9x** - 2.27 ms against 4.37 ms over 200,000 calls. The decision stands on the shape of the code rather than on the number, and the number is recorded as a dated correction rather than quietly edited. Never `MethodInfo.Invoke`. Under replication over 100k items the reflection path is 50–100× slower, which would make lacing unusable |
| E3-T4 | Type compatibility rules and wire refusal at creation time | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** In order: identical/assignable (green) → numeric widening (green) → registered converter (**yellow**, tooltip names it and says if lossy) → reflected user `implicit operator` (yellow) → rank lifting (green) → target is `object` (green) → otherwise **red and refused**, with an "insert `String.ToNumber`" style hint. Widening and upcasts are automatic; narrowing, parsing and lossy conversions never are |
| E3-T5 | Refuse same-`FullName`, different-assembly wires naming both packages | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** Turns an incomprehensible runtime *cannot cast Foo to Foo* into a design-time message. Worth its own rule |
| E3-T6 | Kahn topological sort over the dirty subgraph, producing levels | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** Parallel within a level |
| E3-T7 | Cycle refusal at wire creation and detection at load | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** At creation, flash the closing path. At load, every node in the cycle errors and the rest of the graph still evaluates. **Never hang** |
| E3-T8 | Content-addressed provenance cache | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** `Key(n) = Hash(DefinitionKey, DefinitionVersion, Lacing, Tolerance, RunEpochIfImpure, ∀input: connected ? Key(upstream) : Hash(literal))`. By provenance, **not by value** — hashing a 2M-triangle mesh costs more than recomputing it. Consequence: undo, redo, A/B wire toggling and slider reverts are instant, because the old key is still cached — **and as of `E8-T9` that is measured rather than asserted**: the run after an undo recomputes zero nodes |
| E3-T9 | LRU eviction against a memory budget | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - and half of it had been done for eleven days.** The native-memory budget landed on 2026-08-31 (`5ac4367`, *The cache learns what a solid weighs*): `EvaluationCache` evicts against an entry count **and** `DefaultNativeBudget`, weighing a resident `Brep` through `BrepResidency.NativeBytes`, which the provider answers with `spark_occt_shape_bytes`. The 2026-09-09 audit recorded *its ceiling is a count*, which was wrong. **What was genuinely missing was managed size**: a mesh of two million triangles cost the cache one entry of 4,096 and nothing else. `EvaluationCache` now has a third ceiling, `DefaultManagedBudget` (1 GB), and a count-based estimate - vertices and faces for a mesh, control points for NURBS, points for a polyline, elements for a list - that errs high and **never materialises a resident shape** to weigh it, because `Brep.VertexCount` on a resident shape pulls it across (ADR-0021). 5 tests in `EvaluationCacheManagedBudgetTests`, learning a value's weight from the cache rather than restating the formula; with the managed ceiling unenforced, `TheCacheEvictsOnManagedBytesWithTheCountNowhereNearItsCeiling` fails, and weighing a resident shape by its counts trips `AResidentShapeIsNotMaterialisedToBeWeighed`. Earlier note follows. **Partly built.** Eviction is by last use against an **entry-count ceiling**, not the byte budget this row asks for and not the native-memory budget [ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md) now requires. The implementation says so in its own remarks rather than implying a budget it does not have. By last use and estimated size **Re-checked by the 2026-09-09 audit and still exactly this:** `src/Spark.Engine/EvaluationCache.cs` is the only cache class in the engine and its ceiling is a count. What would close the row is a byte budget it can actually measure — which for a `Brep` means asking the provider, since the managed handle is a few bytes and the shape behind it is not. |
| E3-T10 | Impure node declaration and run epoch | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 by reconciliation - it was delivered under another name, and the re-check below searched for the wrong word.** The declaration is **`[NodeSideEffect]`** (`src/Spark.Api/NodeAttributes.cs`), on a method, constructor, property or type, with an optional reason. `NodeImporter` reads it from the member or its declaring type into `NodeDefinition.IsSideEffect`, and `CacheKey.For` mixes the run epoch into that node's key only (`CacheKey.cs`), which poisons everything downstream because their keys hash the upstream key. **The test the row asks for exists and says what it should**: `EvaluationTests.AnImpureNodeReRunsEveryTimeAndPoisonsEverythingDownstream` runs a declared-impure clock into an `Add` twice and asserts the downstream value moves (11 then 12), **zero cache hits** and both nodes re-evaluated on the second run, beside a pure control, `APureNodeWithTheSameProvenanceIsNotReRun`. The clock and console nodes declare it and are tested for it (`DateAndTimeNodeTests`, `ConsoleFromCodeTests`). Original note follows. **Half built.** The run epoch is plumbed through `EvaluationContext` and mixed into `CacheKey`. **There is no impure-node declaration**: no attribute exists in `Spark.Api`, so nothing can mark itself impure and nothing poisons downstream keys yet. Impure nodes mix a run epoch into their key and poison downstream keys. They must declare themselves; there is no way to detect it **Re-checked 2026-09-09:** `grep -rn Impure src/Spark.Api` returns nothing, so the declaration half has not moved. What would close the row: an `[Impure]` attribute on `Spark.Api`, the importer reading it, and a test that a downstream key changes when an impure node is upstream of it. |
| E3-T11 | `IEvaluationScheduler`: parallel, sequential-deterministic, host-thread | Done | **All three as of 2026-09-01.** `HostThreadEvaluationScheduler` landed with [E12-T3](#e12--embedding-and-release)  — two delegates, no named host type, inline when already on the host thread, and one marshal per batch rather than per operation. Original note follows. **Two of the three.** `SequentialEvaluationScheduler` and `ParallelEvaluationScheduler` exist behind `IEvaluationScheduler`. The **host-thread** scheduler - the one a CAD host actually needs, and a large part of why the seam exists - does not. Parallel for desktop, sequential for CLI and determinism, host-thread for CAD embedding ([E12-T3](#e12--embedding-and-release)). Evaluation never runs on the UI thread |
| E3-T12 | Cancellation between nodes, between replication elements, and inside long kernel loops | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 with step B: the evaluator's token reaches a library node that asks for it.** `NodeImporter` treats a `CancellationToken` parameter as no port - so a node's key, which is its member name plus input-port names only on an overload collision, cannot change - and `NodeInvoker.ForCancellableMethod` compiles an invoker that hands it the evaluation's token; `NodeDefinition.InvokeCancellable` holds it, separately from `InvokeScript`, which is how code blocks are told apart. `Call` prefers the script invoker, then this one, then `Invoke`. **`Spark.Nodes.Core.Surface.ToMesh` declares `CancellationToken cancellationToken = default`** and forwards it, a changed shipped signature recorded with the repository's first `*REMOVED*` baseline line and decided in [D24](PRD.md#13-decision-log). **A defect found on the way**: the code-example generator walked a method's parameters assuming one input port each, so the first token parameter ran it off the end and aborted the import of the whole first-party library ([N155](NOTES.md)). `CancellableNodeTests`, 4 tests over a top-level fixture (the importer does not import nested types): with `Call` ignoring the new invoker, the two delivery tests fail; with the token turned back into a port, `ATokenParameterIsNotAPort` fails. **OpenCascade operations still cannot be interrupted** once started - there is no way to stop a native call from managed code - so a boolean or a fillet stops when it finishes, and the help topic says so. Earlier notes follow. **Step A landed 2026-09-11: the kernel's long loop takes a token.** `Tessellation.Tessellate` and `ToMesh` gained overloads with a `CancellationToken`, checked at entry, per refinement span and per grid row; the token-free forms delegate unchanged, so no caller moves. It leaves the shape unmodified by construction - surfaces are immutable and `ToMesh` throws before `Build`, so nobody holds half a mesh. `TessellationCancellationTests` cancels from inside the sink rather than on a timer, so it is deterministic; with the row check removed, a tessellation cancelled on its first vertex ran on to **260,612** vertices and `CancellingMidGridStopsWithinARow` failed. **What is left is step B**: the evaluator's token does not reach a library node yet - `NodeDefinition.Call` hands it only to code blocks - so the importer must inject the run's token into a node method that declares a `CancellationToken` parameter (not a port, so the node's key is unchanged), and `Surface.ToMesh` must declare one. **OpenCascade calls cannot be interrupted from managed code at all**; `Solid.ToMesh` and every other kernel operation run to the end once started, and the row will say so rather than imply otherwise. Earlier note follows. **Between nodes and between replication elements, yes.** Not **inside long kernel loops**: no kernel operation takes a token, so a single expensive element cannot be interrupted. Cancelling leaves completed nodes cached **Re-checked 2026-09-09:** no file under `src/Spark.Geometry` mentions `CancellationToken`, so the kernel half has not moved. It is also the half that needs the most care — a token threaded into a tessellation loop must leave the shape unmodified when it trips. |
| E3-T13 | Run modes: Automatic, Manual, Periodic | Done | **Asked for directly, against Dynamo's dropdown.** Automatic is what the shell did **unconditionally**: every literal typed, every wire drawn and every slider dragged re-ran the graph, which is right for the first hour and wrong the moment one node takes four seconds. The seam that was missing is `MainWindowViewModel.RequestRun` - **twelve call sites started a run and there was nowhere to put the decision**. They ask the mode now; the Run button and F5 still call `EvaluateAsync` directly, because an explicit run means run and a Run button that consulted the mode would leave a Manual user with no way to run at all. Under Manual the edit is still recorded and `HasPendingRun` is what the status bar reads, because **a graph that quietly stops updating is the most confusing thing an editor can do**; returning to Automatic settles that debt. Periodic runs once a second, and its `DispatcherTimer` is built **lazily** - the view model is constructed in tests, in `spark run` and in an embedder with no UI loop, and a timer in the constructor would make all three depend on something only the desktop application has. **The mode is a session preference and is deliberately not written to the `.spark` file**: a graph that arrived set to Manual would silently do nothing when opened. **Two parts of the original row are not built and are not claimed**: the ~200 ms debounce (runs supersede rather than queue, which is the property that debounce was for), and auto-suggesting Manual past a graph-size threshold |
| E3-T14 | Progress channel; results marshal back so the canvas animates and geometry streams during a run | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - step B, the stroke travels.** §7.4's evaluating stroke is a single 2 px `accent` dash a quarter of the way round the outline, its offset advancing on a 900 ms lap (`motion.ambient`, the only repeating animation in the product); every node's stroke is timed from one epoch, so nodes evaluating together travel together. **The canvas asks for frames only while a stroke travelled in the frame it just drew** - `TopLevel.RequestAnimationFrame` - so a canvas with nothing evaluating draws once and rests. **The budget is the one the canvas already had**: §7.4 said the stroke stops below 55% zoom, and §10.2's animation budget (`CanvasLevelOfDetail.AllowsAnimation`) stops every per-node animation below 60% or above 400 visible nodes - the stricter one is used, so both hold, and §7.4 now says so. An image export is a still and draws the static ring. 4 tests in `EvaluatingStrokeTests`: the phase at four times, and whether a frame animated at working zoom, zoomed out, and with nothing evaluating - asserted through the headless render path, which runs though it draws nothing (N124). A one-second lap, the budget ignored, a travelling stroke that asks for no next frame, and a screen frame treated as a still each fail. Earlier note follows. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - the canvas shows a slow run reaching its nodes.** Half the row landed with `E9-T7`: the engine reports each finished node, and its geometry streams to the viewport. The canvas, though, changed only when the whole result was applied. **Built**: the design language's *Evaluating* state (§7.4) as `CanvasNodeState.Evaluating`. A run still going after a short delay (150 ms) marks every node it has not finished, each node's own report clears its mark, and the applied result stays the authority and clears every mark. **The delay is the design**: dragging a slider starts a run per pointer move, and without it every node would flash on each one - a run inside the delay marks nothing and asks for no repaint. The view model never touches a canvas node off the UI thread: it raises a coalesced `EvaluationProgressed`, and the window posts `ShowProgress` and a repaint. Frozen nodes are never marked, since they are not going to run. Drawn as §7.4's static form: a 2 px `accent` ring, ahead of any error ring left by the run before, and a `…` glyph. 2 tests in `EvaluatingStateTests`, held deterministic by a report hook rather than by timing. A report that does not clear its node, reports never recorded, an applied result that leaves the marks, and no delay each fail. **Step B**: the travelling stroke §7.4 animates on a 900 ms loop, static below 55% zoom. |
| E3-T15 | `SparkDiagnostic` and the `SPK####` code space | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** `{ Severity, Code, Message, Detail, NodeId, PortIndex, ElementPath, HelpTopicId }` |
| E3-T16 | Downstream of an error is greyed as *not evaluated*, never cascaded | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** Cascading turns a one-node problem into a fifty-error wall that hides the cause. Warnings mean output-with-caveats and downstream still evaluates |
| E3-T17 | `.spark` canonical JSON writer and reader | Done | **Built.** `SparkFile` writes and reads it; `GraphDocument` moves between the file and a live `Graph`. Keys are written in a fixed order by hand rather than by a serialiser, nodes are sorted by identity and wires by their endpoints, numbers use the shortest round-trippable form, and each literal carries its kind because JSON cannot tell 1 from 1.0 and Spark's ports are typed. A port still holding its declared default is not written at all: the default is reproducible from the definition, so writing it would be diff noise — and it would drag in types that are not literals, since a port declared `Plane` is seeded with one whether or not anybody typed anything. Stable key order, invariant numbers. Diffable, mergeable graphs matter more for an OSS tool whose users share work on GitHub than container tidiness. Assets over 64 KB go to a sibling folder by content hash |
| E3-T18 | Byte-identical save/load round-trip test | Done | **Built, and it is the load-bearing test of the format.** Read-then-write is asserted byte-identical, and so is the longer path the application actually takes: open a file, rebuild the canvas, save it again. `docs/examples/curves.spark` is committed as a golden file and re-derived from the seeded demo on every run. Also the test that proves [E7-T7](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) |
| E3-T19 | `graph.formatVersion` and JSON-to-JSON migrations | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 on the evidence - the versioning is built, and the migration has had no caller in five versions.** The note below says there is *exactly one format version*; there are **five** (`GraphDocument.BaselineFormatVersion` … `PackagesFormatVersion`: notes and groups, code blocks, a node's title and colour, and the package list), each added without changing or removing anything before it. **A file carries the lowest version that can read it**, not the version of the build that wrote it - forced by ADR-0016's byte-identical re-save - so a graph that uses none of those features is still version 1.
