A file from a newer version is refused whole (`SPK1061`), and files from older versions are read by the current reader, which tests already assert (`GraphNoteTests` reads versions 1 and 2, `NodeAliasTests` version 1, `NodeAppearanceTests` the step to 4). **The JSON-to-JSON migration path has never had a caller**, because no version has yet changed the meaning of anything an earlier one wrote; building it now would be the speculative code this project's own rule refuses (`PreferenceFile`'s remarks). **The rule stands for the first change that is not additive**: it ships with a JSON-to-JSON migration, never against typed models, never deleted, and with a golden-file test. No code changed. Earlier note follows. **Half built.** `graph.formatVersion` exists, is a single monotonic integer decoupled from the product version, and a file from a newer version is refused whole rather than partly read (`SPK1061`). **The JSON-to-JSON migration path does not exist**, because there is exactly one format version and nothing to migrate from — it is written when version 2 is, and not speculatively before. A single monotonic integer, decoupled from product version. **Never against typed models** — typed models drift and silently change what an old migration means. Migrations are never deleted, and each ships with a golden-file test against a real old graph in the corpus **Re-checked 2026-09-09 and deliberately unchanged:** there is still exactly one format version, so there is still nothing to migrate from. This row closes when version 2 is cut, and writing a migration before then would be writing a test for a file nobody can produce. |
| E3-T20 | `.sparkz` container | Done | **Step D, 2026-09-11 - the other door takes a bundle too.** `--open tower.sparkz` - and so a double-click, once the file type is registered - opens a bundle exactly as File ▸ Open does: unpacked into its own folder, then read as the ordinary file it is, so the package record, the trust decision and the package gate apply unchanged. A new internal constructor names the folder, so the test writes into its own and never into the user's profile. 1 test in `BundleWindowTests`; the startup door reading a bundle as a graph, and the named folder ignored, each fail. **Closed 2026-09-11 - step C, the application opens a bundle and shares one.** ADR-0017 warned the bundle *only works if it is discoverable*. **File ▸ Open** accepts a `.sparkz` beside a `.spark` - first in the picker's list, so it is not behind a filter - and `MainWindowViewModel.TryOpenBundle` unpacks it into a folder of its own under the user's local application data, named from the bundle and a hash of its bytes so the same bundle opened twice reuses one folder, then opens the graph there **as a file**: its code blocks are asked about and its packages gated as any file's are, so a bundle is not a way round either question. The status bar says where it was opened, since Save writes there. **File ▸ Share as bundle…** saves first when the graph has no file or has changes, then `ShareAsBundle` packs the file and its package folder into a `.sparkz` beside it. A refused bundle is reported in the diagnostics pane and the graph on the canvas is left as it was. 5 tests in `BundleWindowTests`. Opening a bundle as text with no file, a fresh folder on every opening, a refused bundle thrown at the window, and a shared bundle named `tower.spark.sparkz` each fail. The files help topic describes it. **What a bundle does not carry, recorded with step A**: assets and custom node definitions, which have no file home yet, and a thumbnail. Earlier note follows. **Step B done 2026-09-11 - `spark pack`, and a bundle wherever a graph goes on the command line.** `spark pack GRAPH.spark [--out FILE.sparkz]` writes the bundle - by default named after the graph and put beside it - and prints what went in. `run`, `check` and `export` take a `.sparkz` wherever they take a `.spark`: it is opened into a temporary folder, the graph found there is what is read, and its package folder is what the package gate looks in - so a bundle's packages are gated exactly as a folder beside a graph is. The folder is removed when the verb is done; messages keep the path the user typed. A refused bundle is an `IOException`, which `Main` already reports in one line. 6 tests in `PackVerbTests`: a bundle runs value for value as its graph, the default name, `check` on a bundle is silent, the temporary folder is gone afterwards, a refusal is the kind `Main` reports, and `pack` with no graph says what to type. Reading a bundle as a graph, leaving the folder behind, and naming the default `tower.spark.sparkz` each fail. **Step C**: the application opens a bundle and saves one. Earlier note follows. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - `SparkBundle` packs and opens `.sparkz`.** Nothing existed beyond ADR-0017's promise and a `.gitattributes` line. `Spark.Packages.SparkBundle.Pack` zips a `manifest.json` (bundle format 1 and the graph's name), the `.spark` file under its own name, and its package folder under its own name - leaving out a half-finished download, as Save As does - written beside the destination and moved into place so a failed pack never leaves a truncated bundle. `Unpack` opens one into a folder and returns the graph's path, **recreating exactly the pair every loader already understands**, so nothing that reads a graph has to learn to read a zip. **Refused by name, with nothing written**: a file that is not a zip, a zip with no manifest, a newer bundle format, a manifest naming a graph the bundle does not hold, and an entry that would land outside the folder (the zip-slip) - every entry is checked before the first is written. **Not carried yet, each for a reason**: ADR-0017 also lists assets (nothing writes an `.assets` folder yet), custom node definitions (they have no file home a graph points at) and a thumbnail (a command-line pack has no renderer); the format version is what lets an older build refuse the bundle that adds them. 8 tests in `SparkBundleTests`. Removing the zip-slip check, packing half-finished downloads, opening a newer format, trusting a manifest's missing graph, and writing entries while still checking each fail. **Step B**: `spark pack`, and `.sparkz` accepted by `run`, `check` and `export`. **Step C**: the application opens a bundle and saves one. Earlier note follows. Zips graph plus assets for sharing |
| E3-T21 | Public graph-construction API | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 on the evidence - the API was already public, and all three of its named consumers already use it.** `Spark.Engine.Graph` exposes `AddNode`, `RemoveNode`, `TryConnect` (returning a `ConnectionResult` with the port compatibility and a diagnostic, rather than throwing), `Disconnect` and `SetLiteral`. **The CLI** reads a `.spark` file with `SparkFile.Read` into a `GraphDocument`, which builds its `Graph` through exactly those members; **collapse-to-custom-node** builds its inner graph through them in `CustomNodeLibrary` and `CanvasCollapse`; and **tests** build graphs with them throughout - 14 in `GraphTests` alone. The row was open because nobody had gone back to it, not because anything was missing. No code changed. Earlier note follows. Needed by the CLI, by tests, and by collapse-to-custom-node. One of the four graph-model properties that survive **D8** on their own merits |
| E3-T22 | Document tolerance flowing through `EvaluationContext` into every cache key | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 by reconciliation.** `EvaluationContext` carries the document tolerance and `CacheKey` mixes both its linear and its angular part into every node's key (`src/Spark.Engine/CacheKey.cs`); `EvaluationTests.ChangingTheDocumentToleranceInvalidatesEveryCachedResult` proves that a change reaches the cache. There is no ambient tolerance anywhere, which AGENTS.md forbids by rule. Original note follows. Changing document tolerance must invalidate exactly the affected nodes — the decisive argument against an ambient tolerance |
| E3-T23 | A renamed node still opens the files that name it | Done | **The safety net `E2-T58` needed, landed first and on purpose.** A node's key is written into every file that uses it, and **nothing existed for this**: no alias table, no rename map, no key migration keyed on format version. A rename would have degraded every saved graph to placeholder nodes - `MissingNodePolicy.Placeholder` keeps the wires, refuses to evaluate and reports `SPK1062`, so the user sees a graph that opens, looks nearly right and produces nothing. [E3-T17](#e3--the-file-format) promises a graph outlives the process; a rename without this breaks that for every file at once. **The alias sits on the member it renames**, as `[SparkNodeAlias("Circle.FromCentreRadius")]`, rather than in a central table - a list of old-to-new pairs elsewhere drifts, because deleting the method leaves the entry behind and renaming twice makes a chain somebody has to maintain. Here the history is where the next person to rename it will be standing. **It names the member half of the key only**, never the package: the package comes from the assembly, so a full-key alias would be wrong the moment an assembly was renamed and would let one package claim a name in another. **One lookup carries it** - `NodeLibrary.TryGet`, which is the single place a saved graph turns a key into a node, so every loader gets it including the command line without any of them knowing aliases exist. **A live node beats a dead name**: aliases are consulted only after the real index misses, or renaming `A` to `B` in a library that still has its own `B` would make `B` unreachable. **A file heals itself**: the restored node carries the new definition, so the first save writes the new key |
| E3-T24 | Script text is written readable, not escaped | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11** ([ADR-0026](adr/0026-spark-file-text-is-written-as-typed.md), **D23**). `SparkFile` writes with `JavaScriptEncoder.UnsafeRelaxedJsonEscaping`: `+`, `<`, `>`, `&`, `'` and non-ASCII are written as typed, and a quotation mark as `\"`, which JSON requires. **The one-time diff is accepted**: an older file reads back to the same document and takes the new spelling the first time it is saved, and every file since re-saves byte for byte, so `E7-T7` holds. Writing relaxed only for changed text was rejected - the reader decodes every string, so the writer cannot know an old spelling without carrying it through `GraphDocument`, and a file would hold two spellings of one character for good. **Opening an older file does not mark it modified**: the saved snapshot is a fresh write, not the text on disk. **Not changed**: a character beyond the Basic Multilingual Plane is still an escaped surrogate pair, because no encoder setting allows one ([N150](NOTES.md)). No committed `.spark` file changes. 4 tests in `ScriptTextSpellingTests`; the two spelling tests watched red against the old encoder. Original note follows. **Seen 2026-09-11 in the client's re-saved demo files**: `"script": "return 1 \u002B 1;"`, and `\u0022Spark\u0022` for a quoted string. `SparkFile` writes through `Utf8JsonWriter`'s default encoder, which escapes `+`, `"`, `<`, `>`, `&`, `'` and non-ASCII as HTML-safe - right for JSON embedded in a web page, and wrong for a format [ADR-0017](adr/0017-spark-file-is-plain-json.md) chose so that graphs *review like code*. Code-block text is the text most likely to be read in a diff, and it is where `+` and `"` are commonest. **The fix is small and the decision is not**: a relaxed encoder writes them literally, and it changes the bytes of every saved code block - so `E7-T7`'s byte-for-byte re-save has to be argued rather than assumed. A file written after the change re-saves identically; a file written before it re-saves with `+` in place of `\u002B` the first time it is touched. **Decide**: accept that one-time diff - the JSON is equivalent and every reader accepts both spellings - or write relaxed only for text that changed. Non-ASCII in a node title or a note (`é`, `→`) has the same problem and the same fix |

## E4 — Replication and lacing

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E4-T1 | Write `docs/help/concepts/lacing.md` with the full case table | Done | Written 2026-08-27, **before any replication code exists** — the design instrument, not a description. 917 lines, and the case table went well past the 40 rows budgeted. Crosses declared rank (0/1/2) × actual rank (excess −1 to +2) × input count (1–3) × length relationship (equal, shorter, length-1, empty) × mode, plus promotion, empty-list propagation, null passthrough, ragged nesting, multi-output transpose, three-way cross product, and `[NoReplication]`/`[KeepStructure]`. **Case numbers are stable and never reused**, and the document deliberately does not describe itself by a count — the corpus is expected to grow as the engine finds situations the document did not anticipate, which is the point of writing it first. Cite it as *the case table*, never as *the N-case table*. It also settles ten questions the plan left open, as decisions D1–D10 in §2.16 |
| E4-T2 | `SparkList` as a first-class engine type | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`.** Not `List<object>`, not raw `IEnumerable`. Rank must be O(1) and unambiguous: is a `string` a list of chars? Is `Point3d[]` a list or an opaque value the node wants whole? The wrapper answers this once |
| E4-T3 | `SparkList` marshalling to and from declared collection types, with a standing benchmark | Done | **The benchmark is now a guard rather than a report.** `MarshallingBenchmarks` runs nightly on Windows and Linux against ceilings in `bench/budgets.jsonc`, and the sharp half of those budgets is the half that survives a change of machine: **allocation is deterministic** and is budgeted to within ten per cent, and **the 100 000-against-1 000 scaling ratio is machine-independent**, so an accidental O(n squared) moves it by two orders of magnitude on any hardware ([ADR-0023](adr/0023-performance-budgets-not-a-benchmark-time-series.md), [N29](NOTES.md)). The known asymmetry stays on the record and is now held at a line rather than merely noted: at 100 000 elements the *return* path costs roughly 8x the argument path and allocates 6x as much (6.5 ms and 5.1 MiB against 0.76 ms and 0.78 MiB), because `FromClr` boxes every element through a `List<object?>`. The day that is fixed, the budgets come down with it |
| E4-T4 | Rank computation | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** Value rank: scalar 0, list 1, list-of-lists 2. Port declared rank comes from the signature. `excess(i) = rank(actual) − declaredRank(i)`; `depth = max excess` |
| E4-T5 | Replicate one level and recurse | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** **Never flatten-then-reshape**, so nested structure is preserved exactly |
| E4-T6 | The five lacing modes — four algorithms and one sentinel | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** `Shortest` (zip, truncate, +1), `Longest` (zip, short inputs repeat their last element, +1), `CrossProduct` (all combinations, **+k**), `Disabled` (no replication, 0) — and **`Auto`, which is not a replication algorithm at all**. `Auto` is a sentinel meaning *use this node definition's `DefaultLacing`*, resolved to one of the other four before replication begins; it has no `n`, no pairing rule and no output rank of its own. The plan's "`Auto` = `Longest` but zero-excess inputs never iterate" was **overturned in review**: zero-excess inputs never iterate under *any* mode, so that reading made `Auto` a mode that could provably never differ from `Longest`. See `lacing.md` §2.6, §2.9 and decision **D4**. Consequences the implementation must carry: `Auto` is what a freshly placed node stores, two nodes both on `Auto` may lace differently, `DefaultLacing` may not itself be `Auto`, and a definition declaring no default gets `Longest`. `Disabled` is required for inherently rank-1 nodes like `List.Count` |
| E4-T7 | `CrossProduct` raises output rank by *k*, not by 1 | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** The part implementations habitually get wrong: 10 × 10 must yield a 10×10 nested list, not a flat 100 |
| E4-T8 | Zero-excess broadcast and negative-excess scalar promotion | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** Inputs with zero excess broadcast unchanged; negative excess promotes a scalar into a one-element list |
| E4-T9 | Multi-output lockstep replication and transpose | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** `(area, centroid)` over five items gives two lists of five, **not** one list of five tuples |
| E4-T10 | Per-element failure isolation | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** Element 37 of 500 throwing leaves the rest evaluated, slot 37 `null`, and the node emitting a **Warning** naming the failing indices — not an Error. The fast path runs uncaught until the first failure, then restarts with catching enabled, so the happy path pays nothing |
| E4-T11 | `[NoReplication]` and `[KeepStructure]` | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, as a direct transcription of the specification written before it.** |
| E4-T12 | Turn the lacing case table into the test corpus | Done | **Built in `7ef0919`, and it earned its keep immediately.** `LacingCorpusCoverageTests` parses the specification's own case numbers and fails if the corpus and the document name different sets in **either** direction. It found two errors in the specification: cases 29 and 30 expected `[11,22,32]` where repeating the short input's last element gives 23, and case 45 and the worked example both already said 23. Each row asserts the expected value **and the expected rank separately** — rank bugs are precisely the ones that survive value-only tests |
| E4-T13 | Date and time nodes | Done | **Asked for directly, against Dynamo's set.** Nineteen nodes: `DateTime.Now`, `Today`, `FromDateAndTime`, `Components`, `FromString`, `Format`, `AddTimeSpan`, `SubtractTimeSpan`, `DayOfWeek`, `DayOfYear`, `DaysInMonth`, `IsLeapYear`, and `TimeSpan.Create`, `FromDateDifference`, `Components`, `TotalDays`, `TotalHours`, `TotalMinutes`, `TotalSeconds`. **The part that is about Spark rather than about `System.DateTime` is the cache**: the evaluation cache is keyed by provenance, and `DateTime.Now` has no inputs, so without `[NodeSideEffect]` its key never changes - it is computed once and every later run serves the first answer, a clock stopped at the moment it was placed with nothing on screen to say so. Both clock nodes declare it and a test asserts they do; the mechanism itself is already proven in `EvaluationTests` with a pure control beside it. Formatting and parsing are **invariant**, because a graph is a document that gets opened on other people's machines and `03/04/2026` is two different days. `Components` is the library's first use of `out` parameters for multiple output ports, which the importer supported and nothing shipped had exercised |

## E5 — Node authoring and library

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E5-T1 | The authoring attributes | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** `[SparkNode]`, `[NodePort]`, `[NodeIgnore]` and the replication attributes. For first-party code and willing third parties — never a requirement |
| E5-T2 | Zero-config reflection importer | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** Must produce a usable library from an assembly with **no attributes at all**. This is the load-bearing promise of the whole extensibility story |
| E5-T3 | Member-kind rules | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** Public static and instance methods included, instance becoming input port 0 and the node named `Type.Method`. Public constructors become `Type.ByParamNames`. Property getters included, **setters excluded** — mutation has no place in dataflow. Static readonly fields and consts become zero-input constant nodes. `out` parameters become extra outputs after the return value. `Task<T>` is awaited and the port type is `T`. `void` with no out/ref is excluded unless explicitly marked a side effect. `op_*` operators are **excluded as nodes** — else dozens of `op_Addition` — and harvested as implicit conversions instead. Generics excluded unless closed instantiations are named, or the method is rank-polymorphic identity-like (`List.Reverse<T>`) and closed over `object`, which works because `SparkList` is object-based |
| E5-T4 | Overload handling | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** One node per overload, disambiguated by differing parameter names rather than by declaration order, which reflection does not guarantee. **One node per overload**, grouped under one library entry with a flyout, disambiguated by *differing parameter names* (`ByCenterRadius` vs `ByCenterRadiusNormal`), never `_2`. Rejected: a dropdown (changing it destroys wires) and runtime resolution (dynamic port sets break cache keys, lacing rank and wire validation) |
| E5-T5 | `By*` façade dedup | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** A public constructor is suppressed when a `By*`/`From*`/`Create*` static on the same type returns that type with the same parameter type sequence, and the suppression is recorded as an exclusion naming the factory that superseded it. A public static `By*`/`From*`/`Create*` on `T` returning `T` whose *parameter type sequence* matches a constructor's suppresses that constructor. Anything a factory does not cover still emits its constructor, so nothing becomes unreachable |
| E5-T6 | Two-way node↔member coverage test with an exclusions file | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** Every public member is reachable as exactly one node or is listed with a reason; every node resolves to a live member. **The DoodleSharp `DocumentationAccuracyTests` lesson applied prophylactically rather than after the damage** |
| E5-T7 | Descriptions from the sidecar XML documentation file | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** Any library shipping its `.xml` gets tooltips free |
| E5-T8 | `Angle` parameters render as degree-valued ports | Done | **Built.** The importer types the port from `Angle`, and the properties panel edits it **in degrees** while the kernel holds radians. This is the payoff the convention promised: a bare `double` cannot be told from a length, so no editor could choose the unit a person thinks in. The typed hook that bare doubles could never provide, and it works for third-party libraries automatically |
| E5-T9 | Extension methods presented as instance methods on the extended type | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - an extension method is shown on the type it extends.** `NodeImporter.Classify` no longer excludes extension methods; `OwnerName` keys one as `Receiver.Method`, where the receiver is its first parameter's type, so `CurveExtensions.Bend(this Curve curve)` is found under `Curve.Bend`. Everything else about the node is the static method it is: the receiver is its first input under the author's own name for it, it runs as a static call, its code example is the static call (valid C# for an extension), and it is described and filed as the declaring class says - that class is what shipped it. **Decided rather than left open**: a receiver that is an array or a constructed generic keeps the declaring class's name, since a key is not a type system; a receiver passed `in` or `ref` is still excluded, by the ref-parameter rule; a key colliding with a member the receiver's own type declares, in the same package, is separated by the ordinary overload rule - its port names - and two packages never collide, because the package is part of the key. The first-party library has no extension methods, so no built-in node changed. 3 tests in `ExtensionMethodImportTests` with fixture types. Keying on the declaring class again, and excluding extensions again, each fail. Earlier note follows. **Excluded with a stated reason rather than skipped**, which is the importer's rule: *extension methods are not surfaced on their receiver in this slice*. The two-way diff counts it as accounted for, so it cannot rot into a silent gap. So package extensions look native |
| E5-T10 | Generic method handling | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - the half of `E5-T3`'s generics rule a canvas can use.** `E5-T3`, marked Done, had already written the rule down - *generics excluded unless closed instantiations are named, or the method is rank-polymorphic identity-like and closed over `object`* - and neither half existed. **Built**: a generic method whose type parameters are all unconstrained and all appear in an input is imported closed over `object`, which works because a graph's lists are object-based; the node records the method as declared, so its documentation and the two-way coverage diff still find it, and only its ports and invocation use the closed form. **Refused, each with a reason a user would recognise**: a constrained type parameter (object would break the constraint the author wrote), and one no input mentions (nothing on a canvas could decide it). **Waiting**: closed instantiations an author names need a new public attribute, and no author has asked. Generic *types* stay excluded. The first-party library has no generic methods, so no built-in node changed. 4 tests in `GenericMethodImportTests`: a list reversed and a first item taken through the engine, and both refusals by name. Allowing constrained parameters, allowing uninferable ones, and closing over `string` each fail. Earlier note follows. **Excluded with a stated reason**, as above. Generic types and generic methods are named in the importer's own remarks as outside this slice. See E5-T3 |
| E5-T11 | CI acceptance test: import a well-known third-party NuGet package and assert a sane node count with no crashes | Open | The only honest way to know zero-config actually works |
| E5-T12 | Category taxonomy and library organisation | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** |
| E5-T13 | Curated List, Math, String and Logic node categories | Done | Landed 2026-08-31. A curated `Math` category serves arithmetic in place of the operator nodes E5-T3 excludes. **Every input in the List category is `[KeepStructure]`, and that is the whole category**: these are the nodes that look *at* a list rather than *through* it, so a replicating port would hand `List.Count` one item at a time and it would answer 1 for every element — silently, producing a list of ones where a number was expected. `Logic.Equal` takes an explicit tolerance and defaults it away from zero, because a node answering false to `0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3` is technically correct and useless. Text renders and parses in the **invariant culture** always, or a graph would produce files that differ by locale and defeat what [ADR-0017](adr/0017-spark-file-is-plain-json.md) bought by choosing text. Curated, not exhaustive — a library of two hundred list operations is a search problem; the fifteen people reach for are a tool |
| E5-T14 | Geometry node coverage over `Spark.Geometry` | In progress | **141 node methods over 21 families today** — `Arc`, `BoundingBox`, `Circle`, `Console`, `Curve`, `DateAndTime`, `Display`, `Ellipse`, `Line`, `List`, `LogicAndText`, `Math`, `Number`, `Plane`, `Point`, `PolyCurve`, `PolyLine`, `Solid`, `Surface`, `Vector` and `Watch` — reached through the same zero-config importer any third-party assembly would be, because `Spark.Nodes.Core` cannot reference `Spark.Engine`. The 57 this row recorded was a count taken before the curve, surface and solid families existed. **This row is a number rather than a finish line, and the audit of 2026-09-09 left it open on purpose:** it closes when [E2-T42](#e2--geometry-kernel) … `E2-T45` say what the surface, solid, topology and mesh sets are, and not before — a node count with no target is not a criterion |
| E5-T15 | `Appearance`, `Displayable` and `Display.ByGeometryColor` | Done | **Built in `35107f0`.** These live in `Spark.Api`, **not the kernel**. Unwrapped geometry renders with defaults, so `Spark.Geometry` stays usable entirely on its own, with no notion of colour and no reference to anything above it |
| E5-T16 | Watch and preview node definitions | Done | Satisfied by [E8-T10](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas) on 2026-08-30 rather than by separate work, which is why it closes without a commit of its own. `Watch.Value` is the node — `[KeepStructure]` on its port is the whole thing, since a plain `object` port is rank 0 and would replicate, handing the watch one item at a time when the list is precisely what somebody opened it to see. Preview is the bubble under the hovered and selected nodes, and a `Watch` pins one permanently via the declared `[ShowsValue]` flag the canvas reads without ever naming an engine type |

## E6 — C# code block

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E6-T1 | Port `ScriptRewriter` and `SourceMap` from RCS | Done | **Landed 2026-08-31, and it is one line of arithmetic because two earlier decisions kept it one.** Everything the generated frame adds goes *before* the user's first line, and `GuardWeaver` weaves with no trivia so rewriting adds none — so a diagnostic's line minus a constant is the user's line, rather than a table of ranges that has to be maintained. A compile error now reads *line 3, column 9: ; expected*. **A position inside the frame maps to 0 and is left unplaced** rather than blamed on the user's first line, which would send them to look at code that is correct. `ScriptRewriter` itself is `E6-T4`, which landed earlier the same day |
| E6-T2 | Port `ReferenceCatalog` | Done | **The biggest single time-saver in the port campaign.** Its non-locking read is also what lets [E7-T9](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) offer auto-reload: users can rebuild their library in Visual Studio while Spark is open |
| E6-T3 | Port `ScriptLoadContext` | Done | **Landed 2026-08-31.** Every compiled script now loads into a collectible `AssemblyLoadContext` instead of the default one, where it could never be unloaded — a session spent editing one code block loads dozens of assemblies. **The `Load` override returns null on purpose**: that defers to the default context, so a script's `Point3d` is the graph's `Point3d`. A context that resolved its own copy would hand the script a type with the same name and shape that nothing could be assigned to, which is the most confusing failure this layer can produce |
| E6-T4 | Port `GuardWeaver` | Done | Bounds loop iterations and recursion depth. **The channel it writes against already exists** (`E6-T17`): the generated entry point is `Run(object[] __in, CancellationToken __token)`, so a woven loop check is `__token.ThrowIfCancellationRequested()` against a parameter that is already in scope, plus an iteration counter. Recursion depth has to be bounded rather than caught — `StackOverflowException` terminates the process ([R11](PRD.md#12-risks)). **Landed 2026-08-31.** A `CSharpSyntaxRewriter` between parsing and compiling: a `Tick` at the top of every `for`/`foreach`/`while`/`do` body, one before every `goto` — a label and a jump are the other way to write a loop — and `Enter`/`try`/`finally`/`Exit` around every local function. **Two things it had to do that were not in the plan.** A `static` local function or lambda may not capture, and the woven check reads `__token`, so the weaver drops the modifier rather than emitting `CS8421` naming a parameter the user never wrote. And every woven statement carries **no trivia**, so the tree the compiler sees has exactly the line count the text did — the property `E6-T1`'s source map needs, asserted rather than intended. Reverting it does not turn the suite red so much as **end the test process**, which is `R11` demonstrated |
| E6-T5 | Semantic input-port inference | Done | Compile once against the prelude, collect `CS0103`/`CS0117` diagnostics, take the identifiers in source order. Materially more robust than a syntax walk, because Roslyn has already resolved locals, aliases, globals members and type parameters — all of which a walker gets wrong. Port identity is the **variable name**, so reordering usages does not rewire |
| E6-T6 | Typed input injection | Done | Once a port is connected the upstream type is known, so inject `Point3d center = …;` rather than `dynamic`. **Landed 2026-08-31.** `Create` takes the known types **keyed by port name, not by index** — which identifiers become ports, and in what order, is what compiling decides, so the caller cannot know an index yet. The canvas re-types a block when a wire lands on it and puts it back to `dynamic` when the wire goes, and the types are part of the content hash, because the same source over a `double` is not the same node as the same source over a `Point3d`. **The conversion goes through `ScriptInput.As<T>` rather than a cast**: a cast's message names two CLR types and no port, and it refuses the `int`-where-a-`double`-is-wanted case a graph produces constantly. **Two defects it exposed in `ReplaceDefinition`**, which had only ever run on a script edit: rebuilding a node dropped every wire *leaving* it and took it out of its group |
| E6-T7 | Wire-typed IntelliSense | Done | Type `center.` inside a code block and get `Point3d` members. **The single most compelling thing Spark can demo that Dynamo cannot**. **The language service landed 2026-08-31**: `ScriptCompletion.CompleteAsync` takes the block's ports and their wire types, declares them as a one-line prefix of top-level statements and shifts the caret with it, so `center.` completes against whatever is wired in and an unwired port completes as `dynamic` — which is honest, because that is what the compiler will declare it. **Nothing shows it yet**: there is no editor in the application, so this row closes with `E6-T11` and `E6-T12` and not before. **Closed 2026-08-31 with them**: place a code block, wire a point into `center`, type `center.` and the list is `Point3d`'s members |
| E6-T8 | Named-tuple output ports | Done | `return (area: a, perimeter: p);` gives ports `area` and `perimeter`. Idiomatic C#, statically analysable, no invented syntax. A plain final expression gives one `result` port. Rejected: inferring from "locals never subsequently read", because adding a debug line would silently change the port set |
| E6-T9 | Resident compile cache | Done | Same script, different inputs, zero compilation. This is what makes a slider feeding a code block feel live |
| E6-T10 | Persistent on-disk compile cache | Done | Kills Roslyn cold start on reopen. **Landed 2026-08-31, and the key as written could not be used.** `referenceCatalogVersion` is a counter of changes *in this process* and is 0 in every fresh one, so on disk it would let two different sets of references share an entry: the disk key carries `ReferenceCatalog.Fingerprint` — the references' paths, sizes and write times — plus the guard limits and a `GeneratorVersion` that must be bumped whenever the generated frame changes. **Only the input names are stored beside the assembly**, because they are the one thing that took a compilation to learn (`E6-T5`); outputs come from syntax and input types are already in the key. Written through a temporary file, with the ports file moved last so its presence means the pair is complete. **Every failure is a miss rather than an error** — read-only directory, full disk, half-written file, an assembly from a build with a different frame |
| E6-T11 | AvaloniaEdit editor host | Done | AvalonEdit → AvaloniaEdit is a close API port and mostly mechanical. **Landed 2026-08-31** as `CodeBlockEditor`, in the inspector in place of the plain text box: C# highlighting, line numbers, and text pushed in by the pane rather than bound, because a two-way binding onto a document the user is typing into re-enters on every keystroke and buys nothing |
| E6-T12 | Rework completion-popup placement and focus for Avalonia | Done | **Budget rework here specifically** — this is where AvalonEdit and AvaloniaEdit diverge most, and it was. **Landed 2026-08-31.** The list is **not a `Popup`**: a `Popup` needs a window overlay layer, which the headless session every UI test runs in does not provide, so every assertion about placement, filtering and keys would have become a manual check on a running application. An in-tree `Canvas` overlay is clipped to the pane instead of to the screen and is worth it ([N47](NOTES.md)). Placement is the caret's visual position **minus the text view's scroll offset** — the M1.5 spike's C3 finding, now a test that fails when the subtraction goes. The editor keeps focus while the list is open and forwards Up, Down, Enter, Tab and Escape to it, so typing never stops |
| E6-T13 | Port `ScriptTextRepair` and the `CompletionEngine` invariant from CADScript | Done | Completion must agree with the compiler: same references, same imports, same host object. A list that disagrees with the compiler is worse than no list. **Landed 2026-08-31, and one of the two halves was withdrawn on measurement.** The invariant is a constructor: `ScriptCompletion` over the same `ReferenceCatalog` the factory compiles against, so the two cannot drift. `ScriptTextRepair` was written, measured with and without against eight half-typed snippets, **made no difference to any of them**, and was deleted — Roslyn recovers from an unclosed brace, bracket, parenthesis and lambda unaided. **What it appeared to fix was a different bug**: the completion service added a Roslyn document per request and removed none, so from the second request on it was reading several sets of top-level statements at once and answered with nothing. Every spike test made its own instance, so nothing saw it, and an editor sends one request per keystroke ([N46](NOTES.md)) |
| E6-T14 | The two node types over one pipeline | In progress | An inline **Code Block** and a docked **C# Script Node**. A graph with no script nodes must never load `Spark.Scripting`. **The Code Block landed 2026-08-31**: a toolbar button places one, its source is edited in the properties pane, and it evaluates. Roslyn is untouched until `SparkSession.EnableScripting` is called and the first caller is `PlaceCodeBlock`, so a session that never places one never loads it. **Editing a script re-makes wires by port name, not by index** — indices shift when a script gains an identifier, and reconnecting by index would silently rewire the graph. **The docked C# Script Node is not built**, and that is the whole of what `E6` still owes — the inline Code Block covers every behaviour in this epic, and the docked variant is a second presentation of the same pipeline rather than new machinery **Re-checked 2026-09-09 and unchanged:** `src/Spark.UI` has a full `CodeBlockEditor` — brackets, diagnostics, search, selection, signature help and snippets, in six partial files — and no docked script-node pane. The row stays `In progress` for exactly that one presentation. |
| E6-T15 | Clear callback registries before unload | Done | DoodleSharp's resident-assembly cache comes **with this warning**: delegates into user code pin the collectible context, so an uncleaned registry means the ALC never unloads. **Landed 2026-08-31 with `E6-T3`**, and the warning is exact: `ScriptNodeFactory.Unload` clears the resident cache *before* unloading, and reverting that ordering turns the test red. **The proof is a weak reference that goes dead** — `AssemblyLoadContext.Unload` returns whether or not the context can actually go, so a test asserting "no exception" would pass in precisely the case the row exists to prevent. A second test pins the other half: a definition still on somebody's canvas keeps the context alive, correctly, and still works |
| E6-T16 | Trust posture: no auto-run on open, script-node banner, per-origin content-hash allowlist, `--no-script` | Done | Stated rather than implied: **The seam landed 2026-08-31**: scripting off is `SparkSession.Scripts` being null, a graph containing a code block then refuses to open naming the node, and `DisableScripting` is one-way because a switch any code path could reverse is not a trust boundary. **The rest landed 2026-08-31.** `--no-script` on `spark run` *and* on the desktop, which refuses rather than dropping the executable parts. **A graph containing a code block is opened, drawn and not run** — a banner in the properties pane says so and offers *Run once* and *Always trust this file*, which are two decisions and are offered as two: a store that recorded every run would turn a one-off into a standing permission. The allowlist is `ScriptTrustStore`, keyed on **the origin and the exact content** — the file alone would inherit a colleague's edits, the content alone would let a graph carry its permission wherever it travelled. **One bug this exposed**: a saved graph with a code block could not be reopened in a session that had never *placed* one, because the shell passed whatever `_session.Scripts` happened to be; scripting is now enabled when the document turns out to need it, and only then. Stated rather than implied: **a Spark graph is executable code; opening one from an untrusted source is equivalent to running an unknown program.** .NET has no code-access security, so pretending otherwise would be dishonest |
| E6-T17 | Cancel a runaway script | Done | A deliberately infinite loop must be cancelled rather than hanging. Note the honest limit: `StackOverflowException` cannot be caught in .NET and terminates the process ([R11](PRD.md#12-risks)). **The seam is cut and the loop still hangs**, which is the whole of what this row is worth until `E6-T4` lands: `NodeDefinitionSource.Invoke` is a `ScriptInvocation` taking the evaluation's token, `NodeDefinition.Call` is what the replicator uses so the token reaches the generated `Run(object[] __in, CancellationToken __token)`, and the entry point is bound with `CreateDelegate` so cancellation is not buried in a `TargetInvocationException` ([N42](NOTES.md)). A script therefore never *starts* once evaluation is cancelled — and since `E6-T4` landed on 2026-08-31 the woven `ScriptGuard.Tick` checks it mid-loop as well, so `while (true) { }` stops on Escape. **Closed by the weaver rather than by anything in this row** |
| E6-T18 | A new code block has no inputs | Done | The starter script was `return a;`, which compiles to a block with one input port called `a` that nobody asked for and whose removal requires already knowing that you delete an identifier from the source. An empty script is legal - zero inputs, one `result` output - so the starter became one comment line stating the rule instead. **Asked for directly by the client**: "let the default be zero inputs". Both tests were watched red against the old starter. **The comment went on 2026-09-07, also at the client's request**, and the block now starts genuinely empty: a starter comment is a sentence you read once and delete every time after, and the rule it stated is already the third paragraph of [concepts/code-blocks.md](help/concepts/code-blocks.md) - *"you add an input by using a name the code has not declared"* - which is where somebody looks the second time. **What survives from this row is the constraint, not the comment**: whatever the starter is, it must name no identifier, or the block arrives with a port nobody asked for. The size was the thing worth checking rather than assuming, because a block measures itself from its source ([E8-T39](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas)) and none had ever been measured with none: an empty block is 185 by 57, and `AFreshCodeBlockIsEmptyAndStillASensibleSize` pins it so a future change cannot quietly make a node too small to double-click |
| E6-T19 | Declare an input port's type | Done | A wire is not the only way to learn a type, and it was the only one. An unwired port is `dynamic`, so completion on `radius.` offered the members of `object`, which is worse than offering nothing because it looks like an answer. Every code block input port now has a type dropdown in the properties pane, defaulting to *from the wire*; choosing a type recompiles the block immediately, and **a declaration beats the wire** - a setting that is quietly overruled is worse than no setting. Held by port **name** so it survives an edit, carried across the rebuild the way the wires already are, and round-tripped through `.spark` as a short token rather than an assembly-qualified name. **The client asked for `+`/`-` buttons with name and type dropdowns and took this instead** once the trade-off was put to them: declaring names would put a second source of truth beside the code. An unknown token loses the setting, never the document |
| E6-T20 | A rendering test for the surfaces a person touches | Done, in the half that is reachable | **The cause is found and it is not ours.** A data-bound `TextBlock` with `TextWrapping="Wrap"` inside a `Grid` hangs Avalonia's headless `Window.Show()` - reproduced in nine lines outside `InspectorPane` entirely, after eliminating the session, the window, the pane's construction, the data context, the code editor, the port rows, the row template, `ToolTip.Tip`, the `Border.pane` style and the grid's `*` row one at a time. The same `TextBlock` as a window's direct content renders fine; without wrapping the grid version renders fine. It hangs in `Show()`, **before** any frame, so neither capture nor a layout assertion is available. The real application lays these panes out correctly, so nothing was changed for it. See [N90](NOTES.md). **What is covered:** `GraphCanvas` draws its own text and wraps nothing, so it shows, renders and hit-tests normally - `CanvasWidgetGestureTests` presses actual buttons on the slider and the value field, and three of its six go red if the widget hit test stops running before the node's. **What is not:** `InspectorPane`, and therefore the type dropdown and the port rows, which stay verified by eye. Reopen if Avalonia's headless platform fixes the wrap case |
| E6-T21 | Every syntax colour legible on the editor ground | Done | The editor was put on Spark's palette by naming twenty-four of AvaloniaEdit's highlighting colours and remapping exactly those, leaving anything unrecognised alone on the reasoning that the `.xshd` belongs to somebody else. Backwards: a name this code does not know is the one nobody has checked. Three were missed, and `StringInterpolation` is `#000000` - **1.26:1** on `surface.sunken`, so the code inside a `$"{...}"` hole was black on near-black and the first person to type one reported it. `EditorHighlightPalette.Apply` now sweeps every named colour, giving anything outside its table `text.primary`. `EditorHighlightContrastTests` walks the definition rather than the table - a test over the same twenty-four names would have passed while the defect was on screen - and holds every colour to the same 4.5:1 body-text floor as `PaletteContrastTests`. See [N91](NOTES.md) |
| E6-T22 | Signature help in the code block | Done | Typing `Circle.FromCenterNormalRadius(` said nothing, so the only way to learn that it wants a center, a normal and a radius — in that order — was to finish the line, run the graph and read `SPK1046`. **Reported by the client from the running application.** `ScriptCompletion.SignatureAsync` shares the one Roslyn workspace, the one document and the same port declarations as the completion list, because `E6-T13`'s invariant applies here identically: a signature that disagrees with the compiler is worse than none. **Roslyn publishes no signature-help service** — everything around `ISignatureHelpProvider` is internal — so this is the semantic model directly, and the member group rather than the resolved symbol, because a call being typed does not bind ([N95](NOTES.md)). The popup hangs *above* the caret's line with the list below it, the active parameter in bold, `1/2` when there are overloads and Alt+Up/Down to cycle them. Follows the wires exactly as the list does: `center.DistanceTo(` describes `Point3d.DistanceTo` when a point is wired in, and nothing at all when the port is `dynamic` |
| E6-T23 | The list opens where a person expects it to | Done | It opened on a dot and on Ctrl+Space and on nothing else, which was right for a one-line editor and wrong for the pane this became — **the client typed `var circle = ` and `new ` and got silence, and named both**. Four openings now: a dot, `=` (but not `==`, `!=`, `<=`, `>=` or `=>`), the space after `new` or after an assignment, and the first letter of an identifier — with the rest of the word narrowing the open list rather than asking again, which keeps it to one request per word. **A word narrows the list and anything else ends it and is offered to the trigger rules afresh**, which is the whole reason `= ` can work: the space matches no candidate, so without that the list would close half a keystroke after it opened. Nothing opens inside a line comment or a string, which matters because every new code block starts as a comment |
| E6-T24 | VS Code's Selection menu, in the code block | Done | **The client listed the menu and asked for all of it.** Fourteen commands, VS Code's bindings, in a context menu on the editor — Spark's own *Edit* menu is about nodes, and a *Selection* menu in the main bar would be inert whenever the focus was anywhere but a code block. Select All, Expand and Shrink Selection, Copy Line Up/Down, Move Line Up/Down, Duplicate Selection, Add Cursor Above/Below, Add Cursors to Line Ends, Add Next/Previous Occurrence, Select All Occurrences, the Ctrl+Click switch and Column Selection Mode. **Eight of them need multiple carets and AvaloniaEdit has one**, so there is a caret layer: `TextAnchor` pairs the document moves through every edit, a background renderer that draws them, and text input applied at every caret **inside one document update so that Ctrl+Z takes the whole edit back once** ([N97](NOTES.md)). Expand Selection is structural — word, brackets, line, all — rather than a Roslyn round trip per keystroke, and each step is pushed so Shrink retraces it. Column mode converts the selection to a `RectangleSelection` and lets AvaloniaEdit keep it one; the mouse box is ours, because AvaloniaEdit only makes a rectangle when Alt is held and a mode exists to stop needing the modifier. **Alt+Up/Down cycles overloads while a signature popup is up and moves lines otherwise**, which is the collision VS Code resolves the same way |
| E6-T25 | A code block's output port carries the type it returns | Done | **Found by a user drawing a wire.** Every output port was `typeof(object)`, whatever the script returned, and `object` into a port declared `Curve` is a *narrowing* — which `TypeCompatibility` refuses at the moment the wire is drawn, on purpose. So a block returning a `Circle` displayed the circle in its own watch and could not be connected to anything that wanted a curve. The asymmetry was the defect: inputs have taken their type from the wire since `E6-T7` and from a declaration since `E6-T19`; outputs had no equivalent. `ScriptOutputTypes.Infer` reads the type off the semantic model of the compilation that is being emitted anyway — the return statements whose nearest enclosing *function* is the generated `Run`, one distinct type or `object`, one element per port for a tuple. **When in doubt it stays `object`**, because a port typed wrongly refuses wires that should be legal ([N99](NOTES.md)). **The disk cache had to carry the types**: inferring them needs the compilation `E6-T10` exists to skip, so a port would otherwise be `Circle` in the session that compiled it and `object` in the one that reopened the file. `GeneratorVersion` is 2 and the `.outputs` file is written before the `.ports` file that marks an entry complete. Reverting the inference turns **eight** tests red, including one that wires a block into `Curve.PointAtParameter` and runs the graph |
| E6-T26 | Dynamo's rule: a block with no `return` gives one output port per declared variable | Done | **Asked for by the client with a Dynamo screenshot** — eleven lines, eleven ports. The multi-output machinery was already there (`E6-T8`'s tuple, `E6-T25`'s per-port types, `Unpack`), so this is a **naming rule plus a generated `return`**: the top-level declarations with initialisers, in source order, become the ports, and `Wrap` appends `return (p1: p1, …);` after the user's last line — which keeps `E6-T1`'s source map a subtraction, because the frame still adds nothing between two lines of theirs. **Gated on the script having no top-level `return`**, which is what keeps `E6-T8`'s rejection of *infer from locals* intact rather than reversing it: a return still says exactly what the ports are. Declarations and not assignments, because `x = 5;` on an undeclared name is the `CS0103` that makes `x` an **input** — so the two sets cannot collide. Top level only, or wrapping two lines in an `if` would silently delete two ports; initialised, or the generated return reports `CS0165` on a line nobody can see; not `const`. **Two defects fell out of it.** `Unpack` read `Item1..ItemN` straight off the returned tuple, and a `ValueTuple` nests everything past the seventh in `Rest` — so ports 8 to 11 were filled with null, silently, and nothing had ever produced eight ports to notice. And **the starter script never compiled**: `E6-T18` recorded that an empty script is legal, one `result` port, but a generated method returning `object` with no `return` in it is `CS0161`, reported against the *frame* and therefore dropped by the source map — so every fresh code block was a compile failure that said nothing until something asked it for a value. It now gets `return null;`. `GeneratorVersion` is 3, because the frame changed and a cached assembly from the old one would run the code this build no longer writes ([N110](NOTES.md)) |
| E6-T27 | A block whose last line is an expression returns it | Done | **Asked for by the client**, with `var n = 10 / 5; var p = 8 / 4; n + p;` and `$"test";` — both of which were `CS0201` before this, because an expression is not a statement in C#. **The rule is exactly that error and nothing wider**: a trailing expression is claimed as the result only when C# would refuse it as a statement, so *no script that already compiled changed meaning*, and the set claimed is precisely the set of lines a user could only have meant as a value. **A trailing call stays a statement** — `points.Add(p);` discards its value on purpose and blocks are written that way; widening the rule to any trailing expression would break every one of them, and `ACallIsStillAStatement` is the test that holds the line. `IsStatementExpression` is written from the language's **closed** list — invocation, object creation, assignment, increment, decrement, `await` — so the unlisted case is C#'s own answer rather than a guess about it; the single over-approximation is conditional access, where `a?.M()` and `a?.b` are one node kind and only one of the two possible mistakes is recoverable by typing `return`. Implemented as a seven-character insertion of `return ` at the statement's start rather than an appended trailer, because the statement has to *become* the return — appending a copy would evaluate it twice — and inserting adds no newline, so [E6-T1](#e6--c-code-block)'s source map stays a subtraction. **The range markers from [E10-T15](#e10--documentation) are bumped past the insertion**, or a range on the last line would be lowered as the wrong form rather than reported; that interaction has its own test and no other cover. Ports follow the same rule a `return` already sets: one `result`. `GeneratorVersion` is **4** |
| E6-T28 | One output port per value statement, not just the last | Done | **Asked for by the client with a screenshot**: a block reading `5+3;` then `"Test";`, one `result` port, and the `CS0201` that line 1 still was — *"let the code block give output ports corresponding to each statement, in this case 8 and Test"*. It is [E6-T27](#e6--c-code-block)'s rule read **once per statement instead of once per block**, so it inherits that row's safety word for word: only an expression C# would refuse as a statement is claimed, every line it turns into a port was a compile error before it did, and `ACallIsStillAStatement` is still the test that holds the line. **The ports are `result`, `result2`, `result3` …, and the first keeps the name it had** — wires are re-made by port *name*, so numbering from `result1` would disconnect the wire on every existing one-value block the moment its author typed a second line, which is exactly the edit this row exists to make useful. **Values still *replace* the declared-variable ports rather than joining them**: `var n = 10 / 5; var p = 8 / 4; n + p;` is still one port carrying 4, because `E6-T27`'s decision was the client's and generalising the *count* of values does not reopen it. Each value is captured in place into `var __resultN = …;` and a generated tuple return puts them on the ports, which is what gives each port its own type — `int`, `string`, `double` — through [E6-T25](#e6--c-code-block)'s inference. **The single-trailing-value case keeps `E6-T27`'s bare `return ` insertion untouched**, and that is not tidiness: `var` needs a natural type, so capturing `null;` or a bare lambda would be `CS0815` where `return null;` compiles. **The range markers now shift by a running total rather than a constant** — with several insertions of different lengths, a marker on the third value has three in front of it, and a marker looked for in the wrong place is lowered as the *other* range form, which is a wrong answer and not an error. That is the one defect the single-value code could not have had, and it has its own test. `GeneratorVersion` is **5** |
| E6-T29 | Dynamo's port naming, exactly | Done | **Specified by the client with a Dynamo screenshot** of six lines and six ports - `5;`->`integer`, `5.0 + 6;`->`function`, `"hello";`->`string`, `n = 100;`->`n`, `t = [0..1..#10];`->`t`, `[0..#6..10];`->`list` - and the instruction *let us follow the Dynamo code block exactly*. **It reverses [E6-T27](#e6--c-code-block)'s rule that a trailing value replaces the declared ports**, which the client had asked for six days earlier: `var n = 10 / 5; var p = 8 / 4; n + p;` was one port carrying 4 and is now three. Their call both times, and this is the later one. **`result`/`result2` are gone**: a port is named after its variable when the line declares one and after the expression's *kind* when it does not. **Kind, not value, and that is forced** - the client's first sketch asked for `5 + 9;` to be called `14`, and ports are built before the graph runs while wires are re-made *by port name*, so a name that moved with the value would drop every wire on every edit. Dynamo names from the syntax for the same reason, which is why its own screenshot calls `5.0 + 6;` a **function**: in DesignScript an operator is a function. That is the one place the scheme reads worse than naming the type would, and *exactly* is what was asked. **Repeats take a numeric suffix and the first keeps the bare name**, because two ports called `integer` would be indistinguishable to the by-name wire matching, and renaming the first would drop the wire already on it. **The generated tuple's element names are left off**: nothing reads them, and a port called `double` or `string` cannot be spelt as a C# identifier without `@`. **What survives from `E6-T27` is the half that was never about counting** - only an expression `CS0201` would reject is claimed, so `points.Add(p);` and `new List<int>();` are still statements and no script that compiled ever changed meaning. `ACallIsStillAStatement` still holds that line |
| E6-T30 | The node library is callable from a code block | Done | **Asked for by the client** - *I want all these 136 properties and methods available in CodeBlock* - and **most of them already were**, which is worth saying precisely rather than quietly delivering a smaller thing: the geometry-shaped nodes are thin facades over `Spark.Geometry`, which a block has always imported, so `Circle.FromCenterRadius(pt, 5)` has worked all along. What was genuinely out of reach is the facades with **no geometry equivalent** - and `Solid` alone is 38 of them, every boolean, fillet and shell. **A bare `using Spark.Nodes.Core;` could not be the answer**, which is why the namespace was excluded in the first place: nine of its twenty-three type names collide with `Spark.Geometry` (`Arc`, `BoundingBox`, `Circle`, `Curve`, `Line`, `Plane`, `PolyCurve`, `PolyLine`, `Surface`) and `Math` collides with `System.Math`, so every block anybody had written would break with `CS0104` on a line they did not type. **An explicit alias beats a namespace import**, so each colliding name is pinned to what it has always meant and the other fourteen facades become reachable unqualified. `Math` is pinned to `System.Math` deliberately - a block is C#, and `Math.PI` meaning anything else would be a trap. **The import is gated on the assembly actually being referenced**: `ReferenceCatalog` builds from loaded assemblies, and a host that never loaded `Spark.Nodes.Core` would otherwise be told to import an assembly that is not there and fail *every* compile - the same failure the catalogue already records for `Spark.Geometry`. **Two warts are written down rather than smoothed over**: the four facades whose node name is a C#-shaped type are `ListNodes`, `Text`, `DateAndTime` and `Duration` in a block where the canvas says `List`, `String`, `DateTime` and `TimeSpan`; and a list node counts a `SparkList`, so `ListNodes.Count(new List<object>{1,2,3})` is **1**, because rank is the graph's idea and a code block is on the C# side of that line |
| E6-T31 | `a * b;` on its own is a value, not a pointer declaration | Done | **Found by running the samples in [E10-T16](#e10--documentation)'s guide**, not by a test, on the most natural line a user could type. `width * height;` did not compile and the message was *"Pointers and fixed size buffers may only be used in an unsafe context"* - about a language feature the user did not use. In statement position `a * b;` is genuinely ambiguous, C# can read it as *declare a pointer-to-`a` called `b`*, and the language resolves that in favour of the declaration - so the line never reached [E6-T27](#e6--c-code-block)'s rule as an expression statement at all. **This is that rule reaching a case it could not see rather than a widening of it**: `E6-T27` claims expressions C# *refuses* as statements, and C# refuses this one too, just down a different path in the parser. **Claiming it is safe because the other reading cannot exist here** - a pointer declaration needs `unsafe`, which a code block never has, so the line has exactly one meaning it could ever have had, and nothing that compiles today changes because nothing of this shape compiles today. **Re-parsed rather than rebuilt from the pieces**: pulling `a` off the pointer type and `b` off the declarator handles `a * b;` and misses `a * b * c;`, which the parser mangles into a declaration with a *missing `=`*, and `p.X * b;`, which is a pointer to a qualified name. Asking the parser for the same text as an expression gets every shape for one line of code, and answers with diagnostics when the text really was not one. Seven of the ten tests go red when the branch is disabled. `GeneratorVersion` is **6**, which also carries the bump `E6-T30` should have made and did not |
| E6-T32 | Completion and signature help were blind to the ten aliased names | Done | **Reported by the client**: no completion and no signature help inside a code block, in both the canvas editor and the properties pane. **A seam rather than a fault in either half.** `ScriptNodeFactory` writes the catalogue's imports into the script as `using` **source lines**, where `using Circle = Spark.Geometry.Circle;` is ordinary C#. `ScriptCompletion` passed the same strings to `CSharpCompilationOptions.Usings`, which is the *global usings* list and takes **namespace names** - so all ten aliases were discarded **without a diagnostic** while `using Spark.Nodes.Core;` survived, and every aliased name was ambiguous (`CS0104`) in the workspace that answers the editor while binding perfectly in the compiler that runs the block. Measured before anything was written: `Circle.` listed **6** members instead of 24, `new Circle(` and `Math.Max(` answered nothing, `new Point3d(` answered because `Point3d` is not one of the ten. **The fix splits the imports** - namespaces still go to the options, `X = Y` entries are prepended to the document as `using X = Y;` and every caret is shifted past them, which is the mechanism `Declarations(inputs)` already used for input ports, so the two prefixes simply compose. **The defect lived in the gap between two constructors**: every completion test in the repository built `ScriptCompletion` from an assembly list, which carries no aliases at all, so no amount of extra cases on the existing tests could have found it - the new tests use `ReferenceCatalog`, which is what the shell calls. **Two drafts of one test could not fail** and both were caught by reinstating the defect: `Spark.Nodes.Core.Circle` is a façade carrying factories of the same four names, so the member list cannot tell the two apart, and quick info prints `MinimallyQualifiedFormat`, which drops the namespace that would have. The façade is a **static class**, so a constructor signature is something only the geometry type can produce - that is the assertion that stands. 8 of the 10 tests go red with the defect reinstated; the other two are controls |
| E6-T33 | A missing semicolon is put back when a block is clicked out of | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *if missing, automatically add semicolon at the end of the last statement when user clicks out of the code block.* **The character is worth a rule because of what leaving it off costs**, which is not a squiggle: a source that does not parse declares no variables, so [E6-T26](#e6--c-code-block)'s one-port-per-variable rule yields nothing, the node loses its output ports and the wires go with them. **The parser decides, not a string test.** *Ends with `;` or `}`* is the obvious rule and is wrong in both directions - it appends after a trailing `// comment`, where the semicolon has to go **before** it, and it appends to `if (a > 1) {`, where nothing is missing but a brace. `ScriptTermination.Terminate` asks Roslyn which token is absent, inserts one after the last statement's last **token** rather than at the end of the text, and **discards the result unless it lowers the error count** - so a half-typed block is never *corrected* into a differently broken one. **On commit rather than on every keystroke**: an editor inserting a semicolon while the caret is still on the line is fighting somebody who is mid-statement by definition. **In the view model, not in either editor**, because there are two of them - the in-node one and the properties pane - and a rule living in one would apply depending on where you happened to be typing. Roslyn stays behind the host seam (`SparkSession.TerminateScript`), so `E6-T14`'s promise that a graph with no code blocks never loads `Spark.Scripting` is untouched. Ten tests, six of which assert that the rule does **not** fire |
| E6-T34 | A code block can declare a class, a record, a struct or an enum | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot of two blocks**: one holding `public class TestClass { public static Circle Test() { … } }`, the other calling it. The first did not compile at all, and neither of the two errors named the real problem - `CS1022` *type or namespace definition, or end-of-file expected* and `CS0161` *'Block.Run(object[], CancellationToken)': not all code paths return a value*, both about a method the user has never seen. **The cause is the frame, not the script**: a block's text is emitted inside the generated `Block.Run`, and C# has no local class. **The declaration is blanked to spaces where it stood and re-emitted after the generated class**, which is the decision worth recording: cutting it out instead would shorten every offset after it and join the line above it to the line below, moving [E10-T15](#e10--documentation)'s range markers and turning [E6-T1](#e6--c-code-block)'s source map into a table for the *statements* as well. Overwriting each character with a space and leaving the newlines alone changes neither the length nor the line count, so only the re-emitted declarations need a map segment and a block that declares nothing is byte-for-byte what it was. **A class may therefore be written above the lines that use it**, which a scheme keeping the user's text in one contiguous run could not have allowed. **Three things had to move with it.** `TopLevelReturns` descended into type declarations, so the client's own `return new Circle(p, 1.0);` - a line inside their method - would have been read as the *block's* return and typed its port from a method nobody calls. `InferInputs` reads `CS0103` to find ports, and a declared type cannot see the entry point's locals, so a typo in a class body would have become a socket rather than a squiggle. And `GuardWeaver` wove `Tick(__token)`, naming a parameter of `Run` that is out of scope in a declared type: `ScriptGuard.Begin` now takes the token and puts it on the thread beside the counters, where a parameterless `Tick()` reads it back. **The guard hole was the real risk in this row.** A class with a method that calls itself is the shortest script anybody could write that reaches [R11](PRD.md#12-risks), and a `StackOverflowException` ends the application rather than the evaluation - so methods, constructors, operators and property accessors of a declared type are now bracketed like local functions. `public int X => X;` found the gap by taking the test process down: an expression-bodied property has no accessor node to visit. **One pre-existing defect fell out of generalising the rewrite**: turning `=> expr;` into a block discards the member's own semicolon token, and for `double Twice(double x) => x *;` that token is where the parser hung `CS1525` - so the weaver deleted the only complaint about a line that plainly does not compile and the editor showed nothing at all. A member that does not parse is now left alone, which loses nothing: it never runs. `GeneratorVersion` is **7**. **What this row does not do**, and the client's screenshot asked for it: a type declared in one block is still invisible to another. Each block is its own assembly with no reference to any other, and graph-scope types need an ordering, a cycle rule and an invalidation rule - `E6-T35` |
| E6-T35 | One shared assembly for every type a graph's blocks declare | Done | **The scripting half of the client's second block.** `ScriptNodeFactory.Share` takes every block's source, gathers their top-level type declarations with [E6-T34](#e6--c-code-block)'s own splitter, compiles all of them into **one** assembly and hands it to every block as a reference. **The row's own first framing was wrong and is corrected here rather than quietly dropped**: it claimed three problems - order, cycles, invalidation - and two of them were artefacts of a design nobody had committed to. They only exist if each block keeps its own assembly and consumers reference their definers; **one compilation has no compile order and no cycle question**, because C# has resolved mutual references within a compilation since version 1, and a test declares `Odd` in one block and `Even` in another calling each other to prove it. What is genuinely left is **invalidation**, and it is paid by putting the shared set's fingerprint into the resident key, the disk key and the node's content hash. **A declaration lives in exactly one assembly, and that is forced rather than tidy**: two assemblies each holding `SparkGenerated.Helper` hold two different CLR types, so a block that emitted its own copy *and* referenced the shared one would hand an object down a wire the receiving block could not cast - and the message would name `Helper` twice ([N127](NOTES.md)). The test that would catch that is not the one that compiles both blocks; it is the one that makes an instance in one and reads a field off it in the other. **A block whose script is not in the shared set keeps its own declarations**, so every existing caller - and there are no new ones yet - behaves exactly as `E6-T34` left it. **A shared compilation that does not build switches sharing off** rather than stopping every block in the graph: one unclosed brace returns the product to where it stood an hour ago instead of breaking nine blocks that are fine, and the errors are kept, placed on the lines of the block that wrote them. **The assembly's name carries the fingerprint**, because a second assembly with the same simple name in one load context binds to whichever arrived first - which is a block silently getting the members of a version it was not compiled against, and it showed up as a hard failure the first time a set was shared twice. Ten tests |
| E6-T36 | The graph tells the factory about its blocks | Done | **This is the row that makes the client's screenshot work.** [E6-T35](#e6--c-code-block) built `Share`, proved it and gave it no callers; three now exist, and the choice of *three* rather than the five the row first listed is the thing worth recording. `GraphDocument.Restore` shares before it builds any block, because a block compiled before the document has been described would be told that a class two nodes away does not exist - and at that instant it would be right. `CommitScript` shares the **uncommitted** text, so a block's own declarations are in the shared assembly before it is compiled against them, and rebuilds **every other block** when the declared set moved. **The third is the catch-all and it replaces the other two the row asked for**: a block can arrive or leave by being placed, deleted, pasted, undone, redone, collapsed into a custom node or replaced wholesale, and enumerating gestures is a list that goes stale the next time somebody adds one - so `EvaluateAsync` shares once before the graph runs, which is the one place all of them pass through, and an unmoved set costs a hash. **`CanvasGraph.Retype` needed no change and gets a test saying why**: a wire landing cannot change what any block declares, and its key already carries the fingerprint. **The defect this row found in `E6-T35`**: the fingerprint had gone into the two *cache* keys and not into `ContentHash`, which is the node's **key** - so `RebuildScripts` compared a consumer's old key with its new one, found them equal, and rebuilt nothing. Every cross-block test passed at the scripting level and none worked on a canvas, which is exactly the gap between a unit and a seam. **The assertion that catches it is about the block nobody edited**: rename a class in one block and the block that used it must go red without being touched. **Verified in the application**, with the client's own two blocks in a `.spark` file: both nodes clean, and DIAGNOSTICS reads *2 nodes evaluated, 0 served from cache. No diagnostics.* Six canvas tests, one seam test asserting the *order* of `Share` and `Create`. **That seam test was wrong when written and was corrected on 2026-09-09**: it pinned the order the two blocks appear in the document, and `GraphDocument.Capture` orders nodes by their `Guid` - so it was a coin flip that had come up heads four times before it failed a full run during [E6-T37](#e6--c-code-block). It now asserts what actually matters - one share, holding every script, before any create - and nothing about which block is named first ([N129](NOTES.md)) |
| E6-T37 | Completion, signature help and quick info can see another block's types | Done | **Reported by the client in the same screenshot as [E8-T75](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas)**: typing `Test.` offered snippets and nothing else. `ScriptCompletion` is built once from the `ReferenceCatalog` and **never saw the shared declarations assembly** [E6-T35](#e6--c-code-block) compiles - so a class in the block next door bound perfectly in the compiler that runs the script and did not exist at all in the workspace that answers the editor. That is [E6-T13](#e6--c-code-block)'s forbidden state, and it is the gap in `E6-T35` rather than a regression: before that row there was nothing in another block to see. **The references are swapped in place rather than the service rebuilt**, because Roslyn composes its host services through MEF on first use and that is the most expensive thing in the application to touch; it is keyed on the fingerprint, so the common call - and it is made on every completion, every signature and every hover - is a string comparison. **The update goes in `SparkSession.Completion()`, which is the one place all three consumers pass through**, so none of them can forget. **The reference alone bought nothing, and that is the finding worth keeping**: a block's real source is compiled *inside* `namespace SparkGenerated`, so the user's `Helper` is nameable unqualified there - the completion document is a script in the global namespace, where referencing the assembly makes the type exist and leaves it reachable only as `SparkGenerated.Helper`. The namespace has to go into the project's usings with the reference. Found by the first run of the tests: the reference applied, the call returned true, and the list was still empty. **The three view-model paths now share the uncommitted text first**, the way `DiagnoseScriptAsync` already did, or the service answers a keystroke behind the editor. **Five tests, built through `ReferenceCatalog` and not the assembly-list constructor** - that is the seam `E6-T32` records the last completion defect hiding in - including one that omits the reference and asserts the members are absent. **Not verified in the application, and the reason is worth recording**: posing a caret after a trailing `.` is impossible, because `E6-T33` terminates the statement on commit and the pose commits |
| E6-T38 | A type a block declares is public whether or not it says so | Done | **Reported by the client**: `class Test{ … }` in one block and `Test test = new Test();` in the next answered **`CS0122: 'Test' is inaccessible due to its protection level`**. A type with no access modifier at namespace scope is **internal**, and [E6-T35](#e6--c-code-block) compiles declarations into a *different assembly* from the block that uses them - so it worked inside one block and not across two, **which is the worst shape a rule can have**. And `class Foo` is not an edge case; it is how most people spell it. **`internal` has no meaning a code block can express**: there is no assembly here a user chose, or could see, or would want to draw a line around - the assemblies are an implementation detail of how a graph is compiled.
