So a declared type is emitted `public`, and a block that wrote `internal` gets what it plainly meant rather than a lecture about a boundary it did not know existed. **A text edit rather than a syntax rewrite**, so the line count cannot move: the declaration is re-emitted verbatim into a file whose map is a line map, and only the columns on the one line the word sits on shift ([N122](NOTES.md)). **The insertion point is the part that fails quietly** - `[Obsolete] class C` would become `public [Obsolete] class C`, which does not compile - so it goes before the first modifier when there is one and before the type keyword when there is not, and an enum is not a `TypeDeclarationSyntax` at all. **Both emitters share the helper**, the shared assembly and `E6-T34`'s per-block fallback, because a graph drops to the second whenever the shared compile fails and two emitters disagreeing about a block's own types would make that fallback change *behaviour* rather than only scope. Eleven cases: every spelling, every kind, and a diagnostic inside a promoted class still landing on the user's own line |
| E6-T39 | An auto-import that cannot break a block, and the `using` that overrides it | Done | **Reported by the client an hour after [E7-T21](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) shipped, and they were right twice over**: *take care of the possible ambiguity, as the user cannot specify like `using Circle = SomeNameSpace.Circle;`*. `E7-T21` imported an added library's namespaces wholesale, which is [E6-T30](#e6--c-code-block)'s mistake with a bigger blast radius - `Autodesk.Revit.DB`, the library they actually want, defines `Arc`, `Curve`, `Line`, `Mesh`, `Plane`, `Point` and `Transform`, and so does `Spark.Geometry`, so every geometry block in every graph would have answered `CS0104`. **And a using-alias directive is legal at compilation-unit or namespace scope and nowhere else**, while a block's text is emitted inside `__Block.Run`'s method body - so there was no way out of the hole at all short of fully qualifying every use. **Two halves.** *One*: a namespace is imported only when **none** of its public type names is already spoken for, and a namespace that is refused says so, naming the types that stopped it and the `using` to write instead. Coarse on purpose - aliasing each colliding name, as `E6-T30` did, does not survive `RevitAPI`'s ten thousand public types, and *why does `Foo` resolve and `Bar` not* is a worse question than a namespace that plainly was not imported. *Two*: a `using` at the top of a block is hoisted to namespace scope by `E6-T34`'s own blanking machinery - spaces where it stood, so nothing below it moves - and re-emitted **inside** `namespace SparkGenerated` rather than beside the prelude. **That placement turned out to be worth more than the row asked for** ([N131](NOTES.md#n131--an-inner-using-does-not-collide-with-an-outer-one-it-silently-wins)): C# stops at the innermost scope with an answer, so a block's own directive **shadows** the prelude instead of colliding with it. The rule is one sentence - *what the block writes beats what Spark imported for it, in that block and nowhere else* - and an ambiguity is then only possible between two directives the user wrote, which is precisely where an alias is the answer. **`using var` and `using (…)` are statements and stay put**, which is Roslyn's own verdict rather than a text scan. The graph's directives go into `E6-T35`'s shared assembly too, or a type declared in terms of one would have left half of itself behind. 15 tests |
| E6-T40 | `--open` applies the code-block trust rule that File ▸ Open does | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11, the day it was found — and it had two doors, not one.** The constructor now reads a `--open` file's code blocks before anything is built and applies `E6-T16`'s rule **inside** its one adoption: `evaluate: run`, and the pending count, origin and banner set exactly as `TryOpenDocument` sets them, from one shared banner sentence and one *waiting for trust* diagnostic. A second adoption afterwards is what the constructor's own remarks forbid, because it would be a second run. **The second door was `--screenshot`**: `CaptureWhenReadyAsync` ran the graph itself before the shutter, so `--open FILE --screenshot` executed an untrusted graph's code blocks whatever the constructor did. **A screenshot is not consent** ([N148](NOTES.md#n148--a-screenshot-ran-the-graph-it-was-photographing)): a graph held back for trust is photographed unrun, with its banner, and a trusted file runs as before - the trade this row predicted, taken rather than worked around. 2 tests, the first watched red; the screenshot path checked by running it. Original note follows. **Found 2026-09-11 while putting [E7-T16](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)'s gate on both doors.** The constructor builds a `--open` document with `CanvasDocument.Open` and adopts it with evaluation on; it never asks `ScriptTrustStore`, so a graph containing code blocks **runs** on `--open` where File ▸ Open opens it unrun behind `E6-T16`'s banner. **Not fixed inside `E7-T16`, deliberately**: the installer registers no file association and `StartupOptions` ignores a bare path, so `--open` is typed by a person rather than reached by a double-click - and the fix changes the `--open FILE --screenshot` workflow used to check canvas changes, which would then photograph an unrun graph unless the file is trusted. That trade deserves its own commit. **The package half of this door is already gated.** The constructor's own remarks explain why the fix cannot be a second adoption after construction: two adoptions are two runs, and the last to finish wins |
| E8-T56 | A code block draws no type labels beside its ports | Done | **Found by screenshotting `E6-T29` rather than by a test**, which is why the row exists at all. On an ordinary node the span between the two port tabs is empty and a type label is the best thing that could be in it; on a code block that span **is the source**, so the labels were drawn over the user's code the moment `E6-T29` gave those ports real types to report. Before it, a single `result` port on an `object` had a short tab and no type to draw, which is why nothing had ever collided. **It costs nothing to remove, which is the other half of the argument**: since `E6-T29` the port's *name* is its kind, so a port called `integer` was being labelled `integer` and one called `string` was being labelled `text`. The rank pip still says what the type could not, and the properties pane still says the rest. **A first attempt widened the node instead** - reserving tab *plus* type in `ScriptWidth`, which is the under-measurement `SideWidth`'s own remark records one level up - and it was wrong: it left a permanent empty margin either side of every block's text for a label that should not be there |
| E8-T57 | Spark ships Dynamo's code block font | Done | **Asked for by the client** - *follow the same font as in Dynamo Codeblock*. The face is **Source Code Pro**, and it was identified rather than guessed: Dynamo bundles exactly two monospaced faces at `UI/Fonts/`, Source Code Pro and Courier Prime; Courier Prime is a slab serif and the screenshot plainly is not one; Dynamo's own notification UI names Source Code Pro as its monospace; and the screenshot's **plain zeros** rule out Consolas and Cascadia, which both slash theirs. Adobe, SIL Open Font License 1.1, so Spark may ship it - the licence travels beside it in `Assets/Fonts/OFL.txt`. **Shipped rather than named**, which is the substance of the row: the canvas asked for `Cascadia Mono, Consolas, Menlo, monospace`, a wish list that resolves to a different face per machine and to Dynamo's face on none of them. `CodeFont` is the one place the name lives, because three surfaces have to agree - the drawing on the node, the editor over it and the properties pane's editor - and `ScriptBox`'s remark says why: the drawn text and the editing text occupy the same rectangle. **No layout constant moved, and that was measured**: every glyph advances **0.6 em**, which is 6.6 px at the canvas's 11 px - exactly the `ScriptCharWidth` that Cascadia Mono was estimated against, because 0.6 em is a monospaced convention rather than a coincidence. `CodeBlockFontTests` asserts it through `FormattedText`, the same call the renderer makes, so the constant is a checked fact. **Every failure this row can produce is silent** - a font not shipped, a family name misspelt, a metric that no longer holds - so the tests check the assembly really carries the file (`sfnt` header and 188 KB, not merely a readable stream) and say plainly that the metric test alone cannot tell the shipped face from a fallback, since Cascadia has the same ratio. Help topic code samples take the shipped face too, since Spark now has one. The dropdown is [E8-T58](#e8--the-application-shell) |
| E8-T58 | A block's longest line was clipped by one gap | Done | **Found by screenshotting `E8-T57`**, and it predates it. `GraphCanvas` draws each source line at `box.x + ScriptGap` and clips to the box, and `ScriptWidth` measured the box as *exactly* the text - so the last characters of the longest line of every code block were cut off. It read as a rendering quirk rather than a sizing bug because it only ever touched one line of one block, and Cascadia Mono's slightly narrower 0.586 em hid the worst of it; Source Code Pro's true 0.6 em took the whole semicolon off `var t = 0..1..#10;`. The inset is now counted **twice**, so the text has the same margin on both sides |
| E8-T59 | The code font is a setting, in the properties pane | Done | **Asked for by the client**, who chose **one setting for the whole application** over one per block: a font is about the person reading, not the document - and it leaves the `.spark` format alone, which [E7-T7](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) promises round-trips byte for byte. Stored beside the other remembered choices, and **it triggered the thing `UpdatePreference` said to wait for**: its own remarks read *"when there is a second persisted preference, that is the moment to build the thing that holds both"*, so `PreferenceFile` now holds where the file goes, what counts as a recoverable failure and that every failure is silent - and nothing else, because nothing else has a second caller. **The list is the shipped face plus the machine's monospaced fonts, filtered by measuring**: Avalonia has no is-this-monospaced flag, so a family whose `i` and `W` are the same width and whose ten-character run is ten times its one-character run qualifies. A proportional face is excluded because node width is *characters times one character's width*, so it would draw a block over its own port tabs - `E8-T58` again, on every machine at once. **The advance ratio follows the chosen face** for the same reason: Consolas is 0.55 em where Source Code Pro is 0.6. **Three defects were found by the full suite and not by the change**: applying the preference in the view model's constructor mutated global state once per view model and raced the tests that read it, so `App` applies it once at startup; subscribing to the static in a control's constructor outlived any control never attached, so both surfaces subscribe on **attach**; and a control another thread owns is skipped rather than touched, because it is either dead or will re-apply when it is next attached. Constructing the preference is lazy, since reading a file per view model adds timing to the assembly's known flake ([E11-T27](#e11--tests-and-continuous-integration)) |
| E8-T60 | A code block opens ready to type in | Done | **Asked for by the client**: inserting one should leave it in edit mode with the caret at line 1 column 1, and opening an existing one should put the caret at the end of the last line. **Inserting did not open the editor at all** - both call sites placed the block, selected it and focused the *canvas* - so the change is `RequestScriptEdit(slot)`, the same path a click already takes, and **the `Canvas.Focus()` had to go rather than move**: focusing the canvas after opening the editor takes focus off it, and losing focus is what commits and closes it, so the block would have opened and shut in the same frame. **The caret is one rule rather than two** - it goes to the end of the text, and a new block's text has been empty since [E6-T18](#e6--c-code-block), so *the end* and *line 1 column 1* are the same position; written once and said in the code, so nobody goes looking for the branch. Brought into view as well as moved, or a long script would open scrolled to the top with its caret off screen. **Two documents had been claiming this for days** - `CodeBlock.md` said *with the cursor in it* and `code-blocks.md` said *empty and ready to type* - and neither was true until now; the client asking for it is what exposed the claim |
| E8-T61 | The editor never actually took keyboard focus | Done | **Reported by the client the moment [E8-T60](#e8--the-application-shell) made it visible**: they double-clicked to add a block, the editor opened, and *the caret was not blinking* - they had to click line 1 to start typing. **`FocusEditor` called `TextEditor.Focus()`, and AvaloniaEdit's `TextEditor` declares `Focusable = false`**, delegating to the `TextArea` inside it. So the call returned false, focused nothing, and said nothing. **It had been broken since [E8-T39](#e6--c-code-block) and was invisible for a good reason**: the gesture that opened the editor was always followed by a second click to start typing, and *that* click focused it the ordinary way. `E8-T60` removed the reason for the second click and turned a latent no-op into the whole experience. **Diagnosed by probing rather than by reading**: a throwaway test printed `editorFocusable=False areaFocusable=True`, which is the entire finding. **The retry exists because the pane makes the editor visible and focuses it in the same breath**, and a control can refuse focus until the layout pass that realises it has run; posting only when the direct call fails keeps the common case synchronous, which matters because the caret is placed immediately after. **The test asserts the half that can be asserted**: headless Avalonia grants focus to nothing at all - `Focus()` returns false for every control, activated window or not, established by probing - so `IsFocused` is not an oracle here, but *the control we focus is able to take focus* is, and it is exactly the half that was wrong. `Spark.UI` opens `InternalsVisibleTo` to its test project for it, the seam `Spark.Geometry` already has |
| E8-T62 | Changing the code font did not redraw the open editor | Done | **Reported by the client**: the Font dropdown said `Consolas` and the text did not change. [E8-T59](#e8--the-application-shell) tested the *setting* - that the list is filtered, that the choice is remembered, that a missing font falls back - and **never tested the one thing the setting exists to do**. **AvaloniaEdit caches the visual line it built for each line of text**, and a font change does not invalidate it, so the property held the new face and the glyphs stayed the old one; `TextArea.TextView.Redraw()` is the missing call. **A block opened *after* the change was always right**, which is why it looked like the setting worked everywhere except where you were looking - and is why the diagnosis had to separate *does the face reach the editor* from *does the editor repaint*. **Both halves were established by measurement rather than by reading**: a headless test proved the editor's `FontFamily` really does follow the setting live, and a screenshot A/B proved a chosen face reaches the glyphs at startup - the same two lines in Source Code Pro and in Consolas differ across **36% of the editor region** while the Lacing row above them is **pixel-identical**. **The redraw itself has no test, deliberately, and the first attempt at one was deleted**: this backend returns null from `CaptureRenderedFrame`, and `TextView.VisualLinesValid` is *always* false headlessly because the lines are never built at all - accessing `VisualLines` throws - so the assertion passed with the fix reverted. A test that cannot fail is worse than no test ([N19](NOTES.md), [N20](NOTES.md)) |
| E8-T63 | The arrow keys cycle signature overloads | Done | **Reported by the client**: the popup says `2/2` and the arrows do nothing. **Cycling was bound to Alt+Up and Alt+Down and always had been** - VS Code's own binding for `showPrevParameterHint`, chosen because plain Alt+Up/Down is *Move Line Up/Down* ([E6-T24](#e6--c-code-block)) - so this was as much a discoverability failure as a missing binding: the popup rendered a count with no affordance and nothing on screen named the keys. **Plain Up and Down now cycle as well, gated on there being more than one overload and the completion list being closed** - so the arrows keep reaching the caret in every case where there is nothing to cycle, which is exactly the case where the count is not on screen either. Visual Studio resolves it the same way, and Escape still dismisses the popup and hands the arrows back. The label reads `↑↓ 2/2`. **Three drafts of the negative test, and the first two were unfalsifiable in opposite directions**: one asserted the popup count and that an offset was not negative, neither of which can fail; the next watched the caret cross a line break, which cannot *pass*, because a synthetic `KeyDown` does not move the caret in this harness at all - established by probing with no popup open. What survives asserts `Handled`, which is the one observable difference and the one that matters: declining the key is what hands the arrow to the caret in the real editor |
| E8-T64 | The code-font list offered CJK faces | Done | **Reported by the client**, who was offered `MingLiU_HKSCS-ExtB`. **The measurement was not wrong; the question was too narrow.** Its Latin glyphs really are equal width, so [E8-T59](#e8--the-application-shell)'s monospace test answered honestly - but a code font is a *Latin* face whose letters are equal width, and a CJK face with half-width Latin satisfies only the second half. **Asked of the font's own glyph map, not of its name**: a list of families to exclude rots the moment somebody installs one nobody thought of, while whether the file has a glyph for a given ideograph is a fact about the file. **The first probe was wrong and the diagnostic caught it**: it looked at U+4E00 and U+4E2D, and every family the client saw went on being offered - `MingLiU-ExtB`, `SimSun-ExtB` and their siblings are *Extension B* fonts carrying none of the common characters. Widened to one codepoint per block through Extension H, plus Kana and Hangul; `SimSun-ExtG` survived the second attempt too, which is why it reaches G and beyond rather than stopping where the first guess looked right. **`--code-fonts` prints the list and exits**, added because no test can see what a real machine offers - the headless font manager reports two faces, so a test can assert the *rule* and never the *answer*. On this machine the list went from twenty faces including five CJK families to **fifteen Latin monospaced ones**. **Posted rather than closed inline**: closing the window inside `Opened` ends the process in a `NullReferenceException` from `ClassicDesktopStyleApplicationLifetime` |
| E8-T65 | A code block reads the same whether or not you are typing in it | Done | **Three things the client asked for while testing [E2-T59](#e2--geometry-kernel).** (1) A **note between the title bar and the source** saying why a line can carry no output port - they wrote seven `new Circle(...)` lines, got one port, said the rule was fine and asked that the block say so itself. (2) **Syntax colour always**, not only in edit mode. (3) **Line numbers always**, matching the editor. **The first two were one complaint**: a block changed appearance the instant it was clicked into, so one node looked like two different things. **The colour comes from the editor's own `IHighlightingDefinition`** rather than from a second tokeniser - a second one would be a second opinion about what a keyword is, and the two would drift until the canvas coloured `record` and the editor did not. **Cached per whole script rather than per line**, because colour is a whole-document property: a line inside a block comment is a comment however it reads alone, and a per-line cache would hand one block another block's colours. **The note costs the node height rather than costing the source room** - it has a band of its own above `ScriptBox`, which is also the rectangle the pane lays the live editor over, so the note stays readable while a block is being typed into. **The gutter is inside the measured width**, which is `E8-T57`'s lesson again: a gutter drawn in a box measured without it eats the first characters of the longest line only, and reads as a rendering quirk rather than a sizing bug. **`DocumentHighlighter` verifies dispatcher access**, so the colouring tests run on the UI thread - the canvas renders there, so the product was never at risk, but three tests passed alone and failed in company until they said so. One test asserted `Assert.All` over a list it had not checked was non-empty, which would have passed vacuously exactly when the colouriser stopped working |
| E8-T66 | Every port pill on a side is as long as the longest one | Done | **Asked for by the client over a screenshot of `Colour.FromRgb`**: *make the input port and output pills' length the same as the longest one.* `red`, `green` and `blue` are three, five and four characters, so the three tabs came out three lengths and the node's left edge was a staircase. **A port tab is a target as much as a label** - it is the whole reason ports are lozenges and not dots (`E8-T36`) - and a column of targets that all begin at the same edge and stop at three different ones is harder to aim down than a column of equal ones. **The number already existed and was used for something else**: `TabAllowance(side)` is what a code block's source area has always been placed clear of, because the alternative is text under a lozenge. `PortTab` now draws that same number, which makes the allowance exact rather than generous and removes the one way the two could disagree. **The measurement had to move with it**, or the change would be `E8-T18`'s defect again from the other side: `WidestRow` added *this port's* tab plus its type label per row, and now adds *the side's* tab once with only the type label varying - a node measured from per-port tabs while drawing per-side ones is a node whose longest port name sits under the type label beside it. **The two sides are measured separately**: they face each other across the body, and matching them to each other is a rule nobody asked for. Five tests, and the screenshot shows `Number.Range`, `Colour.FromRgb` and `Display.FromGeometryColour` each with one pill width per side |
| E8-T67 | A faint line and a tint step divide a node's inputs from its outputs | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *have a faint gray vertical center line for nodes, separating the area below title into two, input and output sections, with a very small difference in the tint.* **What it says is the first thing anybody has to learn about a node and the one thing the drawing never said** - the left edge is where wires arrive, the right edge is where they leave. `border.hairline` `#343A45`, the palette's quietest line, because a divider *inside* a surface must not read as an edge *between* two things; the output half is washed **8% black**, a step smaller than one rung of the body ladder. **The wash darkens rather than lightens, and that is Principle 2 rather than taste**: body text on a node is light, so darkening the surface under it can only raise contrast - which is why hover and selection already step the fill *down*. A brightening wash would have to be argued against every text token drawn on it; this one cannot lower a ratio at all, and a test asserts exactly that over all three body colours and all four text tokens. **A wash rather than a fourth body colour**, because a node is drawn in one of three fills and, below 67% zoom, in a lerp of one of those towards its category colour - one overlay tints all of them by the same proportion. **The geometry is `CanvasNode.BodyDivide` rather than arithmetic inside `Render`**, for the reason every other box on that type is a method: a rectangle that exists only in the draw loop is one no test can ask about. **Drawn immediately after the body and before everything else**, so the header, the tabs, the slider, the field and a code block's source all cover it - a block's body is one column of text, not two columns of ports. **Gated at 67%**, which is where the category lerp finishes and where port labels stop being drawn. Seven tests, and `design-language.md` §7.1 carries the anatomy |
| E8-T68 | Double-clicking a node's title edits it in place | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *let the user double click and edit the node title there only. When entering edit mode, keep the entire title text selected.* Renaming was in the properties pane and nowhere else, which is a panel away from the thing being renamed. **The selection is the half that makes it fast**: the commonest rename replaces the name outright - `Number.Value` becomes `radius` - so opening with the text selected makes that gesture type-and-Enter, where a caret would make it select-all-then-type every time for the benefit of the rarer edit. **`CanvasNode.HeaderBox` has three readers and that is the point**: the renderer clips the fill and the title to it, the double-click hit-tests against it, and the pane positions the editor by it - three copies of `Y + HeaderHeight` is three chances for the target to drift away from where the title is drawn. **It narrows `E8-T39` and `E8-T53` rather than making an exception for code blocks.** A block's header is now a rename target, so a single click there no longer opens the source editor - otherwise the first half of the double-click would put an editor over the block before the second half could ask for a rename, and the gesture would be unreachable on the one node kind whose body is text. **A rule with an exception for one node kind is a rule nobody can learn**, and what it costs is the 22 px of a block that was never source. **Escape abandons and Enter commits**, which is the value field's asymmetry and not the code editor's: a name is a word somebody can retype. **Typing the definition's own name back is a reset, not a custom title** - the two look identical and behave differently, because a custom title is written into the document and survives the definition being renamed underneath it. **`--rename-node` poses it for a screenshot**, for the reason `--code-block-in-node` exists: an editor that opens on a double-click exists only while somebody is double-clicking. Ten tests, and one existing test moved off the header with its reason recorded in place |
| E8-T69 | The graph exports to PNG at a chosen resolution | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *provide option to export the graph to png. User should be able to set the export resolution, with option to lock aspect ratio and default Width and Height being the Canvas size.* **A canvas-sized picture is a screenshot anybody could take** - the resolution is what makes it worth a command, because a graph going into a document or onto paper needs more pixels than a window has. **`Render` was the only size the graph could be drawn at**, so its body became `RenderScene(context, bounds, statistics)` and the export asks for a rectangle the control does not have. **The frame counter is a parameter rather than a field read**: it belongs on a screen, not in a picture somebody hands to a client. **The whole graph, not the visible part** - the view is fitted to `ComputeBounds()` for the render and **put back exactly** afterwards, because an export is not an edit and a user who exports and finds their canvas moved has been charged for a file. A test asserts the zoom and both offsets to the bit. **The aspect lock lives in `CanvasExport`, away from the window, and takes the ratio once.** Reading it back out of the two boxes means each keystroke derives one from the other from the first, and the ratio drifts by a rounding error every time; three round-trip cases assert it is stable. **The ceiling is 16,384 px a side** - a gigabyte of pixels, the largest texture most GPUs accept, and the difference between a typo and an out-of-memory. **Past 4× magnification the image grows and the graph does not**, because the fit runs through `CanvasTransform` and that is its ceiling; stated rather than worked around, because the alternative was a second zoom rule that only files obey. **The pixels are not asserted by any test and the reason is written down** ([N124](NOTES.md)): the headless platform draws nothing, so a saved `RenderTargetBitmap` is a zero-byte file whatever the scene was. `--export-graph` and `--export-size` exist for the same reason `--screenshot` does, and `--export-size 2400x1500` on the demo graph is what this row was actually looked at through. Ten tests |
| E8-T70 | A port's type label crossed the body divide | Done | **Found by the client using the application**, over a screenshot of `Solid.Extrude` drawing `Curve` and `Vector3d` past the centre line, `Solid.FilletAll` drawing `number` past it and `Display.FromGeometryColour` drawing all three past it. **Nothing sized a node's halves.** `WidestRow` measured a row as *the sum of its two sides*, which says how wide the row must be and nothing about where the split falls - so a node with a long input type and a short output one came out wide enough overall and drew the input's type across the middle. That was harmless until [E8-T67](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas) drew a line there; a label crossing it now says the opposite of what the line says. **A row is twice its wider half**: each half is `PortInset + sideTab + PortGap + typeWidth + RowGutter/2`. **Twice the wider half is never narrower than the two halves added together**, so the new rule subsumes the one it replaces rather than trading one failure for another, and a test asserts no node went below the floor. **The drawing is bounded as well as the measurement** (`CanvasNode.PortTypeRoom`): the two labels no longer share one budget, so a font that measures wider than the estimate the node was sized from (N24) drops a label rather than crossing the line. **`Point.Origin` is no longer at the minimum width and that is the rule working**: its output side needs `point` and `Point3d`, and that side is half a node. `CanvasGraphTests.ANodeIsWideEnoughForItsWidestPortRow` was asserting the old behaviour and was rewritten with the reason rather than relaxed. **Asserted over the whole shipped library** - 138 nodes, every row, both sides - because `Solid.Extrude` is where it was reported and not where it stops; seven tests, and the sweep names 40-odd offenders the moment the sum rule is reinstated |
| E8-T71 | The watch panel and the script-trust banner drew on top of each other | Done | **Found by the client**, in a graph with code blocks opened but not run with a watched node selected. Both were `Grid.Row="7"` in `InspectorPane.axaml` and they are **not** mutually exclusive, so both were visible and both were drawn. **It is the same mistake the comment at the top of that file already describes, one row later**: the note box, the code editor, the group title and the port list all sat on one row, and the first three hide themselves while the port list does not. That comment ends *the fix is two rows rather than one*, and this is that fix applied where it had not been. The banner gets row 8; the separator and the diagnostics move down one. **The regression test is the general one rather than the specific one**: it constructs the pane, groups the root grid's children by row, and asserts the only shared row is 6 - the note box, the code editor and the group title, which are three views of *what is selected* and genuinely exclusive. A fourth accidental pairing anywhere in the pane now fails |
| E8-T72 | Dynamo's preview bubble: a collapsed strip, a toggle and a pin | Done | **Asked for by the client with two screenshots of Dynamo**: an expandable pane at the bottom of a node, and a pin shown when expanded which keeps the pane after deselecting. **The bubble had two states and nothing between them.** It appeared whole when a node was selected or hovered and vanished whole when it was not, so the only way to keep one value on screen while working elsewhere was to wire a `Watch` node into the graph - a change to the document to answer a question about looking. **Now three**: a collapsed 20 px strip naming the type the node produced (the rank line when the output declares no type, because an empty strip answers nothing); a triangle that opens it onto the rank and the value, which is exactly what the old bubble showed; and a pin that keeps it open after the node stops being selected. **Every rectangle comes from `CanvasNode` rather than from measured text**, and that is what makes the two controls findable at all - the bubble is the node's own width, so `PreviewBox`, `PreviewToggleBox` and `PreviewPinBox` are arithmetic a test can assert with no window. The alternative was remembering where the last frame drew things, which is [the defect this canvas already fixed once](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas) when the spatial index was only rebuilt inside `Render`. **Two rules that are not obvious and that the gesture does not work without.** The hover has to survive the pointer leaving the node for its own bubble, or the strip disappears a frame before the click lands and the toggle is unreachable on any node that is not also selected; and a press has to hit bubbles **before** nodes, because a bubble hangs below its node and over whatever is behind it. A press on the body is swallowed rather than ignored, so it cannot start a marquee across the graph or clear the selection the bubble belongs to. **Closing unpins and pinning opens**, because a pinned closed bubble is invisible state and a pin on a strip keeps a word rather than a value. **The four geometry constants moved off `GraphCanvas` onto `CanvasNode`**, beside `PortTab` and `FieldBox`, which is where a number a test needs lives. **Session state, not document state, and that is a decision rather than an omission**: a bubble left open is the same kind of fact as which node is selected, and `ShowsValue` is the one that persists because a `Watch` node is a thing somebody put in the graph on purpose. Glyphs are `Geometry.Parse` paths like `NodeKindGlyphs`, lazily for the render-interface reason that class records - a pushpin is an emoji on most systems and a missing box on the rest. Thirteen tests, and `--pin-preview` poses the pinned state for a screenshot |
| E8-T73 | The preview bubble vanished on the way to its own pin | Done | **Reported by the client on the first try**: *pane disappears while moving the mouse down to pin it.* `E8-T72` made the hover survive the pointer moving from a node onto its **bubble**, and the bubble is drawn `PreviewGap` **below** the node - so between the two there are six world units that are neither, and that is exactly the ground a pointer crosses on its way to the toggle. The hover dropped in the gap, the bubble went with it, and the retention that was written for this could not help because it needed a hover it no longer had. **The region is the node, the gap and the bubble as one**, which is what `IsInPreviewReach` says and what the eye already sees - a bubble hanging off a node reads as part of it, and the gap is a drawn separation rather than a boundary. **Reach is a hover question and never a click question**: `IsInPreview` stayed strict, so a press in the gap still belongs to the canvas and still starts a marquee. Widening what a click lands on would have swallowed presses on nothing. **`HitTestPreviewReach` considers only bubbles already on screen**, which is what stops it being sticky: `ShowsPreview` is true for the hovered node, so a node whose bubble the pointer is travelling towards keeps itself alive, a node with no bubble showing cannot be reached into, and leaving the region clears the hover like any other move. Two tests, and the first walks the actual journey - on the node, half way down the gap, on the toggle - with the middle point being the one that failed |
| E8-T74 | The ribbon's selection label is gone | Done | **Asked for by the client**, pointing at *Nothing selected* beside the mark. **It said what the properties pane says, two inches away and at the same moment** - and what it said most of the time was a sentence describing the absence of a thing nobody had asked about. A label whose commonest value is *there is nothing here* is a label earning nothing. `SelectionTitle` stays: the pane still shows it under a heading, where somebody looking for it is already looking. **Column 1 is left empty on purpose** and is not removed - it is the `*` that pushes Run and the run-mode dropdown to the right edge, and collapsing it would move the two controls the ribbon exists for |
| E8-T75 | A code block grows while it is being typed into | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot**: the block they were typing into stayed one line tall while the block beside it was seven. **Nothing was missing - something was asked for once and never again.** [E8-T40](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas) built `ReserveScriptSpace`, the reservation that grows a node around its editor rather than letting the editor spill over the port tabs; `CanvasPane` measured the size the editor wanted **once**, in `OnCanvasScriptEditRequested`, from the text as it stood when the editor opened. `Place` was then re-called on every pan and every zoom - and always with that same stale measurement, which is why the bug survived a row specifically about re-placing the editor. **`CodeBlockEditor` gains a `Changed` event**, raised from the `OnTextChanged` it already had and suppressed exactly where that one is, so that setting `Text` from code - a pose, a restore after a commit, opening the editor - does not read as somebody typing. **It is raised before the analysis timer, and the order is deliberate**: diagnostics are meant to run behind the typing, and the size has to be in front of it or the editor spills over a node that has not grown yet. **The one thing that had to be got right is not doing this on every keystroke**: re-placing goes through `ScriptEditorSpace`, which re-measures the node and rebuilds the canvas's spatial index, so the pane compares the *measured* size and returns early when it has not moved - typing inside a line that is not the longest costs nothing. **Three tests, driving the pane rather than the pieces**, because neither piece was broken: the editor raised its changes and the canvas honoured its reservations, and nothing joined them up, so a test of either half alone passes with the defect in place. Two of the three go red when the one wiring line is removed; the third is the control that goes red if it is wired to every keystroke instead. **`CanvasPane` shows in the headless session**, which was not known before this row - [N90](NOTES.md) is about `InspectorPane` and its wrapping `TextBlock`, and the pane that hosts the canvas has no such thing |
| E8-T76 | Spark opens on an empty canvas | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *remove this default graph, keep the canvas clean.* A fresh window opened on nine wired nodes nobody had put there, so the first thing anybody did was delete somebody else's graph - and the demo's `Math.Divide` is **deliberately divided by zero**, so it also opened with a red error in the diagnostics panel, which is a poor thing for a first impression to be. **Nothing is removed**: the four demos are unchanged, all still one click away under the Graph menu, and `--graph demo` still names this one - which needed a **new branch**, because the demo used to be what every unrecognised name *fell through to* and was therefore also what a fresh session got. **The change is one expression and the work was in the tests**: 32 failed, every one of them because it called the parameterless `MainWindowViewModel` constructor and quietly relied on being handed nine nodes - freeze gestures, lacing, undo, node appearance, the missing-package banner, across eight files. Each now says what it needs, `new MainWindowViewModel("demo")`, rather than having the default put back under it; **a test that depends on a populated graph without saying so is exactly why a change this small reached eight files**, and they are better for saying it. **Three new tests**, because nothing asserted the default either way: a fresh session has no nodes, no wires, nothing in the scene and no error in the diagnostics panel; `--graph demo` still yields the nine-node graph; and a name that means nothing opens an empty canvas rather than guessing. **The viewport keeps its grid and axes**, which was checked rather than assumed - the window grab does not composite the GL surface, so the shell screenshot shows a black rectangle and the GPU read-back shows the grid |
| E8-T77 | A code block's editor opens every time, not only the first | Done | **Reported by the client as *cannot type anything in CodeBlock*, and it is a regression from [E8-T75](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas) one commit earlier.** That row gave the pane a helper that measures the size the editor wants and answers **did it move**, so that a keystroke inside a line costs nothing - and then reused the same helper on the *open* path, where the answer is the wrong question. **Most blocks are one line, so most opens want exactly what the last open wanted**: the helper said no, the guard returned early, and the editor was never placed, never shown and never focused. Every keystroke went to the canvas instead, which is a block you cannot type into. **The first open of the first block worked**, because the remembered size starts at zero - which is exactly why `E8-T75`'s own tests passed: each opened one editor once. Opening now always measures and always places, and only the keystroke path uses the answer. **Two tests, and they are about the second time rather than the first**: closing and reopening the same block, and editing a second block of the same shape. Both go red with the defect reinstated, naming what failed |
| E8-T78 | Save and Save as… are two commands, and `Ctrl+S` is the first one | Done | **Reported by the client**: *there is no Save option in Spark, we have Save As only; a file opened, on pressing `Ctrl+S`, shows the dialog for Save As instead of saving.* The menu said so plainly - one item, `_Save as...`, bound to `Ctrl+S`, wired to a handler that always opened a `SaveFilePicker`. **Saving a file you opened five seconds ago was a dialog and a chance to put it somewhere else by accident**, which is the opposite of what `Ctrl+S` means in every other application, and it had got worse with [E7-T18](#e7--packages-and-extensibility), which tells a user to save before the Packages tab will work. **One write, two commands**: `Ctrl+S` writes to the path the document already has and shows nothing; with no path it falls through to the picker, because a first save has a real question to ask. `Ctrl+Shift+S` always asks. **The fix uncovered a worse bug than the one reported, and it was one the silent save would have created**: `NewGraph` and the four demo loaders replace the document *without* clearing the path, so `New` followed by `Ctrl+S` would have written a blank canvas over the file open a moment earlier - no dialog, nothing to notice. Harmless while every save asked; destructive the instant one did not. **The path is now cleared in `AdoptGraph` where the document is replaced**, which is the one place every replacement passes through, and **undo and redo keep it** because they pass `resetHistory: false` and undoing an edit does not change which file you are editing. **A silent save still says something** - the status bar names the file, and the title bar carries it, because a save that shows no dialog and reports nothing is indistinguishable from one that did not happen. **No modified marker**, deliberately: nothing tracks whether the document has unsaved changes, and a dot with nothing driving it would be a lie in one direction or the other - `E8-T79`. 5 tests |
| E8-T79 | A modified document, marked and defended | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *confirm with user to save unsaved changes on exit.* It was already on the register, split out of [E8-T78](#e8--the-application-shell) when that step shipped a title with no marker because nothing tracked modification. **The flag is content, not a counter**: `DocumentHistory.Present` is the document as it stands, maintained by every record, undo and redo, and `IsModified` compares it with what was last written. So **editing and then undoing back to the saved state reads as clean**, which is true, and which a counter would have called modified - a prompt about a document identical to the one on disk is how people learn to dismiss prompts. It also means **one thing to keep in step** rather than a flag set at every mutation site, where the one somebody forgets is the one that loses work. **Three answers, not two.** *Cancel* is the one a two-button dialog cannot offer and the one that matters: somebody who hit close by accident needs a way back that is not *save the thing I did not mean to save*. It is the standing answer, so Escape, the close button and the window manager all keep the document. **`Closing` cannot await and then let the close continue**, so the first close is always cancelled and the window re-closed once the answer is known - the standard shape, named in the code rather than rediscovered. **Save inside the prompt reuses `SaveGraphAsync`**, so an untitled graph still gets the picker and **abandoning that picker cancels the exit** rather than closing unsaved, which is the trap in every dialog of this shape. **Guarding only the exit would have left the same hole in five other doors**, so *New*, *Open* and the four demo graphs ask the same question - `E8-T37`'s `NewGraph` doc comment had said *nothing is asked and nothing is saved* and given the reason, which no longer holds. **The first tests were wrong and the code was right**: they called `RecordEdit` with a label and no change behind it, and a document byte-identical to the one on disk is correctly not modified. 11 tests |
| E8-T80 | A console: a channel, a pane, and three nodes | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *introduce a console with `Console.Write`/`WriteLine`/`Clear` callable from a code block, nodes for these methods, dockable with a visibility toggle in the View menu.* **Three steps, and the first landed 2026-09-09.** `Spark.Api.SparkConsole` is the channel everything else writes to: bounded at ten thousand lines with the oldest dropped and **counted**, because a console that quietly loses the beginning of the output is worse than one that admits it; `Write` appends to a pending line that `WriteLine` completes, which is `System.Console`'s contract; the pending line is **readable before it is finished**, since somebody debugging with a bare `Write` is the person most needing to see it; and the changed event is raised **outside the lock**, because a handler is somebody else's code and may write to the console itself - the test for it is a handler that does, which would deadlock rather than fail if it were raised inside. **In `Spark.Api` because that is the assembly both ends can name**: a code block already imports it, and a node library must not reach for `Spark.UI`. **Two things the investigation settled before any code**: a `void` method is not imported - *it produces no value a graph can carry* - so the node façade has to return something, and `[NodeSideEffect]` already exists to mix the run epoch into the cache key, without which a console node would be served from cache and print nothing on the second run. **Step two landed the same day**: `WorkspacePane` gains a fifth member and the centre column a third pane, under the canvas and the viewport. **Hidden by default and by every preset** - a pane nobody asked for taking room from the canvas is `E8-T76`'s mistake again, and `AllPanes()`, which *Authoring* is written in terms of, is renamed `PresetPanes()` because it stopped being all of them. **When the console is hidden the proportion arithmetic is identical to what it was**, which is what keeps every existing layout test meaningful rather than merely passing. **Its height is a constant, not a fourth number in `WorkspaceLayout`** - that type serialises, so a new field is a format question and a layout saved before it existed would read back a zero-height console. **The subscription was got wrong first and the tests said so.** The view model subscribed to the **static** `SparkConsole.Changed` in its constructor, so every model a test built stayed subscribed and went on posting to a dispatcher whose headless session had ended - unrelated tests turned red at random. The pane owns it now, attached to and detached from the visual tree, which bounds it to something that exists. **Step three finished it the same day.** `Spark.Nodes.Core.Console` is one type that is both the three methods and the three nodes. **Each returns what it wrote**, because a `void` method is not imported at all - *it produces no value a graph can carry* - and passing the text through is what a graph wants anyway: a console node sits in the middle of a chain rather than ending it. `Clear` returns how many lines it removed, which is the one honest thing a clear knows. **The type carries `[NodeSideEffect]`**, without which the cache serves the first run's answer and a graph run twice prints once. **`Console` is pinned to Spark's in the prelude, not to `System`'s** - the opposite direction to `Math`, and for the same reason `Math` goes the other way: a windowed application has no terminal, so `System.Console.WriteLine` writes where nobody can look, while Spark's puts the line where the user asked for it. `System.Console` stays reachable in full, and a test says so. **All five code-block tests were watched going red** with the attribute and the alias taken out. 23 tests |
| E8-T82 | The console's home, and the default layout | Done | **The client ran `E8-T80`, dragged the console out of the centre column and dropped it under **Properties** on the right, and sent the screenshot**: *make this the default layout and Reset layout should set this.* Two changes: where it lives, and that it is **shown** rather than hidden. **It reads better there and the reason is worth keeping**: the centre column is the two views of the *graph*, and the right column is what the graph is *telling you* - what is selected, what went wrong, and now what it printed. **The right column is a `Column(Properties, Console)` inside the row**, so its width is `InspectorFraction` whenever **either** is showing - asking only about the inspector would collapse a column with a console in it and leave the console a sliver. **The centre column goes back to exactly its pre-`E8-T80` arithmetic**, which is a simplification rather than a change. **`E8-T80`'s *hidden by default* reasoning was not wrong, it was about the wrong column**: an unasked-for pane taking room from the canvas is `E8-T76`'s mistake, and in the right column it takes none. **Two doc comments and a help paragraph said the opposite of the truth afterwards** and were reversed with the decision - reversing a decision means reversing what was written to explain it. **One test changed meaning rather than value**: `Properties`' own proportion used to *be* the column width and is now its share of the column, so the factory exposes `RightColumnProportion` and the reset test asserts that instead. 3 tests changed, 1 added |
| E13-T19 | GitHub Actions turned off entirely | Done | **Made true in the tree 2026-09-12.** Switching Actions off in the settings left three workflows that would all fire the moment anybody switched it back on, so `ci.yml`'s `push` and `pull_request` triggers and `nightly.yml`'s `cron` are gone and every workflow is now `workflow_dispatch` only. The files stay rather than being deleted: the open-source release makes a public repository's minutes free again, and restoring the triggers is then the whole of turning CI back on. **The client's instruction**: *we are doing releases locally and do not need CI.* Both halves are true - `E12-T22` cut `v2026.9.0` from a developer machine rather than a workflow, and every run since the repository went private has been refused for billing anyway ([E13-T18](#e13--packaging-installer-and-release)). **Switched off at the repository** (`actions/permissions`, `enabled: false`), which stops every trigger at once and is one setting to reverse. **The workflow files stay in `.github/workflows/` and are inert** - deleting three correct files would throw away work that turning CI back on would need rewritten. **The documents were the larger half of this row**, and they were full of promises CI used to keep: `AGENTS.md` told a contributor that *CI's `dotnet test` leg is what settles it* for the `Zero tests ran` quirk - **nothing settles it now**; that `docs-freshness` fails an undocumented API change - **it cannot run**, and it never ran once even before, being `pull_request`-only on a repository with no pull requests; and that the benchmarks *run nightly against committed budgets*, which is now a manual guard. `README.md` claimed a green Linux leg on `53596ab` - true, and **the last word CI had**. **What is genuinely lost, recorded rather than glossed**: `dotnet test Spark.slnx` does not work on this machine, so CI's was the only run of it; and CI was the only build from a clean clone, so an uncommitted file now fails for the next person rather than for you|
| E13-T20 | CI stopped running when the repository went private, and what it now costs | Done | **Closed 2026-09-12 by the client's decision, which answers the question this row was holding open.** *Neither planning to spend any money for this project... Stop all github actions. We are building and testing locally.* So the cost question is settled by removing the cost: Actions is off, the automatic triggers are gone from the tree, and none of the trimming this row proposed is needed. **What guards the build instead, and it is not nothing**: every step of the marathon runs the full gates locally - a `--no-incremental -warnaserror` build, all ten test executables, and `dotnet format --verify-no-changes` - before anything is committed. **What is genuinely lost, stated rather than glossed**: a clean-room build on a machine that has never seen this tree, and the ubuntu leg, which was a second implementation of the same arithmetic and had caught a real defect Windows could not (N28). Both return free at the open-source release. **The client asked why every run was failing**, with a screenshot of nine red runs. **None of them is a test failure and no job ever started**: *the job was not started because recent account payments have failed or your spending limit needs to be increased*. **The cause is `E12-T22`** - Actions minutes are free for a public repository and metered for a private one, and the repository went private on the morning of 2026-09-09. The last green run is `E7-T17` at 06:17Z, and every push since has failed the same way in four to seven seconds. **Nothing in the tree can fix that**; it is a payment method or a spending limit and only the account owner can set it. **What the tree can fix is the waste, now that minutes cost money.** *Done*: a `concurrency` group with `cancel-in-progress`, so a superseded push stops running a full six-job matrix to answer a question about a commit that has already been replaced - there were nine pushes to `main` on 2026-09-09 alone, and three of the six jobs are `windows-latest`, billed at twice the Linux rate. *Proposed and not done, because each removes a guard and that is the client's call*: the **nightly benchmarks**, which run every night with a 180-minute timeout on a doubly-billed Windows runner whether or not anything changed; the **ubuntu leg** of the build matrix, which `D16`/`D17` already left without a requirement behind it; and skipping the **native and portable** jobs for documentation-only pushes **Re-checked 2026-09-09.** The three proposals in this row were overtaken the same day: `E13-T19` switched Actions off entirely rather than trimming it job by job, so the nightly, the ubuntu leg and the documentation-only skip are all moot while it stays off. What is left of this row is the record of *why*, and the one question for the account owner — whether to pay for minutes or leave it off — which no commit can answer.|
| E13-T21 | The shim meshes a deep copy of the shape, not the shape | Blocked | **Opened 2026-09-11 by `E12-T20`, and blocked on the toolchain rather than on a decision.** `spark_occt_tessellate` should mesh `BRepBuilderAPI_Copy(shape->shape, Standard_True, Standard_False).Shape()` instead of assigning the handle, which shares every face ([N151](NOTES.md)) - two lines and an `#include <BRepBuilderAPI_Copy.hxx>`, already written and reverted rather than committed uncompiled. It would make every tessellation independent of every other, remove the benign residue N151 names (a boolean result inheriting an input's triangulation on faces it did not touch), and let `OcctBrepKernel.Place` drop its fresh-import path. **Blocked because the OpenCascade install is gone from this machine**: `C:\devcpkg` no longer exists, so `scripts/build-native.ps1` refuses. What unblocks it: the `vcpkg install opencascade:x64-windows` in the journal's *Environment facts*, about 1.3 hours at full CPU - not started unasked. |
| E13-T22 | A face or edge index does not survive the crossing into OCCT | Open | **Found 2026-09-12 by testing a claim rather than believing a screenshot.** The managed `Brep` numbers its faces in the order `BrepPrimitives.Box` wrote them; OCCT numbers them in its own explorer order; and `OcctBrepKernel.Shell` passes the caller's indices **straight through to the shim**. **So opening managed face 1 - the top, normal +Z, verified by `BrepFaceView.NormalAt` - opens a side.** Measured on a 3×3×6 block shelled at -0.4: opening a z-face gives 26.896 and a side gives 24.256; managed faces 0 and **2** give 26.896, and managed face 1 gives 24.256. **The same applies to every index list the interface takes**: `Fillet`, `Chamfer`, `Shell` and `Draft`. **Nothing shipped is wrong today, which is why it went unnoticed**: every caller in the product passes an *empty* list meaning *all of them*, and a permutation of a whole set is that set - `Solid.FilletAll` rounds every edge, `Solid.Hollow` opens none. The defect is reachable only through `Spark.Api.IBrepKernel` directly, and **`Solid.HollowOpen` was written, tested, found broken and deleted in one step** rather than shipped. **It is a permutation, not corruption**: exactly two of the six faces behave like z-faces, so the kernel opens a real face every time, just not the one asked for - this is a mapping to be discovered rather than a shape to be repaired. **Pinned by `SolidNodeTests.AFaceIndexDoesNotSurviveTheCrossingIntoOcct` and `TheMismatchIsAPermutationAndNotCorruption`**, which assert the *wrong* behaviour on purpose and **are expected to go red when this is fixed** - whoever fixes it should delete them and write the positive assertions in their place. **Blocked on `E13-T21`**: the honest fix is a shim entry point giving each face a stable identity, and the shim cannot be rebuilt while the OpenCascade install is missing. A managed-side workaround - re-importing and matching faces by centroid - would be a second description of the same fact and is what `E2-T65` argued against. |
| E8-T83 | A slider's value never sits outside its own range | Done | **Reported by the client**: *if min and max values are set for an integer/number slider, do not let the user slide beyond those values; currently the slider slides beyond these values.* **Dragging was already clamped and a throwaway probe proved it first** - a 10-20 slider dragged three hundred pixels past the right end came back 20 - so the report was about a different door and fixing the named one would have changed nothing. **Three routes reach a slider's value and only the drag was guarded**: the properties panel takes any number typed into the value box, and **changing `min` or `max` afterwards leaves a value that was legal when it was set stranded outside the new range**. The second is what reads as the bug: `Number.Slider` clamps its *output*, so the node quietly returns the end of the range while the panel and the label still show the old number - the display and the result disagree, and the display is the half a user believes. **Clamped in `CanvasGraph.SetLiteral`**, which is the one gate every route passes through, so undo and paste are covered without naming them. **An integer slider is written back as an `int`**, or the clamp would leave a `10.0` in a port the graph promised was an `int`. **The cost is asserted rather than hidden**: retyping a range in two steps clamps at each step, so an intermediate state can move the value and the final one does not move it back - which is still better than a stored value its own node disagrees with. 7 tests, all seven watched going red |
| E8-T84 | A code block is tidied on a line break, on a click away, and on demand | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot** of `3;20;` sharing one line: *add line breaks automatically when one clicks out of a code block, or perform an auto-formatting.* **The check that made this safe was done before any code was written**: a block's outputs come from its `LocalDeclarationStatementSyntax` nodes and its inputs from the identifiers it uses without declaring, so **neither is a fact about line breaks** and reformatting cannot change a node's interface or break a wire. That is asserted rather than asserted-in-a-comment - `ThePortsAreIdenticalAfterFormatting` compiles the block both ways and compares the port names, because if the claim were false this feature would silently rewire graphs. **Parsed as a compilation unit, not as a list of statements**, because `E6-T39` lets a block open with `using` directives and a directive is not a statement - parsed as a body it would be an error, parsed as a file it is what it looks like. **Text that does not parse is returned byte for byte**: focus is lost in the middle of half-finished work, and an editor that rearranges a broken block is fighting the person trying to fix it. **Idempotent**, so clicking in and out again does not mark a saved document modified. **The text is only assigned when it actually changed**, because assigning to `TextEditor.Text` resets the undo history. 15 tests  **Extended 2026-09-10, asked for directly: *auto format the code on linebreak*, with a checkbox, on by default — and then *keep a shortcut Alt+Shift+F for manual formatting*.** **The trap is that the obvious implementation deletes the line break it fires on**: `Format` trims trailing newlines, so handing it the whole document the instant <kbd>Enter</kbd> lands takes that newline straight back out, and the feature undoes its own trigger on every line. `FormatAbove` makes the caret's line the boundary — everything above it is finished work and is tidied, the line being typed is not touched, and the newline is re-inserted explicitly. The guard against mangling a half-written `if (x) {` needed no code: the text above the caret does not parse, and unparseable text was already returned untouched. **Two caret mappings, both exact**: on a line break, add the length difference, because only the text above changed; on <kbd>Alt</kbd>+<kbd>Shift</kbd>+<kbd>F</kbd>, count the non-whitespace characters before the caret, because `NormalizeWhitespace` rewrites whitespace and nothing else so that count is invariant. **The document is edited through `Replace`, not by assigning `Text`**, which is what keeps the undo stack — acceptable to reset once on a click away, unthinkable on every line. The setting is a `CodeFormatPreference` beside the code font, application-wide and touching no `.spark` field. **And the checkbox found a layout defect that predated it**: the Properties pane's one `*` row held the editor and the font picker, and with a watch holding a value it was squeezed to nothing and painted over the WATCH panel. Settings moved to an `Auto` row of their own ([N138](NOTES.md)) |
| E8-T85 | A warning carries a warning triangle before its message | Done | **Asked for by the client, 2026-09-11, with a screenshot of the missing-package banner**: *always add warning triangle before the message, both in banner and in properties window.* **"Always" made it a rule rather than two edits**: a vector `SparkWarningGlyph` and a `SparkStateWarningBrush` in `SparkTheme.axaml`, and one `PathIcon.warning` style in `SparkStyles.axaml`, so every warning draws the same triangle at the same size in the same amber, and the next one gets it by writing `Classes="warning"` rather than by drawing its own. **A path, not the U+26A0 character**: Inter has no glyph for it and the fallback is a colour emoji font, which draws it at a different size and in different colours on every machine - `design-language.md` §5.6 already asks for state glyphs drawn as shapes for that reason, and now states the banner rule beside it. Applied to the banner under the menu and to both Properties banners (code-block trust, package trust). **Found in the same screenshot and fixed**: the Diagnostics pane said *Not evaluated: synthetic graphs are a renderer measurement* for the client's own graph, held back for trust - `AdoptGraph` gave every unevaluated graph the benchmark's reason, and now each caller says its own. Watched red first. **Also seen in the client's re-saved files, and registered rather than fixed**: `E3-T24`, script text written with `+` and `"` escaped. **Verified in the running application by screenshot** as well as by test, because the headless test application does not load the theme: the tests assert the structure the rule needs and load the theme dictionary directly. 4 tests |
| E8-T86 | The newest run wins, not the last to finish | Done | **Found and closed 2026-09-11 - when two runs overlap, the newest one's results are the ones left applied, not whichever finished last.** Found by reading, while hunting a UI test that failed once in `E2-T11` step A's gate run and did not reproduce in four more full runs; **it is not claimed to be that failure**. Every constructor that opens a graph starts a run, so a caller's own run racing that one is the ordinary case. `MainWindowViewModel.EvaluateAsync` applied results in the order they reached its apply gate, and a run that had already finished when a later one superseded it could reach the gate second - putting an older graph's results, and its geometry, over a newer one's. The constructor's own remarks had it as *the last one to finish wins*. Each run already took a generation for streaming (`E9-T7`); the view model now remembers the newest it has applied and drops an older result, stream and all. One test in `NewestRunWinsTests`, deterministic through an internal hook (`BeforeApplyingForTesting`) that holds the older run between evaluating and being applied; letting the older result through again fails it. **The gate command now keeps each test executable's output**, so a one-off failure is caught by name rather than by count. |
| E8-T81 | The UI suite is intermittently flaky under headless Avalonia | **Withdrawn** | **Raised on 2026-09-09 in ignorance of [E11-T26](#e11--testing-and-ci) and [E11-T27](#e11--testing-and-ci), which had already found this and diagnosed it better.** `E11-T26` is `Done`: two threads raced `ServerCompositor`'s lazy construction, `HeadlessSession.Run` now takes a semaphore, and the evidence was 2 failures in 16 runs before against 0 in 26 after. `E11-T27` is the **open remainder** and is the row to work: a pure view-model test that shows no window and therefore never passes through that semaphore, because `MainWindowViewModel` posts to Avalonia's **global** `Dispatcher.UIThread`. **This row's own claim was wrong**: it said every failure seen was inside `HeadlessSession.Run`, and `E11-T27` exists precisely because one is not. **The console pane's first version reproduced `E11-T27`'s mechanism exactly** ([E8-T80](#e8--the-application-shell)) - a view model subscribing to a static event and posting to the dispatcher after its session had ended - which is corroboration for that row rather than a new fault. Folded into `E11-T27` |

## E7 — Packages and extensibility

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E7-T1 | The package convention: a NuGet package tagged `spark` with a `tools/spark.json` manifest | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `SparkPackageManifest` reads and writes it, and `PackageStore` owns the on-disk layout — one folder per package **version**, which is exactly the unit `PackageLoadContext` isolates. **Assemblies are named, not discovered**: a package's `lib` folder also holds everything it depends on, and reflecting over all of it would import nodes from libraries whose authors never intended them. **Unknown properties are ignored** so a package built against a later Spark still installs, but a newer *schema* is refused rather than guessed at. Found [N69](NOTES.md). | Reusing NuGet wholesale — protocol, hosting, auth, SemVer, dependency resolution, private feeds, nuget.org reach — rather than building a registry |
| E7-T2 | NuGet client: search, install, resolve | Done | **Finished 2026-09-01, and it began with a blocking defect.** `PackageLoadContext` probed exactly one path, `<folder>/<name>.dll`, while extraction is verbatim — so **no package built by `dotnet pack` could load at all**, since those put assemblies in `lib/{tfm}/`. Every test had passed because every test package was hand-built flat. The context now picks the nearest framework folder with `FrameworkReducer` rather than a hand-written ordering, and keeps the package root last so flat packages still work. **Then dependencies**: the nuspec's nearest framework group is walked breadth-first, each resolved to the **lowest version satisfying the range** (NuGet's own rule, because taking the highest makes two installs on different days quietly differ), downloaded into `.deps/` inside the package's own folder, and probed by the same rule. **The disclosure now lists what will actually be installed**, resolved and transitive, with versions — agreeing to one package should not silently agree to five. An unresolvable dependency refuses the whole install rather than producing a `TypeLoadException` at first use. Verified end to end with a package authored and built by `dotnet pack`. |
| E7-T3 | One collectible ALC per package **version** | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `src/Spark.Packages/PackageLoadContext.cs`, with `PackageIdentity` carrying the version that makes side-by-side possible. Twelve tests in the new `tests/Spark.Packages.Tests`, including two versions of one package loaded at once with distinct `Type`s, and the weak-reference unload proof. **The lock is documented as a consequence rather than discovered as a bug:** loading by path keeps `Assembly.Location` for diagnostics and pins the file on Windows, which is the same fact as best-effort unloading seen from the filesystem, and the same reason restart is the default. Original note follows. | Not per package, which kills side-by-side; not per assembly, which kills intra-package type identity. The `Load` override decides by **file existence in the context's own folder**, not a hardcoded name list — which demonstrably rots the moment a package adds a dependency |
| E7-T4 | Contract assemblies always resolve from the default context | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `ContractAssemblies` names four — `Spark.Api`, `Spark.Geometry`, `Spark.Geometry.Io`, `Spark.Engine` — and the check runs **before** the file check, which is the mechanism rather than an optimisation: NuGet packages routinely ship what they were compiled against. **The decisive test stages a deliberately invalid `Spark.Api.dll` in the package folder and asserts the real one still resolves**; with the order reversed it throws `BadImageFormatException`, which is a kinder failure than the real bug's, a `Circle` that cannot be assigned to a `Circle`. Original note follows. | A `Circle` from package A must be the same `Type` as one from package B or nothing can be wired. **Corollary, and it is an ADR: changes to `Spark.Api` and `Spark.Geometry` are deliberate rather than routine** — they cannot be side-by-sided, so a break means every custom node DLL compiled against them is recompiled or dropped (**ADR-0019**, which supersedes ADR-0009's stricter strictly-additive rule; [R7](PRD.md#12-risks), [E1-T23](#e1--foundations-build-and-ci)) |
| E7-T5 | Upgrade: purge, unload, verify by weak reference, offer restart | Done | **The UI half landed 2026-08-31 with `E7-T10`**: *Remove* collects in a bounded loop until the context is genuinely gone, and when it is not, the status says the nodes are out but the code is still loaded and asks for a restart. **Engine half, same day.** `PackageManager.Unload` removes exactly the node keys the package contributed, unloads its context, and **returns a `WeakReference` rather than a boolean** — because purging the library is necessary and not sufficient, and a method returning `true` would be claiming to know about every cached value, compiled invoker and viewport buffer that might still hold a reference. A test proves the context is genuinely collectable once purged, from a `NoInlining` frame so no local roots it. **Restart stays the documented default.** `NodeLibrary.Remove` was added for this and says on itself that it exists for unloading a package and very little else. Original note follows. | **The unload half is built and proven as of 2026-08-31** — `PackageLoadContext` is collectible, and `AContextUnloadsOnceNothingReferencesIt` asserts a weak reference goes dead while `AContextDoesNotUnloadWhileATypeFromItIsHeld` asserts the converse, so the proof is not one that always passes. **The purge half cannot be written yet**: the registries it has to empty are the package node library, the compiled invokers and the install cache, and none of those exist until `E7-T1` and `E7-T2`. An ALC is pinned by node definitions, compiled invokers, cached values, viewport buffers and undo history. All of them must be purged. **Restart is the documented default**; live unload is a best-effort optimisation, not a promise. Declaring this on day one avoids the entire "why is the old version still loaded" bug class |
| E7-T6 | Missing-package placeholder nodes | Done | **Finished 2026-09-01 with the banner.** `MainWindowViewModel.MissingPackages()` reads the ids **off the placeholders themselves** — the placeholder keeps the `NodeKey` verbatim, so the package to install is written on the node, and recomputing means the banner clears itself once the package resolves. A strip under the toolbar names the packages and offers to find one, which **opens the manager searching rather than installing**: installing from a banner would skip the disclosure, which is the one screen where a user decides to run somebody else's code. **A first reading said a placeholder does not look like one** — its category `Missing` is unknown to `NodeCategoryNames.Parse` and falls to `Custom`. That was wrong: a placeholder throws, so evaluation marks it `Error` and it gets the ring and glyph. A `Missing` colour would have broken Principle 4 as well, since a category fill must never read as a state. Original note follows. | **The preservation half is built and proven as of 2026-08-31** — `PlaceholderNode` and `MissingNodePolicy` in `Spark.Engine`, with `GraphDocument.Restore` defaulting to `Placeholder`. The placeholder keeps the original `NodeKey`, infers its port count from what the file actually uses (the only evidence there is, the definition being absent), and **throws rather than returning null** so a graph downstream of a missing package cannot compute a confident wrong answer. **What is still owed is the banner offering one-click install**, which needs the package manager UI (**E7-T10**) and the NuGet client (**E7-T2**), neither of which exists. Original note follows. **The headline behaviour of this epic.** Placeholders preserve the definition key, every literal and every wire verbatim. A banner offers one-click install. **Nobody's graph is ever damaged by opening it on a machine without a package** |
| E7-T7 | Prove placeholders re-save byte-identically | Done | **Proven 2026-08-31.** `AGraphWithAMissingPackageReSavesByteIdentically`: a real graph is written, one node key is rewritten to a package nothing has — which is exactly what a user's file looks like on a machine without it — and the reopened document re-serialises to the same string. Nine tests in `PlaceholderNodeTests` plus a control that a graph missing nothing is unaffected. **`SparkFileTests.AnUnknownNodeIsNamedRatherThanSkipped` was rewritten rather than deleted**: its property, that a node never silently vanishes, still holds and is now asserted directly, with the old exception assertion moved to the explicit `Refuse` policy. The test that makes E7-T6 a fact rather than an intention |
| E7-T8 | Trust store and install disclosure | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** Install is now **prepare-then-commit**, because the row says the disclosure comes *before* the user agrees: `PrepareAsync` downloads and extracts to staging and returns a `PendingInstall` carrying a `PackageDisclosure`; nothing is installed until `Commit`, and `Dispose` discards. **Every field is read out of the package** — publisher, licence, project URL and dependencies from the `.nuspec`, node assemblies from the manifest — because a disclosure a package could assert about itself would be worth nothing. **Native binaries are found both by NuGet's `runtimes/{rid}/native` convention and by extension anywhere in the tree**, and it over-reports rather than under-reports on purpose. **Signature is reported as *present but unverified*, never as *signed***: Spark builds no certificate chain and the word would imply it does. `PackageTrustStore` records decisions **per version, not per publisher**, because a patch release can acquire a native dependency. Twelve tests. | Publisher, downloads, licence, signature status, transitive dependencies, node count, and **whether the package contains native binaries** — users deserve to know when the no-native-dependencies promise is being broken on their behalf |
| E7-T9 | Local DLL references with content hash and hot reload | Done | **Built 2026-09-01.** `LocalReferenceStore` records `path` + SHA-256 — both halves, because keyed on the path alone a user would still be trusting whatever the file says today, and keyed on the hash alone one copy would trust every copy anywhere. A rebuild therefore re-prompts, which is the row's own sentence. `LocalReferenceWatcher` coalesces a build's several writes into one offer and **never reloads by itself**. A **Local assemblies** tab sits beside packages, since both are code from outside Spark. **The row's non-locking claim turned out to be two claims, not one**: compiling against a file and loading it are separate handles, and only the first was already safe. `ScriptLoadContext` now resolves a user's assembly **from bytes**, proven load-bearing by switching to `LoadFromAssemblyPath` and watching the rebuild fail.
