Also fixed: the catalogue could omit `Spark.Geometry` while still telling every script to import it. Twenty-three tests, one of which calls a method that exists only in the referenced DLL. |
| E7-T10 | Package manager UI | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** Search, the disclosure, install, the installed list and removal, plus a **Packages** toolbar button and `--package-source` for an organisation's own feed. **A view model with a thin window over it, not a control**, because `Spark.Architecture.Tests` forbids `Spark.Engine` under `Views` and `Controls` and a package browser names it on the first line. **The disclosure is a gate rather than a notice**: while one is pending the window offers exactly two answers, and the native-code sentence is set apart in the warning colour because a line that reads like the four above it is a line nobody reads. **Closing discards an unanswered install**, proven in the real application — the staging folder was empty afterwards. Found and fixed two defects: a single `GC.Collect` was not enough to release a package's files before deleting them, leaving a half-deleted folder and a status line blaming a lock; and the installed list showed lower-case ids from folder names. Fourteen tests here, one in `Packages.Tests`, three on the switches. |
| E7-T11 | `.sparkcustom` format and Input/Output nodes | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `CustomNodeFile`, `CustomNodePorts` and `CustomNodeLibrary` in `Spark.Engine`. **The format is the graph format plus one `interface` object** — literally: `Write` splices the block into what `SparkFile.Write` produced rather than running a second serialiser, so there is one set of formatting decisions and the round trip cannot depend on two of them agreeing. `SparkFile.Read` ignores the block, so **a `.sparkcustom` file opens as a plain graph**, which is how a user edits one. Ports come from Input/Output nodes in canvas order (top to bottom, then left to right) — the only ordering rule a user can predict without being told it. The result is a plain `NodeDefinition`, so the replicator, the cache, the canvas and the writer need no special case. Twelve tests. Original note follows. | The same graph schema plus an interface block. Ports are defined by placing Input/Output nodes inside the definition graph. **Graph-in-graph is the same mechanism, not a separate feature** |
| E7-T12 | Collapse selection to custom node | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `CanvasCollapse` in `Spark.UI/Graph/`, split into **Plan** (infers the interface, changes nothing) and **Apply** (bookkeeping), because the inference is the part with judgement in it and a plan that changes nothing can be shown to a user first. **One input port per distinct external source, not per crossing wire** — one node feeding three inner ports is one value arriving. Ports are named after the inner port they attach to, so collapsing a `Circle.FromCenterRadius` gives a node with a `radius`. A selection nothing reads is refused with a reason rather than producing a node with no outputs. Everything is in `NodeId` terms because **slots renumber on removal**. Twelve tests. **Verified in the running application**: two nodes of the curves demo collapsed into `Custom.1`, 18 nodes evaluated with no diagnostics, and the viewport image is **byte-identical** to the run before the collapse | Extracts a subgraph and infers its interface from the cut wires. One of the four graph-model properties that survive **D8** |
| E7-T13 | Refuse recursion at save and at load, reporting the containment path | Done | **Both sides as of 2026-08-31.** The load side refuses direct and indirect recursion when a definition is built and names the whole containment path. The save side, added with `E7-T12` because collapse is what constructs one by accident, refuses in `CustomNodeFile.Write` when a body names its own key — writing it and refusing to open it afterwards would leave the user's work in a file nothing can load. **Only the direct case is checkable at write time** and the method says so: *A contains B contains A* needs every other definition to resolve, and a writer has no library. Original note follows. | **Refused at build, with the path, as of 2026-08-31.** `CustomNodeRecursionException` carries the chain and the message reads *'Acme/A' contains 'Acme/B' contains 'Acme/A'* rather than "recursion detected", which is the difference between something a user can act on and something they cannot. Both direct and indirect cases are tested. **Refusing happens when the definition is built rather than when it runs**, deliberately: refusing at evaluation would mean a graph that opens, looks fine, and hangs the first time somebody presses run. **Still owed: the save-side refusal**, which needs the collapse UI (`E7-T12`) since that is what can construct one by accident |
| E7-T14 | Groups, notes and freeze | Done | **Built 2026-09-01, and `E7` closes with it.** `NodeInstance.IsFrozen`, two new `NodeState` values and a `Freeze` button. **`Frozen` and `UpstreamFrozen` are separate from `NotEvaluated` on purpose**: not-evaluated means something upstream produced nothing, frozen means the user asked for this, and a canvas that greyed both identically would make a deliberate act look like a fault. The frozen node reports itself **once**, as `SPK1070 Information` — cascading would fill the pane with fifty copies of a situation somebody created on purpose — and downstream carries the state with no diagnostic of its own. **A group freezes together**, which is what the row title pairs them for. The flag round-trips, and is written **only when true**, so a graph nobody has frozen anything in saves exactly as it did before freezing existed. Seventeen tests, and the evaluation help topic gained a section because `SPK1070` points at it. |
| E7-T15 | Reserve a `CustomViewKey` seam in the format | Done | **Reserved 2026-08-31.** `CustomNodeInterface.ViewKey`, written and read, used by nothing. A test asserts it survives a round trip *although nothing uses it*, which is the entire value: a file written by a future version that does use it is not quietly stripped by this one. Original note follows. | Reserved **now** so adding custom node UI later needs no format migration. Resolved deliberately rather than left to be rediscovered |
| E7-T16 | The graph-local package folder, loaded only with consent | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 — the folder and its gate in one commit.** `GraphPackageGate`, in `Spark.Host` beside `ScriptTrustStore`, runs on **both doors** - `TryOpenDocument` and the constructor's `--open` path - and runs **before** the document is built, because building a code block compiles it. It lets go of the previous graph's packages, references only assemblies whose SHA-256 is recorded, and holds the rest behind a banner in the Properties pane that names each file and its short hash, with one **Trust and load** button. **One button, and it remembers**: the client's rule is that an unchanged assembly never asks twice, and a *once* answer would ask on every open. **Releasing goes by folder** (`ReferenceCatalog.RemoveUnder`), because *Add as a library…* references what it installs without going through the gate. **Referenced, not loaded**: the catalogue takes metadata references and `ScriptLoadContext` resolves only what the catalogue references. **Four defects found on the way.** `ReferenceCatalog.Remove` kept a removed library's namespaces in the prelude, so after switching graphs every block would have failed `CS0246` ([N144](NOTES.md#n144--removing-a-reference-is-not-removing-an-import)), watched red before the fix; the Packages window and the gate would each have held a `PackageTrustStore` over one file and overwritten each other's decisions ([N145](NOTES.md#n145--two-instances-of-a-record-over-one-file-overwrite-each-other)); and the constructor's `--open` path skips `E6-T16`'s script trust, which is [E6-T40](#e6--c-code-block) rather than this row; and **agreeing rebuilt nothing**, because a block's key is written into the file and so is deliberately blind to the catalogue - agreeing now forces the rebuild and starts a fresh cache epoch ([N146](NOTES.md#n146--a-code-blocks-key-is-written-into-the-file-so-it-cannot-see-the-catalogue)), and [E7-T24](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) is the same gap on the two other paths that change the catalogue. **Not covered**: a Spark package with a manifest inside `<name>.packages` is referenced like any library and contributes no nodes - see ADR-0024. 13 tests, the load-bearing one a refusal that proves nothing was referenced *or loaded*. Original note follows. **The finding and hashing half landed 2026-09-09.** `GraphPackages` finds `<name>.packages` beside a graph and enumerates both of the layouts the client drew - a loose `.dll` at the top level, and one folder per NuGet package with `lib/<tfm>/` inside. The framework choice prefers the newest `netN.0` not newer than this build, then netstandard; a package offering neither falls back to whatever is at its root, which is the hand-assembled case and the one the client asked about first. **Consent is keyed on the assembly's SHA-256**, at the client's instruction, and folded into the existing `PackageTrustStore` rather than a second store: package decisions are `id/version`, assembly decisions are `sha256:…`, and they share one file, one save path and one list a user reads when revoking. **A path-keyed decision would agree once to a filename and then load whatever later occupied it.** **Finding and permitting stay separate** - `GraphPackages` says where an assembly is and what its bytes hash to, and nothing about whether it may be loaded. Fifteen tests, and the ones that matter are not about finding files: nothing is trusted until agreed to, a rebuilt assembly asks again, an unreadable one hashes to nothing and **fails closed**, and two graphs in one folder keep their own packages. **What is left**: nothing consumes the result yet - the reference catalogue and the load context are not wired to it, and until they are a user sees no change **Re-checked 2026-09-09 and the remaining half is unchanged and specific:** nothing consumes `GraphPackages` yet — neither the reference catalogue nor the assembly load context asks it for anything — so every one of its fifteen tests passes and a user still sees no change. That wiring is the whole of what is left. |
| E7-T17 | A `.spark` names its packages first, and they are checked before anything loads | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 with step B — the list is written, and checked before anything is built.** Save records what the file already named **plus** what is in `<name>.packages` now (`GraphPackages.ToRecord`) - listed rather than hashed, because it runs on every save - and **never drops a recorded entry that is missing**, so a graph naming a package this machine does not have saves back byte for byte: `E7-T7` re-proved through the window as well as the document. Open reads the list and checks every entry (`GraphPackages.Absent`) before the gate and before any node is built. **The decision this step had to take: *fail loudly* means the graph opens with a named list, not a refusal** - `E7-T6`'s promise is that nobody's graph is damaged by opening it, and a refused graph cannot be repaired. The names go in the missing-package banner beside absent node packages, and its **Find** button is offered only for those, because no feed search puts back a file. **A recorded path is looked up under the file's current name**, whatever folder it was written under, so a rename in Explorer names both the folder Spark looked in and the one the file names; and it is **confined** - a rooted path or one containing `..` is reported absent and never looked up. **Save now asks where before it serialises**, because the list is relative to the file's own name ([N147](NOTES.md#n147--save-asks-where-before-it-serialises-because-the-file-depends-on-its-own-name)). **Makes [E7-T19](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) urgent**: Save As records the new name, and until the folder is copied with it, the copy reopens with every package named absent. 16 tests in step B - 9 for the checker, 7 through the window. Step A's note follows. **Step A, the format, landed 2026-09-11.** `GraphDocumentPackage(Path)` and a `packages` array written straight after `formatVersion` and before `nodes`, omitted when empty; `PackagesFormatVersion = 5`, written only by a file that carries a list, and `CurrentFormatVersion` 4 → 5. **The row's two open decisions, taken as it recommended.** A path is relative to the `.spark` and kept **verbatim**: the document neither resolves nor confines it, because only the host knows where the file lives, and confinement to the sibling `<name>.packages` is the host's check at open. And **no hash**: whether bytes may run is decided per hash at load by [E7-T16](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)'s gate, and a record that pinned bytes would make every rebuild of a library a change to the graph. **`E7-T7` re-proved at the document level**: a file with the section re-saves byte for byte, a file without it is still version 1 with no `packages` key, and the list is canonical however it was assembled. 7 tests. **Step B is what a user sees**: save records what is in the folder plus what was already recorded, never forgetting a missing entry, and open checks every path before anything is built and names what is absent. Original note follows. **Forced by the client's answer to *what happens when the folder is missing*: fail loudly with a named list.** Discovery by convention alone cannot do that - a folder with less in it is not *missing* anything, and the user would get a compile error naming a **type** rather than a message naming the **package**. **The client then specified the shape**: the references go **first in the file**, each at a location **relative** to the `.spark`, and every one is checked for existence before the graph is built. **First is not cosmetic - it mirrors load order.** The format is JSON written in source order with `formatVersion` first, and `GraphDocument.Restore` builds node definitions as it reads nodes - building a code block's definition *compiles* it - so the assemblies must be loaded before the first node is touched. References at the top let the reader check, alert and load before constructing anything, and let a human opening the file in a text editor see the dependencies first. **A format bump to 5**, alongside `NotesFormatVersion`, `ScriptsFormatVersion` and `AppearanceFormatVersion` - the format already versions per feature and this is the fifth. **[E7-T7](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) has to be re-proved rather than assumed**: it promises a graph naming a package you do not have re-saves byte for byte, and that promise now covers a new section. **Two decisions still open, both recorded rather than guessed.** *How relative is relative*: confined to the sibling `<name>.packages` folder, which keeps [E7-T19](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)'s Save As copy coherent and stops a path escaping into a system directory - or free, which allows a shared folder across graphs (the client's own sketch duplicates `custom_nuget_package1` across two projects) and makes Save As unable to know what to copy. **Recommended: confined now, shared later as its own feature.** And *whether the record carries a hash*: it would catch **changed** as well as **missing**, but a rebuilt DLL is normal during development and [E7-T9](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) already hot-reloads one - **recommended: name and path only**, with trust keyed on the hash separately at load time, which is where the client's *per new hash* answer lives. **Deliberately a record and not a lock**: the folder is still discovered by convention, so a user may add a DLL nobody wrote down and it will load - the record exists to make an *absence* nameable, not to refuse an addition |
| E7-T18 | The Packages window refuses an unsaved graph, and says why | Done | **The client's call, and it removes a whole class of question**: with no file there is no `<name>.packages` folder to install into, so the window would have to invent a location, move it on first save, and explain both. **The row held two sentences that pulled apart, and the second one won half the argument.** *It does not open* and *the existing local-assemblies tab is unaffected* cannot both be true while that tab has no door of its own - refusing the window would have taken away the one path that genuinely needs no graph on disk, which is the path a scratch graph is most likely to want. **The client settled it: the window opens on Local assemblies, and the Packages tab refuses in place** - the refusal stands where the search row and the lists would be, rather than beside them, because a search box that is present but dead invites the question the panel exists to answer. **The refusal names the folder it would have needed, names what still works, and offers the way out**: a **Save graph...** button, raised as an event because the window has no file dialog of its own and a second one would be a second thing to keep in step. **Saving lifts it on the window already open**, which is why the path is pushed into the browser rather than read once at construction - the browser is built once and outlives every document opened in the session. **The rule is not only a disabled button**: `PrepareAsync` refuses at the point a folder would have had to be invented, because a window can be driven from a startup switch. **Remove is not gated** - taking an installed package away needs no folder to put anything in. 7 tests |
| E7-T19 | Save As copies the package folder with the file | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11, the day [E7-T17](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) made it urgent** - since then a saved-as file records its packages under the new name, and without its folder it reopened with every one named absent. `GraphPackages.CopyFolder` copies `<old>.packages` beside the new file when Save As changes the path - **copied, never moved**, so the original still opens - and the view does it **before** the text is produced, so the new file records what is beside it. **One decision taken and recorded: a folder already at the destination is merged, and nothing in it is overwritten.** It belongs to whatever graph was saved under that name before, and replacing its library with a different build would change what that graph computes; files left alone are counted and the status bar says so. **A staged download is not carried.** **The package gate follows the file** (`NoteSavedTo`): the graph now installs into the new folder, and a gate still pointed at the old one would never release what went there - a plain Save leaves it alone, because re-opening it would rebuild every block for nothing. **A failed copy does not stop the save**: the status bar says why, and the banner names whatever did not arrive. **The cost the row predicted is real**: a graph carrying large packages is copied, and Save As stops being instant. 8 tests. Original note follows. **The client's call, and the reasoning is theirs**: *renaming the package folder becomes the user's headache*. Save As is the one rename Spark performs itself, so it is the one Spark can carry the folder through; a rename in Explorer is outside the application and is answered by [E7-T17](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)'s loud failure naming `<filename>.packages` rather than by guessing which nearby folder was meant. **Copy and not move**, so the original graph still opens - Save As leaves the original file where it was and must leave it working. **The cost is honest and worth stating**: a graph carrying two large packages is copied twice, and Save As stops being instant |
| E7-T20 | Install from nuget.org into the graph's folder, with its dependencies | In progress | **The search half landed 2026-09-09, and it is what the client actually reported.** They searched the Packages window for `nice3point` and were told *Nothing found on nuget.org. Spark packages carry the tag 'spark'.* - correct and useless: those are ordinary Revit API libraries, not Spark node packages, and the person searching wanted **a library to write code against**. **Two searches rather than one widened search**: the tagged query is still exactly right for finding node packages, which is what the global store installs, and widening it would have buried twenty node packages under a hundred thousand libraries to fix the new problem by breaking the old feature. A *Spark packages only* checkbox, ticked by default because that is still the window's main job. **The message names the way out**, which is the half the old one was missing - it stated the rule that had just excluded the result and left the user to infer that the rule was the problem, and the client inferred it by asking. It now says *Untick 'Spark packages only' to search every library on the feed.* **Tested against the real feed**, guarded by the existing reachability check: the client's own `nice3point` returns nothing tagged and results untagged, and the mirror of an existing test asserts the same query with one flag gives opposite answers. **Photographed**: 25 packages, with authors and download counts. **What is left**: the install - transitive dependency resolution, target-framework selection against `net10.0`, and two packages wanting different versions of one dependency **Re-checked 2026-09-09:** the search half is in and the install half is not started. What is left is three named problems, in this order: transitive dependency resolution, target-framework selection against `net10.0`, and two packages wanting different versions of one dependency. |
| E7-T21 | Add a NuGet package as a library, and import its namespaces | Done | **The client clicked Install on `Nice3point.Revit.Api.RevitAPI` and was told it *is a NuGet package but not a Spark package*, then asked the right question: *why do we insist?* **The rule is right and the button was wrong.** A Spark package's manifest names which assemblies hold `[SparkNode]` types, and without it Spark would reflect over a package's whole `lib` folder and turn every public static method of every dependency into a node - install Newtonsoft.Json under that rule and the library panel offers `JsonConvert.SerializeObject` as a canvas node. But the client did not want Nice3point's **nodes**; they wanted its **types**, in a code block, which needs no manifest at all. **`PrepareLibraryAsync` is `PrepareAsync` without `ReadManifest`, and that is the entire difference** - the transitive restore that [E7-T20](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)'s row called *the work* had been walking dependencies breadth-first since `E7-T2` and only ever needed asking for without the manifest. **The second half the client asked for**: *I hope the user does not have to declare the using statement.* A library the user added has its namespaces imported automatically, read from **metadata rather than `Assembly.Load`** - loading to ask a question about names would run module initialisers and put the library in the process before anybody agreed to it. **The wholesale import was wrong and the client caught it**, which is [E6-T39](#e6--c-code-block). **The button closed the row on 2026-09-09**, and three things came with it. *One*: it installs **beside the graph**, into a `PackageStore` rooted at `GraphPackages.FolderFor(graph)`, which writes `<id>.<version>/lib/<tfm>/` - the layout `E7-T16`'s finder already reads, so what the install writes is what a graph opened on another machine finds. It therefore needed [E7-T18](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) first. *Two*: **`ReferenceCatalog.SkippedImports` was written and nothing read it**, so a namespace refused for a collision was silent - the worst of `E6-T39`'s three options, because the user then types a type name that plainly exists and is told it does not. It now surfaces where the install finishes. *Three*: two defects the destination exposed - a package's **staged dependencies** were downloaded into `.deps` and never referenced, which fails as `CS0012` on the first dependency type in one of the library's own signatures; and a **half-staged download** sat inside the graph folder looking like a package, so `Discover` would have offered an unanswered disclosure's contents. Both fixed in `GraphPackages`. **The gate says which of the two it will do** - *Install* over a library would promise nodes that are not coming. 9 tests |
| E7-T22 | The package-authoring guide | Done | **Asked for by the client the moment [E7-T21](#e7--packages-and-extensibility) shipped**: *how do we create a Spark package today, and publish to nuget.org?* **The honest answer was that nothing said.** Everything a node author needs was in the code and none of it was written where somebody outside this repository could find it: the `spark` tag and `tools/spark.json`, the reflection rules and the two attributes that only refine them, and the three traps. **The traps are findings rather than advice** - contract assemblies must be referenced from an install and never shipped, or a `Point3d` crossing the boundary is not the graph's; `dotnet pack` writes `lib/<tfm>/` and [N77](NOTES.md) is what assuming otherwise cost; and Spark **reads signatures without verifying them**, which an author must not rely on. **It says what is not proven**, which is the part a guide usually omits: no fixture packs a real project and installs it, so the round trip works and is not guarded - precisely N77's shape. It sits beside [HELP-AUTHORING.md](HELP-AUTHORING.md) rather than in `docs/help/`, because that folder is end-user help and the help window lists all of it. **No tooling exists** - no template, no `spark pack`, no end-to-end test - and the guide says so in its first paragraph rather than letting a reader discover it |
| E7-T23 | A package whose assemblies are in `ref/`, and a target framework with a platform on it | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot**: adding `Nice3point.Revit.Api.RevitAPI 2027.2.0` answered *it carries no assembly this build can use. Its 'lib' folder may target a framework Spark does not run on.* **The message was wrong on both counts** - the package has no `lib` folder, and its target is `net10.0`, which is exactly what Spark runs on. It was raised during a conversation about supporting `.NET 8`, and `.NET 8` had nothing to do with it. **Three defects.** *One*: the assemblies are under `ref/`, NuGet's **compile-time-only** folder, and both `GraphPackages` and `PackageLoadContext` looked only at `lib/`. `ref` is exactly the shape of a CAD API package - the host supplies the implementation at run time - and a graph library exists to **compile** code blocks against, so `ref` is usable rather than empty. `lib` still wins when both are present, because it is the copy that also runs. *Two*: the folder is `net10.0-windows7.0`, and `GraphPackages.Rank` did `double.TryParse` on everything after `net`, got `0`, and refused it. **`Rank` is deleted rather than patched** - its own comment said real compatibility is NuGet's resolver and not worth reimplementing, `PackageLoadContext` already used `FrameworkReducer`, and two rules for one question is how they drift. Both now share `PackageFrameworks`. *Three*: **the platform had to be added to the framework we resolve against**, or NuGet still refuses - a `net10.0-windows` asset is not compatible with a platform-neutral `net10.0` project, which is the right rule for a compilation and the wrong question for a **process already running on Windows**. Asked as `net10.0-windows` on Windows only, so a `-windows` asset is still refused on Linux and `-android` everywhere. **The message now names what the package has** and what Spark is. **The fixtures had hidden all of it**, every one building `lib/` with a bare `netN.0` moniker - [N77](NOTES.md)'s lesson met a second time in the same layer, and the new tests are written from the real package's shape. **Verified against the client's own folder on disk**, not only against a fixture. 5 tests |
| E7-T24 | Adding a library rebuilds the code blocks that failed without it | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11, the day it was found, and the reading was right**: the test was written first and was red. **Fixed in one place rather than on each path.** `EvaluateAsync`'s catch-all (`E6-T36`), which already rebuilt the blocks when the shared declarations moved, now also compares the catalogue's version with the one the canvas's blocks were compiled against; a move forces the rebuild and starts a fresh cache epoch, exactly as agreeing to a graph's packages does. That covers *Add as a library…*, the Local assemblies tab, a graph's package folder and a graph being closed, and none of them had to change. `SparkSession.ReferencesVersion()` reads the version **without** building the script factory, because it is asked on every run and must not load Roslyn. **The version is recorded at the start of `AdoptGraph`**: a graph arrives already compiled, and the release of the previous graph's packages that follows is a change its blocks have not seen. **Not immediate, deliberately**: a block recovers on the next run, not the moment its library lands, so a Manual session shows it red until Run - starting a run from a package gesture would be a second behaviour change. 1 test, watched red. Original note follows. **Found 2026-09-11 by reading the code while closing [E7-T16](#e7--packages-and-extensibility), not by a test.** *Add as a library…* (`E7-T21`) and the Local assemblies tab (`E7-T9`) both add to the reference catalogue and rebuild nothing, and a block's key cannot see the catalogue ([N146](NOTES.md#n146--a-code-blocks-key-is-written-into-the-file-so-it-cannot-see-the-catalogue)) - so a block that failed `CS0246` because its library was not there yet should go on failing after the library arrives, until the user edits it or reopens the graph. **`E7-T16`'s *Trust and load* already does the right thing** - `RebuildScripts(force: true)` and a fresh cache epoch - and the fix is to route the other two through the same method on the view model. **Write the test first**, one per path: a block naming a type from a library, the library added, the graph evaluated, and no error. If that test is green before any change, the reading was wrong and this row is withdrawn with the reason |
| E7-T25 | `spark run` and `spark check` read a graph's package folder | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11.** Both verbs run `GraphPackageGate` **before the graph is built**, reference what `PackageTrustStore` already records, and **refuse** a graph whose folder holds anything else - naming each file and its **full** SHA-256, where the window shows eight characters, because a log is compared against a known build by a script as often as by eye. **`--trust-packages` agrees for that run only**: `GraphPackageGate.AgreeOnce()` references without recording, because a standing permission written by a job nobody is watching would become every later run's default - and the desktop application's, which reads the same record. An unreadable assembly is refused even then; it has no hash to agree to. **Only a graph with code blocks consults the folder**, the only thing that compiles against it, so a graph without one never reads the folder or the trust record and never loads Roslyn (`E6-T14`). A package the file names and the folder lacks (`E7-T17`) prints as a warning, which `--strict` fails. **The row's premise was wrong and the tree was trusted over it**: it said the command line *already refuses to run a graph's code blocks without being told to trust it*, and it does not - code blocks run unasked and only `--no-script` refuses - so there was no switch to reuse. The folder is held to a stricter rule than the blocks, on purpose ([N149](NOTES.md#n149--the-command-line-holds-a-graphs-dlls-to-a-stricter-rule-than-its-code-blocks)). `Run` now takes its two streams, as `Check` does, so that it could be tested at all. **7 tests**; reverting the call in `check` turns 5 red, among them the one proving a graph with no code block never reads its folder. Original note follows. **Noticed 2026-09-11 while preparing the client's demo of [E7-T16](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)**: `spark check demo.spark` answered *The name 'Greeter' does not exist* for a graph whose `demo.packages` held the library. `E7-T16` wired the folder into the desktop application only; the command line builds its own session and never looks for it, so a graph that runs in the window fails from a script. **The trust question is the work, not the wiring**: the command line has no banner to ask through, and it already refuses to run a graph's code blocks without being told to trust it (`E6-T16`). The defensible shape is the same answer for the folder - reference only what `PackageTrustStore` already records, name the rest in the refusal, and let the explicit trust instruction agree to them **for that run only** rather than recording anything on a machine nobody is watching. **Write the refusal test first**, as `E7-T16` did |

## E8 — UI shell and node canvas

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E8-T1 | Avalonia application shell, DI and main window | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** `Spark.Desktop` |
| E8-T2 | Docking via `Dock.Avalonia`, serialisable layout, reset, workspace presets | Done | **The shell is a real `DockControl`.** Landed in two steps because it had to: the four panes became `UserControl`s first, since a `Tool`'s content is a *template* and pane markup written inline loses the window's `x:Name`s ([N34](NOTES.md)). `Shell/SparkDockFactory.cs` builds the tree once and adjusts it in place — rebuilding would re-parent the OpenGL viewport and cost a black frame — and `WorkspaceLayout` drives it, so *Modelling*, *Authoring*, *Presenting* and *Reset layout* visibly rearrange the shell. Two failures that looked like successes are recorded as [N35](NOTES.md) and [N36](NOTES.md); `SparkDockFactoryTests` is the guard against both. **Do not port RCS's from-scratch dock manager.** The *idea* — a serialisable, testable layout model — is what was ported. **Still not wired:** the layout serialises and round-trips under test but is never written to disk, so a dragged arrangement does not survive a restart. **One regression it caused**, found three commits later by reading the frame statistics in a screenshot: Dock lays its content out later than the `Grid` did, so the startup *zoom to fit* began arriving before the canvas had a size and was silently dropped ([N39](NOTES.md)). Fixed by deferring the fit to the first arrange |
| E8-T3 | Port `SceneIndex` from DoodleSharp | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** Pure managed data-structure code with no WPF in it. Culling and hit-testing come free with it |
| E8-T4 | Immediate-mode canvas rendering over the retained index | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** One Avalonia control for the whole canvas. One control per node collapses somewhere between 500 and 2000 nodes because layout and hit-test costs are per-visual. Drawing a few thousand rounded rectangles and beziers through Skia is trivial by comparison. **One Skia backend only** — DoodleSharp's three-backend arbitration with hysteresis is deliberately left behind |
| E8-T5 | Hybrid overlay for the node under interaction | Done | **Built 2026-09-01, driven by the client's ask for Dynamo-style text and number input nodes.** Every node is drawn immediate-mode; the one being typed into gets a real Avalonia `TextBox` positioned over the drawing, one at a time by construction because there is one editor. `[NodeField]` on a method declares that its **first** input is typed on the node itself; `Number.Value` and `String.Value` carry it. The canvas draws the box and the value, names the rectangle in control coordinates and hosts nothing - it is a `Control`, not a `Panel`, and a caret, a selection, an input method and a clipboard are not things to re-implement in a draw loop. **A wired input shows no field**, because a wire beats a literal everywhere else in Spark. Text that will not parse commits nothing and is not an error: half-typed `-` or `1e` is not a mistake yet, and falling back to zero would discard what somebody was typing. **Escape clears the slot before hiding the editor**, because hiding it moves focus and `LostFocus` commits - without that, Escape would save the thing it was asked to discard |
| E8-T6 | Canvas interaction: pan, zoom, box select, drag, wire, unwire, delete, group, note, align | Done | **Pan, zoom, box select, drag, wire, unwire, delete and align are built**, and driven by headless tests using real pointer gestures - which found a bug vigilance would not have: hit-testing depended on a frame having been painted, because the spatial index was only rebuilt inside `Render`. **Align** landed 2026-08-30 as `Canvas/CanvasAlignment.cs` — six alignments and two distributions, pure arithmetic over `CanvasBounds` with no Avalonia type in the signature, reached from an **Align ▾** flyout and applied by `GraphCanvas.AlignSelection`. It is an edit that does not require a run, exactly as a drag is. Distribution equalises **gaps** rather than centers, because nodes are not all the same size. **Note** landed 2026-08-30 as far as the model and the file: `GraphDocumentNote`, `CanvasNote`, and a `notes` array in `.spark`. The format version written is now the **minimum version that can read the file** rather than a stamp of the build — forced by [ADR-0016](adr/0016-no-dynamo-interoperability.md)'s byte-identical re-save, so a graph with no notes is still version 1 to the byte. **Notes are on the canvas** as of the same day: drawn behind the wires and the nodes on `canvas.group`, hit-tested topmost-first by a linear scan rather than through `SceneIndex` (a graph with thousands of *notes* is not a thing anybody has), dragged, deleted, and created from a **New note** toolbar button. The text is edited **in the properties pane, not on the canvas** — the canvas hosts no controls ([ADR-0013](adr/0013-immediate-mode-node-canvas.md)), so a caret in it would mean writing a text editor to avoid writing a binding. Selecting a note clears the node selection, because the two cannot be deleted together without Delete becoming ambiguous. *Zoom to fit* now fits notes too. **Group** landed the same day and closes the row: a group stores **which nodes it contains** and derives its rectangle from them, rather than storing a rectangle and deciding membership by containment — the second is what most editors do, and it is why a node can quietly join a group it was merely dragged past. Only the **title strip** takes a click, because a group's rectangle is mostly the gap between its own nodes and a frame that swallowed those clicks would make a node inside a group unclickable. Deleting a group deletes the frame and **keeps every node**. |
| E8-T7 | LOD below 40% zoom | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** |
| E8-T8 | Node library panel with camel-hump search ranking | Done | **`NodeSearch`: exact → prefix → camel-hump → substring → category → description**, with ties broken by how close the match was, then by **`Create` → `Action` → `Query`**, then alphabetically, then ordinally so the order is total — a result list that reshuffles between keystrokes cannot be clicked. **The two tie-break keys were name length until 2026-09-07**, when the client photographed `circ` returning `FromPlaneRadius`, `FromThreePoints`, `FromCenterRadius`, `FromCenterNormalRadius` and asked for the order the library panel already files nodes in. Length is a real rule that nobody reading the list can see; kind and alphabet are two a user can predict. **Kind is a tie-break and deliberately not the first key** — sorting by it above the match strength would put a node that merely mentions the query in its description over one whose name *is* the query, which is the ranking this class exists to avoid, so it enters only where relevance has declared a draw. `NodeSearchCandidate` carries the three things an ordering needs, because six arguments to `Compare` is a call nobody makes correctly. `cfcr` now finds `Circle.FromCenterRadius`, which the old `Contains` filter could not. Two details earned their place: the part after the dot is searchable on its own and ranks one step behind the whole name, so `circle` puts `Circle.*` above `Arc.ByCircle*`; and a query containing a separator is not treated as capitals, because `c.b` is somebody typing a name out. **The same ranking serves the library panel and the canvas creation box** ([E8-T19](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas)) — two search boxes ranking differently is how a user learns that one of them is the one that works |
| E8-T9 | Undo and redo | Done | **A bounded stack of whole-document `.spark` snapshots ([ADR-0022](adr/0022-undo-by-document-snapshot.md)), 64 steps, on Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Y / Ctrl+Shift+Z and the toolbar.** Snapshot rather than inverse commands **for coverage, not for simplicity**: a node's position never enters the engine graph, so a command stack over the engine's own mutations would have missed moves and would go on missing every future canvas-side edit. Three rules earn their place — an edit that changed nothing is not a step, an edit whose document cannot be written clears the history rather than letting a later undo jump over it, and replacing the document starts a new history. Made instant by [E3-T8](#e3--graph-engine), and this is the row that finally **exercises** that claim rather than repeating it: after an undo the run recomputes zero nodes and serves every one from the cache, asserted in `UndoRedoTests`. Consequence to know: an undo reopens the document, so canvas slots renumber and `CanvasNode` objects are new ([N23](NOTES.md)) |
| E8-T10 | Watch panel and preview bubbles | Done | Must show **rank**, not only value — rank is what users get wrong. **Preview bubbles landed 2026-08-30.** A bubble under the node carries two lines: `rank 1 · 8 items` and then the value, with rank on its own line rather than buried in a clause, because a scalar and an empty list must never read alike and `[[1], [2]]` looks like `[1, 2]` at a glance. **Only the hovered node and the selected nodes get one**, which is a budget decision as much as a design one — laying out text for two thousand nodes would spend `E8-T15`'s whole 16.7 ms frame on strings nobody is reading — and it is why the permanent readout is a `Watch` node rather than a per-node toggle, which would also have meant a new field in the file format. `CanvasGraph.Summarise` stopped prefixing rank and length when `RankLine` arrived, so the bubble and the properties pane render the same two lines rather than the same fact twice. **`Watch.Value` and the watch panel landed the same day**, which closes the row. The node takes anything and returns it unchanged, and `[KeepStructure]` on its input port is the whole node: `object` is rank 0, so without it the engine replicates the watch and hands it one item at a time - and the list is exactly what a user opened a watch to look at. The canvas learns a node is a watch from a **declared flag**, `[ShowsValue]` surfacing as `NodeDefinition.ShowsValue`, travelling the route `Category` already travels; the canvas never names an engine type ([ADR-0005](adr/0005-api-engine-host-layering.md)). **A new `NodeCategories.Watch` was rejected** - it would have meant inventing a design-language colour and a contrast-verified row to answer a question that is not about colour. The panel renders the value in full, capped at 20,000 characters with the cut announced, because a truncation that trails off is one a reader mistakes for the end of their data. **No format change: a watch is a node, and nodes have always been in the file.** |
| E8-T11 | MVVM with CommunityToolkit.Mvvm source generators; compiled bindings on by default | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** Not ReactiveUI: fewer concepts for contributors, no runtime reflection on property change (which matters at 2000 nodes), and Avalonia no longer presumes it. Compiled bindings make binding errors compile errors. **Views never touch `Spark.Engine`** — enforced by [E11-T8](#e11--quality-and-verification) |
| E8-T12 | Settings | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - Settings is one window over the preferences that already persist.** The row was bare and `docs/EPICS.md` had no criterion for it, so one was written: *the preferences Spark remembers are in one Settings window, and each is saved as it changes*. There was no settings class and there is still no settings framework - `PreferenceFile` (`E8-T59`) keeps one answer per small file, and its remarks argue against a framework until something needs one. What was missing was a place to find them: the update check was a Help-menu tick, the code font a list in the properties pane, tidying on Enter a checkbox beside it. `SettingsViewModel` goes through **one writer per preference** - the main view model's `CodeFontName` and `FormatsOnLineBreak`, and the same `UpdatePreference` the Help menu's tick reads - because a second instance over the same file would hold a stale answer and skip a write. `SettingsWindow` is code-only like the About box, reached from *File > Settings...* and `Ctrl+,`, with no OK or Cancel because every preference already writes itself; turning the update check on there moves the Help tick and checks at once, as the tick does. The old entry points still work and read the same preferences. 4 tests in `SettingsTests`, in a collection that runs alone because the code font and the tidy switch are application-wide statics, and every preference pointed at a temporary file so a test run never changes what the person running it chose (a new internal `MainWindowViewModel.UseCodeFontPreference` makes that possible for the font). Not writing the update preference fails the two tests that read it back; the window not wiring its tidy switch fails the window test. The first time that first mutation ran, the tidy test failed as well - the window test, failing before it closed its window, left it open for the next test in the collection - so the window is now closed in a `finally`, and the class passed ten runs in a row. |
| E8-T13 | Aggressive autosave and crash recovery | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11** (FR-72). **`RecoveryStore`** in `Spark.Host` keeps one working copy per window session under the user's local application data - a JSON file holding the process id, the file the graph belongs to, and the graph's `.spark` text - written by replacing a temporary file so a crash mid-write leaves the previous copy. **`MainWindowViewModel.KeepRecovery`** runs from `RefreshHistory` and `MarkSaved`, so the copy follows `IsModified`: every edit, undo and redo writes it, and a save, an undo back to the saved state, a clean close or disposal deletes it. **The next start offers a leftover in a banner under the menu**, with the warning triangle and **Restore** and **Discard**, never silently: a restored graph opens *modified*, still belonging to its file, through the ordinary open so code-block trust applies; a copy identical to its file is removed without a word; a copy whose process is still running (id *and* name, because ids are reused) is somebody's live work and is not offered. **Recovery is off in the view model until the window turns it on** ([N153](NOTES.md)) - otherwise every test run would leave graphs behind for the real application to offer as lost work. **10 tests in `CrashRecoveryTests`**, every store a scratch folder and a crash faked through the store's liveness check; removing the write from `RefreshHistory` failed six of them, and ignoring the liveness check failed `ARunningSessionsCopyIsNotOffered`. **Checked in the real application**: launched with `--collapse 2`, the process wrote a working copy carrying its own id; `Stop-Process` on it left the copy; the next start showed the banner, photographed. `AccessibilityTests.TheRibbonKeepsOnlyTheRunControls` excludes the banner's two buttons by name, as it already did the other banners'. Original note follows. Necessary because [R11](PRD.md#12-risks) means the process can die with no chance to save |
| E8-T14 | Help panel and F1 | In progress | **Re-described 2026-09-11 by reconciliation.** F1, and **Help ▸ Help for the selected node**, open one reused `HelpWindow` at the selected node's topic (`MainWindow.OpenHelp`, `HelpWindow.NavigateToNode`); `HelpTopicSchemaTests` covers the topics it reads. **What is missing is the drill-down into generated reference**, and there is nothing to drill into until the post-1.0 Help pass generates it ([D19](PRD.md#13-decision-log)). Original note follows. Shows the hand-written topic first; the generated reference is the drill-down |
| E8-T15 | 2000-node canvas benchmark, nightly from M2 | Blocked | **Re-described 2026-09-12: nothing in the tree blocks this, and nothing here needs redesigning.** Actions is off and its triggers are removed at the client's instruction, because a private repository's minutes are billed and no money is being spent on this project. **It unblocks itself at the open-source release**, when a public repository's minutes are free again. Earlier note follows. **The measurement is real; the schedule is switched off, and those are different claims** ([N28](NOTES.md) is the note about why). The benchmark is **proven locally end to end** on 2026-08-29 — 2 000 nodes, 500 measured frames, **1.60 ms median and 2.90 ms p95** — against ceilings in `bench/budgets.jsonc` that are **ADR-0013's own claim rather than a widened measurement**: 16.7 ms median is one frame at 60 fps and 33.3 ms p95 is two. It is proven to *fire*, too: a log with no result in it fails with *the application printed nothing this check recognises*. `.github/workflows/nightly.yml` drives `--canvas-benchmark 600` on Windows — and **has never run on a hosted runner and now cannot**, because Actions is switched off (`E13-T19`). **Moved from `In progress` to `Blocked` by the 2026-09-09 audit.** The open question is unchanged and worth keeping: whether a GitHub runner with no GPU can open a window at all. If it cannot, the honest answers are a headless measurement or deleting the step, never `continue-on-error`. The standing guard on [R5](PRD.md#12-risks)|
| E8-T16 | Banners: missing package, graph contains script nodes | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 by reconciliation - the rows that built both banners closed without closing this one.** The code-block banner is `E6-T16`'s: `ScriptBanner` in `MainWindowViewModel`, offering *Run once* and *Always trust this file* (`ScriptTrustTests`, 16 tests). The missing-package banner is `E7-T17`'s: `AbsentPackagesMessage`, naming the folder Spark looked in (`MissingPackageBannerTests`, 5). `E7-T16`'s package-consent banner sits beside them (`PackageBanner`, `GraphPackageGateTests`), and all three carry `E8-T85`'s warning triangle. |
| E8-T17 | Group and note rendering on the canvas | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 by reconciliation.** `GraphCanvas.DrawGroups` and `GraphCanvas.DrawNotes` draw both, built with group and note under `E8-T6`, and covered by `CanvasGroupTests` (11), `GraphCanvasGroupTests` (8), `CanvasNoteTests` (9) and `GraphCanvasNoteTests` (10). |
| E8-T19 | Double-click empty canvas to create a node there | Done | **Dynamo's gesture, and the request that prompted it asked for Dynamo's *result* — a code block — which does not exist and cannot be faked.** `Spark.Scripting` is an empty project, a code block needs per-instance node definitions the engine does not have, and its script text needs a `.spark` format change: that is [E6](#e6--c-code-block), M4, gated by the AvaloniaEdit spike ([E11-T21](#e11--quality-and-verification)) that is still untaken. What is built is the half that is not blocked, and it is the half the gesture is *for*: double-clicking empty canvas opens a ranked search box at that point, and Enter places the node there. The canvas reports **where** and never **what** — it has no library, and naming an engine type would break ADR-0005 — so the shell decides, and when the code block lands the same box gains "if what you typed is an expression rather than a name, make one of those". Also the first real inhabitant of the hybrid overlay ([E8-T5](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas)): a genuine Avalonia control in screen space over the immediate-mode drawing |
| E8-T18 | Every port shows the type it wants, on the node and in the properties panel | Done | **Found by using the application, not by reading the plan.** A port called `center` told a user nothing about what to plug into it, and the two things that would have — the library entry's signature and the colour of a wire being dragged at it — are both somewhere other than the node. `PortTypeName` is the single source both the canvas and the inspector take the words from, phrased for the person typing the value (`number`, not `Double`; `degrees`, not `Angle`). Three rules keep it from becoming noise: the name wins the row, a name that already says its type does not say it twice (`circle` returning a `Circle`), and listness stays with the port's ring rather than being repeated in text. Drawn at 82% zoom and above, by the same eight-pixel arithmetic as every other threshold in design language §7.3 — and **only when the row has room**, because a node is sized from an estimate before any text exists to measure ([N24](NOTES.md)) |
| E8-T24 | Library grouped by category | Done | A flat list of 108 nodes is a list nobody reads: it is scrolled past on the way to the search box, so the panel only ever answers a question the user already knew how to ask - and somebody who does not know `Surface.ByLoft` exists cannot search for it. **Asked for directly, against Dynamo's library.** A `TreeView` rather than a stack of `Expander`s over `ListBox`es, because several list boxes have several selections and "the selected entry" would stop being a single thing. Categories are the same strings the canvas colours by, so a node's colour and its place in the panel cannot disagree. Alphabetical with `Custom` last; groups closed with no query and opened by a search, which also narrows the tree to the categories that matched |
| E8-T25 | Number and integer sliders | Done | **Asked for directly, against Dynamo's.** A slider is a node whose value you set by *dragging* it, which is the one thing a properties-panel text box cannot do however convenient it is - the point is sweeping a value and watching the geometry answer, and that is why this node kind earns a widget when no other does. `[NodeSlider]` on a method declares a **positional** contract: four inputs, value then min then max then step. Positional rather than by name because a name would be a string matched nowhere and wrong silently; the shape is asserted over *every* node carrying the attribute, and a node of the wrong shape draws no slider rather than a misleading one. The three range inputs are ordinary ports, so they save, they appear in the panel and they can be wired - more than Dynamo allows. **The node clamps and snaps as well as the widget**, because the value port can also be typed into. **Reopened and closed again 2026-09-10 on a report from a running graph:** a slider with `min` and `max` wired to -10 and 50 read **89.69**. The track was drawn from the *literals*, which a wire never writes to, so wiring a range drove what the node computed and left the drawing at the defaults 0 to 100 - and the attribute's own remarks had said so, correctly and wrongly, since the row was first closed. The track now reads the value that came down the wire, from the last `EvaluationResult`, and falls back to the literal for the one moment before the first run. **And the value port takes no wire at all** (`[NodeUnwired]`, `SPK1015`): the thumb is its editor, and a number with two authorities has to arbitrate between them, which is a surprise to somebody whichever way it goes. The canvas draws no row for it, so a slider is one row shorter than its port count - `InputRow`/`InputAtRow` are where port index and drawn row stop being the same number ([N137](NOTES.md)). Dragging **evaluates on every pointer move and records one undo step on release**: `RecordEdit` serialises the whole document, so an entry per pixel is not an undo stack - hence `GraphEditedEventArgs.RecordsUndo`. See [N92](NOTES.md) for the two traps: a conditional whose branches are `int` and `double` boxes a `double`, and `Math.Round`'s banker's rounding sends adjacent midpoints opposite ways |
| E8-T26 | Box select: the marquee is visible, and its direction chooses window or crossing | Done | **Asked for directly, and half of it was a drawing bug of the plainest kind.** `DrawMarquee` built its rectangle with `new Rect(start, end)`; Avalonia's two-point constructor **subtracts rather than ordering**, so a drag going right-to-left or upwards had a negative width and drew nothing at all — while `CommitMarquee` selected normally on release, which is exactly the report: *the windows are not seen, however nodes are getting selected*. The fill was also `accent` at 14%, two or three code values per channel against `canvas.bg`, so even the left-to-right box was barely there; it is 27% now. The second half is the pair a CAD user already expects: **dragging right selects only what the box wholly contains, dragging left selects everything it touches**, told apart by a **solid versus dashed outline** rather than by hue, on the same shape-not-colour rule the anchor ticks follow. `CanvasBounds.Contains(CanvasBounds)` is the window rule; the spatial index already answered *intersects*, which is the crossing rule. `GraphCanvas.MarqueeRectangle` is public so the ordering can be asserted — headless drawing produces no pixels, so the rectangle is the only part of "it is visible" a test can reach |
| E8-T27 | Double-click empty canvas places a code block; the node search moves to right-click | Done | **Asked for directly, against Dynamo.** There, double-clicking empty canvas drops a code block, and double-click-then-type is how a Dynamo user writes a number, a formula or a list without hunting for the node that does it. Spark opened a search box on that gesture, so a user arriving with the habit got a dialog. The search was not deleted — both gestures are worth having and only one of them was contested — so it moved to **right-click on empty canvas**, which had no other job. Right-clicking a node, a port or a wire still does nothing: a context menu is a real feature with a real menu behind it, and half of one taught now would have to be untaught. The block lands **at the point that was double-clicked**, not at the next free slot the toolbar button uses, because this gesture is the user pointing |
| E8-T28 | An orange halo around a selected node | Done | **Asked for directly.** A six-pixel translucent `#FF8A34` band hugging the node, drawn under the crisp accent selection ring rather than instead of it. **Deliberately not `state.warning`'s amber**, which is a neighbouring hue: the two are separated by *shape* — a wide soft band against a crisp two-pixel stroke drawn further out and over the top of it — so a selected node carrying a warning still reads as both. Inflated 2.5 px against the state rings' 4 px so the halo hugs the node; a halo outside them would separate a node from its own state stroke. Verified by capture, not by test: headless rendering produces no pixels, which is why `--select N` exists beside `--freeze` and `--collapse` |
| E8-T29 | The library splits each category into Create / Action / Query, with rails and Dynamo's icons | Done | **Asked for directly, against Dynamo, and the classification is the whole of the work.** [E8-T24](#e8--desktop-shell-and-canvas) turned a flat list of 136 into ten headings and `Solid` alone still held thirty-eight; split three ways a user who wants to *make* a solid reads sixteen names rather than thirty-eight. **Dynamo can read this off the CLR member and Spark cannot** — a zero-touch node *is* the member, so a constructor is a Create and a property getter is a Query, while a Spark node is a static method on a static facade (`Point.Translate(Point3d, …)`) and every one of them looks identical to reflection. So `NodeImporter.InferKind` reads what is honestly there — the `By`/`From`/`Create` prefix or no inputs at all is a **Create**; exactly one input with no output of that same type is a **Query**, which is what separates `Curve.Length` from `Curve.Reverse`; everything else is an **Action** — and `SparkNodeAttribute.Kind` overrides it, which twenty-four first-party nodes use because their shape and their meaning disagree (`Number.Value` takes a number and returns one; `Solid.Box` is the same shape as `Solid.Union` to reflection and the opposite thing to a person). `NodeMemberKind.Auto` is the "not stated" sentinel, on `LacingMode.Auto`'s pattern and refused by `NodeDefinition`'s constructor for the same reason. Rail colours and the three glyphs are in `NodeKindGlyphs`, drawn as geometry rather than font characters because the bolt is in no font — and **lazily**, because `Geometry.Parse` needs a render interface and a static field initialiser beside `BrushOf` made every view-model test throw. **The row spacing the client also asked for is padding inside each rail border, never a margin between rows**: a margin would have chopped the vertical line into dashes |
| E8-T30 | The properties pane rebuilt as a compact spec sheet | Done | **Asked for directly, and the type dropdown was the specific complaint.** It was a full-width control on a row of its own *under* the value box: one of those is tolerable, a node with six inputs is six of them, and the pane becomes a column of dropdowns with the values it is actually about squeezed between. It is now a small control **in the left column**, where the static type label sits on every other node's row — so declaring a type costs no extra row and no extra width. The pane also gained a title/description block, hairline rules between sections and an INPUTS heading that hides when there are none. **Two things the capture found that no test could:** the `(wired)` marker was on the *name*, which is the half that cannot afford it in a narrow column — `direction (wi…` — so it moved to the type line as `Point3d · wired`; and `from the wire` did not fit the dropdown at all, so it reads `from wire`. **And one that was a real defect in the description text itself**: `XmlDocumentation.Collapse` dropped every tag whole, and the empty ones carry the nouns — `Number.Range` arrived as *"A list of numbers from up to , stepping by ."*. `paramref`, `see cref` and `see langword` now contribute the word they name |
| E8-T31 | Lacing can be changed, from the properties pane | Done | **"Lacing cannot be changed anywhere" — the client, and they were right.** `Graph.SetLacing` has existed since [E4-T6](#e4--replication-and-lacing), the mode round-trips through the file, [E3-T8](#e3--graph-engine)'s cache keys on it and the replicator obeys it; **nothing in the shell ever called it.** The pane printed `Lacing: Longest` into the description as prose, which told a user about a setting and then offered no way to reach it. A dropdown at the top of the pane, offering **all five** including `Auto` — leaving Auto out would make going back to the node author's own answer impossible once a mode had been picked — with a line underneath saying what Auto resolves to *for this node*, because two nodes both on Auto can lace differently and that is the one thing about it a user has to be told. One undo step, and a re-run, because lacing is in the cache key. **The guard is the interesting part**: the pane pushes the selected node's mode into the bound property, which to a change handler is indistinguishable from a user picking it — without `_showingLacing`, every click on a node would record "Set lacing" and start a run. `docs/help/concepts/lacing.md` said *right-click → Lacing*, which was never built |
| E8-T32 | The toolbar becomes a menu bar, under a ribbon carrying the logo | Done | **Asked for directly: *remove all buttons on top and place them all in a proper menu bar*, *add a top ribbon and include the logo*, *add logo to window favicon*.** The toolbar was **twenty-six buttons across two wrapped rows** - the wrapping was the right fix for controls falling off the right edge, and the wrong shape for twenty-six things: everything equally prominent is nothing prominent, and two rows of chrome is two rows the canvas does not get. Six headings now - File, Edit, Insert, View, Graph, Help - grouped by what a user is doing rather than by which pane implements it, every one with an Alt access key and no two sharing a letter. **The stated exception is Run and the run-mode dropdown**, which stay in the ribbon because they are what a graph author reaches for over and over; `TheRibbonKeepsOnlyTheRunControls` is what stops that exception growing back into a toolbar. **There was no logo anywhere in this repository**; the client asked for one to be designed, so `spark-logo.svg` is the editable source, `scripts/make-logo.py` renders it to a PNG and a seven-size ICO with no image library at all, and both files are swappable without touching the shell. Two icons and they are different things: `Window.Icon` is the title bar, `<ApplicationIcon>` is the task bar and Explorer. `AccessibilityTests` was rewritten around the new shape - it asserted twenty toolbar buttons, which would have gone green forever on a menu bar with none |
| E8-T33 | Reset layout has to rebuild the shell, not adjust it | Done | **A user dragged all four panes around, the window ended up empty, and *Reset layout* did nothing.** Dock removes a `ToolDock` from the tree when its last tool is dragged out and makes new ones where tools are dropped, so the docks `SparkDockFactory` recorded are orphans afterwards: setting a proportion on one succeeds and changes nothing, and `RestoreDockable` returns a tool to an owner that is not in the tree ([N101](NOTES.md)). *Reset layout* now raises its own event, and the window **builds the layout again** from the same four pane controls — the same instances, because they hold the canvas, the viewport's scene and whatever is half-typed — dropping the old tree first, since a control cannot be in two visual trees at once. Any floating windows the dragging produced are closed by the rebuild, or the shell would show the same panes twice. The regression test drags every tool into one dock and then rebuilds; with the old `Apply(Default)` in its place it fails with *the library should be back* |
| E8-T34 | Two clicks connect a wire, and a port is big enough to hit | Done | **Both asked for directly.** Dragging between two small targets is precise work and worse on a trackpad, so a click on a port now *arms* it — the wire follows the pointer with no button held — and a click on a second port connects. **The drag is untouched**: it is what anybody who has used a node editor before will try first, and this is an addition rather than a replacement. A press that travels more than 3 px is a drag and ends where it is released; one that does not is a click and arms. Escape abandons a pending wire, and so does a click on empty canvas — which then still does whatever that click would have done, because a pending wire must never swallow a selection. **And the port disc grew from 5 px to 7 px with an 18 px hit target** (was 14), so a click 8 px off center still lands on it. The design language's §7.4 row, its zoom table and its level-of-detail rows moved with it, because those numbers are load-bearing there |
| E8-T35 | A node's own name and header colour | Done | **Asked for by the client.** After twenty nodes a graph reads better when the six that matter say what they are *for* rather than what they are. A node carries a `CustomTitle` and a `ColourOverride`, both edited in the properties pane, both undoable, both round-tripping through `.spark` at a new `AppearanceFormatVersion` written **only** when something has been restyled — so a graph nobody has touched still saves byte for byte as it did. **The definition's name is kept beside the custom one rather than replaced by it**: it is the field's placeholder, so *what is this really?* stays answerable, and clearing the field puts it back. Renaming re-measures the node, or a longer name is clipped by a header measured from the old one. **The colours are the ten category fills and nothing else** — the palette whose contrast against the header text is already measured at rest, hovered and desaturated, so a recoloured node is legible by construction; an arbitrary picker needs the title to flip by luminance and is a separate feature. An unknown colour token loses the colour and never the graph |
| E8-T36 | Ports are lozenges carrying their own name, as Dynamo's are | Done | **The client asked for Dynamo's look and named the reason: its ports are very easy to connect to.** A port is now a tab flush with the node's edge, 15 px tall, as wide as the port's name with 8 px either side and never wider than two fifths of the node — and **the whole tab is the hit target**, so clicking the word `radius` starts the wire that `radius` wants. That is a fifty-pixel target instead of the seven-pixel disc `E8-T34` had just grown. The tab is an inset well in `surface.sunken`, because a port is a socket rather than a button; a connected one fills 45% towards `port.connected`; the hovered one takes the accent outline everything hoverable takes. The name moved out of the port-label row and into the tab, so the type label still starts clear of it. The disc stays as the wire's anchor and as §7.6's rank carrier, and it is all that is left below the zoom that draws names — a lozenge with no room for a word is a rectangle that means nothing. **The node and wire styling is not this row**: this is the half with the stated benefit |
| E8-T37 | File → New, and Control+drag copies a node | Done | **Both asked for directly.** *New* empties the document and starts a fresh history, on Ctrl+N and in the File menu above *Open*; it asks nothing and saves nothing, which is exactly what *Open* already does — a prompt about unsaved work needs a dirty flag the document does not have, and adding one to this command alone would make *New* the only careful door into an unguarded house. **Control+drag leaves the original and takes a copy with the pointer**, which is what Dynamo, Grasshopper and every drawing application do. It is **armed on the press and done on the first movement**, so a Control+click still adds to a selection exactly as it always has and a canvas cannot fill with copies made by people selecting things. **The deselection half of Control+click waits for the release**, which is what makes copies chain: the client reported that copying twice took an extra click, because a copy lands selected and the Control+press on it toggled it straight back out of the selection, leaving the drag nothing to copy. On a click the toggle still happens; on a drag it never does. A copy is the same definition with the same literals, lacing, freeze, declared input types, name and colour — everything but its identity — **and the wires between copied nodes are copied too**, because duplicating a chain and getting unconnected nodes is what makes people stop using the gesture |
| E8-T38 | A node is grey until somebody colours it | Done | **Asked for by the client**, and it is what Dynamo does. Every node used to take its library category's fill, which spends all of the canvas's colour saying *what kind of node this is* — a fact the node's own name already states. Grey gives the colour back to the user, so a coloured node now means **somebody chose to mark this one**; `E8-T35`'s dropdown is how they do it. `cat.custom` is the grey rather than a new colour, because its contrast against the header text is already measured at rest and hovered. **What it costs is §7.2's silhouette claim**: below 40% zoom the header fill was the only thing carrying identity and now carries none — recorded in the design language as the trade it is, rather than left to be discovered. **The library panel still colours its rows by category**, which is where *what kind of node is this* belongs |
| E8-T39 | The code editor inside the code block node, as Dynamo's is | Done | **Asked for by the client alongside [E6-T26](#e6--c-code-block)**, and the two are halves of one thing: eleven output ports are only worth having if the eleven lines are on the canvas beside them. **This was not a fight with [ADR-0013](adr/0013-immediate-mode-node-canvas.md), it is the case that ADR describes** — the canvas draws every node and hosts none, and *the one node being interacted with gets a real Avalonia control positioned over the drawing*. So every block on the canvas is a **drawing** of its source, and the block being typed into gets the same `CodeBlockEditor` the properties pane hosts, in the overlay layer that already carried the creation box and `E8-T5`'s value field. **Landed 2026-09-02.** The source rides on `CanvasNode.Script`, because the renderer's contract is that it never calls an engine API and the text changes only when the definition is replaced — which builds a new node anyway. **The source sits between the port tabs rather than below them**, so a block whose every line is a port comes out the height of its ports; the node is measured wide enough for the text *plus* an allowance for both columns of tabs, computed from the **uncapped** tab width because `PortTab` clamps to two fifths of a width this is in the middle of deciding. **Nothing new is stored in `.spark`**: the size is derived from the source, which was already saved, so ADR-0016's byte-identical re-save is untouched and there is no size to get out of step with the text. **The commit rule is not the value field's**: Enter is a newline, click-away commits, and **Escape commits too** rather than abandoning — a field holds a number somebody can retype and an editor holds a screenful of code, and undo can take an edit back where nothing can bring the typing back. Recompiles on commit and never per keystroke; the squiggles are live because `Diagnose` touches neither cache. **`--code-block-in-node` photographs it**, and getting that switch right cost three attempts and is worth reading: `Opened` fires **before the first layout**, so the canvas has no size, its `ZoomToFit` does nothing, and a screen rectangle taken from the still-identity transform is a *world* rectangle wearing a screen rectangle's name ([N111](NOTES.md)). **`ViewLayerTests` caught the first version** naming `Spark.Engine.NodeId` in a view file, which is exactly what that test is there for |
| E8-T40 | The in-node editor makes room for itself instead of covering the port tabs | Done | **Reported by the client the moment [E8-T39](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas) landed**, and it is the first of the two things that row left open. **The editor cannot simply be sized to the source rectangle**: it is drawn at a fixed legible size while the rectangle is scaled by the zoom, so on a short block — or at any zoom much below 100% — the rectangle is smaller than a usable editor, and the floors that made up the difference landed on the lozenges either side. Sizing it down instead would mean 6 px text, which is not text anybody can edit. **So the block grows.** The pane says what the editor needs *in screen pixels*, because the editor's metrics belong to the pane that hosts it; `GraphCanvas.ScriptEditorSpace` divides by the zoom, reserves that much source area on the node, re-measures it, and answers with the rectangle the editor is then placed in **exactly** — every floor that used to be applied after that call was a port tab covered up. The tabs move out to the node's new edges. **It is a reservation, not a resize**: a node's width is derived from its content every time it is measured and only a `CanvasNote` persists a size, so nothing here can reach `.spark` however the edit ends — and the room goes back **before** the commit, because committing replaces the node and a release aimed at it afterwards would either do nothing or shrink whichever node had taken its slot. The longest line is what the width is asked for, clamped at 760 px, past which the editor scrolls sideways like every other editor  **Reopened and closed again 2026-09-10 on a second client report** — *node selection has some issues when codeblocks are present* — because the room this row reserves went into `CanvasNode.Bounds`, and `Bounds` is what the spatial index is built from. A block of 287×70 became **584×305 while its editor was open, and the extra was invisible**: empty canvas below and to the right of the block answered every click with the block, and a node standing in that stretch could not be clicked at all. Only code blocks reserve anything, which is why the report named them. **One rectangle was doing two jobs** — *how big is this drawn* and *where may it be clicked* had been the same question for every node until one node could be drawn bigger than it is. `SelectionBounds` splits them: `Bounds` still grows so the cull keeps the drawn node on screen, and hit-testing and the marquee read the node without its reservation. **The enlarged target was never reachable on purpose anyway**: the editor is a real control on top of the canvas, so clicks inside it never reach the canvas — the phantom could only ever be hit by somebody aiming at something else ([N140](NOTES.md)) |
| E8-T41 | The in-node editor's completion list is not trapped inside the node | Done | **Reported by the client from the running application**, with a screenshot: `Circle circle = new ` on a one-line block, and the list a two-pixel sliver inside it. **The cause is a trade `E6-T12` made deliberately and a host it did not have at the time.** The list is a `Canvas` overlay inside the control rather than a `Popup` — because a `Popup` needs a window overlay layer the headless session does not provide, which would have made every assertion about placement, filtering and keys a manual check. That trade is right in a properties pane the editor fills, and useless on a node two lines tall: the list is clipped to the control, and `Fit` clamped it into the control's own bounds. **`CodeBlockEditor.PopupArea`** is the fix — the area the popups may occupy, in the control's own coordinates, which the canvas pane sets to the pane rectangle and therefore hands over with a *negative* origin. Setting it turns the control's own clipping off, because a control that lets its popups leave its bounds cannot also clip to them. `Fit` now clamps into that area rather than into `Bounds`, **and checks the bottom edge as well as the other three** — a list with no room below the caret goes above it, which every editor does and this did not, because the only host until now was a pane tall enough that the case never arose. The signature popup takes its `MaxWidth` and its own above-or-below decision from the same area. **The screenshot harness cannot photograph this one**, and that is the defect the journal already lists rather than a new one: the pose now *awaits* the completion request instead of racing it, and the capture still renders a frame with the list absent. Verified by three tests over `PopupArea` and by eye in the running application — the harness is on the queue |
| E8-T42 | Bracket completion was cancelling signature help | Done | **Reported from the running application** — `new Circle(` described nothing — and it was never about the node: **typing `(` has produced no signature help anywhere since bracket completion landed in `E6-T11`.** The mechanism is two text changes where the user made one.
