Typing `(` raises `TextChanged` with the caret inside the call, which starts a request; `OnTextEntered` then inserts the closing `)`, which raises `TextChanged` again while the caret is momentarily *after* the closer — and that second request **cancels the first** and asks Roslyn from a position outside the argument list, which correctly answers nothing, which closes the popup. The fix is one re-request in `OnTextEntered` after the caret has been put back between the pair, so the last request standing is the one asked from the right place. **Three separate verification paths all missed it and none of them was lazy** ([N112](NOTES.md)): `E6-T22` was checked through `PoseCodeEditor`, which calls `RequestSignatureAsync` directly; the `--code-block` screenshot is the same pose; and every editor test in `CodeBlockEditorTests` reaches the document through a helper that *writes* text rather than *entering* it, so `TextEntered` — and therefore bracket completion — had never run in a test at all. The regression test raises real text input and uses a signature stub that answers **null for a caret outside the parentheses**, because the stub it would otherwise have used answers whatever it is asked and therefore cannot fail |
| E8-T43 | The in-node editor follows a pan and a zoom instead of closing | Done | **Asked for by the client after watching one notch of the wheel shut an editor they were typing in.** `E8-T40` resolved the collision by focusing the canvas, which commits and hides — honest rather than clever, and it reads as the application snapping shut. **A control positioned in screen coordinates over a surface that pans and zooms has two possible answers: move with it, or get out of the way.** This is the first one. `GraphCanvas.ViewChanged` is raised by a single `ViewMoved` helper that replaces the `InvalidateVisual` at every site that moves the transform — the wheel, the pan drag, `ZoomToFit`, `CenterOn` and the deferred fit — which they all had to call anyway, so routing them through one place costs nothing and removes four chances to forget. `CanvasPane` re-runs `ScriptEditorSpace` with **the same screen size it asked for originally**: the reservation is that size divided by the zoom, so re-asking keeps the editor constant and legible while the block grows and shrinks around it — `E8-T40`'s rule applied at every zoom rather than only at the one the editor opened at. **The overlay now clips to the pane**, because an editor that follows its block can be followed off the edge of the canvas, and an unclipped overlay would draw it across the viewport and the library instead of letting it leave. A block whose slot has gone takes the editor with it. **The consequence worth stating**: zoomed a long way out, the block inflates in world units to hold a constant-size editor, and can cover its neighbours until the edit is committed. That is the same rule behaving consistently rather than a second defect, and it reverts on close |
| E8-T44 | The running version, in the ribbon | Done | **Asked for by the client.** The version was in the About window and nowhere a user glances, so *what am I running?* — the first thing any bug report has to establish — cost a menu, two clicks and a dialog. `MainWindowViewModel.VersionLabel` is `"v" + SparkVersion.Of(...)`, **get-only and never notified**, because an application cannot change the version it is running while it runs and an observable property would be advertising a change that cannot happen. It sits under `SPARK` in the ribbon's left corner, **at 11 px like the name above it**: the design language puts the floor at 11 px and says nothing is smaller at any scale in any state, so a version made quieter by being tinier was not available — it is quieter by being second. `HasVersion` hides the line rather than showing a `v` with nothing after it, which is what an assembly built with no tag in reach would give. A test holds it to the same string the About window reports, because two places showing a version is two places that can disagree, and the day they do one of them is lying to somebody filing a bug |
| E8-T45 | A pane dragged off the shell was deleted rather than floated | Done | **Reported by the client: dragging any of the four panes away from the main window made it disappear, and only *Reset layout* brought it back.** Dock ships **no default host window** — `FactoryBase.GetHostWindow` reads `HostWindowLocator` and `DefaultHostWindowLocator`, `Dock.Model.Avalonia.Factory` fills in neither, and `InitLayout` does not either — so the lookup answers null and floating carries on regardless: the tool is taken out of its dock and handed to a `DockWindow` with nothing to present it in ([N116](NOTES.md)). Every step of that is a successful call with no error anywhere, which is why it presented as a pane simply ceasing to exist. `SparkDockFactory.InitLayout` now supplies both locators, keyed and default — both, because the keyed one is what Dock asks for first and a null default reopens the hole for any path that creates a window under another key. **The test that existed asserted the wrong half**: `TheViewportCanBeFloated` called `FloatDockable` and then asserted nothing, and passed for as long as the defect lived; `AFloatedPaneGetsAWindowToLiveIn` asserts the window has a `Host`, and is red without the fix. Confirmed in the running application — *Float* on the library's title-bar menu now produces a window with the library in it |
| E8-T46 | The pin and the chevron come off the pane title bars | Done | **Asked for by the client.** Dock draws a button per capability, so `CanPin` and `CanDockAsDocument` go false on the four panes — the pin was auto-hide, which collapsed a shell pane into an edge strip that then showed **nothing at all** when clicked, and *Dock as Document* could only ever have failed because this shell has no `DocumentDock`. `CanClose` was already false. The chevron has no capability behind it and `ToolChromeControl` draws it unconditionally, so it went by style at first and by an owned template in the end (`E8-T47`). A docked pane is a title and nothing else. **The floating half of this row was wrong twice and is `E8-T47`** — what was written here originally, `ToolChromeControlsWholeWindow = false`, bought the operating system's decorations and silently killed every drag back into the shell |
| E8-T47 | One title bar on a floated pane, and it is Dock's | Done | **Two client reports, one root cause: `HostWindow.ToolChromeControlsWholeWindow` reads like a presentation flag and is the switch on the whole drag-and-dock path.** `MoveDrag` opens with `if (!ToolChromeControlsWholeWindow) return;` and is what starts the window drag *and* the dock tracking, while the drag helper `ToolChromeControl` hangs on `PART_Grip` is created with `isEnabled: () => hostWindow.ToolChromeControlsWholeWindow`. **Setting it false** (as `E8-T46` first shipped) gave a pane that floated, looked right and **could not be docked anywhere**. **Overriding the two properties it sets** kept docking and produced **two title bars stacked on each other**, only the lower of which docked — reported with a screenshot. So Dock's chrome is left alone and the window buttons are put **on** it: `src/Spark.UI/Theming/DockChrome.axaml` replaces the `ToolChromeControl` control theme with Dock 12.1.0.4's own, structurally unchanged so that `PART_Grip`, `PART_Title`, `PART_Border`, `PART_Panel` and `PART_ContentPresenter` keep the names Dock finds them by, and adds minimise, maximise/restore and dock-back, floating only. **Minimise cannot be styled in** — `ToolChromeControl` wires only `PART_CloseButton` and `PART_MaximizeRestoreButton` by name and a style cannot attach a handler — so it is a command bound to `SparkDockFactory.MinimiseFloatingWindow`, the mechanism Dock's own template uses for `Owner.Factory.FloatDockable`. **The close button is deliberately not named `PART_CloseButton`**: Dock binds that name to a handler that takes the window away with the pane inside, so it is `PART_ReturnButton` and means *dock back*, with `OnWindowClosing` doing the same for every other route to a closed window ([N117](NOTES.md)). **And a floating window outlived the shell** — `DockSettings.CloseFloatingWindowsOnMainWindowClose` defaults false, so closing the main window left a pane showing a graph that had gone and the process alive behind it. **None of the three was catchable by a gate**: the headless session loads no Dock theme, so the properties read their defaults either way and the first test written passed on the defect. The tests assert the factory sets **none** of the three chrome properties — the fix as a negative — and the behaviour was measured with a throwaway probe against the real `App` |

| E8-T48 | Show and hide the Library and Properties panes from View, and `F4` | Done | **Asked for by the client.** The four named workspaces each set all four panes at once, so a user who wanted the properties pane out of the way had to accept whatever the preset also did to the viewport. `TogglePane` flips **one** pane and leaves the other three, proportions included; `IsLibraryVisible` and `IsInspectorVisible` drive the ticks. **The notification lives in one `RaiseLayoutChanged` used by the toggle, the presets and the reset alike** — a tick that only followed the toggle would go stale the moment *Modelling* hid the inspector, and a menu claiming a hidden pane is showing is worse than a menu with no tick. `IsChecked` is bound **one-way**: a checkable `MenuItem` flips its own state before the command runs, so a two-way binding writes the flipped value into the layout and the command flips it back — a tick that toggles and a pane that does not. `F4` is a `Window.KeyBinding` rather than a case in `OnKeyDown`, because that handler exists for menu entries that are `Click` handlers with no command to bind and this one has a command. **Verified with real operating-system input, and that mattered**: a synthesised `KeyEventArgs` raised on the window changed nothing and looked like a broken binding, so the probe was switched to `keybd_event` — the same measurement error as `E8-T47`'s, in the opposite direction. With a real F4 the pane hides in the shell as well as in the model, the tick settles to agree with it after a menu click, and the library and properties toggle independently. Seven tests |
| E8-T49 | Dragging a wire off an input port takes the wire with it | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot of the alternative.** `OnPointerPressed` starts a wire from whatever port is under the pointer without branching on which side it is, so pulling on a **wired input** started a *new connection from that input* — which can only be refused, so the gesture drew a red wire and a `✕` and did nothing. Correct feedback about the wrong gesture. The wire now comes off the port and the drag continues from its **source output**, which is what makes it read as picking a wire up; dropping it on another input moves it, and dropping it anywhere else removes it. **The detach is on the first movement, not on the press**, and that is the safety of it: detaching on the press would make a *click* on a wired input delete the wire, and a click on a port is not a mistake — it is `E8-T34`'s gesture for arming one. Escape abandons a pending wire without touching the graph, so the wire would not come back from the gesture that looks like a cancel. **Nothing is committed until the button comes up**: the wire is lifted only visually (skipped in `EnsureWireVisuals`, which also drops it from `_connectedPorts` so the port draws unconnected), so a drag that ends back where it started costs no edit and no undo entry. **One `GraphChanged` for the whole gesture**, because undo is a document snapshot taken when that event is raised — announcing the disconnect and the reconnect separately would cost two presses of Control+Z to put one wire back ([N118](NOTES.md)). Needed `CanvasGraph.WireInto`, since `IsInputWired` found the wire and threw it away. Five tests, two of them red without the change and three guarding the gestures that must not have moved; and confirmed in the running application with real mouse input, photographed mid-drag. **Left alone and written down**: over empty canvas the lifted wire still draws in the *rejected* colour, which is accurate about connecting and misleading about consequence — saying it properly needs a fourth drag state, and design-language §V1 refused a wire-only ramp |
| E8-T50 | The two-click gesture lifts a wire too | Done | **Reported by the client: the drag disconnected an input and the two clicks did not.** `E8-T49` lifted only once the pointer had travelled, and gave a reason — a click that removed a wire would be an accident Escape could not undo, because Escape abandons a pending wire without touching the graph. **The reason did not survive the design it was written for**: nothing is committed until the gesture ends, so a click that lifts a wire commits nothing either, and `StandDownPendingWire` — now the restore — puts it back for free. Caution carried over from a version of the code that had already been replaced. A click on a wired input now lifts it exactly as a drag does, and the second click lands it through the same `DropDetachedWire`: another port moves it, the port it came from puts it back, anywhere else removes it. `E8-T34`'s rule is kept — a click on empty canvas drops the wire **and then still does whatever that click would ordinarily have done**, because a lifted wire must no more swallow a selection than a pending one may. Four tests, two red without the change, plus the `E8-T34` guards; confirmed in the running application with real mouse input — 8 wires after the first click, 7 after the second — and photographed between the two clicks with the wire off the port ([N118](NOTES.md)) |
| E8-T51 | *Clean up node layout*, and `Ctrl+L` | Done | **Asked for by the client, against Dynamo's *Edit → Cleanup Node Layout*, and it takes Dynamo's key on purpose: a user arriving from there presses `Ctrl+L` before reading a menu.** `CanvasLayout` is pure arithmetic over boxes and index-space links, beside `CanvasAlignment` and for the same reason — no Avalonia type in the signature, so every case is a unit test rather than a window and a gesture. A node's column is **one past the furthest column any of its inputs sits in**: longest-path layering, because taking the *nearest* input lets a node sit level with something it consumes whenever a second, longer path also reaches it, and a wire that runs backwards is what the operation exists to remove. Columns are ordered by the average placed height of what feeds them, which is what keeps two independent chains from interleaving, and are cleared by their **widest** member rather than by whichever node the wire happens to leave. The arrangement is anchored at the top-left corner the nodes already had — a tidy-up that also teleported the graph would be two operations wearing one name. **The selection is the scope only when it holds more than one node**: clicking a node to look at it and then pressing the key means *tidy this graph*, never *move this one node nowhere*. **A cycle cannot hang it** — `TryConnect` refuses one but a `.spark` file can be loaded carrying one (`SPK1014`), so the relaxation is bounded by the node count. **The undo guard cost a note**: comparing positions exactly recorded a step on every press, because a box's width is `MaxX - MinX` and is not stable under moving the box ([N119](NOTES.md)). Verified by 20 tests, one of them red before the fix, and by photographing the demo graph before and after — `--clean-up-layout` exists because the gesture is a keystroke and a keystroke is what a headless run cannot make |
| E8-T52 | The open editor follows a *dragged block*, not only a moved view | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot**: drag a code block by its title while its editor is open, and the block slides out from under the editor, which stays where it was. [E8-T43](#e8--the-application-shell) had already solved this for the pan and the zoom and solved it *in the right place* - one funnel that redraws and announces - and then the two node drags each called `InvalidateVisual` directly and announced nothing. **So the event was misnamed rather than missing**: its one consumer re-places the editor whenever a *node's screen rectangle* changes, and the pan and the zoom are two of the four ways that happens. `ViewChanged` is now `ContentMoved` and `ViewMoved()` is `AnnounceMove()`, and every site that moves a node goes through it - both drags, `Align` and `Tidy`. The last two are unreachable with an editor open today, because the editor holds the keyboard and reaching a menu commits it; they are routed anyway, because the rule that survives is *moved a node, say so*, not *moved a node in a way somebody has checked matters*. **Two tests, and they fail differently on purpose**: `DraggingABlockIsAnnounced` goes red without the announcement, and `TheEditorsRectangleFollowsADraggedBlock` asks `ScriptEditorSpace` - the same question the pane asks, whose answer becomes `Canvas.Left` - before and after the drag, so a fix that raised the event and answered the same rectangle would still be caught |
| E8-T53 | A single click opens a code block's editor | Done | **Asked for by the client**, who found the double-click a keystroke too many on the node they type into most. **On the release and not the press**, because a press is the start of a drag and a block has to stay draggable: a gesture that never travelled past `ClickSlopScreen` is a click, one that did is a move, and a move opens nothing. **Slop rather than net displacement**, which is the distinction that makes it trustworthy - `_dragTotalX == 0` is the right question for *did this edit anything* (a node dragged out and back is not a move) and the wrong one for *was this a click*, because a hand that trembles one pixel has moved the node one pixel and a gesture a tremor swallows is one users stop trusting. `_wireDragMoved` already drew the line in the right place. **The slop needs its own anchor**: `_dragStartWorld` is advanced on every pointer move, since a node drag applies a *delta*, so measuring against it compares one mouse-move's worth of travel and calls every slow drag a click - `_nodeDragAnchorWorld` holds where the press landed and is never advanced. **The modifier is recorded at the press, not read at the release**, for the reason `_duplicateOnDrag` already is: the gesture is the one the user started. The first version read `KeyModifiers` off the release and a Control+click opened an editor. Control arms a copy ([E8-T37](#e8--the-application-shell)) and Shift extends the selection; both are about *which nodes* and neither is a request to type into one. **Double-click still works** - the second click of a double now lands on the editor the first one opened, so that path is usually dead, but *usually* is not *always*. Four tests, and the load-bearing one is `DraggingABlockOpensNothingButATremorStillClicks`, which drags in **sixty two-pixel steps**: a drag that jumps 120 pixels in one move passes whichever anchor is used, and only a slow one catches the wrong one |
| E8-T54 | Deleting a block left its editor on the canvas | Done | **Reported by the client with two screenshots, and the second one is the diagnosis**: the editor stayed after the block had gone, and *cleared itself on the next zoom*. The pane already hides an editor whose block is missing, and that check lives in the handler for `ContentMoved` - so the zoom reached it and nothing else did. `DeleteSelection` called `InvalidateVisual` and never `AnnounceMove`, which is the **same omission [E8-T52](#e8--the-application-shell) fixed in the two node drags, in the one site `E8-T52` did not go looking for**. Deleting is a move: every slot after a deleted one shifts down. **The second half is worse and silent.** The pane held the editor's block as a *slot*, and a slot is an index into an array that renumbers - an editor open on slot 5 while slot 2 is deleted ends up on what used to be slot 6, and if that is also a code block the placement succeeds, the text stays, and the commit lands on the wrong node. The pane now holds a `CanvasNodeHandle` and re-derives the slot on every announcement. **The handle is opaque because of a layering rule that caught the first draft**: no file under `Controls` or `Views` may name `Spark.Engine`, and `ViewLayerTests.NoViewFileReferencesTheEngine` reads the source to enforce it - it went red on a `NodeId` field. `CanvasGraph` is the seam allowed to know both, so it mints and redeems the handle |
| E8-T55 | The application opens maximised | Done | **Asked for by the client.** A graph editor is a whole-screen application, and the declared 1480x900 is a window somebody resizes before they do anything else on a large display. **The screenshot and canvas-benchmark paths keep the declared size**, because both exist to be *compared across runs*: a maximised screenshot would make every documentation image the size of whatever display took it, and the canvas benchmark reports per frame drawn. **The decision lives on `StartupOptions.OpensMaximised`, not in the window**, which turns it from a claim only a source scan could check into three real assertions - and the test immediately earned it by catching `--benchmark`, which is not a switch (it is `--canvas-benchmark`) and parses silently to the default |
## E9 — 3D viewport

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E9-T1 | `IViewportRenderer` seam | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** Everything goes through it; no backend type escapes. This is the mitigation for [R6](PRD.md#12-risks) |
| E9-T2 | Scene, camera and navigation | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** |
| E9-T3 | `RenderPackage` | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** Immutable: `{ NodeId, PortIndex, ElementPath, Positions, Normals, Indices, EdgeIndices, Appearance }` |
| E9-T4 | OpenGL 3.3 core backend on Avalonia's `OpenGlControlBase` | Done | **Built in `85e3183`, with a finding that changes how every shader here is written.** Avalonia on Windows defaults to **ANGLE**, so the surface is OpenGL ES 3.0 over Direct3D 11, never desktop GL 3.3. The first run failed on a missing precision qualifier; shaders are now dialect-adaptive and a test asserts the precision statement precedes any declaration in every dialect - a defect that passes on a Linux desktop-GL machine and fails on the platform we ship to. Chosen over Silk.NET (adds a dependency without solving surface interop) and Veldrid (effectively unmaintained since around 2023 — a poor bet on a multi-year horizon) |
| E9-T5 | Software renderer | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `Spark.Viewport/Software/SoftwareViewportRenderer.cs` and `SoftwareFramebuffer.cs`: a CPU rasteriser with a depth buffer, near-plane polygon clipping, **perspective-correct** attribute interpolation, and the GL path's draw order, two-sided lighting model, camera-relative key light, selection tint and `0.0006` edge depth bias all reproduced deliberately. Three divergences are documented on the type and all three are in the direction of reproducibility: an integer-hash dither rather than `fract(sin(...))`, a DDA line walk rather than GL's diamond-exit rule, and no multisampling. **13 tests read pixels back**, including that the same scene renders byte-identically across two independently constructed renderers — the property `E9-T12` is built on. Found [N63](NOTES.md) |
| E9-T6 | One GPU buffer set per `(NodeId, PortIndex)` | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** So re-evaluating one node re-uploads one buffer |
| E9-T7 | Parallel tessellation, streamed during a run | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - tessellation is parallel and streams during a run.** Step B2: `SparkSession` gains `IProgress<NodeCompleted>?` overloads of `Evaluate` and `EvaluateAsync`, and `MainWindowViewModel.EvaluateAsync` hands the session a `GeometryStream` - built per run over a snapshot of the preview keys taken on the calling thread, since the canvas graph is the UI's to edit mid-run. It tessellates a finished node's preview ports through a `SceneBuilder` of its own, outside any lock, puts the packages in the scene (which locks itself) and raises `GeometryStreamed`, which the window posts to the UI thread as a repaint. The full publish stays the authority: the stream is closed before it, a report from a superseded run is dropped by generation, and **keys a report streamed are retired like published ones** - without that, a run superseded after streaming a node that the user then deleted would have left its geometry on screen, since only a completed run's keys were ever retired. 2 tests in `StreamedGeometryTests`: the demo's points are in the scene and the view told before `EvaluateAsync` returns; after it, the scene is the full publish. Running the session without the listener fails the first, and telling the view without putting anything in the scene fails it too. `UndoRedoTests.TheRunAfterAnUndoRecomputesNothing` raced an automatic run against an explicit one and turned flaky under the new timing; it now awaits the run its edit starts. **Step B1 done 2026-09-11 - the engine reports each node as it finishes.** `GraphEvaluator.Evaluate` gains an overload taking `IProgress<NodeCompleted>?`, and the old signature delegates with none. `NodeCompleted(Node, Outputs, FromCache)` is reported from `Run.Complete` - the one ending every node with output reaches, fresh or cached - outside the run's lock, on the scheduler thread that ran the node; a failed or skipped node reports nothing. The listener is a parameter scoped to one run rather than an event or a field on `EvaluationContext`, which `NextRun()` carries from run to run. 3 tests in `NodeCompletedReportTests`: a chain reports each node once in dependency order with the result's own values; a cached run reports all three as cached; a failed node and its downstream report nothing while an unrelated node does. Removing the report fails all three - the third because the unrelated node then goes unreported; reporting from `Fail` too fails only the third. **B2**: the session and the view model publish from the reports. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - tessellation is parallel.** It was eager and serial: `SceneBuilder.Collect` called `surface.ToMesh` and `BrepKernel.Current.Tessellate` as it walked, and `CurveDrawable`'s constructor tessellated. Now surfaces, solids, curves and meshes become drawables that keep their geometry and their bounds - which is all the marker radius needs - and do the expensive half in a `Prepare` pass that `Build` runs with `Parallel.For` up to `MaximumParallelism` (the processor count); emitting stays serial and in key order, so the packages are the one-thread build's. A solid the kernel cannot tessellate moves to `UnrenderableCount` in that pass, and `Keys()` leaves out a key that drew nothing. Safe for the kernel because nothing serialises tessellation globally and the one meeting point - the same resident solid twice - is the case `E12-T20` made safe. 3 tests in `SceneBuilderParallelTests`: a parallel build equals the serial one, key for key and buffer for buffer; two surfaces meet at a barrier inside the pass, observed rather than timed; forcing the pass onto one thread fails the barrier test, and skipping it in a parallel build fails both. **Step B**: streamed - a per-node report on the run, published from as it arrives. |
| E9-T8 | Picking through the kernel's BVH ray caster | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 - a click on geometry selects the node that drew it.** Step B: a plain left press in `ViewportControl` is a click until it is released, and released within four layout units it calls `PickAt`, which scales the point by the camera's own size over the control's bounds - device pixels on the GPU path, layout units on the software one - and raises `GeometryPicked` with the hit. The window turns the hit's `GeometryKey` into a slot through `MainWindowViewModel.SlotDrawing`, because a view may not name the engine's types (`E8-T11`), and selects it with `Canvas.SelectOnly`, so the one selection event that a canvas click raises does the rest - the inspector, and `ShowSelectionInViewport` (`E9-T9`). Any modifier keeps the press a camera gesture, and a click on nothing leaves the selection alone. 3 tests in `ViewportPickSelectionTests`; an off-by-one in `SlotDrawing` fails all three. Checked in the running application: a click on a point marker of the demo selected `Display.FromGeometryColour` on the canvas and the inspector showed it. **Seen in passing**: the selected node's point markers looked no different in that screenshot, so whether `E9-T9`'s outline shows on point markers is worth a look. **Step A done 2026-09-11 - the ray and the hit, in `Spark.Viewport`.** `Camera.RayThrough(x, y)` builds the ray from the look-at basis and the vertical field of view rather than by inverting `ViewProjection`, whose inverse loses precision at CAD's far-to-near ratio. `ViewportPicker.Pick(camera, packages, x, y)` puts the packages' boxes in the kernel's `BoundingVolumeHierarchy` (`E2-T15`) and lets `FirstHit` prune; the exact test is Moller-Trumbore for triangles, both windings, and a tolerance of `DefaultPixelTolerance` (5) pixels at the candidate's depth for edges and points, with each box inflated by that tolerance so a line on its edge is never pruned. It answers a `ViewportHit(Key, ElementPath, Distance)` or null. 5 tests in `ViewportPickerTests`, the first projecting points with the renderer's own `ViewProjection` and requiring them on the ray through their own pixels; flipping the ray's vertical sign fails it and the triangle pick, and zeroing the tolerance fails only `ALineIsPickedWithinAFewPixels`. **Step B**: a plain left click in `ViewportControl` picks, and the hit's node is selected on the canvas. Earlier note follows. [E2-T15](#e2--geometry-kernel) serves this and mesh booleans from the same code |
| E9-T9 | Selection sync between canvas and viewport | Done | **Driven 2026-08-31, and the row's claim held exactly.** `ViewportScene.SetSelectedNodes` marks every package a selected node produced, across all its ports, and `MainWindow` calls it from the same place it rebuilds the inspector — one event, two consequences. **No extra bookkeeping and no GPU work**: the scene is already keyed by `(NodeId, PortIndex)`, `WithAppearance` shares the geometry arrays, and the renderer's reconcile step already recognised a package whose buffers are the same object. Seven tests, including that the arrays are shared rather than copied and that setting the same selection twice reports no change so a repaint can be skipped. **The rendering of a selection was already covered** by the `E9-T12` golden image, whose right-hand box is selected; only the driving was missing. Original note follows. | `RenderPackage` carries `IsSelected` and the renderer honours it, but no canvas selection sets it. The row's claim that this falls out of node-keyed identity still holds; it has simply not been wired. **Falls out of node-keyed identity with no extra bookkeeping.** The `(NodeId, PortIndex, ElementPath)` tuple keys viewport buffers, selection, diagnostics and the watch panel alike — better than an object ID because it survives recomputation |
| E9-T10 | Shaded display, edge display, `Appearance` application | Done | **Built in `85e3183`/`35107f0`.** |
| E9-T11 | Headless thumbnail rendering | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `Spark.Viewport/Software/ThumbnailRenderer.cs` renders a scene to top-down RGBA with no window and no device, framing automatically when no camera is given and correcting a supplied camera's viewport size to the requested image — a camera framed for one aspect ratio and rendered at another crops silently. Nine tests. The same step wired the rasteriser into `ViewportControl` as the real GL-failure fallback and added **`--software-renderer`**, which is what makes the fallback reachable on purpose; verified by running the application and reading the PNG, not only by test. Found [N64](NOTES.md)
| E9-T12 | CI visual regression through the software renderer | Done | **Built 2026-08-31.** `tests/Spark.Viewport.Tests/VisualRegressionTests.cs` renders a fixed scene through `ThumbnailRenderer` and compares it byte for byte against `tests/corpus/viewport/reference-scene.png`. **No new CI job was needed and that is the point** — it is an ordinary test, so `dotnet test Spark.slnx` already runs it on both CI legs on every push. Failures print how many pixels moved, by how much and where, and write the render and an amplified difference map beside the golden (`E11-T11`'s complaint answered for images). `SPARK_UPDATE_GOLDEN=1` rewrites the golden and **fails anyway**, so a golden is never updated silently. **Proven to fail** by perturbing one lighting coefficient by 0.1%: 3,415 pixels differ, worst 1/255 at (200, 53). Needed a PNG codec — `Spark.Viewport/Software/PngImage.cs`, since `Spark.Viewport` takes no UI dependency — which also unlocks `spark render`
| E9-T13 | Docking the viewport must not exit the application | Done | **Reported by a user dragging the viewport out of its dock, and it was a process exit rather than an error.** Re-parenting a control is a detach followed by an attach; `ViewportControl` disposed its CPU rasteriser on detach, and a disposed rasteriser throws `ObjectDisposedException` from `Initialise` — so the next frame threw from inside `Render`, on the compositor's own dispatch, where nothing catches it ([N100](NOTES.md)). The renderer is now **replaced** rather than merely disposed, which is what the GL path already did through `OnOpenGlDeinit`/`OnOpenGlInit`. **The reason no test caught it is worth as much as the fix**: the headless session has no OpenGL, so a viewport under test is still waiting for a context when it draws and never reaches the software branch; `ForceSoftwareRenderer` puts it into the state a real machine is in just after a re-dock, and the crash then reproduces in under a second. `ViewportDockingTests` covers the re-parent, a move into another dock and a float |
| E9-T14 | The viewport's status message stopped being a watermark | Done | **Reported by the client with a screenshot: `OpenGL ready. Version 'OpenGL ES 3.0 (ANGLE …)'` across a viewport that was drawing a perfectly good scene.** The plate is the viewport's own status overlay, and `Render(DrawingContext)` already returned before drawing it once the renderer was initialised — the condition was right and had been all along. **A GL control has two surfaces and they are repainted by two different requests** ([N115](NOTES.md)): `RequestNextFrameRendering` queues a composition update and repaints the GPU surface, while the status plate **and the entire software frame** come from `Render(DrawingContext)`, which runs only when the control's *visual* is invalidated. Nothing invalidated it, so the plate recorded during startup stayed for ever and only a window resize cleared it. Two things hid this: the GL callbacks did call something that reads like *repaint*, and `OpenGlControlBase` **hides** `InvalidateVisual` with `[Obsolete] public new void InvalidateVisual() => RequestNextFrameRendering()`, so the obvious call compiles and asks for a GL frame — reaching the real one takes `((Visual)this).InvalidateVisual()`. **It was never only cosmetic**: on the software path an orbit, a re-run and a capture request all reached the compositor and none reached the screen. Every frame request now goes through `RequestFrame`, which asks for both. `ViewportRepaintTests` pins it through a capture that cannot complete without the invalidation, and is red without it. **`--screenshot` could not have caught this** — `RenderTargetBitmap.Render` re-records the overlay from scratch, so the stale plate never appears in it; verified in the running application |
| E9-T15 | The viewport exports to PNG at a chosen resolution, and its geometry to STEP or IGES | Done | **Asked for by the client**: *provide option to export the Viewport geometry into ACIS solid and png (with option to set resolution).* **The solid is STEP, not ACIS, and the substitution was agreed rather than assumed** - nothing in this repository can write ACIS, OpenCascade has no ACIS writer, and hand-rolling a SAT serialiser would produce a file nobody here could prove valid. The client was asked and chose STEP, with IGES beside it because the kernel already writes it. **The picture is rasterised on the CPU rather than read back off the GPU**, and that is a decision: `TakeCapture` hands back the frame the GL surface drew, which is exactly the size of the control, and resizing a live swap chain to 4,000 pixels to take a photograph is not something a user's window should be put through. `ThumbnailRenderer` renders any scene at any size with no display connection - it is what `spark render` uses and what the viewport itself falls back to on a machine with no GPU. **What that costs is stated**: the software rasteriser is not pixel-identical to GL, so an export is a picture of the same scene through the same camera rather than a photograph of the window. **The camera is copied, not borrowed** - rendering sets a camera's viewport size to the image, and a camera framed for one aspect and drawn at another crops silently, so handing over the live one would leave the user's view framed for a file they have already saved. A test asserts the on-screen camera is untouched. **Several solids become one file through `Brep.Join`** (`E2-T61`) rather than several files or a boolean union. **The one honest limitation is written down and asserted rather than glossed** ([E13-T18](#e13--occt-provider)): a kernel-held solid writes as `MANIFOLD_SOLID_BREP`, and a joined model writes as closed shells in a `SHELL_BASED_SURFACE_MODEL`, because joining materialises the parts and `spark_occt_import` builds shells. Most applications stitch a closed shell back into a solid on import. **Both refusals are diagnostics rather than exceptions** - no run, or no kernel - because both are ordinary. Seven UI tests plus `--export-viewport` and `--export-solids`, and `--graph solids` at 1600x1000 is what this was looked at through |

## E10 — Documentation

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E10-T1 | PRD, EPICS, TASKS, TODO, NOTES, AGENTS, CONTRIBUTING, README | Done | Written 2026-08-27 in CADScript's house style: stable IDs, tick-box acceptance criteria, a decision log naming the rejected alternative, a **Last updated** date per file, a *Known and deliberately accepted* section, and the honest distinction between compile-verified and confirmed working |
| E10-T2 | ADR-0001 to ADR-0019 | Done | Written 2026-08-27, with an index in `docs/adr/README.md`. Mostly transcription of decisions already made, **which is the point of having made them explicitly**. Three arrived by a different route and are the more interesting ones: ADR-0017 (`.spark` is canonically-formatted JSON, not a container) and ADR-0018 (property-based tests on the kernel from M1) were written because build files cited decisions that had never been recorded — see **E1-T29** — and **ADR-0019** was written because ADR-0009's premise turned out to be a misread requirement. ADR-0009 is now `Superseded by ADR-0019`, keeping its number and its text: the supersession convention exists for exactly this, and using it is better than quietly rewriting a record. Every ADR citation in the repository resolves, checked on every `dotnet test` |
| E10-T3 | Help skeleton under `docs/help/` | In progress | Three topics now exist under `concepts/`: `lacing.md` (**E4-T1**), which is the shape every other topic should follow; `geometry-basics.md`, the first topic about the kernel — points versus vectors, right-handedness, unitless coordinates, `Angle` as a type, and tolerance, with every worked example **run against the assembly** rather than written from memory; and `design-language.md`, owned by `spark-ui` and landed alongside this reconciliation, which this pass records but has not reviewed. **Recounted 2026-08-31: eleven topics exist, not three** — `concepts/evaluation.md` and `concepts/lists.md` were written the same day, the first because five diagnostic codes pointed at it and it did not exist, the second because `lacing.md` had linked to it since M0. Originally recounted as nine: — the three above plus `curves.md`, `files.md`, `finding-nodes.md`, `undo.md`, `code-blocks.md` and `solids.md`, 3,755 lines in total — and **`docs/examples/` is no longer empty**, holding `curves.spark`, `surfaces.spark` and `solids.spark`. Still missing: an index, the concept topics `lacing.md` links to as `related` (`concepts.lists`, `concepts.evaluation`), and **a topic per node family — one topic names nodes at all, and it names ten of 108**. **Deferred to the post-1.0 Help pass by [D19](PRD.md#13-decision-log)**, with the rest of E10 **Re-checked 2026-09-09:** still `In progress` by deliberate deferral rather than neglect. **D19** put the rest of Help after 1.0, and this row is the placeholder that stops the deferral being mistaken for completion. |
| E10-T4 | Help topic authoring guide | Done | **Written 2026-09-01** as [docs/HELP-AUTHORING.md](HELP-AUTHORING.md): the front-matter schema, the two words a `Status:` may be and what earns the second, what a topic owes its reader, the two kinds of sample and why they compile differently, the style rules, the commands to run before committing, and **what is generated rather than written** — node and diagnostic pages come from the live library, and `DocGenerator.cs` is named as the thing not to rebuild. **It lives in `docs/` rather than `docs/help/`**, and the existing harness is what established that: everything under `docs/help/` is end-user help and is checked as a topic, and a contributor guide is not one. Every topic must contain a worked example, and the harness enforces it |
| E10-T5 | Generated API reference pages from XML doc comments | Done | **Extended 2026-09-02: every node page now also shows the node as code**, under an *In a code block* heading — the code-block body that calls the node's underlying member, with the node's own port names as its identifiers, so pasting it gives a block with the same ports. Asked for by the client in exactly those terms. **Written by `NodeImporter` and carried on `NodeDefinition.CodeExample`, because a node key is a display name and not a code path**: `Integer.Slider` is `Number.IntegerSlider`, `List.Count` is `ListNodes.Count` and `TimeSpan.Components` is `Duration.Components`, so building the call from the key prints code that does not compile for three of the 136 — the worst possible failure rate, high enough to mislead and low enough that spot-checking finds nothing ([N93](NOTES.md)). `NodeCodeExampleTests` therefore compiles **all** of them through a real `ScriptNodeFactory` and asserts the ports it infers are the node's own. **Fully qualified deliberately** — a code block cannot host a `using` directive at all, and `Spark.Nodes.Core` must not join the default imports because it declares a `Math` ([N94](NOTES.md)). Original note follows. | **Built 2026-08-31, and generated at runtime from the live `NodeLibrary` rather than written to files.** That is the strongest available form of this row: a page produced from the definition it describes cannot drift, because there is no second copy to drift. Add a node and it has a page; rename a port and the page renames. **The content was already there** — CS1591-as-error means every node carries an XML summary and every port a description — so `NodeReference` only arranges it, and the reference was complete the day it was switched on. That is exactly what `DocGenerator`'s 1,478 hand-maintained entries were not. Original note follows. | Nobody writes it; nobody can forget it. **`DocGenerator.cs` is explicitly not ported** — 6,784 lines emitting WPF `FlowDocument`s around three hand-maintained dictionaries. Its absence is a welcome forcing function |

So the page is now **generated** by `scripts/build-progress.py` and **gated** by [`E11-T31`](#e11--quality-and-verification). **The split is the whole design**: every NUMBER is derived on each run from TASKS, the parity manifest, DYNAMO-COVERAGE's section headers, the journal's *Current state* and git; every piece of NARRATIVE - what a milestone means, what waits on a person - lives in the script's `NARRATIVE` block, because generated prose is prose nobody reads. **Two traps, both hit while writing it.** The status column is *prose* as often as it is a word (`Blocked 2026-09-11 on the toolchain`, `Done, in the half that is reachable`), so it is normalised on its leading clause and an unrecognised cell **fails** rather than being bucketed; and the column is not at a fixed index, because E13's table carries an `Est` column the other twelve do not - reading position 2 counted seventeen estimates as seventeen statuses. **The page shows the two disagreeing parity figures side by side** under a warning triangle rather than averaging them, because re-counting DYNAMO-COVERAGE's headline is [`E2-T46`](#e2--geometry-kernel)'s job |

It is that exact shape only - `a * 2;`, `a * (b);`, `2 * a * b;` and every other operator work bare - and it is recorded in the guide as a named subsection rather than smoothed over, and separately as [E6-T31](#e6--c-code-block) |

| E11-T1 | `tests/Spark.Docs.Verify`, green on an **empty corpus** | Done | Stood up 2026-08-27, before there is anything to document, so it can never be retrofitted. It has already earned itself: the ADR-citation check caught two dangling citations in build files the day it was extended to scan them — **E1-T29** |
| E11-T2 | Compile every ` ```csharp ` fence and every XML `<example>` | Done | **Every help fence compiles**, in `DocumentationSampleTests`, through the same `ReferenceCatalog` a real code block gets. **Two kinds of sample, compiled two ways**: a sample in `code-blocks.md` *is* a code block — bare identifiers are input ports, `return` produces a value — so it goes through `ScriptNodeFactory`; everywhere else a fence is ordinary C# and compiles on its own. Classified by topic id rather than by heuristic, because a heuristic would reclassify an ordinary sample containing a typo as a code block and then compile it. **Found three samples quoting variables declared nowhere**, and `AllowedSkips` is **3** — zero until 2026-09-09, when `E7-T21`'s three nuget.org samples, which cannot compile here by construction, made it a decision somebody had to take on purpose. **The XML `<example>` half landed 2026-09-10 and closed the row.** `EveryXmlExampleInTheSourceCompiles` reads the `.cs` sources rather than the generated XML — a test reading `bin/Debug/net10.0/*.xml` depends on the configuration, the target framework and on `GenerateDocumentationFile` staying on, which is three ways to turn green by finding nothing. **It caught the defect it was built for on its first run**: `SparkNodeAliasAttribute`'s example ended in a literal `…` and had never been C# ([N136](NOTES.md)). **One example is a declaration, not statements** — an attribute sits on a member — so `<code spark-scope="class">` says so on the element, written by the author rather than guessed, and what a reader copies stays clean. `EveryExampleElementInTheSourceYieldsACheckedSample` asserts that the number of `<example>` elements equals the number of samples compiled, because a parser that recognises none of them is otherwise green. `AllowedSkips` is unchanged at **3**, and no `<example>` may opt out at all | **Using the exact references and imports a real code-block node gets.** `<!-- spark:skip -->` opts out, sparingly — every skip is a sample nobody is checking |

Four checks: it opens, it evaluates without errors, it produces output, and it **re-saves byte-identically**. **Two modes, because running without a kernel provider is supported rather than broken** (ADR-0021, D18): with OpenCascade installed every example must evaluate completely and the count is asserted exactly; without it the solid examples are counted **unchecked** rather than passed, and the run still fails if nothing at all was fully evaluated. On this machine all three evaluate through the real kernel — booleans, fillets and shelling included |

| E11-T10 | Property-based kernel tests with CsCheck | Done | `tests/Spark.Geometry.Properties` exists, is in `Spark.slnx`, and its **43 properties pass** — the value layer and the curve layer. Pairs with [E2-T33](#e2--geometry-kernel), where the remaining work is named: there is no surface or solid property file, so union volume, watertight tessellation and surface `ClosestPoint` have no property behind them. **The lesson worth carrying forward is about generators, not properties:** a property whose generator never produces a value near the boundary it is testing cannot fail, and it looks exactly like a passing test  **Closed 2026-09-10 by `SurfaceProperties.cs`, and it paid for itself four times over on its first run.** Eight properties over all eight surface types at every scale: `TransformedBy` commutes with `PointAt`, a point on a surface is its own closest point, the answer is never worse than the seed grid, normals are unit and perpendicular, bounding boxes contain their surface, declared closedness matches the seam, tessellations land on the surface, and a torus tessellates watertight. **Four defects in `Surface.ClosestPoint`, one symptom** ([N139](NOTES.md)): numeric derivatives were **half** their true value at every open boundary and second derivatives about a **million times** too large, because `Sample` clamps and the denominator did not; a seed landing *on* a pole left Newton with a singular Jacobian and no way to move, so a point beside a sphere's pole came back as the pole, out by 1.6e-2; the iteration returned wherever it stopped rather than the best point it had seen; and a budget of eight iterations, sized for full Newton steps, was too small once a backtracking line search could halve them. **The traps the scouting found are in the file's own remarks**: `NormalAt` *throws* at a degeneracy, and `ToMesh` at the default tolerance collapses a 1e-6 surface to no faces at all — which then reports `IsClosed`, because an empty mesh is vacuously watertight. **And the generator lesson arrived on schedule**: written first with evenly-spaced interior fractions, the closest-point property passed against the pole defect it was meant to catch, because that defect lives inside half a seed cell of the boundary. The fractions now crowd towards both ends |

M1.5 is complete.** Five criteria, written before it ran: **C1** the editor hosts and reports a caret inside the headless session — pass; **C2** Roslyn completes a member access against a type that came from an *expression* (`p.` after `var p = new Point3d(...)` gives `X`, `DistanceTo`, `EqualsWithin`) — pass, **after two silent failures worth reading** ([N33](NOTES.md)); **C3** the caret's visual position is the caret's and survives scrolling — pass, with the finding that `GetVisualPosition` answers in document coordinates and a popup anchor is that minus `ScrollOffset`; **C4** steady-state completion is interactive — pass, and the first call is slower because Roslyn composes MEF once, which decides whether the code block warms up on open; **C5** completion crosses into the UI as Spark's own record and never as a Roslyn type — pass, asserted structurally. **Kept rather than deleted, unlike spikes (a) and (b):** their findings were prose, and this one's central claim is executable, so deleting it would keep the finding and remove what notices when it stops being true. `ScriptCompletion` in `Spark.Scripting` is the apparatus and is the first code in that project. **What the harness cannot answer:** horizontal caret placement, because headless drawing has no font metrics and every glyph measures zero wide — vertical is real, and a popup one character off is a cosmetic complaint where a popup on the wrong screenful is not ([E6-T12](#e6--c-code-block)) |

A ceiling is the right shape for this rule in any case — it holds across the whole window before the planar pipeline arrives and after, whereas an exact set had to be edited twice for one round trip. `Spark.Architecture.Tests` remains at six tests |
| E11-T23 | Dynamo parity manifest and the two-way diff test that keeps it honest | Done | **Closed 2026-09-12 by step B - the reverse direction, and the 89 name matches reviewed.** `tests/corpus/dynamo-parity-exclusions.tsv` answers the fourth check the row asks for. `Spark.Geometry` declares **877** public members by the manifest's own counting rule (methods, properties, enum values and operators; not constructors, not property accessors - `SparkGeometryMembers` was tightened to that rule, which is why the 1,021 an earlier count gave is not this number), and **790** are named by no parity row. The file excuses them **by rule and never member by member**, because 790 hand-written excuses would be the drifting artefact §7 exists to prevent: **47 rules** - five member names that are .NET plumbing (`Equals`, `GetHashCode`, `ToString`, `Deconstruct`, `op_*`) and **42 whole types Dynamo has no counterpart for** (`Transform`, `Tolerance`, `Interval`, `Angle`, `Quaternion`, `Ray`, the analytic surfaces, the Brep views, the planar layer), each carrying the §2 or §3 sentence that says why. **What no rule reaches is counted, not waved through**: **278** members sit on types that *do* map to a Dynamo type, and the budget is checked for **exact** equality rather than as a ceiling, so the number falls as rows are assessed and cannot quietly climb back. **Three rules the check enforces rather than trusts**: a type some parity row names cannot be excused wholesale (otherwise the file is a way to make the check green), an exclusion with no reason fails, and a rule that excuses nothing any more must be deleted. **The 89 review**: 88 confirmed and their reasons rewritten from *matched by name; not yet reviewed* to what was actually checked - 24 of them .NET plumbing, which is presence and nothing more - and **one demoted**, `PolySurface.Surfaces()`, where Spark returns the untrimmed surface table and Dynamo the trimmed faces ([N156](NOTES.md), `E2-T64`). Watched red four ways: a type exclusion deleted, a named type excused wholesale, a reasonless rule, and a dead rule. **Step A, 2026-09-11 - the manifest, and three of the four checks.** `tests/corpus/dynamo-parity.tsv` has **837 rows**, one per public ProtoGeometry member, generated from the **metadata** of `ProtoGeometry.dll` as shipped with Revit 2026 - types and signatures only, nothing decompiled - which is the reference surface FR-81 names. The counting rule was calibrated before a row was written: public methods including overrides, public properties and enum values, and not constructors or accessors, reproduces DYNAMO-COVERAGE's 51 types, 837 members, 618 methods, 215 properties and 4 enum values, and every per-type count in its §3 tables (Revit 2024's copy has 49 types and 756, and was not the source). **Seeded honestly**: the 169 T-Spline members `Needs a decision` (§6.2, Q12); §5's refusals `Not planned`, each with its section's reason; **89** `Done` where the member exists by the same name on the mapped Spark type, marked *matched by name; not yet reviewed*; and **472** `Unassessed` rather than guessed. **The manifest found the document's arithmetic rot on its first run**: §5 says 93 refused, its own lists add to 104, and the primitive solids carry 14 parameter-recovery properties, not 11 - so 107, and a committed surface of 561 rather than 575; DYNAMO-COVERAGE now says so. **Checks in `Spark.Docs.Verify.DynamoParityChecks`**: every row well formed (a known status, a reason for every refusal and open question, a Spark member for every Done, no member twice); the manifest agrees with DYNAMO-COVERAGE's §2 totals and §3 per-type counts; and **the rename-catcher** - every Done row names a member `Spark.Geometry.dll` declares, read from its build output as metadata so the harness still references no Spark project, and failing with the words *present, never equivalent*. It refuses to pass with no Done rows at all. A renamed member, a refusal with no reason, a deleted row and an unknown status each fail. **Step B**: the reverse direction (every public `Spark.Geometry` member named by a row or excused in an exclusions file), and reviewing the 89 name matches. Earlier note follows. The mechanism behind [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §7](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#7-how-this-document-is-kept-true), and the reason that document is a register rather than a snapshot. A checked-in `tests/corpus/dynamo-parity.tsv` carries one row per ProtoGeometry member — type, member, status, the fully-qualified Spark member, and a required reason for anything `Not planned` or `Needs a decision` — and a check in `Spark.Docs.Verify` fails when a row says `Done` and the named Spark member does not exist (**the rename-catcher**), when a public `Spark.Geometry` member is named by no row and is in no exclusions file (the reverse direction), or when the totals in the document disagree with the row counts. `Spark.Docs.Verify` is the right host precisely because it **references no Spark project** and loads the assemblies from disk, so the test cannot constrain the thing it observes. Same two-way shape as [E5-T6](#e5--node-authoring-and-library) and [E11-T13](#e11--quality-and-verification). **State the limit in the failure message:** it proves a member is present, never that it is equivalent — [ADR-0016](adr/0016-no-dynamo-interoperability.md) says equivalence is unprovable, and a green run must not be read as a claim of it. This is the `DocGenerator` lesson applied prophylactically: 1,478 hand-maintained string-keyed entries drifted until 101 of 108 constructors rendered blank, and 837 hand-maintained Markdown rows are the same artefact in a different file extension |
| E11-T24 | The screenshot harness photographs what is on screen | Done | **Four steps running were verified by a switch this journal lists as a verification step, and the switch proved less than it claimed.** Three separate faults, none of them the Roslyn race the queue item named — that one was real, was fixed with `E8-T41`, and was not the whole of it ([N114](NOTES.md)). **(1) The capture path ran against a window with no size.** `Opened` fires before the first layout, so `Canvas.Bounds` was `0,0,0,0`, `ZoomToFit` fitted into nothing, `CenterOn` centerd against a zero viewport and the in-node editor's screen rectangle was a world rectangle wearing a screen name ([N111](NOTES.md)). `UpdateLayout()` does not fix it — the platform has not sized the window yet, so measuring measures nothing — so the path now **waits** for the first layout that produces a size, with a five-second deadline because a screenshot switch that hangs is worse than one that photographs the wrong thing. **(2) A just-shown control has never been arranged.** `RenderTargetBitmap.Render` draws the tree rather than running the loop, so a completion list opened for the photograph came out with `Bounds` of `0,0,0,0` and no pixels while every property a test could read said it was open. `WriteCaptures` lays out before it renders. **(3) The pose was asked for twice and the second cancelled the first** — a fire-and-forget request from the layout handler raced the awaited one in the capture path, so the list was closed at the shutter. The layout handler no longer asks. **Two switches close the gap `N112` named**: `--frame-node` centers on the block being photographed rather than on the graph, whose bounds include a block inflated to hold its own editor; and `--code-block-type` **types** through the input path via `CodeBlockEditor.TypeText`, so bracket completion and the completion triggers actually run — the editor tests now use the same method, so the harness and the suite exercise one path. **The proof is a photograph that could not be taken before**: the in-node editor with a completion list hanging out of the block over the canvas, `Circle` preselected, from text that was typed rather than pushed |

| E11-T25 | The architecture gate scanned the build's own output | Done | **Found by running the gates on a machine that had ever run the benchmarks, which is the point of running them before starting.** `OnlyTheNativeProviderAllowsUnsafeCode` walks every `.csproj` under the repository root and skipped `obj`; BenchmarkDotNet writes a generated project per job under `bench/**/bin/Release/` and puts `AllowUnsafeBlocks` in it, so the test failed naming `BenchmarkDotNet.Autogenerated` — an offender that is not in the repository and cannot be fixed by editing anything that is. The artefact on this machine was from 2026-08-28, so the gate had been red locally for over a week while CI, which builds clean, stayed green. `obj` and `bin` are now skipped together through one `IsBuildOutput` helper, used by both loops that walk the projects — the other one, `NoProjectTargetsAWindowsSpecificFramework`, had the same hole and had simply not been unlucky yet |
| E11-T26 | One dispatch at a time into the headless session | Done | **Found by running the gates before a tag, which is the entire reason that rule exists.** `Spark.UI.Tests` failed one full run in eight with *The calling thread cannot access this object because a different thread owns it*, thrown from inside `ServerCompositor`'s constructor — a stack naming nothing in this repository — and **named a different victim each time**: `TheWheelDolliesTheCamera`, `TheDeferredFitDoesNotRepeatOnEveryLayout`. That is why it had read as unrelated flakes rather than as one defect. **There is one headless session by necessity** — `StartNew` twice in a process leaves both broken, which `E11-T21` already paid for — 26 test classes reach it, and xunit dispatches into it from sixteen threads — so two threads raced the compositor's lazy construction. `HeadlessSession.Run` now takes a semaphore, which serialises **only** the tests that need the one UI thread and leaves the arithmetic majority parallel; disabling parallelism assembly-wide was the alternative and is a much larger hammer for the same fix. **Evidence, since a flake is only fixed by not recurring**: 2 failures in 16 runs before, 0 in 26 after, and 10/10 green at `v0.3.0` — so `E8-T51`'s two new window-showing classes did not create the race but did make it likelier to fire. [N120](NOTES.md) |
| E11-T27 | A rarer flake in the same family: Avalonia's global dispatcher across parallel classes | Done | **Recorded rather than chased, mid-release, and the boundary is drawn deliberately.** After `E11-T26`, one run in 26 still failed — `MainWindowViewModelTests.APresetMovesTheTicksTheSameWayAToggleDoes`, a **pure view-model test that shows no window** and therefore never passes through `HeadlessSession.Run`'s turnstile. The mechanism is the same family rather than the same instance: `MainWindowViewModel` posts to `Avalonia.Threading.Dispatcher.UIThread` and lazily builds a `DispatcherTimer`, and that dispatcher is process-global state the headless session owns — so a plain unit test touching it while a session test is dispatching is a race by construction. **What is not established**: whether this predates `E8-T51` (the exception was not captured, and it has not recurred in 26 runs since), and whether the real fix is one xunit collection over every Avalonia-touching class or `DisableTestParallelization` for the assembly, which costs about 16 seconds. Deciding that is `test-engineer`'s and is not something to smuggle into a release **A third victim, 2026-09-08**: `ViewportNavigationTests.TheMiddleButtonPans` failed once in a full run during `E8-T64`, then passed in isolation and in three consecutive full runs. Same family, same shape - a pure test that shows a window, failing in company and never alone. **A fourth and a fifth, 2026-09-08**, during `E2-T60` and inside five minutes: `CodeBlockOnCanvasTests.ASingleClickOnACodeBlockOpensTheEditor` failed one full run and passed the class in isolation, and `PackageBrowserTests.TheRowActionsNeedARow` failed a later full run having passed the two before it - **a different test each time, which is the tell**: a defect in any one of them would not move. Neither is anywhere near what `E2-T60` changed. **A sixth during `E8-T65`**: `CompletionGlyphTests.TheEditorIsSetUpTheWayRcsSetsItUp` failed one full run and then passed three consecutive ones. **The row now names six, across five test classes**, which retires the argument that this is rare enough to leave: at six victims the suite fails roughly one full run in three, and a suite that cries wolf is a suite people stop reading. **A tenth victim on 2026-09-09**: `GraphCanvasAlignmentTests.AnUnselectedNodeStaysWhereItIs` failed one full run during [E8-T75](#e8--ui-shell-and-node-canvas), then passed its own class 7/7 and the next full run 2810/2810. **That row is a plausible contributor rather than a bystander** and it is worth saying so: it added three tests that *show windows*, so the number of Avalonia-touching classes went up in the same commit that produced the new victim. It is a correlation of one and not a measurement, but it is the second time this row has grown while somebody was doing something else, which is the argument for taking it. **Closed 2026-09-09 by taking the blunter of the two options the row itself named.** `[assembly: Parallelization(Mode = ParallelMode.None)]` on `Spark.UI.Tests`. **The other option - one xunit collection over every Avalonia-touching class - was rejected for being a rule somebody has to keep**: a collection must gain each new class as it is written, forgetting is silent, and forgetting is exactly how this row reached ten victims across five classes while people were doing something else. An assembly attribute cannot be forgotten. **The cost was measured rather than estimated, and the row's estimate was low**: 31.7s parallel against 52-60s serialised, so **about 25 seconds, not sixteen**. **Evidence, since a flake is only fixed by not recurring**: the run immediately before the change failed, and ten consecutive full runs after it were green - which is the standard `E11-T26` set for itself and met. **`E8-T81` was raised in ignorance of this row and is withdrawn into it**; the console pane's first version reproduced this exact mechanism, a view model posting to the global dispatcher after its session had ended, which is corroboration for the diagnosis rather than a new fault. **Taken now because [E13-T19](#e13--packaging-installer-and-release) turned CI off**: the local suite is the only evidence there is, and one that fails a third of the time is not evidence. |
| E11-T28 | A guard that the solution and the working tree agree about which projects exist | Done | **Written red, on a real defect, on 2026-09-09.** `SolutionMembershipTests` asserts two things and they catch opposite failures: **every `.csproj` under `tests/` is named by `Spark.slnx`** — a test project nothing builds reports no failures because it reports nothing at all — and **no directory under `tests/` holds a `bin/` without a `.csproj` beside it**, which is what a deleted project leaves behind and what the standing verification loop will happily execute. The second assertion was red when it was written and green after `tests/Spark.Geometry.Io.Tests/` was deleted, which is AGENTS.md step 7 done in the honest order. **Membership is decided by *holds a project*, not by *is a directory*:** the first version asserted over directories, went red on `tests/corpus/` — which is data and has no project by design — and was narrowed before it was a day old, because a check that flags a legitimate thing is a check somebody suppresses. Half of the long-standing queue item *a guard that no test project reports zero tests* ([N30](NOTES.md)); the other half, a project that builds and discovers nothing, is still open |
| E11-T29 | `tests/Spark.Cli.Tests` — the first test project over the command line | Done | **Landed 2026-09-09 with `spark check`, and the gap it fills is older than that verb.** `spark run` and `spark export` shipped with **no automated test of any kind** and were verified by eye; a verb whose contract is an **exit code** is exactly the thing a person reading console output is worst at checking. Eight tests over `check`: clean graph exits zero **and says nothing whatsoever**, an erroring graph exits one and names the node, `--strict` and the wholly-failed replication that motivates it, every diagnostic on one line, a bare path and `--open` agreeing, an unrecognised option refused rather than ignored, and a file that is not a graph throwing the kind `Main` catches. **`Check` takes a `TextWriter` rather than reaching for `Console.Error`**, which is the whole reason any of that is assertable, and it is the shape the four remaining verbs will need. The project references `Spark.Cli` — an `Exe`, which is supported — rather than shelling out to the built `spark.exe`: shelling out makes the test depend on build ordering and on a path, and turns an assertion about a return value into an assertion about a process |
| E11-T30 | The parity register reads one assembly, and Spark's geometry is delivered by three | Done | **Filed and done 2026-09-12.** The rename-catcher now reads `Spark.Geometry`, `Spark.Api` and `Spark.Nodes.Core`, named once in a `Delivering` array, and a `Done` row may name a member of any of them; the failure message changed from *which Spark.Geometry does not declare* to *which no delivering assembly declares*. **The reverse direction was deliberately NOT widened** and still reads `Spark.Geometry` alone: it asks whether the **kernel's own** surface has drifted from the plan, its residue budget is a measure of that surface, and `Spark.Nodes.Core` is a node library whose public surface exists to be imported by reflection and would swamp the number. **The budget being unchanged at 285 across this commit is the proof the scoping held.** `Spark.Docs.Verify` still references no Spark project and reads every assembly from disk as metadata. **Twelve rows went from `Planned` to `Done` with no geometry written** - loft, sweep, patch, union, difference, thicken, fillet, chamfer - and eight stayed `Planned` for reasons that are now about the *operation*: `Loft` has no guide, `Sweep` takes one rail and its `bool` is **cap** where Dynamo's is keep-profile-orientation, `Thicken` has no both-sides flag, and `Offset` / `Join` want a `Surface` where the kernel takes a `Brep`. **`Surface.Repair()` is the one to remember**: it stood at `Not planned` on an argument that was entirely true - healing is behind the seam by decision (`E13-T10`) - and the conclusion was wrong, because behind the seam is still delivered. It is `IBrepKernel.Heal`. Watched red on a misspelt `Spark.Api` member. §3.3 rises to **48 of 106**. [N158](NOTES.md). **Filed out of `E2-T42` step A, which is where it first cost something.** [`DynamoParityChecks`](../tests/Spark.Docs.Verify/DynamoParityChecks.cs) reads `Spark.Geometry.dll` and nothing else - both the rename-catcher and the reverse direction. That was right while the register covered values, curves and surfaces, and it stopped being right at the modelling operations: **loft, sweep, thicken, the surface and solid booleans, fillet and chamfer are delivered by Spark**, through `Spark.Api.IBrepKernel` and the `Spark.Nodes.Core` families over it, and **twelve `Surface` rows therefore sit at `Planned` describing a capability that already works**. That is the register lying in the safe direction, which is still lying. **The rule to decide first, because it decides the rest**: is the register's subject *`Spark.Geometry`*, or *what a Spark user can do*? [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §1](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md) says capability, and FR-81 says capability, so the subject is the second - which means the check should read the assemblies that make up the delivered geometry surface (`Spark.Geometry`, `Spark.Api`, `Spark.Nodes.Core`) and a row should name whichever one holds the member. **What must not change**: `Spark.Docs.Verify` still references no Spark project and loads every assembly from disk as metadata ([§7](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#7-how-this-document-is-kept-true) is explicit that a test which referenced what it polices would constrain it), and the reverse direction's residue budget stays a per-assembly number or it becomes meaningless. **Expect the residue to jump** when `Spark.Nodes.Core` joins, because a node family is a large public surface with a different purpose; that may be the argument for keeping the reverse direction on `Spark.Geometry` alone while the rename-catcher reads all three. |
| E11-T31 | The dashboard is checked against the register on every build | Done | **The mechanism behind the client's standing instruction of 2026-09-12**, and the reason [`E10-T17`](#e10--documentation) is a generator rather than a page. AGENTS.md's own sentence is the argument: *a rule nobody enforces is a preference*. `ProgressDashboardChecks`, five tests in `Spark.Docs.Verify`. **It re-derives rather than re-renders**: the generator writes the numbers it used into a JSON island at the foot of the page, and the check parses `docs/TASKS.md` and `dynamo-parity.tsv` *itself*, by its own independent reading, then compares - so green means two readings of the register agree with the page, not that a script is deterministic. Same shape as [`E11-T23`](#e11--quality-and-verification). Checks the overall counts, **every epic's breakdown separately** (two epics can move opposite ways and leave the total alone), the five parity statuses, and that the suite figure on the page is the journal's rather than invented. **Proven to fire before being trusted**, which is the standard [N18](NOTES.md) set: closing `E2-T30` in TASKS without regenerating turned two tests red naming *the register has 359, the dashboard says 358* and the command that fixes it. **What it cannot check is named in its own remarks**: prose, layout and colour are a human's to read, and a dashboard can be accurate and still mislead |
## E12 — Embedding and release

| ID | Task | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| E12-T1 | `SparkSession` composition root and lifetime | Done | **Closed 2026-09-01 with evidence rather than code.** `SparkSession` has been the composition root since `E3`: it owns the node library, the graph, the evaluation context and the scheduler; it serialises mutation and evaluation through one gate, and cancels a run in flight rather than queueing behind it. **No UI anywhere in its reference graph**, which `Spark.Architecture.Tests` asserts rather than the comments claiming it, and `E12-T3`'s tests now evaluate a whole graph through it on a foreign thread. Scripting and packages are lazy off it, so a session that opens neither loads neither. |
| E12-T2 | `IHostServices` seam | **Deferred past 1.0** | **By [D20](PRD.md#13-decision-log), with `E12-T4`.** The contract a CAD host implements — and an interface designed without a host to try it against is speculation. Designing it after the first real add-in costs nothing that designing it now would save. **The seam that actually mattered already existed**: `IEvaluationScheduler`, and its host-thread implementation landed with `E12-T3` |
| E12-T3 | Host-thread `IEvaluationScheduler` | Done | **Built 2026-09-01.** `HostThreadEvaluationScheduler` takes **two delegates and no named host type**: whether the calling thread is the host's, and how to run a delegate on it and wait. Revit calls the second an external event, AutoCAD a document-lock invoke, a dispatcher an `Invoke`; asking for the shape rather than the name keeps the file free of all three. **Running inline when already on the host thread is not an optimisation** — a host thread services its own marshalled work, so posting to it and then blocking it is a deadlock, and it is the *first* thing that happens because an add-in evaluates in response to the host calling it. Proven load-bearing: the fake host throws on a re-entrant marshal, and removing the check fails that test in a second rather than hanging the run. The batch is marshalled **once**, not per operation, and an exception crosses back with its original stack. Nine tests over a real second thread and a real work queue. |
| E12-T4 | Prove `Spark.Host` inside a real Revit or AutoCAD add-in | **Deferred past 1.0** | **By [D20](PRD.md#13-decision-log), 2026-09-01.** Spark ships standalone: `Spark.Desktop` references `Spark.UI` and the provider and nothing else, and the only mentions of either CAD product in the source tree are doc comments on `IEvaluationScheduler`. The embedding **mechanism** ships in 1.0 and is tested — `E12-T3` runs a whole graph on a foreign thread — but a seam tested against a *fake* host has not been run against Revit's external events or AutoCAD's document lock, which is where the surprises live. **Until this is done the public wording is *designed to be embedded, seam tested, not yet run inside a CAD application*.** [PRD Q5](PRD.md#14-open-questions), which asks which host, defers with it and was always this row's first obstacle |
| E12-T5 | `spark run / check / render / export / pkg / docs / graph` | In progress | **Three verbs of seven, and `spark check` is the newest.** *`spark export`* landed 2026-08-29 and is M1's demoable: it opens a `.spark` file, restores the graph against the node library, evaluates it with **no window anywhere**, and writes the geometry — OBJ, STL, PLY, glTF, STEP and IGES, the format taken from the extension. *`spark run`* landed 2026-08-30 and prints **what a graph's watch nodes saw**, `--all` for every node, which is what a diff wants. *`spark check`* landed **2026-09-09**: the same evaluation with the printing taken away, **silent on success**, exit 1 when a node errored or the file would not open. Three decisions on it are worth more than the code. **It names the node**, which `run` does not — a build log is read by somebody who was not watching, and a code with no node attached costs an hour. **Every diagnostic is one line**, because `ArgumentOutOfRangeException` puts *Actual value was 0.* on a line of its own and half a diagnostic has lost its file, its node and its severity; the canvas keeps the break, the command line does not. And **`--strict`**, which exists because building the verb exposed a real asymmetry: a radius of zero on an unreplicated node is `SPK1046`, an **error**, and fails — the same radius fanned over eight points is `SPK1042`, *8 of 8 elements failed*, a **warning**, and passed. Both readings are defensible, so the gate asks rather than deciding. **The row's real requirement — output identical to the desktop app's — is met structurally rather than by care:** the rendering lives in `Spark.Api.ValueText`, which the canvas, the properties pane and the CLI all call, and `ValueRenderingTests` fails the day somebody reintroduces a second one. Values print in the **document's** order, because `Graph.Nodes()` walks a dictionary and two runs of one file could otherwise print the same values in a different order. `Console.OutputEncoding` is UTF-8, or the rank line's `·` mangles the moment output is redirected. **`render`, `pkg`, `docs` and `graph` are what is left**, and each arrives with the milestone that gives it something to do |
| E12-T6 | Publish the CLI as the `Spark.Tool` dotnet global tool | **Withdrawn** | Spark publishes nothing (**D11**). `PackAsTool`, `ToolCommandName` and `PackageId` have been removed from `Spark.Cli.csproj`, which now sets `<AssemblyName>spark</AssemblyName>` and produces `spark.exe`.
