The CLI ships inside the installer and the portable zip beside the desktop application, so the commands themselves are unaffected — that work is [E12-T5](#e12--embedding-and-release), and what is withdrawn is only the delivery mechanism |
| E12-T7 | NuGet packaging for the publishable projects | **Withdrawn** | **There are no publishable projects.** `IsPackable` is `false` in `Directory.Build.props` for all twelve, and no `PackageId` or package metadata exists anywhere (**D11**, [NOTES.md N14](NOTES.md)). The assembly-name-versus-package-ID splits this row used to track — `Spark.Cli` → `Spark.Tool`, `Spark.Nodes.Core` → `Spark.Nodes`, `Spark.Engine` → `Spark.Graph` — do not exist; each project's assembly name is its only name |
| E12-T8 | Self-contained single-file ReadyToRun Windows build | Done | **Answered 2026-09-01 by [D21](PRD.md#13-decision-log), and the answer is three answers.** The row's title names three things that do not share a fate. **Self-contained and single-file: no**, on the relink obligation. **ReadyToRun: no, and not for a licence reason at all** — measured at **+43.3 MB on the CLI** and **+60.5 MB on the desktop** for **51 ms against 52 ms** and **2,035 ms against 2,032 ms**. Nothing, twice. The desktop harness cannot see a saving under ~150 ms and `D21` says so; even granting one, 60 MB for a tenth of a second is a poor trade. `PublishReadyToRun` is one MSBuild property, so this is the reversible one. `publish.ps1` already did the right thing and now says why. Original note follows. **Constrained by [ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md), and the constraint arrived after the task.** The LGPL relink obligation means **no `PublishSingleFile` that seals the native libraries in, and no NativeAOT over OCCT** — OCCT ships as unmodified replaceable shared libraries beside the application. Whether single-file, trimmed or AOT publishing is compatible at all is **counsel question 2 of six** ([Q13](PRD.md#14-open-questions)); *nothing here is legal advice*. Pairs with [E13-T16](#e13--occt-provider) |
| E12-T9 | Signed Inno Setup installer | Withdrawn | **Withdrawn 2026-09-12: no certificate is being bought** (*Forget it. We are not buying any certificates*). The installer itself exists and is verified (`E13-T17`); what this row asked for beyond it was a signature, and there is no identity to sign with. **What a user sees, recorded so nobody rediscovers it as a defect**: SmartScreen shows *Windows protected your PC* on first run and needs *More info* then *Run anyway*. Three things already soften it and none of them needs a certificate: the install is per-user into `%LOCALAPPDATA%` and **unelevated**, so Windows never asks for administrator over an unsigned binary; a SHA-256 is published beside every artefact; and the release notes say plainly that nothing is signed. **A self-signed certificate would not help** - SmartScreen's reputation follows the issuer, not the presence of a signature. Reopen the day an organisation identity exists. |
| E12-T10 | Portable zip | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11 by reconciliation.** `scripts/pack-portable.ps1` packs `spark-portable-win-x64.zip`, and every release since `v0.1.0` has shipped one - `v2026.9.0`'s is 52.1 MB, in `harilalmn/Spark-Releases`. The CI job that unpacked and ran what it packed (`E12-T14`) is inert since `E13-T19`; what gates the zip now is `scripts/check-version.ps1` before upload, per AGENTS.md *Cutting a release*. |
| E12-T21 | The update check, and the badge that announces it | Done | **Built 2026-09-02, asked for by the client alongside the installer.** `UpdateCheck` in `Spark.Host` asks GitHub for Spark's own latest release, compares it with the running assembly's **informational** version, and the shell shows an accent pill in the ribbon when it is behind. Clicking it opens the release page; it does not download or install anything, and [D22a](PRD.md#13-decision-log) says why not while nothing is signed. **`SparkVersion` exists because string comparison answers this question wrongly**: `"0.10.0" < "0.9.0"` as text, so a naive check goes quiet at the tenth minor release and every test written before then passes. Prerelease ordering is implemented properly too, because MinVer stamps a local build as `0.1.1-alpha.0.5` — ahead of the release that exists, behind the one that does not — and treating the tail as noise would give every Spark developer a permanent badge. **[D22](PRD.md#13-decision-log) answers NFR-13 rather than stepping around it**: nothing is collected, but it is an outbound request, so it is documented, switchable off from the Help menu, refusable for a session with `--no-update-check`, and the endpoint is not configurable. **Every failure is silence** — offline, proxied, rate-limited and captive-portal HTML are all tested and all produce nothing. Fixed on the way past: `spark --version` printed `0.0.0.0` for every build ever made, because it read `Assembly.GetName().Version`, which MinVer truncates to `major.0.0.0`. **Asked for again on 2026-09-07 — a pill at the top, linking to the latest release, shown only when a newer one exists, checked at startup — which is this row, already built.** It sits in the ribbon beside Run, reads *Update available: 0.3.0*, and opens the release page. **What was genuinely outstanding was the verification**, and `v0.3.0` being published answers half of it: the real `UpdateCheck` against the real endpoint, from a pretend running version of `0.2.0`, returns `0.3.0` and `https://github.com/harilalmn/Spark/releases/tag/v0.3.0`; from `0.3.0` and from `9.9.9` it returns nothing, which is the newest-build and ahead-of-release cases. **The half still outstanding is a person with an installed older build watching their own shell light up** — the request, the comparison and the URL are now proven against production, the wiring from that result to a visible pill is proven only by `--update-badge`, which pretends |
| E12-T23 | The documents catch up with the source going closed | Done | **The client moved the repository to private and said *let us not keep it opensource*.** The tree said the opposite in a dozen places: `LICENSE` was MIT, `README.md` opened *Spark is an open-source…*, `CONTRIBUTING.md` invited pull requests under it, and **`ADR-0006` recorded MIT-plus-DCO as a deliberate decision**. **An ADR is superseded, not edited**: `ADR-0006` keeps every word and gains a status line, because its reasoning was right when written and deleting it would destroy the record that this was ever the answer - which is the one thing an ADR exists to preserve. [ADR-0025](adr/0025-source-closed-releases-public.md) says what changed. **Three things it is careful not to overstate.** The **MIT grant already made is irrevocable** for anybody who took a copy - this changes the terms going forward and no document may imply otherwise; whether anybody took one was *checked* rather than assumed, and at the moment of the change the repository had **0 forks and 0 stars**. The **OpenCascade obligations are untouched**, because they come from somebody else's licence and do not care what Spark's own says - the standing source offer moved to `Spark-Releases`' README when the repository that used to satisfy it went dark. And there is **no contributor question**, because every commit is by one author; had there been one this would have needed their agreement rather than a note. **`LICENSE` is honest about what it is not**: all rights reserved, and then plainly *not an end-user licence* - it governs this source and says nothing about what somebody downloading an installer may do, which is counsel's document and does not exist yet. Drafting something lawyerly to fill that gap would have been worse than an acknowledged placeholder. **The Autodesk trademark attribution went in the same pass**, being the same paragraphs: the README's opening line was two claims, *open-source* (now false, gone) and *independent alternative to Autodesk Dynamo Sandbox* (accurate, kept), now carrying the non-affiliation notice Autodesk's published guidelines ask for and the project had never had. It is the one item from the licensing review that needed no legal advice |
| E12-T24 | `spark export` refuses every graph with a code block | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11.** `Export` now builds a script factory when the document has code blocks, settles the package folder through `AdmitPackages` before anything is built, and takes `--no-script` and `--trust-packages` with `run`'s meanings. **A refused export writes no file**, so a script checking for the output cannot mistake a refusal for an empty graph. It takes its two streams now, as `Run` and `Check` do, which makes this the first time `export` is tested at all: 4 tests, three in `ExportVerbTests` and the package refusal in `PackageFolderTests` beside the same refusal for the other verbs. **Two mutations, each killed by a named test**: dropping the factory from `Restore` fails `ACodeBlocksCurveIsExported`; skipping `AdmitPackages` fails `ExportRefusesTheSameWayAndWritesNoFile`. Original note follows. **Found 2026-09-11 while closing [E7-T25](#e7--packages-and-extensibility)**, and confirmed by running it: `spark export --open demo.spark --out out.obj` on the client's package demo answers *This graph contains a code block and scripting is not available* and exits 1. `Export` restores the document with **no script factory at all**, so a graph holding a code block is refused whatever the block does - including when every piece of geometry comes from ordinary nodes. `run` and `check` build a factory when the document has scripts and, since `E7-T25`, settle the package folder first through `AdmitPackages`; `export` should do the same, and take `--no-script` and `--trust-packages` with the same meanings. **Not taken inside `E7-T25`** because it changes what a verb with its own row (`E12-T5`) accepts, which is a decision about that verb rather than part of reading a folder |
| E12-T11 | Release workflow gating on version == tag | Done | **Done 2026-09-02, and the workflow now publishes rather than drafts.** The gate itself — `check-version.ps1`, reading the version out of the built assembly rather than out of the build inputs — was already there; what changed is what happens after it passes. Drafting was the right call while the installer needed a person, and became a hand-crank in the middle of an automated pipeline the moment `pack-installer.ps1` existed. A tag with a hyphen publishes as a **prerelease**, so *Latest release* and the in-application update check both keep pointing at the last stable one. The release notes live in `.github/release-notes.md` rather than inside the YAML, because a PowerShell here-string inside a block scalar is two quoting rules fighting — and because those sentences deserve editing without touching the pipeline. **The notes say the build is unsigned rather than hiding it.** Pairs with [E1-T22](#e1--foundations-build-and-ci) | **Amended 2026-09-02, by the first tag failing.** `v0.1.0` built, tested and formatted and then stopped at *Build the native provider*: the job called `build-native.ps1` bare, as a developer machine can, and a hosted runner has vcpkg at `C:\vcpkg` with **no ports built in it**. No installer was packed, `gh release create` never ran, and the release page carried only the source archives GitHub generates itself. `ci.yml` had had the three steps that make this work since `E13-T15` — cache key from the port manifest, `vcpkg install opencascade:x64-windows`, then the script with `-VcpkgRoot $env:VCPKG_INSTALLATION_ROOT` — and the release job never got them. It has them now. **A step that exists in two workflows is two steps** ([N102](NOTES.md)) |
| E12-T20 | The tessellation cache ignores the tolerance it was asked for | Done | **Closed 2026-09-11, in managed code, and the native half is `E13-T21`.** The cache was OpenCascade's own: the shim's *copy* of a shape was an assignment, which shares every face, and meshing writes the triangulation onto the face - so a held shape kept its first mesh, and the mesher's rule of keeping a triangulation finer than asked for served it to every coarser request ([N151](NOTES.md)). `OcctBrepKernel.Place` now meshes a held shape in place **once**, and every later tolerance meshes a fresh import that shares nothing with it, falling back to the old behaviour if that import ever fails; a per-shape, per-tolerance cache (`ConditionalWeakTable`, four meshes a shape) serves exact repeats, so the viewport's one-tolerance case costs what it did. `ClaimInPlace` also ends the race of two threads meshing one shape. **4 tests in `TessellationToleranceTests`, every shape asserted kernel-held** - a managed shape is imported afresh on every call and could never show the defect. Removing the isolation reproduced N87 exactly: the coarse request after a fine one returned **5,756 triangles where a fresh shape gives 92**, failing `ACoarseRequestAfterAFineOneIsAsCoarseAsOnAFreshShape` and `AShapeWithTrimmedFacesKeepsItsVolumeWhenMeshedAgain`; removing the cache lookup failed `TheSameRequestTwiceReturnsTheSameMesh`. Original note follows. **Found 2026-09-01 while measuring [E12-T19](#e12--embedding-and-release)** ([N87](NOTES.md)). Tessellating one shape at 0.5 deg and then asking the same shape for 28.6 deg returns the **fine** mesh — 1,099,460 triangles where a fresh shape gives 1,332. The cache is keyed on the shape and not on the tolerance. **It does not produce a wrong picture**, since a finer mesh is still valid geometry, which is why it went unnoticed; it does mean a coarse request cannot make anything cheaper once a fine one has been made, and it makes any parameter sweep over `Tessellate` measure the cache instead of the mesher. Recorded separately rather than fixed in the same step |
| E12-T19 | The solids demo stalls for 15 s between evaluation and the first frame | Done | **Fixed 2026-09-01** ([N87](NOTES.md)). One line: `SceneBuilder.DisplayTolerance` asked for an angular deflection of **0.5 degrees**, about fifty-seven times finer than the half a *radian* a mesher of this kind defaults to, and the cost against it is nowhere near linear — **17,440 ms and 1,110,772 triangles**, against **61 ms and 11,636 at six degrees**. Now six degrees: **286x faster**, a cylinder still sixty segments, and the render checked by looking. Desktop **18.2 s to 2.0 s** over four runs, matching the points demo. **Two tests keep it**, and they assert the **triangle count** rather than a time, because a wall-clock ceiling on a shared machine is the flakiest test there is and the count is what moved. **Three hypotheses were wrong first**, including a sweep that concluded the tolerance did not matter — every row after the first was a cache hit, because `Tessellate` caches against the shape and not the tolerance. Original note follows | **Found 2026-09-01 while staging a build for a hands-on** ([N86](NOTES.md)). `--graph solids` takes **18.2 s** against 2.1 s for points and 3.1 s for curves. **It is not the solver and not the evaluator**: `spark export` runs the same 26 nodes through the same provider in **~290 ms**, and `GraphEvaluator.Evaluate` on the same file does it in **31-77 ms**. **Nor is it the scheduler** — parallel is the faster of the two on this graph, 33 ms against 77 ms. What is left is tessellating a BRep into a mesh and building the viewport buffers, for **three objects**. This is precisely the gap [E12-T12](#e12--embedding-and-release) named and did not measure, and no budget covers it. **The first hypothesis was wrong and the probe that tested it was wronger**: it reported 3 ms because the test host had no kernel installed, so every solid operation failed immediately — asserting the diagnostic count is what caught it | **Wired into the nightly on 2026-09-02, by the first nightly run turning red on both legs.** The budget existed and the measurement was never taken: *tessellation: budgeted, and no `--tessellation` log was given*. The Windows leg now builds the shim the way `ci.yml`'s native job does — same cache key, so it is a restore rather than an hour — runs the `tessellate` verb and passes its log to the check; the Linux leg says `--no-tessellation`, because the shim is win-x64. **The check was right to fail**: a budgeted-but-unmeasured number is exactly what it exists to catch, and it caught it the first time it ran |
| E12-T12 | Performance pass before 1.0 | Done | **2026-09-01, and most of it was already done — which reading before measuring is what established.** Budgeted and checked nightly already: evaluation cold and warm, marshalling both directions, the scene index, and **the canvas frame against ADR-0013's own ceiling** of 16.7 ms median. `R15`'s payload was measured and reconciled 2026-08-31. Measured today, Release, 2 000 nodes: **render pass 1.2-1.4 ms median, 2.8-3.7 ms p95** — thirteen times inside the ceiling. **Two findings.** [N81](NOTES.md): the benchmark's second number, wall clock at 24 fps, reads as the claim being missed and is not; its floor is **~27 ms and does not scale with node count** (35 fps at 100 nodes), which turns the budget file's reasoning into a measurement. The output now says which number is judged — the nightly's regexes were re-run to confirm they still match. [N82](NOTES.md): **startup was measured by nothing**; it is now **48 ms** for the CLI and **3.0 s** launch-to-rendered-shell for the desktop, recorded but deliberately not budgeted in CI ([N29](NOTES.md)). **What this pass does not cover, said plainly:** the viewport's own render, and opening or saving a large document. Neither is budgeted and neither was measured |
| E12-T13 | Accessibility pass before 1.0 | Done | **2026-09-01.** The bar was written first and it is two checkable sentences ([N83](NOTES.md)): **every gesture reachable without a mouse**, **every control named**. The colour half was already done and done properly — contrast figures asserted by `PaletteContrastTests`, and Principle 4 forbidding colour as the only carrier of a state. **`AutomationProperties` appeared nowhere in the application**, so every control was anonymous to a screen reader; twenty-eight now carry names that say what the control does rather than repeating its label. **Opening, saving and running had no keyboard path at all** — the three things a user does most — and now have `Ctrl+O`, `Ctrl+S` and `F5`, with the keys in the tooltips. Ten tests read the markup as text, including one asserting the toolbar still has twenty buttons, because a regex that matches nothing passes silently. It caught the banner's install button on the first run. **What it cannot claim: no screen reader was run**, so a name is asserted to exist and not to be the label repeated; whether it reads well aloud is a judgement nobody here can make |
| E12-T14 | Ship a runnable build from M2 onward, not only at 1.0 | Done | **Built 2026-09-01.** A `portable` CI job stages the full thing, packs the zip and **runs what it packed**, on every push to `main`. Running it is the half that matters: a staged folder missing an assembly uploads perfectly and nothing else would notice. It unpacks **outside the workspace** and exports a solid, because [N79](NOTES.md) — the kernel resolver walks up to `artifacts/native/win-x64`, which is the folder CI just filled, so an in-tree check would pass on a zip with no native payload at all. Original note follows. Part of the mitigation for [R2](PRD.md#12-risks): every milestone releasable. Previously worded as *publish packages from M2 onward*; the mitigation survives **D11** intact but its vehicle is a downloadable build rather than a package feed, because nothing is published to nuget.org |
| E12-T15 | State publicly that exact NURBS booleans and solid fillet/chamfer are out of scope for 1.0 | **Withdrawn** | **The sentence is no longer true.** Exact booleans, fillet and chamfer are **in** 1.0 under [ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md). Withdrawn rather than done, and it is worth saying what replaces it: the thing that now has to be said loudly, in our own words and first, is **that Spark ships OpenCascade and why that is not the dependency Spark exists to remove** ([R13](PRD.md#12-risks)). That is the README positioning paragraph, and it landed with this change rather than being left for M8 — which was the failure mode |
| E12-T16 | Crash-reporting decision, deferred | Open | **No telemetry of any kind in v1.** Opt-in crash reporting considered post-1.0, with graphs excluded from any payload. Recorded so it is not re-litigated |
| E12-T18 | Prominent notice of the OCCT dependency in the About box, and the LGPL and exception texts shipped | Done | **Closed 2026-09-01 by checking rather than by building.** The row said the licence texts and the build key were still owed. They were not: `publish.ps1` already copies `LICENSE`, `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` and `licences\*`, and the native staging copies the whole provider folder, which contains `spark_occt.buildkey.json`. **A full staged build was made and every one of them listed.** The `licences/LGPL-2.1.txt` in the repository was fetched from gnu.org and compared: identical, 26,419 bytes. What was genuinely missing was a **guard** — every one of those is a `Copy-Item` a future edit could drop, and the application runs perfectly without any of them, so nothing would notice. `publish.ps1` now refuses to finish a staged build that is missing a file it is obliged to ship, and naming the missing one; verified by moving a licence aside and watching it throw. Original note follows. **The About box is built 2026-08-31.** `ProductNotice` in `Spark.Api` holds one text that both `spark --version` and the new `AboutWindow` print, and seven tests assert the obligation itself rather than the wording: with a kernel loaded the notice must name Open CASCADE, LGPL-2.1, dynamic linking and replaceability; without one it must not claim to link something absent. `IBrepKernel.Description` was added as a **default interface member** so the shell can name the provider's version without referencing it — additive, so a provider built against the old contract still works. **This row was listed as blocked on a person and was not**: it needed a dialog. **Still owed: shipping the licence texts and the build key**, which belongs with the installer. The user-facing half of [E13-T16](#e13--occt-provider). Ships the licence texts, the OCCT version, and the build key `(occt-tag, vcpkg-baseline, shim-source-hash, rid)` so the source offer can be honoured against a **specific artefact** rather than approximately ([R21](PRD.md#12-risks), [R22](PRD.md#12-risks)). *Nothing in this register is legal advice* **The command-line half landed 2026-08-31**: `spark --version` prints the OpenCascade version and the sentence the exception requires, or says no kernel is installed. `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` and `licences/` are written and are staged beside the binaries. **The About box itself is still owed** and is the only part of this row a dialog is needed for |
| E12-T17 | Settle whether `Spark.Host` publishes, and under which ID | **Withdrawn** | **Settled by settling the general case.** `Spark.Host` does not publish, because nothing does: `IsPackable` is `false` repository-wide and its `IsPackable=true` has been removed (**D11**). The original diagnosis was right — it was an omission rather than a deliberate choice — but the answer arrived from a direction the question did not anticipate. Withdraws [PRD Q10](PRD.md#14-open-questions) |

## E13 — OCCT provider

Seventeen tasks, all `Open`, none started. **Nothing of this epic exists in the tree** — there
is no `native/` directory, no `Spark.Geometry.Occt` project and no OCCT anywhere in it. Epic
context is [EPICS.md E13](EPICS.md#e13--occt-provider); the decisions are
[ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md) and
[ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md).

### M1.6 — the pass/fail criteria, written before the spike

**M1.6 gates [ADR-0020](adr/0020-occt-via-c-abi-shim.md) the way M1.5 gated
[ADR-0001](adr/0001-avalonia-not-wpf.md).** Two weeks, throwaway scaffolding, and its job is to
build OCCT from a pinned tag on both operating systems, drive one boolean end to end through a
minimal shim, and **measure the things nobody has measured**. It is written here rather than in
[EPICS](EPICS.md#e13--occt-provider) because a criterion is a task-register fact: it has a date, an
owner and an outcome.

**The part that actually needs deciding beforehand is what a *failure* means**, and it is the part
a spike run without criteria never gets. M1.5 is this project's own evidence that the rule works:
its bets were stated first, one of spike (b)'s three measurements failed, and the failure was
**diagnosed** — a drop-shadow blur threshold between 81% and 83% zoom — rather than argued away or
quietly rounded into a pass. Nobody had to decide in the moment whether 57 fps counted, because
that had been settled while nobody had a result to defend.

**The bars below are judgements, not measurements, and the moment to argue with them is now.**
Several are round numbers chosen for being defensible rather than derived, and each says which it
is. Arguing one down after the spike has produced a number against it is the failure mode this
whole section exists to prevent.

**What the spike builds.** A vcpkg manifest pinning OCCT 8.0.1 and a baseline; a `native/spark_occt/`
containing the smallest shim that can carry one boolean and one materialisation; a
`Spark.Geometry.Occt` with nothing in it but `LibraryImport` declarations and enough marshalling to
call them; and a harness that runs the measurements below. **The manifest and the build recipe are
kept. Everything else is deleted**, in the M1.5 sense — the shim written here is a probe, not the
first 2% of `E13-T2`.

| # | The criterion | How it is measured | Pass | What a failure means |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **M1.6-C1** | **OCCT builds from a pinned tag through a vcpkg manifest, from clean, on Windows *and* Linux** | Two from-clean builds, wall-clock recorded, with the exact `(occt-tag, vcpkg-baseline, triplet)` written down and the resulting artefact weighed | Both succeed and are reproducible from the recorded key alone | **Reopens the *packaging* half of ADR-0020 and nothing else.** The fallbacks are CMake from source without vcpkg, or prebuilt upstream binaries; both cost `E13-T15` its cache design and neither touches the choice of engine. **Budget it at two days of effort per operating system** — beyond that the vcpkg route is the wrong route, which is a finding rather than a defeat **Answered 2026-08-31, in the half that survives.** `opencascade:x64-windows` 8.0.1 built from clean through vcpkg at baseline `abb6dda5`, **1.3 hours** wall-clock on this machine, 57 DLLs installed. **The two-operating-system requirement is void under [D17](PRD.md#13-decision-log)** - **D16** made Windows the only supported system, so a Linux build is a *test technique* question (`Q15(c)`) rather than a release one. The key is `(V8_0_1-2f604eca2a, vcpkg abb6dda5cc32914d2e64d7d72b974dc301d1fc8a, x64-windows)` |
| **M1.6-C2** | **One boolean runs end to end**: managed call → `LibraryImport` → `spark_occt` → OCCT → shape back | A box minus a cylinder, on both operating systems, with the result's volume compared against the analytic value; and **a deliberate `throw` inside the entry point, to watch `catch(...)` hold** | Volume matches analytically to a relative 1e-9, on both; the deliberate throw returns an error code rather than crossing into managed frames | **This is the one criterion whose failure reopens ADR-0020 itself** — specifically the binding half. The alternatives it would send us back to are C++/CLI, which is Windows-only and reverses **D1**, and a third-party binding, which was rejected on auditability. Neither is attractive, which is exactly why this is measured in week one and not week two **PASSED 2026-08-31, and ADR-0020 stays.** `TwoOverlappingBoxesFuse` in `Spark.Geometry.Occt.Tests`: two managed `Brep`s built by `BrepPrimitives`, through `ModelWriter` and `LibraryImport` into `spark_occt_import`, fused by `BRepAlgoAPI_Fuse`, back as a resident `Brep`, tessellated, **volume 42.0** against the arithmetic's 42 and twelve faces where each box had six. The same trip runs in **C** in `native/spark_occt/test/smoke.c`, so a failure in one and not the other says which half is wrong. **One operating system, which is now all of them** |
| **M1.6-C3** | **The per-RID binary footprint is measured rather than bracketed** | Weigh the shipped payload for `win-x64` and `linux-x64`: full build, and again with Visualization excluded. Uncompressed, counting only what the installer would actually carry | **Measured and recorded, replacing [R15](PRD.md#12-risks)'s 40–160 MB bracket.** A number cannot fail this row — not taking it can | **A number over 100 MB uncompressed on `win-x64` reopens "OCCT ships in the default install"**, which is currently forced by **FR-81** and is a client-facing trade rather than an engineering one. `50a9935` expects **55–70 MB** ([the packaging survey](reference/occt-packaging-practice.md)); a measurement far outside that means the survey's per-toolkit arithmetic is wrong and `E13-T17` inherits a bigger problem than it is scoped for **MEASURED 2026-08-31: 52.0 MB uncompressed, win-x64, 58 DLLs** - what `build-native.ps1` stages, which is every OpenCascade DLL beside the shim. **The toolkits the shim actually links are 28.4 MB** (the fifteen in `CMakeLists.txt` plus `spark_occt.dll`); trimming to that set is `E13-T17`'s work and its transitive load-time dependencies are **not yet verified**, so the 52.0 is the honest figure to plan against. Either number **replaces [R15](PRD.md#12-risks)'s 40-160 MB bracket**, is inside [the packaging survey](reference/occt-packaging-practice.md)'s 55-70 MB expectation, and is **well under the 100 MB that would reopen shipping OCCT by default** **Refined 2026-08-31 by walking the closure rather than weighing the directory: 45.1 MB across 33 DLLs**, with STEP and IGES included. That is the number to plan `E13-T17` against; the 52.0 MB above is what the build script stages, which is everything the port installed |
| **M1.6-C4** | **What a `Materialise` costs on a realistic shape** — [ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md)'s entire rule rests on it being paid once | Build a ten-operation chain ending in a fillet or a shell, on a shape of **500–1 000 faces**; time the chain, then time one materialisation of its result. Report **both the absolute cost and the ratio** | Ratio **≤ 1** and absolute **≤ 250 ms**. The ratio is the honest half — it survives a change of machine, exactly as [N29](NOTES.md) argues — and 250 ms is a judgement about what feels interactive, not a measurement | **Between 1x and 5x: ADR-0021 is right and load-bearing**, and the lazy rule is the only thing making a chain affordable — record it and move on. **Above 5x, or above one second absolute: ADR-0021 is revisited**, because a rule whose cost is paid once is no comfort when once is too expensive. That revisit is caching the materialised model, or materialising partially, and it is a decision rather than a tweak **Not taken.** The chain test asserts a materialisation happens *once*; nobody has timed one on a realistic shape **ANSWERED 2026-08-31: 0.44 ms.** On a drilled plate - six holes cut into a 20x12x2 block, twelve faces and thirty edges after the cuts - the first structural question costs **0.44 ms** and **two thousand further questions cost 0.04 ms**. The arrays are built once and everything after is a field access, which is what ADR-0021 claims. The assertion in `AMaterialisationIsPaidOnceAndTheSecondQuestionIsFree` is on the **ratio**, because a bound on the milliseconds would be a bound on this machine. [N57](NOTES.md) |
| **M1.6-C5** | **A first read on OCCT's thread-safety envelope** ([Q14](PRD.md#14-open-questions), [R20](PRD.md#12-risks)) | Read the upstream source of the specific packages the shim calls, **and** stress the shim from `ParallelEvaluationScheduler` at the machine's real thread count on a replication-shaped workload | **A written rule with evidence behind it** — what may be called concurrently, at what granularity, and how we know. Not a verdict of "safe" | **Only "we did not look" fails this row.** A bad answer is a finding: the fallback is the conservative single-writer policy ADR-0020 names, and **that fallback does not exist today** — `ParallelEvaluationScheduler` sets no `MaxDegreeOfParallelism` at all, so a cap is unbuilt work that would land on `E3-T11`. A crash under concurrency is a result, not a failure of the spike **Not taken.** The shim's error state is thread-local and shapes are independent handles, which is a design that anticipates the answer rather than the answer **ANSWERED 2026-08-31, by stressing rather than by reading.** Twenty threads x twenty-five union-and-tessellate = **500 results in 2.73 s, zero failures**, every volume 42 - a race corrupting a shared table would show as a *wrong number*, which is why the assertion is on the volume. The thread-local error channel is checked too: twenty threads failing at once each read their own reason. **What is not claimed**: a single handle from two threads at once is still undefined. What is shown is that *independent work is independent*, which is the shape replication produces. [N56](NOTES.md) |
| **M1.6-C6** | **Whether `ShapeFix` can be constrained to a policy we choose** | Configure it against a shape that needs healing and see what it refuses to leave alone; read the upstream source where configuration is unclear | A yes or a no, with the evidence | **Either answer passes; only not asking fails.** If it *can* be constrained, ADR-0021's drift argument weakens and **that record's framing should be revisited**, which its own Notes already anticipate. If it cannot, the framing is right and `E13-T10`'s policy row is the substance it claims to be **Not taken, and now more pressing than it was.** `ShapeFix_Face` and `ShapeFix_Solid` are both on the import path - see [N50](NOTES.md) and [N51](NOTES.md) - so the policy question is no longer only about `Heal` **ANSWERED 2026-08-31, and the answer is yes.** `ShapeFix_Face` and `ShapeFix_Solid` expose their fixes as individually settable modes (`FixOrientationMode`, `FixMissingSeamMode`, `FixShellOrientationMode`), which is the mechanism the question was about, and the import path sets exactly the ones it wants. Measured on the behaviour rather than the API: **healing a shape that needs nothing changes nothing that can be seen** - a healed box keeps 6 faces, 12 edges, 8 vertices, 1 shell, every surface still a plane, its volume to 6 decimal places and its eight corners to 9. **ADR-0021's drift argument is therefore about what ShapeFix is *allowed* to do rather than what it does unprompted**, and its own Notes anticipated exactly that revisiting |
| **M1.6-C7** | **Whether excluding the Visualization module drops the FreeType dependency** — ADR-0020's open item 2 | Configure a build with Visualization off and inspect the resulting link | Answered from the link, not from documentation | A finding either way. It feeds **C3**'s trimmed measurement and `E13-T17`; nothing is reopened by either answer **Partly answered, by observation rather than by experiment.** The vcpkg port installs `opencascade[core,freetype]`: **FreeType is a default feature of the port**, not something the Visualization module alone drags in, so excluding Visualization is a question about the *port's* features and not only about the link. `TKV3d`, `TKOpenGl` and `TKService` are built regardless and are simply not linked. The experiment C7 actually asks for is untaken **ANSWERED 2026-08-31, from the link, and the answer is no.** The transitive DLL closure of `spark_occt.dll` contains `freetype.dll`, `TKV3d` and `TKService`, pulled in by the interchange toolkits rather than by anything Spark asks for. **Excluding Visualization would not drop FreeType while STEP is in the build.** [N53](NOTES.md) |
| **M1.6-C8** | **Whether STEP can be used without pulling in XCAF** — ADR-0020's open item 3 | Attempt a `STEPControl`-only read and write and see what the linker demands | Answered from the linker | A finding either way, and it feeds **C3** and `E13-T12`. If XCAF is unavoidable, the payload grows and the AP242 assembly/colour/name story gets *better*, which is an odd shape for a bad answer to have **Not taken.** `TKDESTEP` is present and unlinked; nothing has been asked of the linker **ANSWERED 2026-08-31, from the linker, and the answer is no.** `spark_occt.dll` does not import `TKXCAF` *directly* - fifteen toolkits, none of them XCAF - but `TKDESTEP` does, and the closure therefore carries `TKXCAF`, `TKLCAF`, `TKCAF`, `TKVCAF` and `TKCDF`. **STEP cannot be shipped without XCAF.** A finding either way passes, and this one costs nothing: the closure is **45.1 MB**, which is smaller than the 52.0 MB the staging step copies. [N53](NOTES.md) |
| **M1.6-C9** | **`E13-T3` re-estimated on evidence** — shape lifetime, the handle table and the native memory budget | Whatever C2 and C4 forced the spike to learn about handles and lifetime, converted into an estimate with its basis stated | The 2–4 week bracket is replaced by a number with a reason, or is **explicitly reaffirmed as still unknown** | Reaffirming the bracket is a pass. Quietly leaving it at 2–4 weeks while having learnt something is the failure, because the next person reads a stale bracket as a considered one **Not taken as an estimate, but the row it re-estimates has largely landed** - see `E13-T3` **ANSWERED 2026-08-31 by the row being spent rather than estimated.** `E13-T3` is `Done`: `SafeHandle`s, `OcctResidency`, `spark_occt_shape_bytes` and the cache's native budget all landed inside this run, so the 2-4 week bracket was never re-estimated - it was overtaken. **The honest form of the answer is that the bracket was wrong in the safe direction**, and the reason is [N49](NOTES.md): the ABI turned out to be thirty entry points rather than hundreds, so the handle table this row was budgeting for is three `SafeHandle` subclasses |

**The three kinds of failure, so they are not treated alike.** **C2** reopens a decision — it is the
only row that can, and it reopens the binding rather than the engine. **C1, C3 and C4** change the
plan without changing the decision: a different build route, a different distribution shape, a
different residency rule. **C5 through C9** cannot fail on their answers at all, only on not being
attempted; they are the rows where the spike is buying information rather than confirming a bet.

**What must not count as a failure**, because ADR-0020 has already accepted it and paid for it:
that OCCT is hard to debug across the boundary (**R16**), that upgrades are slow (**R17**), that its
booleans have numerical failure modes (**R18**), or that it is poor at mesh booleans. Discovering
any of those again at M1.6 is a rediscovery, not a result, and none of them reopens anything.

**What M1.6 explicitly does not answer.** Two of ADR-0020's seven open items are not build
questions and cannot be settled by a spike: **the counsel question** — whether `spark_occt` is a
work that uses the Library or a derivative work under §5 — and **whether `OcctNet.Wrapper` has a
source repository at all**. The first goes to counsel ([Q13](PRD.md#14-open-questions), items 1 and
3 before M6); the second goes to a publisher. Neither belongs on a spike's critical path, and
neither should be reported as "not done" afterwards.

**The stop rule, and the priority order if two weeks runs out.** **C1, C2 and C3 are the gate** and
must land; **C5's first read** matters most of what remains, because `R20` is a top-three risk and
`M6` needs the answer rather than the reading. C4, C6, C7, C8 and C9 are next, in that order. **A
spike that overruns is stopped and reported at whatever it reached**, with the unanswered rows named
— extending it converts a de-risk exercise into the beginning of the implementation, which is the
one thing a throwaway spike must not become.

**Who decides.** The engineering outcomes are settled by the measurements above. **A failure that
moves scope or cost — C2's binding, or C3 forcing OCCT out of the default install — is the client's
decision, not the spike's**, and the spike's job is to hand over a measured number and the named
alternatives rather than a recommendation dressed as a result.


**About the estimates.** The column below totals **24.5 weeks at the low end of every range**,
which is where the epic's headline figure of *roughly 24 weeks* comes from. `E13-T3` is the one
row carrying a genuine range rather than an estimate and `E13-T14` carries an explicit unknown,
so the honest total is **24.5 to 26.5 weeks, plus whatever the threading answer costs**. Two
weeks of it is M1.6 and lands before M2; almost all of the rest lands inside M6, which goes from
14 weeks to **20–24**. Every number here is a first estimate of work in a codebase nobody on
this project has built against, and **M1.6 exists to replace the worst of them with
measurements.**

| ID | Task | Est | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| E13-T1 | **M1.6 de-risk spike: OCCT built and driven end to end** | 2 wk | Done | **Its pass/fail criteria are written — [above](#m16--the-passfail-criteria-written-before-the-spike), nine of them, `M1.6-C1` … `M1.6-C9`, each with what a failure would mean decided in advance.** That was the half of this row that could be done before the spike, and it landed on 2026-08-29; the spike itself has not been taken. **The gate on ADR-0020, and it gates it the way M1.5 gates ADR-0001.** Build OCCT 8.0.1 from a pinned tag through a vcpkg manifest on Windows **and** Linux; expose one boolean through a minimal `spark_occt`; call it from `LibraryImport` and get a shape back. **Four of the seven open questions are answered here or nowhere:** the real per-RID binary footprint (currently bracketed **40–160 MB uncompressed and unmeasured**, [R15](PRD.md#12-risks)); whether excluding the Visualization module drops FreeType; whether STEP can be used without pulling in XCAF; and the first honest read on OCCT's threading envelope ([Q14](PRD.md#14-open-questions), [R20](PRD.md#12-risks)). Also measure what a `Materialise` costs on a realistic shape, since ADR-0021's whole rule rests on it being paid once, and find out whether `ShapeFix` can be constrained to a policy we choose. **Throwaway in the M1.5 sense only for the spike scaffolding** — the vcpkg manifest and the build recipe are kept **Taken 2026-08-31, and it was not thrown away.** Three of the nine criteria are answered - **C1** (Windows only, which is now all of them), **C2** (the boolean, which is the one that could have reopened ADR-0020, and it did not) and **C3** (52.0 MB staged, 28.4 MB linked). **C4 to C9 remain open** and are annotated row by row above. The spike was not a spike in the M1.5 sense: the shim, the vcpkg recipe and the managed provider are all kept, because they were built to the standard of the thing rather than to the standard of a probe **Done 2026-08-31: all nine criteria are answered.** `C1` (Windows, whose two-OS half is void under D17), `C2` (**passed** - the one that could have reopened ADR-0020), `C3` (52.0 MB staged, 45.1 MB closure, 224.4 MB whole payload), `C4` (0.44 ms), `C5` (500 concurrent operations, zero failures), `C6` (**yes**), `C7` and `C8` (**both no**, from the linker), `C9` (spent rather than estimated). **Six of the nine came back with the answer nobody was hoping for and every one of them cost nothing**, which is what writing the criteria in advance was for |
| E13-T2 | `native/spark_occt` skeleton: C ABI conventions, error model, entry-point discipline | 2 wk | Done | C++, **MIT, ours**, in `native/spark_occt/`. The shape of every entry point decided once and applied uniformly: opaque handles, out-parameters, an integer status, no C++ types in the signature, no exceptions crossing. **`catch(...)` in every single entry point** and `OSD::SetSignal(false)` at initialisation, because a C++ exception unwinding into a managed frame is undefined behaviour and OCCT installs signal handlers that fight the CLR's ([R19](PRD.md#12-risks)). Target an order of **350–500 exported entry points** over roughly 2–3% of OCCT's class surface — calibrated against `opencascade-rs`, which declares 538 for a comparable job. **The small surface is the upgrade strategy**, not an aesthetic preference ([R17](PRD.md#12-risks)) **Landed 2026-08-31.** `native/spark_occt/` - one header with **no C++ in it**, three translation units, CMake + Ninja + MSVC, and a smoke test written in **C** so the header's claim is checked by a compiler rather than by a reviewer. Every fallible entry point returns `spark_status` and goes through one `guard()`; the `void` releases and `spark_occt_shape_bytes` carry their own `catch(...)`, because a `void` entry point has nowhere to report from. `OSD::SetSignal(false)` runs from a function-local static, so it cannot be missed at a call site ([R19](PRD.md#12-risks)). **The entry-point estimate was 350-500 and the answer is about thirty** - the estimate was right about the work and wrong about the shape, and [N49](NOTES.md) says why |
| E13-T3 | Shape lifetime, the handle table, and the native memory budget | 2–4 wk | Done | **The row whose real cost nobody knows, and it is recorded as a range rather than an estimate.** A `Brep` stops being a pure value and carries a finalizable native resource: lifetime, disposal, finaliser ordering, and shapes outliving the provider that made them. The budget half is a requirement rather than a nicety — **[NFR-4](PRD.md#7-non-functional-requirements) is wrong as originally specified**, because an LRU evicting by estimated *managed* size cannot see native bytes, so a graph holding 200 cached `Brep`s may hold gigabytes of OCCT heap while reporting megabytes. The shim **reports** a native budget; nothing infers one. M1.6 produces the first estimate worth trusting **Largely landed 2026-08-31, and the range was never re-estimated because it was spent rather than planned.** `OcctShape`, `OcctMesh` and `OcctModel` are `SafeHandle`s - reference-counted, finalizable, released exactly once - and `OcctResidency` holds one for the life of a `Brep`. `spark_occt_shape_bytes` reports the native figure and `Brep.NativeBytes` surfaces it **without materialising**. **What is not done is the consumer**: the evaluation cache does not read the number yet, which is the NFR-4 half of this row **Done 2026-08-31: the consumer landed.** `EvaluationCache` now evicts against **two** ceilings - an entry count and a **native-memory budget** of 512 MB by default, walked out of each entry's outputs through lists and `Displayable`s. **NFR-4's failure is now a test that can be written**: ten entries against a ceiling of a thousand, evicted anyway, because what they hold is over budget. One entry is always kept, because a single result bigger than the budget would otherwise be evicted the instant it was stored and every lookup would miss on something just computed. A shape held twice is counted twice, which over-estimates and therefore evicts sooner rather than later |
| E13-T4 | `Spark.Geometry.Occt`, the `LibraryImport` layer, and the two architecture tests that bound it | 1 wk | Done | A new managed project implementing `IBrepKernel`. **Two build-policy conflicts, both real, both small, and both resolved by adding rather than relaxing.** (1) `AllowUnsafeBlocks=false` is repository policy ([NFR-15](PRD.md#7-non-functional-requirements)) and the `LibraryImport` source generator *emits* unsafe code and requires it true: keep the default `false`, opt in **for this project only** with a comment naming ADR-0020, and add an architecture test asserting it is the **only** project doing so. (2) `SparkGeometryTakesNoThirdPartyDependencyBeyondClipper` **stays exactly as it is** and gains a **companion** rule asserting `Spark.Geometry.Occt` is referenced only by composition roots. **Relaxing either test would be the wrong repair** ([NFR-5b](PRD.md#7-non-functional-requirements)) **Landed 2026-08-31.** `Spark.Geometry.Occt` implements `IBrepKernel` over `LibraryImport`, and **both** build-policy conflicts were resolved by adding: `AllowUnsafeBlocks=true` on that project alone, asserted by `OnlyTheNativeProviderAllowsUnsafeCode`, and the reference rules by `OnlyAnApplicationReferencesTheNativeProvider` and `TheNativeProviderIsALeaf`. `SparkGeometryTakesNoThirdPartyDependencyBeyondClipper` is untouched. **`Brep.Residency` is `internal` with an `InternalsVisibleTo`** rather than public: the provider is the only assembly permitted to see a handle, and a public member would have leaked the existence of a provider into the kernel's own surface |
| E13-T5 | `Import`: Spark `Brep`, curves and surfaces to `TopoDS_Shape` | 1.5 wk | Done | One of exactly **two** crossings ([ADR-0021](adr/0021-brep-kernel-residency.md)). The index-based BRep decision ([E2-T22](#e2--geometry-kernel)) pays off here and was made partly for this reason: a flat array of indices is what marshals well across a C ABI **Landed 2026-08-31, for everything Spark can currently express.** A `Brep` crosses as one `spark_model_desc` of seventeen flat arrays; curves go as line, circle, arc, ellipse or NURBS and surfaces as plane, cylinder, cone, sphere, torus or NURBS, with **analytic staying analytic** and everything else converting exactly. **A face is built from its loops**, and the pcurves Spark does not carry are computed by `ShapeFix_Face` - the first version skipped the loops and produced a correct box and a cylinder with **square caps**, which meshes convincingly and refuses every boolean ([N51](NOTES.md)). **What is left**: a genuine curve-to-NURBS conversion for polycurves and offsets, which today interpolates and says so via `ModelWriter.Approximated` **Done 2026-08-31.** The last approximation on this path is gone: **a profile is a wire, not a curve**, so a `PolyCurve` and a `PolyLine` go out as their own segments - lines as lines, arcs as arcs - instead of through an interpolating NURBS fit. The loop table already said how; `build_wires` was reading only the curve table ([N62](NOTES.md)). What remains approximate is a *surface* with no analytic equivalent - an extrusion, a revolve, a ruled surface - which is `E2` work and is flagged by `ModelWriter.Approximated` rather than hidden |
| E13-T6 | `Materialise`: `TopoDS_Shape` to Spark `Brep`, including curve and surface extraction | 2.5 wk | Done | The other crossing, and the harder one. Includes the seam move ADR-0003's *operations only* framing does not obviously predict: **`Face.SurfaceGeometry` and `Edge.CurveGeometry` partially cross**, and they are the load-bearing members of the topology parity register ([E2-T44](#e2--geometry-kernel)). **Equality and hashing are defined on the materialised model, never on the handle** — two handles to equivalent geometry are not equal handles, and a hash of a pointer changes between runs. **Round-trip is not identity, and no test may assert that it is:** assert tolerance-bounded equivalence — volume, area, bounding box, topology counts, watertightness **Landed 2026-08-31.** `spark_occt_read` walks the shape into the same flat tables and `ModelReader` rebuilds Spark geometry from them - **a cylinder comes back a cylinder**, not a spline that happens to be round, which is the property the whole of ADR-0020 was bought for. Faces are numbered shell by shell so a shell's faces are contiguous, and **degenerate edges are left out of the numbering entirely**, so an index a caller took from a read is the edge it meant when it reaches `Fillet`. `Face.SurfaceGeometry` and `Edge.CurveGeometry` did not need the seam move this row anticipated: the surfaces and curves arrive as Spark types **Done 2026-08-31.** Nothing M6 needs is missing from this path: analytic surfaces come back analytic, curves come back as the five kinds Spark has, faces are numbered shell by shell so a shell's faces are contiguous, and degenerate edges are excluded from the numbering so an index taken from a read is the edge it meant when it reaches `Fillet`. The `Face.SurfaceGeometry` seam move this row anticipated was not needed |
| E13-T7 | Booleans, split and trim behind `IBrepKernel` | 2 wk | Done | Union, difference, intersection, split, trim. **The 32 members of [DYNAMO-COVERAGE §6.1](DYNAMO-COVERAGE.md#61-parity-on-solid-and-surface-commits-us-to-exact-solid-modelling) that cannot exist without these are the reason this epic exists.** Expect [R18](PRD.md#12-risks): booleans on tangency are a known-hard case and a mature kernel still fails them. `Result<T>` is the mechanism, and it was specified before anyone knew whose failures it would carry **Union, difference and intersection landed 2026-08-31**; **split and trim are not started**. One `spark_occt_boolean` entry point with an opcode rather than three, because every entry point is a thing that has to keep working across an upgrade. **A refusal is a value**: two solids that do not touch return `SPK1081` with the reason, which `SolidsThatDoNotTouchRefuseToIntersect` asserts **Split and trim landed 2026-08-31, so this row is done.** `spark_occt_split` uses `BRepAlgoAPI_Splitter` and keeps every piece, which a `Cut` cannot: `ASplitKeepsEveryPieceAndADifferenceDoesNot` measures both and shows the split's pieces adding back up to the block. **`Trim` is a *managed* composition of split and a point test** rather than a seventh entry point - the ABI stays small and `spark_occt_shape_contains` is useful on its own |
| E13-T8 | Fillet, chamfer, shell, thicken, draft, offset | 2 wk | Done | The other half of §6.1. Fillet on complex vertex blends is the second known-hard case ([R18](PRD.md#12-risks)) **Fillet, chamfer and shell landed 2026-08-31**; **thicken, draft and offset are not started**. Two findings worth having: a fillet that does not fit is a **refusal**, not a crash (`AFilletThatDoesNotFitIsRefused`), and **[R18](PRD.md#12-risks) is real and was met on the first realistic shape** - filleting every edge of a box fused to a tangent cylinder and then drilled took **48 seconds** at a radius that fits and refused outright at the radius that looked right. The demo graph rounds a plain box for that reason. Also found: `MakeThickSolid` with **no** face opened returns the *offset* solid rather than the wall, so hollowing subtracts it - a 4-cube shelled by 0.5 measured 27 where it should measure 37 **Offset and thicken landed 2026-08-31**; **draft is not written** and is the only thing left in this row. `Thicken` is `MakeThickSolidBySimple` and needed the same orientation fix the importer did - **a sheet has no inside until the call gives it one**, and `MakeThickSolidBySimple` follows the sheet's face normal rather than asking, so thickening a world-XY plate upwards measured -8 **Done 2026-08-31: draft angles landed and this row closes.** `spark_occt_draft` over `BRepOffsetAPI_DraftAngle`, `IBrepKernel.Draft` taking a **neutral plane** because *tilt this face by two degrees* does not say around what, and a `Solid.Draft` node. **The interesting part is that draftability is decided before asking rather than by asking**: a failed `Add` poisons the algorithm so every later one raises, and recovering with `Remove` got past that only for `Build` to raise instead. Skipping a plane whose normal is parallel to the pull is simpler and is what a moulder means. [N58](NOTES.md) |
| E13-T9 | Extrude, revolve, loft, sweep, patch | 1.5 wk | Done | Replaces [E2-T25](#e2--geometry-kernel), withdrawn. These are among the 38 members §6.1 puts behind the same seam **Extrude, revolve and loft landed 2026-08-31**; **sweep and patch are not started.** A profile crosses as the same `spark_model_desc` carrying curves and nothing else, so there is no second encoding. An **open** profile is not capped - `BRepBuilderAPI_MakeFace` on an open wire can report *done* and hand back something unusable, and the failure then surfaces two calls later as `BRep_API: command not done`, which names nothing **Done 2026-08-31: sweep and patch land and this row closes.** `spark_occt_sweep` is `BRepOffsetAPI_MakePipe` along a rail - the general case of extrude, kept separate because the straight case is what most graphs want and needs no second curve. `spark_occt_patch` is `BRepFill_Filling`, and takes **edges rather than a wire**, because a patch does not require its boundary to be one connected circuit - which is also the difference from a loft, and the reason they are separate nodes: a loft goes *through* profiles in order, a patch is handed a circuit and finds a surface for it |
| E13-T10 | Sew, heal, validate, and the `ShapeFix` policy | 1 wk | Done | Replaces [E2-T24](#e2--geometry-kernel), withdrawn. **The policy question is the substance of this row, not the API.** `ShapeFix` may legitimately merge vertices, re-parameterise edges and split faces at seams; deciding what it is *allowed* to change, and recording it, is what keeps ADR-0021's fidelity argument true in practice. E13-T1 finds out whether it can be constrained at all **Sew and heal landed 2026-08-31; validate and the `ShapeFix` policy did not.** The policy question this row calls its substance is **more pressing than it was**: `ShapeFix_Face` and `ShapeFix_Solid` are now on the *import* path, not only behind `Heal`, so what they are allowed to change is a question about every shape that crosses. See `M1.6-C6`, [N50](NOTES.md) and [N51](NOTES.md) **Done 2026-08-31.** Sew and heal landed with the provider; **validate** is `OcctBrepKernel.Check` over `BRepCheck_Analyzer`, naming how many bad faces, edges, vertices and wires; and **the policy question is answered by `M1.6-C6`** - `ShapeFix`'s fixes are individually settable modes, the import path sets exactly the ones it wants, and healing a shape that needs nothing changes nothing that can be seen. ADR-0021's drift argument is therefore about what it is *allowed* to do rather than what it does unprompted |
| E13-T11 | `Brep` tessellation behind the seam, and the NFR-8 question | 1 wk | Done | Tessellating a trimmed BRep face is genuinely hard and OCCT solves it, which is why this moves. **The consequence must be answered rather than absorbed: [NFR-8](PRD.md#7-non-functional-requirements)'s watertightness property now tests a third party's mesher**, and OCCT's is not guaranteed watertight at default deflection. Either the property holds at a deflection we choose, or the requirement is restated to say exactly what it guarantees. **It must not quietly become a suppressed test.** Mesh tessellation stays ours ([E2-T26](#e2--geometry-kernel)) **Landed 2026-08-31; the NFR-8 question is untouched.** `spark_occt_tessellate` meshes a **copy** - meshing writes into the shape and residency makes a shape shared - and takes normals from the **surface** at each node's own (u, v) rather than from the triangles, so a cylinder shades round. **A deflection is clamped to a hundred-thousandth of the bounding-box diagonal**, because a tolerance is a request for work and a curved solid will honour it without limit: 1e-6 on a two-metre sphere reached **31 GB** before the process was killed ([N52](NOTES.md)) **The NFR-8 question is answered as of 2026-08-31, and the answer is a restatement rather than a suppression.** The provider's mesh of a box has **24 naked edges**, and welding it by default would be the wrong repair: every kernel tessellates a BRep face by face, so a shared edge carries two copies of every vertex - **the mesh is geometrically closed and topologically split**, and the split is what makes shading right, because a vertex carries one normal and a welded cube shades like a ball. So `Mesh.Welded(tolerance)` is an **operation**: ask for it when you need the topology - a volume, an STL, a printer - and not when you need the shading. Measured: a box 24 vertices to 8, a cylinder 1442 to 720, a drilled plate 8676 to 4328, all closing. [N55](NOTES.md) |
| E13-T12 | STEP AP203/AP214/AP242 and IGES, read and write | 1.5 wk | Done | **Closed 2026-09-12 on the client's own verification, which is exactly the evidence this row demanded.** The discipline was *a public corpus and a third-party viewer, never our own reader* - and the client exported a `.step` from Spark, imported it into **AutoCAD**, worked on it there and reported no issues. That is a third-party CAD system reading our output and treating it as editable geometry, which is the claim STEP export exists to support. **R12 retires in practice as well as in principle.** What this does not prove, and is not claimed: IGES, AP242, assemblies, names, colours and units, none of which was in the file. Earlier note follows. Replaces [E2-T36](#e2--geometry-kernel), withdrawn, and **[R12](PRD.md#12-risks) retires**. We get more than the documented subset would have contained — assemblies, names, colours and units — and IGES free. **The validation discipline carries over verbatim and is the actual work in this row:** a public corpus and a **third-party viewer, never our own reader**. *OCCT wrote the exporter* is not evidence that our use of it is correct, which is also why [Q7](PRD.md#14-open-questions) is downgraded rather than closed **Landed 2026-08-31, and [R12](PRD.md#12-risks) can be read as retired in practice as well as in principle.** Two entry points with a format opcode, not four: `spark_occt_write_file` and `spark_occt_read_file`, dispatching on the extension (`.step`, `.stp`, `.iges`, `.igs`) with a refusal that names what it does know. STEP goes out as **AP214** - the schema most CAD systems read most reliably; AP242 is richer and nothing here has assemblies, names or colours to put in it yet. IGES goes out in **BRep mode**, because the alternative loses the topology. **A read is healed on the way in**, which is the one place ADR-0021's caution about `ShapeFix` does not apply: a shape that has just crossed a file format has no parameterisation of ours to drift from. **`spark export --out model.step` works end to end** - the solids demo writes 9 solids and 74 faces into 188 KB. **What is NOT done is the validation this row calls the substance**: a public corpus and a *third-party viewer*. The tests assert the file names `CYLINDRICAL_SURFACE` and `ADVANCED_FACE` and not `POLY_LOOP`, which is evidence a round trip cannot give and is still not a viewer **Re-checked 2026-09-09 and unchanged in substance:** the writer works and the validation this row calls the substance is still owed, because it needs a public STEP corpus and a **third-party viewer** — neither of which is a thing to write. It stays `In progress` rather than `Blocked` because the corpus half can be assembled here; only the viewer needs a person.|
| E13-T13 | Diagnostics across the boundary | 0.5 wk | Done | **[R16](PRD.md#12-risks) is the risk most likely to be underestimated by anyone who has not done this: a boolean that returns a wrong-but-valid shape is diagnosable only inside code we do not own.** Three mitigations, all cheap and all in this row: pipe OCCT's `Message_Report` into `SparkDiagnostic` so its own complaints reach the user; run `BRepCheck_Analyzer` in Debug builds; and **attach a Draw-Harness-compatible `.brep` dump to every failure**, so a bug reproduces upstream in the form OCCT's maintainers accept. The last one is what converts *we cannot fix this* into *somebody can* **The cheap third landed 2026-08-31.** Every failure carries the provider's own words: `spark_occt_last_error` is thread-local, `guard()` names the operation, and `NativeErrors.Describe` turns a status into a sentence with a per-status fallback so *refused* and *failed* never read alike. **The `Message_Report` translation and the `.brep` dump are not written**, and `BRepCheck_Analyzer` runs only inside `spark_occt_shape_is_solid` **Done 2026-08-31.** All three mitigations are in. **(1)** The provider's own words reach the diagnostic, and an algorithm's `Message_Report` alerts are appended by key when a boolean or a split fails - not a translation of every alert type, which are OpenCascade's own and meant for its developers, and deliberately better than *the operation did not complete*. **(2)** A **Draw-Harness-compatible `.brep` dump** of the operation's inputs, written when `SPARK_OCCT_DUMP` names a directory and never otherwise - an exact kernel refuses constantly and correctly, so a build that wrote a file on every refusal would fill a disk with evidence of things working. **The first version dumped the managed values and therefore dumped nothing**: an imported box has no residency of its own, and the shapes worth writing are the handles actually handed across. **(3)** `BRepCheck_Analyzer` through `OcctBrepKernel.Check`, which names *how many* bad faces, edges, vertices and wires rather than only that something is wrong |
| E13-T14 | Threading policy, decided on evidence | 0.5 wk + unknown | Done | **[R20](PRD.md#12-risks) is a top-three risk and it is unresolved rather than mitigated.** Whether the parallel evaluator may call the shim concurrently, and at what granularity, is not known; documented guidance is thin. Establish it by reading the upstream source of the packages we actually call and by stressing the shim at the evaluator's real thread count, then write the rule down. The fallback is a conservative single-writer policy, which would cost throughput on exactly the workload replication makes common. [Q14](PRD.md#14-open-questions) **Decided 2026-08-31, on evidence.** The policy is: **distinct shapes may be operated on concurrently; one shape must not be touched by two threads at once.** The first half is measured - 500 concurrent operations, zero failures, every answer right - and the second is the conservative half nobody needs evidence for and the header states. R20's single-writer fallback is therefore **not** needed for the case replication actually produces, which is the case it would have cost the most. [N56](NOTES.md) |
| E13-T15 | Per-RID build and cache pipeline, and rescuing the Linux rot-guard | 1.5 wk | Done | **Landed 2026-08-31, and the Linux question it turned on is decided.** A `native` job on `windows-latest` builds the shim behind **two** caches: OpenCascade on `(occt-tag, vcpkg-baseline)` and nothing else — editing the shim must not throw away an hour of dependency build — and the shim itself on the full `(occt-tag, vcpkg-baseline, shim-source-hash, rid)`, which is **the same key `build-native.ps1` writes beside the binaries**, because an artefact and the thing that identifies it must not be able to disagree. **The job fails on a non-zero SKIP count**, because a green provider run that skipped everything is the failure this job exists to catch. **The ubuntu leg survives and never builds the provider** — see **D18**: the rot-guard argument is void under D16, the *second implementation of the same arithmetic* argument stands on its own, and the leg becomes a standing test of the supported no-provider configuration for free. **Closed by the 2026-09-09 audit.** The pipeline is written and its local half is exercised on every commit — `scripts/build-native.ps1` is what a developer runs and what the workflow calls. Its hosted half is inert with the rest of Actions (`E13-T19`), and that is [E1-T21](#e1--foundations-build-and-ci)'s blocker to carry, not a piece of unbuilt work in this row |
| E13-T16 | Licence obligations, met by the pipeline rather than by remembering | 0.5 wk | Done | Link dynamically, never statically; ship OCCT as **unmodified replaceable shared libraries**; **no `PublishSingleFile` sealing the natives and no NativeAOT over OCCT**, which constrains [E12-T8](#e12--embedding-and-release); preserve the user's right to relink; ship the LGPL text and the exception text; prominent notice in About, README, installer and release notes ([E12-T18](#e12--embedding-and-release)); offer source against a pinned tag and vcpkg baseline; keep any OCCT modification as a **numbered patch file**, never an edited tree. Spark's own code, the shim included, stays MIT. **This is not legal advice**, and **six questions go to counsel — items 1 and 3 must be answered before M6** ([Q13](PRD.md#14-open-questions), [R21](PRD.md#12-risks)) **Largely done 2026-08-31: the obligations are met by the pipeline.** **`THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md`** names OpenCascade, its licence and the exception, carries the sentence the exception actually requires, and maps every obligation to where the build meets it. **`licences/`** ships the LGPL-2.1 text and the Open CASCADE exception rather than linking them. **`spark_occt.buildkey.json`** is written beside the binaries by `build-native.ps1` recording `(rid, configuration, occt-version, vcpkg-baseline, shim-source-hash, spark-commit)` - **the shim hash is over the source files rather than a git commit on purpose**, because an uncommitted edit changes the artefact and must change the key ([R22](PRD.md#12-risks)). The notices and licences are **staged beside the binaries**, because a notice left in a source tree is one nobody who received the software can read. `spark --version` carries the notice. **Four architecture tests** assert all of it, including that nothing turns on `PublishSingleFile` or `PublishAot`. **What is not done**: `Q13`'s six counsel questions, which cannot be settled by writing more, and the About box (`E12-T18`), which needs a dialog that does not exist yet **Closed by the 2026-09-09 audit**, because the two things it still listed are not this row's to do: `Q13`'s six counsel questions cannot be settled by writing more code, and the About box landed as [E12-T18](#e12--embedding-and-release) on 2026-09-01. Every obligation this row names is met by the build and asserted by a test. |
| E13-T17 | Distribution: the per-RID matrix, installer size, signing, antivirus | 1.5 wk | Done | **The installer exists as of 2026-09-02, and Spark is no longer a portable app by default.** `installer/spark.iss` (Inno Setup, chosen because it is preinstalled on `windows-latest` and needs no tooling step) plus `scripts/pack-installer.ps1`: 173 MB staged compresses to a 35 MB setup. **Per-user, into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\Spark`, unelevated** — Spark needs nothing outside its own folder, and an unsigned installer that also demanded administrator is the worst combination to hand SmartScreen. **A fixed `AppId` is what makes *installing removes the previous version* true**, and `InstallerTests` fails the build if it ever moves; the GUID is written a second time in that test on purpose, because a shared constant would let one careless edit move both. **The runtime is chained rather than bundled**, keeping `publish.ps1`'s no-`--self-contained` line intact — and it is `Microsoft.NETCore.App`, not `Microsoft.WindowsDesktop.App`, read off `Spark.Desktop.runtimeconfig.json` rather than assumed from the words *desktop application*. **Verified by installing it**: clean install, then a second version over the top leaving one Add/Remove entry at the new version, then an uninstall leaving no folder, no entry and no shortcut. **Still needs a person: signing and the antivirus submissions.** Original note follows. | **Closed 2026-09-12.** The installer half was done on 2026-09-02 and verified by installing it; **the signing and antivirus half is withdrawn with `E12-T9`** on the client's decision not to buy a certificate, so what remained of this row is a decision rather than work. The per-RID matrix is a single RID under `D16`, and the size is measured. Earlier note follows. [R15](PRD.md#12-risks). The installer grows by an amount **currently bracketed at 40–160 MB uncompressed and not yet measured** — E13-T1 measures it, and until then the bracket is the honest statement rather than a placeholder to be quietly narrowed. Code signing covers binaries we did not compile from our own source tree, and freshly signed unfamiliar native DLLs are exactly what triggers antivirus false positives **Measured and staged 2026-08-31; the parts that need an identity are not done.** `scripts/publish.ps1` produces a **folder that runs**: the desktop application, `spark.exe`, the native provider, the licence texts and the notices, all in one place, verified by running `spark --version`, `spark export --out part.step` and the solids demo **from the staged folder**. **The number R15 bracketed is now measured: 224.4 MB total, of which OpenCascade is 52.0 MB - 23%.** The other 172.4 MB is the framework-dependent .NET publish, which was there before ADR-0020 and which nobody had weighed either; **if the installer is ever too big, OpenCascade is not where to look first**, which is the opposite of what R15's framing suggests ([N60](NOTES.md)). **Trimming the kernel to its measured 45.1 MB closure is available and not done.** **What needs a person**: the installer, code signing and the antivirus submissions, none of which a script can invent an identity for **Re-checked 2026-09-09 and the row is down to its last item:** everything a script can do is done, including the installer, which shipped in `v2026.9.0`. What is left needs an identity to sign with — Authenticode signing and the antivirus submissions — and until then a first run shows SmartScreen and the release notes say so rather than hiding it.|
| E13-T18 | An imported model's closed shells should come back as solids | Blocked | **Blocked 2026-09-11 on the toolchain, beside `E13-T21`.** The fix is C++ in the shim - the promotion `spark_occt_sew` already does, moved into `spark_occt_import` - and the OpenCascade install the shim is built against is gone from this machine (`C:\dev\vcpkg`), so `scripts/build-native.ps1` refuses. What unblocks it is the `vcpkg install` in the journal's *Environment facts*, about 1.3 hours at full CPU, not started unasked. Earlier note follows. **Found by `E9-T15` and asserted rather than assumed.** `OcctBrepKernel.Borrow` hands the writer the provider's own shape when the `Brep` is resident, and otherwise re-imports the nine managed arrays through `spark_occt_import` - and the importer builds `TopoDS_Shell`s, never `TopoDS_Solid`s, even when a shell is closed. So a kernel-held solid writes to STEP as `MANIFOLD_SOLID_BREP` and any model rebuilt from arrays writes as closed shells inside a `SHELL_BASED_SURFACE_MODEL`. **This is pre-existing** - it is equally true of `BrepPrimitives.Box` and of anything read back from a file - and it became visible because exporting several solids joins them, which materialises them. **The fix is the promotion `spark_occt_sew` already does**, five lines from that function moved into the import path: if a shell closes, say so in the topology rather than leaving a shape that looks like a solid to a human and does not to a kernel. Needs the C++ toolchain and a rebuild of the shim. `InterchangeTests.AResidentSolidIsWrittenAsASolidAndAJoinedOneAsShells` records the behaviour today and will go red the moment it changes |
